

# SECTION: Cardiovascular System (Count: 140 Questions - INTERMEDIATE)


### Question 1
A client admitted with an acute exacerbation of heart failure is receiving intravenous furosemide. The nurse is monitoring the client's response to therapy. Which new assessment finding best indicates a developing complication of the condition requiring immediate intervention?

A) A dry, hacking cough that worsens when lying flat.
B) Urine output of 600 mL over the last 2 hours.
C) Trace bilateral pitting edema in the lower extremities.
D) Weight loss of 1.5 lbs (0.7 kg) since admission yesterday.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**Rationale:** A dry, hacking cough that worsens when lying flat (orthopnea) is an early sign of worsening pulmonary edema, a severe complication of a heart failure exacerbation. It indicates fluid backing up into the alveoli, requiring immediate intervention (such as oxygen therapy, positioning, and potentially more diuresis). Urine output of 600 mL (Option B) and weight loss (Option D) are expected, therapeutic responses to IV furosemide. Trace peripheral edema (Option C) is a baseline or expected finding in a client with an acute heart failure exacerbation, not a new complication requiring immediate intervention.
</details>

---

### Question 2
A patient with a history of atrial fibrillation is prescribed a daily dose of oral digoxin. Morning laboratory results show a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L (3.1 mmol/L) and a serum digoxin level of 1.2 ng/mL (1.54 nmol/L). Based on these laboratory results, what is the appropriate nursing action?

A) Administer the digoxin and document the laboratory results in the chart.
B) Request an order for a 12-lead ECG before administering the digoxin.
C) Administer the digoxin alongside an oral potassium chloride supplement.
D) Hold the digoxin and notify the healthcare provider of the laboratory results.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**Rationale:** The client's serum digoxin level is within the normal therapeutic range (0.8 - 2.0 ng/mL), but the potassium level is low (hypokalemia, normal is 3.5 - 5.0 mEq/L). Hypokalemia increases the heart's sensitivity to digoxin, placing the patient at a significantly higher risk for digoxin toxicity (such as fatal dysrhythmias) even if the digoxin level is strictly within the normal range. The safest action is to hold the medication and notify the provider to correct the potassium level first. Nurses cannot prescribe potassium supplements (Option C). Administering the medication (Option A) is unsafe due to the high risk of toxicity.
</details>

---

### Question 3
The nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the chronic management plan and lifestyle modifications?

A) "I should elevate my legs above my heart when I am resting in the recliner."
B) "I will walk until the pain starts, rest until it stops, and then resume walking."
C) "I will apply a heating pad to my lower legs on a medium setting if they feel cold."
D) "I need to wear tight, graduated compression stockings every day."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**Rationale:** The cornerstone of conservative management for peripheral arterial disease (PAD) is a structured walking program. Clients are taught to walk until they experience intermittent claudication (pain), stop and rest until the pain subsides, and then continue walking. This promotes the development of collateral circulation. Elevating the legs above the heart (Option A) decreases arterial blood flow to the extremities and worsens PAD pain (elevation is for venous disease). Using a heating pad (Option C) is contraindicated because PAD causes decreased sensation, increasing the risk of severe burns. Tight compression stockings (Option D) restrict arterial flow and are contraindicated in PAD; they are used for venous insufficiency.
</details>

---

### Question 4
A client is admitted to the progressive care unit with a severe heart failure exacerbation. On Day 1, the client's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) level is 1,200 pg/mL. On Day 3, following continuous intravenous diuretic and vasodilator therapy, the BNP level is 450 pg/mL. How should the nurse interpret this trend?

A) The client is experiencing worsening ventricular stretch and requires an increased diuretic dose.
B) The client is at risk for cardiogenic shock and needs immediate fluid resuscitation.
C) The client's condition is improving as indicated by a decrease in ventricular wall tension.
D) The client has developed a tolerance to the diuretic therapy and needs an alternative medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**Rationale:** B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a hormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching of heart muscle cells (ventricular wall tension), which occurs in heart failure. A normal BNP is typically less than 100 pg/mL. A drop from 1,200 pg/mL to 450 pg/mL over three days demonstrates a positive response to treatment (diuretics and vasodilators), indicating reduced fluid volume overload and decreased ventricular stretch. It does not indicate worsening stretch (Option A), cardiogenic shock (Option B), or tolerance to therapy (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 5
A client with new-onset atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response (RVR) has been treated for the past 48 hours with a continuous diltiazem infusion and was transitioned to oral diltiazem earlier today. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the multi-day treatment goals for this condition are being met?

A) The client's resting heart rate is maintained between 70 and 80 beats per minute.
B) The client's apical pulse remains irregularly irregular at 115 beats per minute.
C) The client exhibits normal sinus rhythm on the telemetry monitor.
D) The client reports occasional palpitations when ambulating to the bathroom.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**Rationale:** The primary goal in the acute management of atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response (RVR) is rate control, typically defined as maintaining a resting heart rate of less than 100 beats per minute (preferably 60-100 bpm). Diltiazem, a calcium channel blocker, slows conduction through the AV node to achieve this goal. Maintaining a resting heart rate between 70 and 80 bpm (Option A) indicates the medication is working properly. An irregularly irregular pulse of 115 bpm (Option B) indicates ongoing RVR and failed rate control. While returning to normal sinus rhythm (Option C) is an ideal outcome (rhythm control), calcium channel blockers are primarily used for *rate* control; normal sinus rhythm is not the guaranteed or primary pharmacological goal of diltiazem. Occasional palpitations (Option D) suggest the client is still symptomatic.
</details>
### Question 6
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the patient indicates an accurate understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I will apply a heating pad to my lower legs at night to help improve the blood flow."
B) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart whenever I am sitting down."
C) "I will walk until the pain starts, rest until it stops, and then begin walking again."
D) "I need to wear tight compression stockings daily to prevent blood from pooling."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**Reasoning:** The most effective non-pharmacological treatment for peripheral arterial disease (PAD) is a structured walking program. Patients should be taught to walk until they experience intermittent claudication (pain), rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. This process helps develop collateral circulation, improving blood flow over time. 
*Incorrect Options:* 
Option A is incorrect because patients with PAD often have decreased sensation and tissue perfusion; applying direct heat can cause severe burns. 
Option B is incorrect because elevating the legs decreases arterial blood flow to the distal extremities; legs should be kept in a dependent (lowered) position to let gravity assist arterial flow. 
Option D is incorrect because compression stockings are indicated for peripheral *venous* disease, not arterial disease. Compression further restricts arterial blood flow in PAD.
</details>

### Question 7
A patient with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed a daily dose of digoxin for rate control. The nurse reviews the patient's morning laboratory results, which reveal a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L and a serum digoxin level of 1.2 ng/mL. What is the most appropriate nursing action?

A) Administer the digoxin and encourage the patient to eat a potassium-rich snack with breakfast.
B) Hold the digoxin and notify the healthcare provider due to the risk of medication toxicity.
C) Request an order for a 12-lead ECG to confirm the presence of the atrial fibrillation rhythm.
D) Administer the digoxin as ordered, because the digoxin level is currently within the normal range.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**Reasoning:** Even though the patient's serum digoxin level (1.2 ng/mL) is within the generally accepted therapeutic range (0.5 to 2.0 ng/mL), the patient is hypokalemic (potassium 3.1 mEq/L; normal is 3.5-5.0 mEq/L). Hypokalemia increases the myocardium's sensitivity to digoxin, significantly increasing the risk of digoxin toxicity and fatal dysrhythmias regardless of the serum drug level. The nurse must hold the medication and notify the provider. 
*Incorrect Options:* 
Options A and D are incorrect because administering the drug in the presence of uncorrected hypokalemia is unsafe. 
Option C is incorrect because while an ECG may be done later to check for signs of toxicity or rhythm changes, it does not address the immediate safety concern of the drug-lab interaction.
</details>

---

### Question 8
A patient was admitted three days ago with a severe heart failure exacerbation. The treatment plan includes daily intravenous furosemide, a 1,500 mL fluid restriction, and a low-sodium diet. Which assessment finding provides the best evidence that the multi-day treatment goals are being successfully met?

A) The patient's body weight has decreased from 86 kg to 82 kg since the day of admission.
B) The patient reports feeling significantly more thirsty than usual during the evening hours.
C) The patient's urine output has averaged approximately 1,500 mL per day since admission.
D) The patient has clear lung sounds but continues to have 3+ pitting edema in the lower extremities.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**Reasoning:** Daily weight is the most reliable indicator of fluid volume status. A weight loss of 4 kg represents approximately 4 liters of fluid removed, demonstrating that the diuretic therapy and fluid restriction are effectively resolving the volume overload associated with the heart failure exacerbation. 
*Incorrect Options:* 
Option B is a side effect of fluid restriction and diuretics, not a metric of treatment success. 
Option C is expected, but an output of 1,500 mL matched with an intake of 1,500 mL would mean the patient is simply balancing fluids rather than actively diuresing excess volume. 
Option D indicates mixed results; while clear lungs are good, the persistence of 3+ pitting edema means significant volume overload remains.
</details>

### Question 9
A patient with recurrent, symptomatic atrial fibrillation is receiving an intravenous infusion of amiodarone. Which assessment finding represents a dangerous adverse effect requiring immediate intervention, rather than an expected side effect of the medication?

A) A progressive decrease in blood pressure from 130/80 mmHg to 118/72 mmHg.
B) A decrease in the resting heart rate to 62 beats per minute.
C) The patient reporting mild nausea and a lack of appetite during mealtime.
D) The development of a new, dry, non-productive cough and dyspnea on exertion.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Adverse vs Side Effect
**Reasoning:** Amiodarone can cause severe pulmonary toxicity, which often presents early on as a dry cough, dyspnea, or pleuritic chest pain. This is a dangerous, life-threatening adverse effect that requires immediate discontinuation of the drug and notification of the provider. 
*Incorrect Options:* 
Option A (mild hypotension) and Option B (bradycardia) are expected therapeutic effects/manageable side effects of the drug's antiarrhythmic properties. 
Option C (gastrointestinal upset) is a common, non-life-threatening side effect of amiodarone administration that does not require immediately stopping the infusion.
</details>

### Question 10
The nurse is caring for a patient admitted for a severe acute heart failure exacerbation. The patient's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels over the last three days are as follows: Day 1: 1,450 pg/mL, Day 2: 980 pg/mL, Day 3: 410 pg/mL. Based on this data trend, what is the most appropriate interpretation and subsequent action?

A) The trend indicates an improvement in cardiac workload; continue the current medical regimen.
B) The patient is experiencing worsening ventricular stretch; prepare to administer an inotropic infusion.
C) The rapid drop in BNP indicates severe dehydration; anticipate an order to decrease diuretic therapy.
D) The results suggest an impending myocardial infarction; obtain stat cardiac troponin levels.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**Reasoning:** B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching, which occurs during heart failure exacerbations (fluid overload). Normal is typically < 100 pg/mL. The steadily decreasing trend from 1,450 to 410 pg/mL indicates that the ventricular stretch is decreasing, meaning the heart failure exacerbation is resolving in response to treatment. The nurse should continue the current successful medical regimen. 
*Incorrect Options:* 
Option B is incorrect because the decreasing levels show *improved*, not worsening, ventricular stretch. 
Option C is incorrect because a dropping BNP does not measure hydration status directly, only cardiac stretch. 
Option D is incorrect because BNP is not a primary biomarker for acute ischemia or myocardial infarction (troponin is).
</details>
### Question 11
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client who is recovering from a severe heart failure exacerbation. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to manage their condition at home?

A) "I should restrict my daily fluid intake to at least 3 liters to keep my kidneys flushed."
B) "I will weigh myself every morning after voiding and report a gain of 3 pounds in two days."
C) "I can use salt substitutes freely on my meals to help season my food without using sodium."
D) "I should stop taking my carvedilol immediately if I feel slightly dizzy when standing up."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Heart Failure Exacerbation

**Rationale:** Daily weights are the most reliable indicator of fluid status in heart failure. Clients should be taught to weigh themselves at the same time every day, wearing similar clothing, usually in the morning after voiding. A weight gain of 3 pounds in 2 days or 3-5 pounds in a week indicates fluid retention and impending exacerbation, requiring notification of the healthcare provider. 
*Option A is incorrect* because heart failure clients typically require fluid restriction (often 2 liters or less per day), not an increased intake. 
*Option C is incorrect* because many salt substitutes are high in potassium. Heart failure clients are often on ACE inhibitors, ARBs, or potassium-sparing diuretics, which retain potassium; using salt substitutes can lead to dangerous hyperkalemia. 
*Option D is incorrect* because beta-blockers like carvedilol should not be stopped abruptly due to the risk of rebound tachycardia and worsening heart failure. Mild orthostatic hypotension can be managed by changing positions slowly.
</details>

---

### Question 12
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed oral digoxin and sotalol. Morning laboratory results reveal a serum potassium of 2.9 mEq/L and a serum magnesium of 1.4 mEq/L. Which action by the nurse is the most appropriate?

A) Administer the medications and encourage the client to eat a banana with breakfast.
B) Hold the digoxin, administer the sotalol as prescribed, and recheck the labs in four hours.
C) Hold both medications and immediately notify the healthcare provider of the laboratory results.
D) Administer the digoxin, hold the sotalol, and request an order for a 12-lead electrocardiogram.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting hypokalemia (normal: 3.5-5.0 mEq/L) and hypomagnesemia (normal: 1.5-2.5 mEq/L). Digoxin toxicity risk is significantly increased in the presence of low potassium and low magnesium levels, even if the digoxin level is within the normal therapeutic range. Sotalol is an antiarrhythmic medication that can prolong the QT interval; administering it in the presence of hypokalemia and hypomagnesemia drastically increases the risk of life-threatening ventricular arrhythmias, such as Torsades de Pointes. Both medications must be held, and the provider must be notified to correct the electrolyte imbalances before administration. Options A, B, and D are unsafe as they involve administering medications that could cause lethal complications given the current laboratory values.
</details>

---

### Question 13
A client with a long history of peripheral vascular disease (PVD) is admitted for medical management. The nurse is performing a lower extremity assessment. Which clinical finding indicates the development of an acute complication requiring immediate medical intervention?

A) Sudden onset of severe, unrelenting resting pain and paresthesia in the right foot.
B) Thickened, opaque toenails with a complete loss of hair on the lower legs.
C) Dependent rubor of the foot that turns pale and blanches when elevated.
D) The presence of +1 pitting edema and a brownish discoloration around the medial malleolus.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** Peripheral Vascular Disease

**Rationale:** A sudden onset of severe, unrelenting resting pain accompanied by paresthesia (numbness/tingling) indicates acute limb ischemia, likely due to an acute arterial occlusion (thrombus or embolus). This is a medical emergency that requires immediate intervention to save the limb, guided by the "6 Ps" of arterial occlusion: Pain, Pallor, Pulselessness, Paresthesia, Paralysis, and Poikilothermia (coolness). 
*Options B and C are incorrect* because thickened toenails, hair loss, and dependent rubor with elevation pallor are expected, chronic signs of peripheral arterial disease (PAD). 
*Option D is incorrect* because edema and brownish (hemosiderin) staining around the ankles are classic, chronic signs of peripheral venous disease, not an acute emergency.
</details>

### Question 14
A client was admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation. The nurse reviews the client's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels over the duration of the admission: Day 1 (1,200 pg/mL), Day 2 (850 pg/mL), Day 3 (410 pg/mL). Based on this data trend, which assessment finding should the nurse expect?

A) Increased jugular venous distention when the client is positioned at a 45-degree angle.
B) A new-onset S3 ventricular gallop upon cardiac auscultation.
C) A transition from clear vesicular lung sounds to bilateral basilar crackles.
D) A noticeable reduction in lower extremity edema and improved exercise tolerance.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** Heart Failure Exacerbation

**Rationale:** B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a hormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching, which occurs during heart failure exacerbations. A normal BNP is typically less than 100 pg/mL. The downward trend from 1,200 to 410 pg/mL indicates that the stretch on the ventricles is decreasing and the client's heart failure is improving in response to therapy (e.g., diuresis). Therefore, the nurse would expect to see signs of clinical improvement, such as a reduction in peripheral edema and better breathing/exercise tolerance. 
*Options A, B, and C are incorrect* because increased jugular venous distention, a new S3 gallop, and new basilar crackles are all clinical signs of worsening fluid overload and deteriorating heart failure, which contradicts the improving BNP trend.
</details>

### Question 15
A client with symptomatic atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response (RVR) has been receiving scheduled oral diltiazem and warfarin for 48 hours. Which clinical finding most clearly indicates to the nurse that the primary treatment goals for this multi-day plan are being successfully met?

A) The client reports a resting heart rate of 76 beats per minute and denies shortness of breath.
B) The client's electrocardiogram reveals an absence of discrete P waves with a variable R-R interval.
C) The client's International Normalized Ratio (INR) has decreased from an admission baseline of 1.4 to 1.1.
D) The client experiences transient dizziness when transitioning from a supine to a standing position.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

**Rationale:** The primary goals for treating atrial fibrillation with RVR are controlling the ventricular rate, preventing thromboembolism, and relieving symptoms. Diltiazem, a calcium channel blocker, is given to slow conduction through the AV node, effectively reducing the heart rate. A resting heart rate of 76 beats per minute (under 80-100 bpm is typically the goal) alongside the resolution of symptoms (such as shortness of breath) indicates that rate control goals are being met. 
*Option B is incorrect* because this merely describes the underlying rhythm of atrial fibrillation; it does not indicate that treatment goals (rate control) have been achieved. 
*Option C is incorrect* because warfarin is given to prevent blood clots, and the therapeutic goal for INR in a client with atrial fibrillation is typically 2.0 to 3.0. A decrease to 1.1 means the client is subtherapeutic and at high risk for stroke. 
*Option D is incorrect* because transient dizziness is a potential adverse effect of blood pressure-lowering medications like diltiazem, not a therapeutic goal.
</details>
### Question 16
A client is admitted to the progressive care unit with a left-sided heart failure exacerbation. During the morning assessment, the nurse notes a change in the client's baseline status. Which clinical manifestation indicates to the nurse that the client is developing an acute and life-threatening complication of the disease process?

A) Bilateral 1+ pitting edema in the lower extremities  
B) Frothy, pink-tinged sputum with severe orthopnea  
C) A documented weight gain of 1.5 pounds over the last 24 hours  
D) A dry, hacking cough when lying in a supine position  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Lens:** Complication Recognition  
**Rationale:** Frothy, pink-tinged sputum combined with severe orthopnea is a hallmark sign of acute pulmonary edema, a severe and potentially fatal complication of left-sided heart failure exacerbation where fluid rapidly shifts into the alveoli. Immediate medical intervention is required. Bilateral 1+ pitting edema (Option A) and a 1.5-pound weight gain (Option C) are expected findings of fluid retention in heart failure but are not immediate life-threatening complications. A dry, hacking cough when supine (Option D) is an expected early sign of left-sided heart failure, whereas frothy sputum indicates the condition has progressed to an acute emergency.
</details>

---

### Question 17
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed daily oral warfarin to prevent thromboembolism. The client's morning laboratory results show an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 3.8. What is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A) Administer the warfarin as prescribed since the INR is within the therapeutic range for atrial fibrillation.  
B) Prepare to administer intravenous vitamin K to rapidly reverse the prolonged INR.  
C) Hold the scheduled dose of warfarin, notify the healthcare provider, and monitor for bleeding.  
D) Request an order for a stat activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) level before deciding whether to administer the medication.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction  
**Rationale:** The therapeutic INR range for a client with atrial fibrillation on warfarin is typically 2.0 to 3.0. An INR of 3.8 is supratherapeutic and places the client at an increased risk for bleeding. The nurse should hold the dose, notify the provider for a potential dose adjustment, and monitor the client. Option A is incorrect because 3.8 is above the therapeutic range. Option B is incorrect because while the INR is elevated, routine administration of IV vitamin K is typically reserved for extreme elevations or active, severe bleeding; simply holding the dose is often the first step for mildly supratherapeutic levels. Option D is incorrect because aPTT is used to monitor heparin therapy, not warfarin.
</details>

---

### Question 18
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required for this condition?

A) "I can use a heating pad on my lower legs at night to keep them warm."  
B) "I will wear tight compression stockings every day to improve my circulation."  
C) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting."  
D) "I will walk until the pain starts, stop and rest, and then resume walking."  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D  
**Lens:** Complex Teaching  
**Rationale:** A structured exercise program is a primary intervention for PAD. Walking until the onset of claudication (pain), stopping to rest until the pain subsides, and then resuming walking helps promote collateral circulation. Option A is incorrect because clients with PAD often have decreased sensation; using a heating pad poses a severe risk for burns. Option B is incorrect because tight compression stockings can further restrict already compromised arterial blood flow; they are used for venous, not arterial, disease. Option C is incorrect because elevating the legs above the heart decreases arterial blood flow to the distal extremities, exacerbating PAD symptoms.
</details>

### Question 19
A nurse is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a cardiovascular step-down unit. Which task is most appropriate for the nurse to delegate to the UAP for a clinically stable client with a history of atrial fibrillation?

A) Assisting the client with a partial bed bath and applying emollient lotion to the lower extremities.  
B) Assessing the client's radial pulse for an irregular rhythm before assisting them to the bathroom.  
C) Evaluating the client's telemetry strip for a return to normal sinus rhythm.  
D) Instructing the client on bleeding precautions while shaving with an electric razor.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A  
**Lens:** Delegation  
**Rationale:** Assisting with hygiene and applying lotion are standard activities of daily living (ADLs) that fall within the UAP's scope of practice for a stable client. Option B is incorrect because assessing for an irregular rhythm requires nursing judgment and assessment skills. Option C is incorrect because evaluating diagnostic data (telemetry) is strictly a nursing responsibility. Option D is incorrect because providing initial teaching or instruction is the responsibility of the registered nurse and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

### Question 20
A client has been receiving treatment for a severe heart failure exacerbation over the past three days, which includes continuous intravenous furosemide and a strict fluid restriction. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the treatment plan is effectively meeting its primary clinical goal?

A) The client's resting heart rate has decreased from 102 beats per minute to 88 beats per minute.  
B) The client's urine output was 3,500 mL over the last 24-hour period.  
C) The client's lung sounds are clear to auscultation bilaterally in all lung fields.  
D) The client reports an increase in appetite and consumed 75% of their breakfast.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals  
**Rationale:** The primary goal of diuretic therapy and fluid restriction in a heart failure exacerbation is to relieve pulmonary congestion and systemic fluid overload. Clear lung sounds indicate that the pulmonary edema has resolved, proving the treatment has effectively met its ultimate physiological goal. Option A is a positive sign but does not directly confirm the resolution of fluid overload. Option B (high urine output) is the *mechanism* by which the treatment works (the expected effect of the diuretic), but clear lungs (Option C) are the definitive proof that the output was sufficient to resolve the critical complication. Option D is an incidental positive finding but not the primary indicator of heart failure treatment success.
</details>
### Question 21
A client is currently receiving medical treatment for a severe heart failure exacerbation. While performing hourly rounds, which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the client is developing a severe, acute complication requiring immediate medical intervention?

A) A dry, hacking cough when the client attempts to lie flat in bed.
B) A new onset of pink, frothy sputum accompanied by severe dyspnea.
C) An increase in pitting edema in the bilateral lower extremities.
D) A reported weight gain of 2 pounds (0.9 kg) over the last 24 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** Heart Failure exacerbation

**Rationale:** A new onset of pink, frothy sputum with severe dyspnea is a classic hallmark of acute pulmonary edema, a life-threatening complication of a heart failure exacerbation. As pressure builds in the pulmonary venous system, fluid and red blood cells are pushed into the alveoli, requiring immediate emergency intervention (e.g., supplemental oxygen, IV loop diuretics, vasodilators). A dry cough when lying flat (orthopnea), peripheral edema, and weight gain are expected findings during a general heart failure exacerbation and do not represent a new, acute emergency compared to pulmonary edema.
</details>

### Question 22
A client admitted with new-onset atrial fibrillation is receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion and is transitioning to oral warfarin to prevent embolic events. The morning laboratory results reveal an activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) of 60 seconds and an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 1.4. Based on these laboratory results, what is the nurse's most appropriate action regarding the scheduled oral warfarin dose?

A) Administer the warfarin as scheduled because the INR is below the therapeutic target.
B) Hold the warfarin until the continuous heparin infusion is permanently discontinued.
C) Hold the warfarin because the client's aPTT is already within the therapeutic range.
D) Administer half the scheduled dose of warfarin to prevent an over-correction of the INR.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

**Rationale:** Warfarin takes several days (typically 3 to 5 days) to achieve a therapeutic INR level, which for atrial fibrillation is generally between 2.0 and 3.0. Because the client's INR is currently 1.4 (subtherapeutic), the nurse must administer the scheduled dose of warfarin. The client requires "bridging therapy," meaning the IV heparin is continued concurrently with the oral warfarin until the INR reaches the therapeutic range. Therefore, holding or halving the dose is incorrect and compromises client safety by increasing the risk of a stroke.
</details>

### Question 23
The nurse is providing comprehensive discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral vascular disease, specifically peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this chronic condition?

A) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting to reduce my swelling."
B) "I will apply a heating pad directly to my legs on the highest setting whenever they feel cold."
C) "I will walk until I feel pain, rest until the pain goes away, and then continue walking again."
D) "I need to wear tight, knee-high elastic compression stockings every day to improve my blood flow."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Peripheral Vascular Disease

**Rationale:** A key lifestyle modification for managing peripheral arterial disease (PAD) is an exercise program that promotes the development of collateral circulation. Clients are taught to walk until they experience intermittent claudication (leg pain), rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. Elevating the legs above the heart decreases arterial blood flow and worsens PAD symptoms. Applying a heating pad is dangerous due to decreased sensation, which drastically increases the risk of thermal burns. Tight compression stockings are indicated for venous disease but are contraindicated in PAD because they further restrict arterial blood flow to the lower extremities.
</details>

---

### Question 24
A registered nurse (RN) is planning care for a client admitted with peripheral vascular disease and a chronic history of atrial fibrillation. Which nursing task is most appropriate to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assess the client’s bilateral pedal pulses using a Doppler ultrasound device.
B) Educate the client on the importance of inspecting their feet daily for ulcers.
C) Determine if the client's irregular heart rate requires a 12-lead electrocardiogram.
D) Apply moisture barrier cream and lotion to the client's intact lower extremities.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Delegation
**System Focus:** Peripheral Vascular Disease & Atrial Fibrillation

**Rationale:** Applying moisture barrier cream and lotion to intact skin is a standard, routine task related to hygiene and skin protection that falls well within the scope of practice for a UAP. Assessing pedal pulses with a Doppler, educating the client on foot care, and clinically evaluating an irregular heart rate (atrial fibrillation) to determine the need for diagnostic testing all require the nursing process, clinical judgment, and advanced knowledge. These tasks must be performed by the RN.
</details>

### Question 25
A client has been receiving aggressive intravenous loop diuretics and a strict fluid restriction for the past three days to treat a severe heart failure exacerbation. Which set of evaluation data provides the strongest evidence that the multi-day treatment goals are successfully being met?

A) The client reports feeling less fatigued, and the documented urine output for the past 24 hours is 800 mL.
B) The client's lung sounds are clear to auscultation, and body weight has decreased by 4 kilograms.
C) The client's heart rate has decreased from 110 to 98 beats per minute, and pedal edema remains 3+.
D) The client is able to ambulate to the bathroom with assistance, and the brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) is 850 pg/mL.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** Heart Failure Exacerbation

**Rationale:** The primary goals in treating a heart failure exacerbation are to remove excess fluid volume and improve respiratory status. Clear lung sounds indicate resolution of pulmonary congestion, and a 4 kg weight loss accurately reflects the elimination of approximately 4 liters of excess fluid. A 24-hour urine output of 800 mL is relatively low, especially for a patient on IV diuretics. Persistent 3+ pedal edema indicates fluid volume excess is still present. A BNP of 850 pg/mL is significantly elevated (normal is typically < 100 pg/mL), indicating ongoing heart failure strain.
</details>
### Question 26
A client admitted with a left-sided heart failure exacerbation is receiving continuous intravenous furosemide. The nurse is closely monitoring the client's cardiovascular status. Which assessment finding is the earliest indication that the client's cardiac output is decreasing and the exacerbation is worsening?

A. Increasing dependent pitting edema in the bilateral lower extremities.
B. New onset of an S3 gallop and a resting heart rate of 112 beats/min.
C. A decrease in blood pressure from 138/86 mm Hg to 132/80 mm Hg.
D. A sustained urine output of 45 mL/hr over the past two hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
An S3 heart sound (ventricular gallop) is a classic, early sign of worsening left ventricular failure and fluid volume overload. Resting tachycardia is an early compensatory mechanism attempting to maintain cardiac output when stroke volume decreases. 
Option A is incorrect because dependent edema is primarily a sign of right-sided heart failure and is a late or chronic manifestation rather than an early sign of acute left-sided deterioration. Option C is incorrect as a slight decrease in blood pressure is an expected, therapeutic effect of diuresis and offloading of fluid. Option D is incorrect because a urine output of 45 mL/hr is within normal limits (>30 mL/hr) and indicates adequate renal perfusion.
</details>

---

### Question 27
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed scheduled morning doses of oral digoxin and warfarin. The nurse reviews the client’s morning laboratory results, which reveal a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L (3.1 mmol/L), an INR of 2.4, and a serum digoxin level of 1.1 ng/mL. Based on these findings, which action should the nurse take?

A. Administer both the digoxin and the warfarin as prescribed.
B. Hold the warfarin, administer the digoxin, and notify the healthcare provider.
C. Hold the digoxin, administer the warfarin, and notify the healthcare provider.
D. Hold both medications and immediately assess the client for signs of bleeding.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
The client has hypokalemia (normal potassium is 3.5-5.0 mEq/L). Hypokalemia sensitizes the myocardium to digoxin, significantly increasing the risk of life-threatening digoxin toxicity (e.g., dysrhythmias), even if the serum digoxin level is within the normal therapeutic range (0.8–2.0 ng/mL). Therefore, the nurse should hold the digoxin and notify the provider to replace the potassium. The client's INR is 2.4, which is within the expected therapeutic range (2.0–3.0) for a client with atrial fibrillation, so the warfarin should be administered as prescribed.
</details>

---

### Question 28
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client with peripheral arterial disease (PAD) who experiences frequent intermittent claudication. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required for this condition?

A. "I will apply a heating pad to my calves on a high setting whenever they begin to ache."
B. "I need to elevate my legs above the level of my heart on two pillows while I am sleeping."
C. "I should wear tight compression stockings every day to prevent blood from pooling in my feet."
D. "I will walk until I feel pain in my legs, rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
Walking is the most effective exercise for individuals with claudication from PAD. A structured walking program where the client walks until near-maximal pain is reached, rests until it resolves, and then resumes walking helps to develop collateral circulation, ultimately improving blood flow and walking endurance. 
Option A is incorrect because clients with PAD often have decreased peripheral sensation and impaired blood flow; direct application of heat can easily cause severe burns. Option B is incorrect because elevating the legs above the heart increases resistance to arterial flow, worsening ischemia and pain in PAD. Option C is incorrect because tight compression stockings are indicated for venous disease, not arterial disease, as they can further restrict arterial blood flow to the extremities.
</details>

---

### Question 29
The nurse is reviewing the daily laboratory results for a client being treated for a severe heart failure exacerbation with continuous intravenous bumetanide. 

Day 1: BNP 1,200 pg/mL, BUN 18 mg/dL, Creatinine 0.9 mg/dL 
Day 3: BNP 600 pg/mL, BUN 36 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.8 mg/dL 

What is the best interpretation of these trending laboratory values?

A. The client's fluid volume overload is improving, but the diuretic therapy is causing renal impairment.
B. The diuretic dose is insufficient to manage the heart failure and needs to be increased immediately.
C. The heart failure is worsening, leading to decreased renal perfusion and acute kidney injury.
D. The client is experiencing expected therapeutic effects of the treatment plan with no complications.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
Brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a marker of ventricular stretch and fluid overload. The decrease in BNP from 1,200 pg/mL to 600 pg/mL indicates that the heart failure exacerbation and fluid volume overload are improving. However, the doubling of the serum creatinine (from 0.9 to 1.8 mg/dL) and the elevated BUN (from 18 to 36 mg/dL) indicate acute kidney injury, likely due to over-diuresis and intravascular volume depletion from the bumetanide. 
Option B and C are incorrect because the dropping BNP demonstrates that the heart failure is actually improving, not worsening or being insufficiently treated. Option D is incorrect because doubling of creatinine is an adverse complication (acute kidney injury), not an expected outcome without complications.
</details>

---

### Question 30
A registered nurse (RN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) are caring for a client with new-onset rapid atrial fibrillation. The client is receiving a continuous diltiazem infusion and is scheduled for a transesophageal echocardiogram (TEE) and cardioversion later in the day. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A. Verify that the continuous diltiazem infusion rate matches the provider's ordered parameters.
B. Explain to the client the purpose of remaining strictly NPO prior to the scheduled procedures.
C. Apply the sequential compression devices (SCDs) to the client's lower extremities while resting in bed.
D. Assess the client's radial pulse for a pulse deficit while the RN simultaneously auscultates the apical pulse.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Delegation
Applying sequential compression devices (SCDs) is a standard, routine task that falls safely within the scope of practice of a UAP, as it does not require clinical nursing judgment. 
Option A requires verifying an active intravenous continuous medication drip, which is solely the responsibility of the RN. Option B involves patient education regarding a procedure, which cannot be delegated to a UAP. Option D involves a simultaneous clinical assessment to determine a pulse deficit, which requires nursing judgment and assessment skills beyond the UAP's scope of practice.
</details>
### Question 31
A patient with chronic heart failure is admitted for a severe exacerbation. The nurse is closely monitoring the patient for signs of acute pulmonary edema. Which assessment finding indicates an early complication of pulmonary edema?

A. A new onset of extreme anxiety and a persistent dry, hacking cough.  
B. Expectoration of copious amounts of pink, frothy sputum.  
C. Weight gain of 2 pounds over the last 48 hours.  
D. Bilateral 3+ pitting edema extending to the knees.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A </strong>  
<strong>Lens:</strong> Complication Recognition  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Acute pulmonary edema is a severe, life-threatening complication of left-sided heart failure. Early signs include extreme anxiety, restlessness, tachycardia, and a persistent dry or hacking cough caused by early fluid accumulation in the alveoli. Pink, frothy sputum (Option B) is a classic but *late* sign of severe pulmonary edema. Weight gain (Option C) and bilateral pitting edema (Option D) are signs of systemic volume overload and right-sided heart failure, not early specific signs of acute pulmonary edema.
</details>

---

### Question 32
A nurse is preparing morning medications for a patient with atrial fibrillation. The patient is prescribed oral digoxin and warfarin. Which laboratory result requires the nurse to withhold a scheduled medication and contact the healthcare provider?

A. Serum digoxin level of 1.2 ng/mL.  
B. Serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L.  
C. International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 2.6.  
D. Serum sodium level of 138 mEq/L.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B </strong>  
<strong>Lens:</strong> Drug-Lab Interaction  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Hypokalemia (normal potassium is 3.5 to 5.0 mEq/L) increases the myocardium's sensitivity to digoxin, significantly raising the risk of life-threatening digoxin toxicity (e.g., dysrhythmias) even if the serum digoxin level is within the normal therapeutic range. The nurse must hold the digoxin and notify the provider. An INR of 2.6 is highly therapeutic for a patient on warfarin for atrial fibrillation (target goal is generally 2.0-3.0). Digoxin and sodium levels provided are within normal limits.
</details>

### Question 33
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of the required lifestyle modifications?

A. "I will apply a heating pad to my calves at night to improve blood flow."  
B. "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart whenever I am sitting down."  
C. "I will walk on the treadmill until my legs start to cramp, stop to rest, and then walk again."  
D. "I need to wear tight compression stockings every day from the moment I wake up."  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C </strong>  
<strong>Lens:</strong> Complex Teaching  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> A structured exercise program is the most effective non-pharmacological intervention for PAD. Patients are taught to walk until they experience intermittent claudication (ischemic pain), stop and rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. This promotes the development of collateral circulation. Applying a heating pad (Option A) is dangerous due to decreased sensory perception in the affected limbs, posing a severe burn risk. Elevating the legs (Option B) decreases arterial blood flow to the distal extremities against gravity and worsens PAD pain. Compression stockings (Option D) are utilized for venous insufficiency, not arterial disease, as they further restrict arterial blood flow.
</details>

### Question 34
The nurse is planning care for a team of patients, including a stable patient with atrial fibrillation and a patient recovering from a heart failure exacerbation. Which task is appropriate to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A. Teach the patient with heart failure how to record their daily fluid intake at home.  
B. Assess the radial pulse for a pulse deficit in the patient with atrial fibrillation.  
C. Check the capillary refill time on the toes of the patient with peripheral vascular disease.  
D. Obtain and record the daily morning weight for the patient recovering from a heart failure exacerbation.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D </strong>  
<strong>Lens:</strong> Delegation  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The RN can delegate routine, stable tasks that do not require nursing judgment or the nursing process to a UAP. Obtaining and recording daily morning weights on a stable patient is a standard, task-oriented action suitable for a UAP. Teaching patients (Option A) and conducting clinical assessments or evaluations (Options B and C) are strictly within the RN's scope of practice and cannot be delegated to unlicensed personnel.
</details>

### Question 35
A patient was admitted three days ago with a severe heart failure exacerbation. The multi-day treatment plan includes daily intravenous furosemide, a 1,500 mL fluid restriction, and a low-sodium diet. Which finding best indicates that the collaborative treatment goals are being successfully met?

A. The patient reports a noticeable decrease in thirst despite the fluid restriction.  
B. The patient has lost 4 kilograms (8.8 lbs) since admission and lung fields are clear.  
C. The patient’s daily urine output currently matches their daily oral fluid intake.  
D. The patient's brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) level remains at 950 pg/mL.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B </strong>  
<strong>Lens:</strong> Evaluation of Goals  
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The primary goal of treating a heart failure exacerbation is diuresis (removal of excess fluid volume) to improve cardiac output and respiratory function. A 4 kg weight loss indicates a successful removal of approximately 4 liters of excess fluid. Clear lung fields indicate that pulmonary congestion has resolved. A decrease in thirst (Option A) is subjective and does not objectively measure heart failure improvement. During an exacerbation, output should temporarily *exceed* intake to achieve a negative fluid balance (Option C). A BNP of 950 pg/mL (Option D) is severely elevated and indicates persistent ventricular stretch and ongoing heart failure, rather than goal achievement.
</details>
### Question 36
A nurse is educating a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I will apply a heating pad to my calves on a low setting if they start aching at night."
B) "I should keep my legs elevated above the level of my heart when I am resting in my recliner."
C) "I will walk until the pain starts, rest until it stops, and then continue walking."
D) "I will wear tight compression stockings every day to help improve the blood flow to my feet."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
- <strong>Option C is correct:</strong> For clients with peripheral arterial disease (PAD), a structured walking program is recommended to promote collateral circulation. Clients should be taught to walk until they experience claudication (pain), rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. 
- <strong>Option A is incorrect:</strong> Clients with PAD often have decreased sensation and poor perfusion, making the use of direct heat (like heating pads) a severe burn risk.
- <strong>Option B is incorrect:</strong> Elevating the legs above heart level decreases arterial blood flow to the distal extremities, which worsens PAD symptoms. Legs should be kept in a dependent or neutral position. 
- <strong>Option D is incorrect:</strong> Compression stockings are indicated for venous insufficiency to promote venous return; they are contraindicated in severe arterial disease as they further restrict arterial blood flow.
</details>

### Question 37
A client with a severe heart failure exacerbation is receiving intravenous furosemide twice daily. The client is also scheduled to receive their daily dose of oral digoxin. The nurse is reviewing the client's morning laboratory results. Which finding requires the nurse to withhold the digoxin and notify the provider?

A) Serum sodium 136 mEq/L
B) Serum potassium 2.9 mEq/L
C) Serum creatinine 1.1 mg/dL
D) Blood urea nitrogen 18 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
- <strong>Option B is correct:</strong> Furosemide is a loop diuretic that causes potassium depletion. A serum potassium level of 2.9 mEq/L indicates significant hypokalemia (normal is 3.5 to 5.0 mEq/L). Hypokalemia greatly increases the client's risk for digoxin toxicity, which can lead to life-threatening dysrhythmias. The nurse must hold the digoxin, report the lab value, and likely administer a potassium supplement.
- <strong>Option A is incorrect:</strong> A serum sodium of 136 mEq/L is within normal limits (135-145 mEq/L) and does not require holding the medication.
- <strong>Option C is incorrect:</strong> A serum creatinine of 1.1 mg/dL is within normal limits and does not necessitate holding the digoxin.
- <strong>Option D is incorrect:</strong> A BUN of 18 mg/dL is within normal limits (10-20 mg/dL).
</details>

---

### Question 38
The nurse is caring for a client admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation. The nurse reviews the client’s daily Brain Natriuretic Peptide (BNP) levels over the course of admission: 
Day 1: 1,250 pg/mL 
Day 2: 980 pg/mL 
Day 3: 520 pg/mL 
Based on this data trend, what is the appropriate nursing action?

A) Prepare the client for emergency bedside echocardiography.
B) Place the client on a strict fluid restriction of 500 mL/day due to worsening failure.
C) Administer PRN intravenous hydralazine to rapidly decrease the BNP.
D) Document the finding and continue the current diuretic treatment plan.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
- <strong>Option D is correct:</strong> BNP is a hormone secreted by cardiomyocytes in the heart ventricles in response to stretching caused by increased ventricular blood volume. High levels indicate heart failure. A downward trend (1,250 -> 980 -> 520) indicates that the heart failure exacerbation is resolving and the current diuresis and treatment plan is effective. Documenting and continuing the plan is the correct action.
- <strong>Option A is incorrect:</strong> The downward trend is a positive sign; there is no indication of an emergency requiring immediate echocardiography.
- <strong>Option B is incorrect:</strong> The condition is improving, not worsening. A 500 mL/day restriction is extreme and unnecessary given the positive trend.
- <strong>Option C is incorrect:</strong> The BNP is decreasing as expected with effective therapy. Hydralazine is a vasodilator used for hypertension and severe heart failure, but it is not administered simply to "rapidly decrease BNP" when the client's current trend is already demonstrating clinical improvement.
</details>

---

### Question 39
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation has been taking prescribed amiodarone for several months. The nurse is assessing the client during an outpatient follow-up visit. Which finding represents a dangerous adverse effect requiring immediate intervention, rather than an expected side effect of the medication?

A) The client reports a persistent, dry cough and increasing shortness of breath.
B) The client complains of occasional nausea when taking the medication on an empty stomach.
C) The client's skin has developed a slight bluish-gray tint in sun-exposed areas.
D) The client states that food tastes slightly bitter since starting the medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
- <strong>Option A is correct:</strong> Amiodarone can cause fatal pulmonary toxicity (amiodarone-induced interstitial lung disease). Symptoms such as a progressive dry cough, shortness of breath, and dyspnea are early warning signs of this life-threatening adverse effect and require immediate medical intervention and discontinuation of the drug.
- <strong>Option B is incorrect:</strong> Mild gastrointestinal upset is a common, manageable side effect and can often be mitigated by taking the medication with food.
- <strong>Option C is incorrect:</strong> A bluish-gray discoloration of the skin ("smurf syndrome") is a known, expected side effect of prolonged amiodarone use. While distressing cosmetically, it is not an immediate life-threatening adverse effect like pulmonary toxicity.
- <strong>Option D is incorrect:</strong> Altered taste is a minor, expected side effect of many antiarrhythmics and does not pose a danger to the client.
</details>
