### Question 40
A client with a history of atrial fibrillation and chronic heart failure is receiving care on a medical-surgical unit. The nursing team consists of a registered nurse (RN), a licensed practical nurse (LPN), and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP). Which task related to this client's care can the RN safely delegate to the LPN?

A) Developing a discharge teaching plan regarding new dietary sodium restrictions.
B) Administering a scheduled dose of oral warfarin based on an established INR.
C) Assessing the client's heart sounds for the presence of a new S3 gallop.
D) Evaluating the effectiveness of a newly prescribed intravenous diuretic.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
- <strong>Option B is correct:</strong> An LPN is within their scope of practice to administer routine oral medications, including anticoagulants like warfarin, to stable clients. Because the INR is already established, this is a routine administration task.
- <strong>Option A is incorrect:</strong> Developing a teaching plan requires the specialized education and judgment of the RN. An LPN may reinforce teaching, but cannot create the initial plan.
- <strong>Option C is incorrect:</strong> Assessing for new, complex heart sounds (like an S3 gallop indicating fluid overload) is considered a comprehensive assessment, which falls strictly within the scope of the RN.
- <strong>Option D is incorrect:</strong> Evaluating the clinical effectiveness of a new treatment or medication (such as an IV diuretic) is part of the nursing process (Evaluation) and cannot be delegated to an LPN.
</details>
### Question 41
A client admitted with a severe heart failure exacerbation has been receiving intravenous furosemide for the past 48 hours to manage fluid volume overload. Which assessment finding is the earliest indicator of a complication related to this specific treatment?

A) Increasing bilateral pedal edema
B) New onset of premature ventricular contractions
C) Resting blood pressure of 118/76 mmHg
D) Total weight loss of 2 kilograms over 48 hours

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Furosemide is a potassium-wasting loop diuretic commonly used in heart failure exacerbations to reduce preload and clear pulmonary and peripheral edema. A major complication of aggressive diuresis is hypokalemia. Premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) and other cardiac arrhythmias are early clinical indicators of potassium depletion and require immediate intervention to prevent life-threatening dysrhythmias. Increasing pedal edema (A) indicates treatment failure, not a complication of the treatment itself. A blood pressure of 118/76 (C) is a normal, stable finding. Weight loss of 2 kg (D) is the desired therapeutic effect of diuresis, demonstrating successful fluid removal.
</details>

---

### Question 42
The nurse is caring for a client with a history of chronic atrial fibrillation who is scheduled to receive their daily dose of oral warfarin. The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results, which show an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 4.8. Based on these results, which action should the nurse take?

A) Administer the medication as the INR is within the expected therapeutic range.
B) Give the scheduled dose and instruct the client to use a soft-bristled toothbrush.
C) Hold the medication and notify the healthcare provider of the elevated result.
D) Request a prescription for an increased dose to achieve a target INR of 5.0.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
For a client with atrial fibrillation, the therapeutic goal for warfarin therapy is typically an INR between 2.0 and 3.0. An INR of 4.8 is supratherapeutic and places the client at a significantly increased risk for spontaneous bleeding. The nurse must hold the scheduled dose of warfarin and notify the healthcare provider so the dosage can be adjusted or an antidote (such as Vitamin K) can be prescribed if indicated. Administering the medication (A and B) or increasing the dose (D) would further elevate the INR, exacerbating the risk of a severe hemorrhagic event.
</details>

---

### Question 43
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the lifestyle modifications needed to manage this condition?

A) "I will start wearing compression stockings every day to improve my blood flow."
B) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting."
C) "I will apply a heating pad directly to my calves whenever they feel cold."
D) "I should walk until I feel pain, rest until it stops, and then continue walking."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
The most effective exercise program for a client with peripheral arterial disease (PAD) is walking. Clients are encouraged to walk until they experience claudication (pain), rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. This promotes the development of collateral circulation, improving blood flow to the ischemic extremities. Compression stockings (A) and elevating the legs above the heart (B) are interventions for peripheral *venous* disease, but they impede arterial blood flow and worsen PAD symptoms. Applying a heating pad directly to the legs (C) is strictly contraindicated because PAD causes decreased sensation, increasing the risk of severe thermal burns.
</details>

---

### Question 44
The nurse is reviewing the laboratory trends of a client admitted three days ago for an acute heart failure exacerbation. The client's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) level on admission was 850 pg/mL. Yesterday, it was 620 pg/mL, and today it is 310 pg/mL. How should the nurse interpret these laboratory trends?

A) The current treatment plan is effective, indicating a decrease in ventricular stretch.
B) The client is developing acute kidney injury due to aggressive diuresis.
C) The client's condition is deteriorating, and a change in medication is required.
D) The client is experiencing an impending myocardial infarction and needs immediate intervention.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a neurohormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching of heart muscle cells, which occurs in heart failure. A normal BNP is typically less than 100 pg/mL. A decreasing trend in BNP levels over three days indicates that the volume overload is resolving, ventricular stretch is decreasing, and the current treatment regimen for the heart failure exacerbation is highly effective. It does not indicate acute kidney injury (B), deterioration (C), or an impending myocardial infarction (D).
</details>

---

### Question 45
A registered nurse (RN) is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a cardiac step-down unit. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Teaching a client with peripheral vascular disease how to inspect their feet daily.
B) Evaluating the effectiveness of a fluid restriction for a client with a heart failure exacerbation.
C) Applying sequential compression devices to the lower extremities of a client with chronic atrial fibrillation.
D) Assessing the bilateral pedal pulses of a client returning from a femoral-popliteal bypass graft.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Applying sequential compression devices (SCDs) is a routine, standard, and non-invasive procedure that does not require the nursing process, making it safe and appropriate to delegate to an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP). Teaching (A), evaluating outcomes of care (B), and performing complex, system-specific assessments (D) all require nursing judgment, specialized knowledge, and critical thinking. Therefore, these tasks fall squarely within the RN scope of practice and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 46
A client is admitted with a heart failure exacerbation. The nurse is monitoring the client's clinical status throughout the shift. Which assessment finding indicates a developing, severe complication requiring immediate medical intervention?

A) Dependent pitting edema in the lower extremities (+2).
B) Development of a new S3 heart sound and pink-tinged sputum.
C) Weight gain of 2 pounds over the last 24 hours.
D) Increasing fatigue when walking to the bathroom.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Development of a new S3 heart sound and pink-tinged sputum.

**Rationale:** 
An S3 heart sound combined with pink-tinged, frothy sputum indicates the development of acute pulmonary edema, a life-threatening complication of heart failure exacerbation. This requires immediate intervention (such as oxygen, diuretics, or vasodilators) to prevent respiratory failure. Dependent pitting edema (Option A), a 2-pound weight gain (Option C), and fatigue upon exertion (Option D) are expected, chronic, or baseline signs of a heart failure exacerbation that need to be managed, but they do not represent an acute, life-threatening emergency like pulmonary edema.

**Lens:** Complication Recognition
</details>

---

### Question 47
A nurse is reviewing the morning laboratory results for a client with chronic atrial fibrillation who receives a daily dose of oral warfarin. The client's current International Normalized Ratio (INR) is 4.8. What is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A) Administer the scheduled dose of warfarin and document the results.
B) Request an order for intravenous protamine sulfate.
C) Hold the warfarin dose and notify the healthcare provider.
D) Administer the warfarin and encourage intake of leafy green vegetables.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Hold the warfarin dose and notify the healthcare provider.

**Rationale:** 
For a client with atrial fibrillation, the therapeutic target for INR is typically between 2.0 and 3.0. An INR of 4.8 is supratherapeutic and places the client at a high risk for spontaneous bleeding. The nurse must hold the scheduled dose of warfarin and notify the provider for a dose adjustment or potential antidote administration (like Vitamin K). Administering the dose (Options A and D) is dangerous due to the bleeding risk. Protamine sulfate (Option B) is the reversal agent for heparin, not warfarin; the antidote for warfarin is Vitamin K. 

**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
</details>

---

### Question 48
The nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I will elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting."
B) "I should wear compression stockings every day to improve the blood flow."
C) "I will apply a heating pad to my legs on high heat when they feel cold."
D) "I should walk until I feel pain, stop and rest, and then resume walking."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) "I should walk until I feel pain, stop and rest, and then resume walking."

**Rationale:** 
A progressive walking program is the most effective exercise for individuals with PAD. Walking until the onset of claudication (pain), resting until the pain subsides, and then continuing to walk promotes the development of collateral circulation, improving blood flow over time. Elevating the legs above the heart (Option A) and wearing compression stockings (Option B) are interventions for peripheral *venous* disease, not arterial disease, and would actually impair arterial blood flow to the distal extremities. Applying a heating pad (Option C) is contraindicated because PAD causes decreased sensation, placing the client at a high risk for severe burns.

**Lens:** Complex Teaching
</details>

---

### Question 49
A client admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation has been receiving continuous intravenous furosemide. The nurse analyzes the client's daily B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels: 
- Day 1: 950 pg/mL
- Day 2: 600 pg/mL
- Day 3: 350 pg/mL

How should the nurse interpret this data trend?

A) The treatment plan is effective, and myocardial wall stretch is decreasing.
B) The client is developing renal failure due to diuretic toxicity.
C) The client is experiencing an acute worsening of the heart failure.
D) The medication dosage needs to be immediately increased to prevent pulmonary edema.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The treatment plan is effective, and myocardial wall stretch is decreasing.

**Rationale:** 
B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a hormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching, usually due to fluid overload in heart failure. A normal BNP is generally < 100 pg/mL. The downward trend from 950 to 350 indicates that the intravenous diuretic therapy is effectively removing excess fluid, thereby decreasing myocardial stretch and improving the exacerbation. Therefore, the treatment is working (Option A), and there is no indication of worsening heart failure (Option C) or a need to increase the dosage (Option D). While diuretics can affect renal function, BNP levels do not measure renal failure (Option B); creatinine and BUN would be used for that assessment.

**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
</details>

---

### Question 50
The registered nurse (RN) is coordinating care for a client with stable, chronic atrial fibrillation who is hospitalized for a routine procedure. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Assessing the client's apical pulse to check for an apical-radial pulse deficit.
B) Educating the client on the signs and symptoms of a transient ischemic attack.
C) Assisting the client with ambulation from the bed to the bathroom.
D) Evaluating the effectiveness of the client's newly prescribed diltiazem.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Assisting the client with ambulation from the bed to the bathroom.

**Rationale:** 
Assisting a stable client with ambulation and activities of daily living (ADLs) falls within the scope of practice for a UAP. Assessing an apical-radial pulse deficit (Option A) requires nursing judgment and assessment skills, which cannot be delegated to a UAP. Educating the client on stroke symptoms (Option B) and evaluating the clinical effectiveness of a medication (Option D) fall strictly under the RN's scope of practice according to the nursing process (Assessment, Teaching, and Evaluation cannot be delegated).

**Lens:** Delegation
</details>
### Question 51
A nurse is preparing to administer scheduled morning medications to a client with chronic atrial fibrillation. The client is prescribed digoxin 0.25 mg PO daily and furosemide 40 mg PO daily. The morning comprehensive metabolic panel reveals a serum potassium level of 2.9 mEq/L and a serum calcium of 9.5 mg/dL. Which action should the nurse take based on these laboratory results?

A) Administer both medications as prescribed and document the laboratory results.
B) Hold the digoxin but administer the furosemide to manage fluid status.
C) Hold the furosemide but administer the digoxin to control the heart rate.
D) Hold both medications and notify the healthcare provider immediately.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> DRUG-LAB INTERACTION<br>
Furosemide is a potassium-wasting loop diuretic that can cause or worsen hypokalemia. The client's potassium level is already dangerously low at 2.9 mEq/L (normal is 3.5-5.0 mEq/L). Administering furosemide would exacerbate this imbalance. Furthermore, hypokalemia significantly increases the risk of digoxin toxicity, which can lead to life-threatening dysrhythmias. Therefore, giving the digoxin is also unsafe. The nurse must hold both medications and contact the healthcare provider for orders to correct the potassium deficit.
</details>

---

### Question 52
A client is admitted with a severe heart failure exacerbation. Over the past three days of hospitalization, the client's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels were documented as 1,200 pg/mL on Day 1, 850 pg/mL on Day 2, and 410 pg/mL on Day 3. Concurrently, the client's weight decreased by 4 kg, and daily urine output averaged 2,500 mL. How should the nurse interpret this data trend?

A) The client is experiencing severe dehydration and requires intravenous fluid replacement.
B) The current treatment plan is effectively reducing ventricular stretch and fluid overload.
C) The client's renal function is rapidly deteriorating due to aggressive diuretic overuse.
D) The client is developing right-sided heart failure secondary to prolonged pulmonary hypertension.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> DATA TREND ANALYSIS<br>
B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a hormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching, usually due to increased blood volume (fluid overload). A normal BNP is typically less than 100 pg/mL. While the client's BNP is still elevated, the steady downward trend (1,200 to 410 pg/mL), combined with a 4 kg weight loss (representing roughly 4 liters of fluid removed) and adequate urine output, indicates that the diuretic and heart failure therapy is successfully reducing fluid volume and decreasing ventricular stretch.
</details>

---

### Question 53
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the lifestyle modifications and symptom management required for this condition?

A) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting in bed."
B) "I will apply a heating pad to my lower legs on a high setting if they feel cold at night."
C) "I will walk until the pain starts, rest until it stops, and then resume walking."
D) "I need to wear tight compression stockings every day to help push blood back up my legs."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> COMPLEX TEACHING<br>
A graded exercise program is a primary treatment for intermittent claudication associated with PAD. Walking until the onset of pain, resting until it subsides, and then walking again promotes the development of collateral circulation, which improves blood flow over time. Elevating the legs (Option A) and wearing compression stockings (Option D) are interventions for *venous* insufficiency, not arterial; these actions actually impede arterial blood flow to the distal extremities. Applying a heating pad (Option B) is strictly contraindicated due to decreased sensation in PAD, which severely increases the risk for burns.
</details>

---

### Question 54
A client with a history of left-sided heart failure is receiving intravenous diuretics for an acute exacerbation. During the morning assessment, the nurse notes the client has developed a new, dry, hacking cough and becomes increasingly restless when the head of the bed is lowered. The client's respiratory rate has increased from 18 to 26 breaths per minute. What complication should the nurse suspect is developing?

A) Pulmonary edema
B) Spontaneous pneumothorax
C) Cor pulmonale
D) Deep vein thrombosis

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> COMPLICATION RECOGNITION<br>
A dry, hacking cough, restlessness, orthopnea (inability to lie flat), and tachypnea are early, classic signs of pulmonary edema, a life-threatening complication of left-sided heart failure exacerbation. As pressure builds up in the left side of the heart, fluid backs up into the pulmonary vasculature and eventually leaks into the alveoli. The early cough will often progress to the production of pink, frothy sputum if not rapidly treated. Pneumothorax, Cor pulmonale (right-sided heart failure), and DVT do not present with this specific triad of left-sided cardiac decompensation signs.
</details>

---

### Question 55
The registered nurse (RN) is managing care alongside an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a cardiovascular step-down unit. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Evaluating the pedal pulses of a client admitted with an acute exacerbation of peripheral vascular disease.
B) Assisting a stable client with chronic atrial fibrillation to the bathroom for morning hygiene.
C) Educating a client with a recent heart failure exacerbation on daily fluid restriction guidelines.
D) Monitoring the telemetry rhythm of a client who recently started a continuous diltiazem drip.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> DELEGATION<br>
Delegating the task of assisting a stable client with chronic atrial fibrillation to the bathroom is appropriate for a UAP. UAPs can perform standard activities of daily living (ADLs) and assist with mobility for stable clients. Evaluating pedal pulses (Option A) is an assessment. Educating a client (Option C) is teaching. Monitoring and interpreting telemetry strips for a client on a high-alert medication drip (Option D) requires nursing judgment and assessment. Assessment, teaching, and nursing judgment fall strictly outside the scope of practice for a UAP and must be performed by the RN.
</details>
### Question 56
A client with atrial fibrillation is receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion to prevent clot formation. Morning laboratory results reveal a platelet count of 85,000/mm³ (baseline 220,000/mm³) and an activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) of 60 seconds. What is the appropriate nursing action based on these findings?

A) Decrease the heparin infusion rate according to the facility's titration protocol.
B) Stop the heparin infusion immediately and notify the healthcare provider.
C) Administer a scheduled dose of aspirin to prevent further platelet aggregation.
D) Continue the infusion at the current rate as the aPTT is within the therapeutic range.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

A drop in platelet count by more than 50% from baseline or below 150,000/mm³ in a client receiving heparin strongly indicates Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT), a life-threatening, paradoxical clotting disorder. Even though the client's aPTT (60 seconds) is within the expected therapeutic range for heparin therapy (typically 1.5 to 2 times the normal baseline), the drastic drop in platelets makes continuing the medication dangerous. The nurse must hold the infusion and notify the provider immediately so alternative anticoagulation (e.g., argatroban) can be initiated. Administering aspirin is contraindicated as it does not treat HIT and increases bleeding risk, while decreasing the rate will not stop the immune-mediated HIT reaction.
</details>

---

### Question 57
The nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I will elevate my legs above the level of my heart on a pillow when I am resting."
B) "I should apply a heating pad to my calves on a low setting if they ache at night."
C) "I will walk until the leg pain starts, rest until it stops, and then begin walking again."
D) "I need to wear tight, graduated compression stockings every day to improve my circulation."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
**System Focus:** Peripheral Vascular Disease

A structured walking program is the most effective exercise therapy for PAD. Clients are taught to walk until they experience intermittent claudication (pain), stop and rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. This process helps develop collateral circulation. Elevating the legs above the heart decreases arterial blood flow to the distal extremities and worsens PAD symptoms (legs should be kept dependent). Applying a heating pad is dangerous because clients with PAD often have decreased sensation and reduced blood flow, severely increasing the risk of thermal burns. Compression stockings are indicated for peripheral *venous* disease, not arterial disease, as tight compression further impedes arterial blood inflow.
</details>

---

### Question 58
A client was admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation. The nurse reviews the client’s daily morning B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels in the electronic health record:
- Day 1: 1,400 pg/mL
- Day 2: 950 pg/mL
- Day 3: 400 pg/mL

Based on this laboratory trend and the current treatment plan, what clinical finding should the nurse expect to observe during the physical assessment?

A) A decreased urine output over the last 24-hour period.
B) An increase in the frequency of a dry, hacking cough.
C) A reduction in dependent edema and clear bilateral lung sounds.
D) A new onset of a third heart sound (S3 gallop) upon auscultation.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
**System Focus:** Heart Failure exacerbation

B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a hormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching and volume overload. A rapidly decreasing BNP trend (from 1,400 to 400 pg/mL) indicates that ventricular stretch is reducing and the heart failure exacerbation is resolving. Therefore, the nurse would expect to see signs of clinical improvement, such as a reduction in peripheral edema and clear lung sounds (resolution of pulmonary edema). Decreased urine output, an increasing cough, and a new S3 gallop are all signs of worsening fluid overload and cardiac decompensation, which would correlate with an *increasing* BNP trend.
</details>

---

### Question 59
A client with a heart failure exacerbation has been prescribed fluid restriction, daily morning weights, and intravenous loop diuretics for the past 48 hours. Which assessment finding provides the most objective evidence that the multi-day treatment plan is successfully meeting its primary goal?

A) A decrease in the client's daily weight by 2.2 kg (4.8 lbs) since admission.
B) An oxygen saturation of 94% while receiving 2 liters per minute via nasal cannula.
C) The client reporting significantly reduced fatigue when walking to the restroom.
D) A calculated 24-hour intake of 1,500 mL and a urinary output of 1,600 mL.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
**System Focus:** Heart Failure exacerbation

The primary goal of administering intravenous diuretics and imposing fluid restriction during a heart failure exacerbation is the removal of excess total body fluid. Daily weights are the most objective and reliable indicator of fluid volume status in heart failure clients. A weight loss of 2.2 kg equates to the removal of approximately 2.2 liters of excess fluid. While a 24-hour intake and output (I&O) gives an idea of fluid balance, it is notoriously inaccurate compared to daily weights. Subjective reports of reduced fatigue are positive but not objective. An oxygen saturation of 94% on supplemental oxygen does not independently confirm fluid removal as effectively as a downward trend in weight.
</details>

---

### Question 60
A client with a history of atrial fibrillation has been taking amiodarone for several months to maintain normal sinus rhythm. During a routine clinic visit, the client reports a variety of new symptoms. Which symptom should the nurse identify as a dangerous adverse effect requiring immediate provider notification, rather than an expected side effect?

A) Occasional nausea after taking the medication on an empty stomach.
B) A gradual change in facial skin tone to a bluish-gray color.
C) Mild sensitivity to sunlight when spending time outdoors.
D) Development of a new, persistent dry cough and increasing shortness of breath.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** ADVERSE VS SIDE EFFECT
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

Amiodarone is a highly effective antiarrhythmic but carries a "black box" warning for pulmonary toxicity, which can be fatal. Manifestations of amiodarone-induced pulmonary toxicity include a new persistent dry cough, progressive dyspnea, and pleuritic chest pain. This is a dangerous adverse effect that requires immediate medical intervention and discontinuation of the drug. Gastrointestinal upset (nausea) is a common, manageable side effect. Photosensitivity and a bluish-gray discoloration of the skin (often called "smurf syndrome") are well-documented, cosmetic side effects of long-term amiodarone therapy that, while bothersome to the client, are not life-threatening.
</details>
### Question 61
A client with a long-standing history of severe peripheral arterial disease (PAD) reports suddenly experiencing severe, continuous pain in the right lower leg that is not relieved by resting in a dependent position. Upon assessment, the nurse notes the extremity is cold to the touch, and the skin appears pale and mottled. Which complication should the nurse suspect is occurring?

A) Deep vein thrombosis (DVT)
B) Acute arterial occlusion
C) Neuropathic pain exacerbation
D) Acute compartment syndrome

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting signs of acute arterial occlusion (acute limb ischemia), a medical emergency. The classic signs are known as the "6 Ps": Pain, Pallor, Pulselessness, Paresthesia, Paralysis, and Poikilothermia (coolness). This sudden, severe lack of blood flow can lead to tissue necrosis and loss of the limb if not treated immediately. 

Deep vein thrombosis (DVT) typically presents with unilateral swelling, warmth, and erythema, not pallor and coldness (Option A). Neuropathic pain usually presents as burning, tingling, or numbness, and would not acutely change the temperature and color of the limb (Option C). Acute compartment syndrome typically occurs after trauma or surgery and involves severe swelling and pressure within the muscle fascia, but the sudden onset of pale, cold, mottled skin in a client with PAD strongly points to arterial occlusion (Option D).

**Lens:** Complication Recognition
</details>

---

### Question 62
A nurse is reviewing the morning laboratory results for a client with chronic atrial fibrillation who is prescribed continuous telemetry and daily oral warfarin. The client's most recent International Normalized Ratio (INR) result is 4.8. Based on this laboratory finding, what is the most appropriate action for the nurse to take?

A) Administer the scheduled dose of warfarin and document the result in the medical record.
B) Administer a stat dose of intravenous vitamin K before giving the scheduled warfarin.
C) Hold the dose of warfarin, assess the client for bleeding, and notify the healthcare provider.
D) Request a prescription for a stat platelet count to evaluate for heparin-induced thrombocytopenia.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** The therapeutic range for INR in a client taking warfarin for atrial fibrillation is typically 2.0 to 3.0. An INR of 4.8 is supratherapeutic and places the client at a high risk for spontaneous bleeding. The nurse must hold the scheduled dose of the medication, perform a focused assessment for signs of bleeding (such as petechiae, hematuria, or melena), and immediately notify the healthcare provider for further orders, which may include a dose adjustment or administration of an antidote.

Administering the scheduled dose would further elevate the INR and increase bleeding risk (Option A). While vitamin K is the antidote for warfarin toxicity, the nurse cannot administer it without a specific prescription from the healthcare provider, and giving warfarin afterward would be counterproductive (Option B). Heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT) is a complication of heparin therapy, not warfarin therapy, and is monitored via platelet counts, not INR (Option D).

**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
</details>

---

### Question 63
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client recovering from a severe heart failure exacerbation. The client asks how to properly manage their new fluid restrictions and low-sodium diet at home. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex teaching?

A) "I will avoid using salt substitutes because they often contain high amounts of potassium."
B) "I should weigh myself every week on Sunday mornings to track any fluid buildup."
C) "If I feel thirsty, I can suck on ice chips since they don't count toward my fluid restriction."
D) "I will increase my daily intake of canned soups to get more vegetables without adding solid food weight."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Rationale:** Clients with heart failure are often prescribed angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitors, angiotensin II receptor blockers (ARBs), or potassium-sparing diuretics. These medications can cause potassium retention. Many over-the-counter salt substitutes replace sodium with potassium, which can lead to dangerous hyperkalemia. Therefore, avoiding these substitutes demonstrates a correct understanding.

Clients with heart failure must weigh themselves daily, not weekly, to monitor for rapid fluid retention (Option B). Ice chips melt into water and absolutely must be calculated into the client's daily fluid restriction, typically at half their volume (Option C). Canned soups are notoriously high in sodium, which will worsen fluid retention and heart failure symptoms; clients should be taught to choose fresh or frozen vegetables instead (Option D).

**Lens:** Complex Teaching
</details>

---

### Question 64
A client was admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation accompanied by a rapid ventricular response from underlying atrial fibrillation. The nurse is reviewing the client's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels over the past three days: 
Day 1 (Admission): 1,450 pg/mL 
Day 2: 980 pg/mL 
Day 3: 410 pg/mL 
How should the nurse interpret this trend in the laboratory data?

A) The client is experiencing worsening renal perfusion requiring an intravenous fluid bolus.
B) The current treatment plan is ineffective, and the client needs higher doses of cardiac glycosides.
C) The client is developing severe left ventricular hypertrophy and requires a stat echocardiogram.
D) The client is responding appropriately to diuretic therapy and fluid offloading.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D**

**Rationale:** B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a hormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching, such as that caused by increased blood volume in heart failure. A normal BNP is typically less than 100 pg/mL. A decreasing trend from 1,450 to 410 pg/mL indicates that the ventricular stretch is decreasing, which signifies that the treatment plan (diuretics, fluid offloading, and heart rate control) is effectively reducing fluid overload. 

Worsening renal perfusion would be monitored via BUN and creatinine, and administering a fluid bolus to a patient recovering from a heart failure exacerbation is contraindicated (Option A). The decreasing trend shows the treatment *is* effective, not ineffective (Option B). While a high BNP relates to ventricular stretch, a decreasing trend does not indicate new-onset hypertrophy requiring a stat echocardiogram; it indicates resolving volume overload (Option C).

**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
</details>

---

### Question 65
A registered nurse (RN) is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a medical-surgical cardiovascular unit. The RN is caring for a client with chronic peripheral vascular disease (PVD) and stable atrial fibrillation. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Assess the client's pedal pulses using a Doppler ultrasound device.
B) Educate the client on the risk of bleeding while taking their newly prescribed oral anticoagulant.
C) Apply prescribed knee-high graduated compression stockings before the client gets out of bed.
D) Evaluate the client's respiratory rate and breathing effort after walking down the hallway.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** The application of graduated compression stockings is a standard, routine procedure that is within the scope of practice for an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP). It is safe to delegate this task for a stable client with chronic PVD.

Assessing pedal pulses, especially with a Doppler, requires nursing judgment and assessment skills, which cannot be delegated to a UAP (Option A). Client education regarding medication side effects and safety falls strictly under the RN's scope of practice (Option B). Evaluating a client's physiological response to an intervention (such as exertion during ambulation) requires clinical judgment and must be performed by the RN (Option D). Remember the TAPE acronym: UAPs cannot Teach, Assess, Plan, or Evaluate.

**Lens:** Delegation
</details>
### Question 66
A client with a history of chronic left-sided heart failure is receiving intravenous normal saline at 100 mL/hr for mild dehydration. While assessing the client, which finding indicates to the nurse an early complication of fluid administration related to the client's cardiovascular diagnosis?

A) A dry, hacking cough when the client is lying flat
B) Dependent edema graded at 1+ in the bilateral ankles
C) A documented weight gain of 0.5 lbs (0.2 kg) over the past 24 hours
D) A resting heart rate of 88 beats per minute

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> COMPLICATION RECOGNITION<br>
Clients with a history of heart failure are highly susceptible to fluid volume overload during intravenous fluid administration. A dry, hacking cough when supine (orthopnea) is an early clinical manifestation of left-sided heart failure exacerbation and impending pulmonary edema. As fluid accumulates in the pulmonary interstitium, it irritates the airways. Dependent edema (B) is a common, chronic finding in right-sided heart failure and not an early indicator of acute pulmonary complication. A minimal weight gain of 0.5 lbs (C) and a normal resting heart rate (D) do not immediately point to an acute complication of fluid overload.
</details>

---

### Question 67
A client with atrial fibrillation is prescribed a daily oral dose of digoxin and furosemide. Upon reviewing the morning comprehensive metabolic panel, the nurse notes a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L (3.1 mmol/L). What is the nurse's most appropriate action regarding the scheduled medications?

A) Request a prescription to increase the furosemide dose to balance the client's electrolytes.
B) Administer the digoxin as prescribed and encourage the client to eat potassium-rich foods.
C) Withhold the scheduled digoxin dose and notify the healthcare provider of the laboratory result.
D) Administer the digoxin with a full glass of water to promote renal clearance of the drug.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> DRUG-LAB INTERACTION<br>
The normal serum potassium level is 3.5 to 5.0 mEq/L. A level of 3.1 mEq/L indicates hypokalemia. Furosemide is a potassium-wasting loop diuretic that frequently causes hypokalemia. Hypokalemia increases the sensitivity of the cardiac myocardium to digoxin, significantly increasing the risk of life-threatening digoxin toxicity and dysrhythmias, even if the serum digoxin level is within the normal therapeutic range. The nurse must hold the digoxin and notify the provider to correct the potassium deficit before administration.
</details>

---

### Question 68
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client with newly diagnosed peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to manage lifestyle changes associated with the condition?

A) "I will apply a heating pad to my calves on a low setting when they ache at night."
B) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart while watching television."
C) "I will wear firm, knee-high compression stockings to help push blood back to my heart."
D) "I need to walk until I feel pain, rest until it subsides, and then resume walking."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> COMPLEX TEACHING<br>
A highly encouraged lifestyle modification for peripheral arterial disease (PAD) is a structured exercise program. Walking until the onset of claudication (pain), resting until the pain stops, and resuming walking helps promote the development of collateral circulation, ultimately improving blood flow to the ischemic limbs. Applying direct heat (A) is contraindicated due to decreased peripheral sensation, which increases burn risk. Elevating the legs above the heart (B) and wearing firm compression stockings (C) are interventions for venous insufficiency; in PAD, these actions further restrict arterial blood flow to the lower extremities and will worsen ischemia.
</details>

---

### Question 69
A client was admitted three days ago for a severe exacerbation of heart failure. The nurse reviews the client's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels drawn each morning: 
- Day 1: 1,200 pg/mL
- Day 2: 850 pg/mL
- Day 3: 400 pg/mL

Based on this data trend, what is the most accurate interpretation of the client's physiological status?

A) The client's renal function is progressively deteriorating due to diuretic use.
B) The treatment plan is effective, and cardiac ventricular stretch is decreasing.
C) The downward trend indicates fluid volume deficit, and fluids should be increased.
D) The client is developing myocardial ischemia and requires cardiac enzyme testing.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> DATA TREND ANALYSIS<br>
B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a neurohormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching and pressure overload, typically seen in heart failure exacerbations. A normal BNP is generally <100 pg/mL. While the Day 3 value of 400 pg/mL is still elevated, the significant downward trend indicates that the treatment (usually diuretics and vasodilators) is effectively reducing fluid overload and ventricular stretch. It does not indicate renal deterioration (A), myocardial ischemia (D), or that the patient is in a deficit requiring IV fluids (C), as 400 pg/mL still represents a degree of residual volume/stretch.
</details>

---

### Question 70
A client with persistent atrial fibrillation was admitted 48 hours ago with a rapid ventricular response (RVR). The client received a continuous diltiazem infusion and was transitioned to oral diltiazem earlier today. Which evaluation finding best indicates that the primary pharmacological goal of the multi-day treatment plan has been achieved?

A) The client's apical pulse remains stable between 70 and 85 beats per minute.
B) The telemetry monitor displays a normal sinus rhythm with regular PR intervals.
C) The client's blood pressure has increased from 100/60 mmHg to 130/80 mmHg.
D) The most recent partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) is within the therapeutic range.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> EVALUATION OF GOALS<br>
Diltiazem is a calcium channel blocker utilized in atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response (RVR) to slow AV node conduction, thereby achieving rate control. An apical heart rate consistently between 70 and 85 bpm indicates successful rate control, which is the primary pharmacological goal of this drug in this context. Converting persistent atrial fibrillation to normal sinus rhythm (B) is rhythm control, which typically requires antiarrhythmics (like amiodarone) or cardioversion, not just a rate-controlling calcium channel blocker. Diltiazem is not used to increase blood pressure (C) and actually has antihypertensive effects. It does not affect aPTT (D), which is monitored for heparin therapy.
</details>
### Question 71
A client with a history of chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed daily oral warfarin. The nurse reviews the morning laboratory results and notes an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 4.8. Which action should the nurse take next?

A) Administer the scheduled dose of warfarin and carefully monitor for bruising.
B) Hold the scheduled dose of warfarin and notify the healthcare provider.
C) Request a prescription to administer intravenous vitamin K immediately.
D) Assess the client's apical pulse for a full minute before administering the medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Lens:</strong> DRUG-LAB INTERACTION
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The therapeutic INR range for a client on warfarin for atrial fibrillation is typically 2.0 to 3.0. An INR of 4.8 is supratherapeutic and places the client at a significantly increased risk for spontaneous bleeding. The nurse must hold the scheduled dose of warfarin to ensure client safety and notify the healthcare provider, who will likely adjust the dosage or order an intervention. While vitamin K is the antidote for warfarin, an INR of 4.8 without active bleeding generally requires holding the medication or administering oral (not intravenous) vitamin K based on provider orders; IV vitamin K is reserved for severe bleeding or extremely high INRs due to the risk of anaphylaxis. Assessing the apical pulse does not address the critical laboratory abnormality.
</details>

---

### Question 72
A client is being discharged with a new diagnosis of peripheral arterial disease (PAD). The nurse is providing comprehensive teaching regarding lifestyle modifications and managing the chronic condition at home. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the teaching?

A) "I need to wear tight, knee-high compression stockings every day to improve my blood flow."
B) "I will elevate my legs above the level of my heart on a pillow when I am resting in bed."
C) "I will walk until the leg pain starts, rest until it goes away, and then resume walking."
D) "I should apply a warm heating pad to my calves if they start cramping during the night."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Lens:</strong> COMPLEX TEACHING
<strong>Rationale:</strong> For clients with peripheral arterial disease (PAD), a structured walking program is a primary non-pharmacological treatment. Walking until intermittent claudication (pain) occurs, resting until it subsides, and then continuing to walk helps promote the development of collateral circulation, improving blood flow over time. Compression stockings and elevating the legs above the heart decrease arterial blood flow to the distal extremities and are contraindicated in PAD (though they are used for venous insufficiency). Applying a heating pad is dangerous because clients with PAD often have decreased sensation, placing them at high risk for severe thermal burns.
</details>

---

### Question 73
A client admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation has been receiving continuous intravenous bumetanide. The nurse reviews the client's daily comprehensive metabolic panel results:
Day 1: BUN 20 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.0 mg/dL
Day 2: BUN 32 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.4 mg/dL
Day 3: BUN 45 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.9 mg/dL

Based on this data trend, which complication should the nurse suspect the client is developing?

A) Digoxin toxicity
B) Worsening pulmonary edema
C) Dilutional hyponatremia
D) Pre-renal acute kidney injury

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D

<strong>Lens:</strong> DATA TREND ANALYSIS
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The laboratory trend over three days shows a progressively rising Blood Urea Nitrogen (BUN) and serum creatinine, indicating deteriorating renal function. In the context of aggressive diuresis (intravenous bumetanide) for a heart failure exacerbation, this trend strongly suggests pre-renal acute kidney injury (AKI) caused by intravascular volume depletion and decreased renal perfusion. Worsening pulmonary edema would present with respiratory distress and crackles rather than an isolated rise in renal labs. Dilutional hyponatremia is characterized by low serum sodium, not elevated BUN and creatinine. Digoxin toxicity is assessed via serum digoxin levels and presents with gastrointestinal and visual disturbances or arrhythmias.
</details>

---

### Question 74
The registered nurse (RN) is coordinating care for a group of clients on a cardiac step-down unit. Which task related to a client with chronic atrial fibrillation is most appropriate to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Obtaining and recording the client's daily morning weight.
B) Assessing the client's baseline radial pulse before medication administration.
C) Administering the client's scheduled oral dose of diltiazem.
D) Teaching the client about the importance of reporting unexpected bruising.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Lens:</strong> DELEGATION
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Obtaining and recording daily weights is a routine, standard procedure that falls directly within the scope of practice for unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP). It does not require nursing judgment. Assessing a baseline pulse prior to medication administration, teaching the client about bleeding precautions, and administering medications are all tasks that require the specialized skill, knowledge, and clinical judgment of a licensed nurse and cannot legally or safely be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

### Question 75
A client with a severe heart failure exacerbation has been receiving a multi-day medical treatment plan that includes fluid restriction, sodium restriction, and loop diuretics. The collaborative care plan includes a primary goal to decrease fluid volume overload. Which assessment finding provides the most reliable indication that the treatment plan is effectively meeting this goal?

A) The client exhibits 2+ pitting edema in the bilateral lower extremities.
B) The client's apical heart rate has decreased from 105 to 88 beats per minute.
C) The client's daily weight has decreased by 4.4 lbs (2 kg) over two days.
D) The client reports feeling less anxious when transferring out of bed.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Lens:</strong> EVALUATION OF GOALS
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The most reliable and objective indicator of fluid volume status, and therefore the effectiveness of treatment for fluid volume overload, is daily weight. A loss of 2.2 lbs (1 kg) is equivalent to the loss of 1 liter of fluid. A decrease of 4.4 lbs (2 kg) indicates an effective removal of roughly 2 liters of excess fluid. The presence of 2+ pitting edema indicates that fluid overload is still present, though it does not provide a comparative trend without baseline data. A decreased heart rate and subjective reports of reduced anxiety are positive signs of general clinical improvement, but they do not specifically quantify fluid volume loss as accurately as daily weights.
</details>
### Question 76
A patient is receiving treatment for a severe heart failure exacerbation. While performing a morning assessment, the nurse notes several clinical findings. Which assessment finding most strongly indicates that the patient is developing a life-threatening complication?

A) Bilateral 2+ pitting edema in the lower extremities  
B) Jugular venous distention visible at a 45-degree angle  
C) Production of frothy, pink-tinged sputum  
D) A documented weight gain of 2 pounds over the last 24 hours  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Production of frothy, pink-tinged sputum  
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION  

**Rationale:** Frothy, pink-tinged sputum is a hallmark sign of acute pulmonary edema, a severe and life-threatening complication of a heart failure exacerbation. This indicates that fluid has rapidly backed up into the alveoli and requires immediate medical intervention (e.g., oxygen, rapid-acting diuretics, vasodilators). Bilateral pitting edema (Option A), jugular venous distention (Option B), and daily weight fluctuations (Option D) are expected findings of fluid volume overload in a patient with an active heart failure exacerbation, but they do not represent an acute, sudden respiratory emergency.
</details>

---

### Question 77
A patient with a history of chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed daily digoxin and furosemide. The morning laboratory results reveal a serum potassium level of 3.0 mEq/L (3.0 mmol/L) and a serum digoxin level of 1.1 ng/mL. What is the nurse's most appropriate action regarding the morning medications?

A) Withhold the furosemide and notify the healthcare provider regarding the potassium level.  
B) Administer both the digoxin and furosemide as prescribed.  
C) Withhold the digoxin and administer the furosemide to prevent toxicity.  
D) Request a prescription for a higher dose of digoxin due to the potassium level.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Withhold the furosemide and notify the healthcare provider regarding the potassium level.  
**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION  

**Rationale:** Furosemide is a loop diuretic that causes potassium depletion. The patient's potassium level is 3.0 mEq/L, indicating hypokalemia (normal is 3.5-5.0 mEq/L). Hypokalemia significantly increases the heart's sensitivity to digoxin, increasing the risk of life-threatening digoxin toxicity (e.g., dysrhythmias), even if the serum digoxin level is within the normal therapeutic range (0.8-2.0 ng/mL). Therefore, the nurse must hold the furosemide to prevent worsening hypokalemia and notify the provider. Administering both (Option B) puts the patient at immediate risk. Holding the digoxin (Option C) does not correct the root problem of worsening hypokalemia caused by the furosemide. Requesting a higher dose of digoxin (Option D) is contraindicated.
</details>

---

### Question 78
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I should use a heating pad on my lower legs to improve circulation when they feel cold."  
B) "I will elevate my legs above the level of my heart while sleeping to reduce swelling."  
C) "I must wear snug knee-high compression stockings from morning until night."  
D) "I will walk until I experience pain, stop and rest, and then resume walking."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) "I will walk until I experience pain, stop and rest, and then resume walking."  
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING  

**Rationale:** A structured exercise program is a primary intervention for PAD. Patients are taught to walk until they experience intermittent claudication (ischemic pain), stop and rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. This promotes the development of collateral circulation. Applying a heating pad (Option A) is contraindicated because PAD decreases sensation and tissue perfusion, making the patient highly susceptible to severe burns. Elevating the legs above the heart (Option B) impairs arterial blood flow to the lower extremities; legs should be kept in a dependent or neutral position. Compression stockings (Option C) restrict arterial blood flow and are indicated for venous, not arterial, insufficiency.
</details>

---

### Question 79
A patient was admitted three days ago for a heart failure exacerbation. The nurse is analyzing the patient's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels over the past 72 hours:

- Day 1: 1,450 pg/mL
- Day 2: 980 pg/mL
- Day 3: 520 pg/mL

Based on these trended laboratory values, which clinical finding should the nurse expect to observe during the physical assessment?

A) A steady increase in the patient's daily weight over the past three days.  
B) A reduction in adventitious lung sounds such as bibasilar crackles.  
C) Development of a new S3 gallop upon cardiac auscultation.  
D) Increased episodes of paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) A reduction in adventitious lung sounds such as bibasilar crackles.  
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS  

**Rationale:** BNP is a neurohormone secreted by the cardiac ventricles in response to ventricular stretch and increased pressure (volume overload). A normal BNP is typically <100 pg/mL. The trending downward of the patient's BNP levels from 1,450 to 520 pg/mL indicates that the heart failure exacerbation is improving and the heart is experiencing less ventricular stretch. Clinically, this correlates with a reduction in fluid overload symptoms, meaning the nurse would expect to hear fewer bibasilar crackles. An increase in daily weight (Option A), a new S3 gallop (Option C), and increased paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea (Option D) are all signs of worsening fluid volume overload, which would correlate with an *increasing* BNP trend.
</details>

---

### Question 80
A registered nurse (RN) is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a cardiac step-down unit. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP for a patient with chronic atrial fibrillation?

A) Palpating the radial pulse to determine if the heart rate remains irregular.  
B) Measuring the patient's blood pressure and oxygen saturation prior to morning ambulation.  
C) Monitoring the patient for sudden onset of facial drooping or slurred speech.  
D) Reinforcing education on using an electric razor due to prescribed anticoagulants.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Measuring the patient's blood pressure and oxygen saturation prior to morning ambulation.  
**Lens:** DELEGATION  

**Rationale:** The delegation of tasks must fall within the UAP's scope of practice, which includes basic, non-invasive interventions such as obtaining routine vital signs on stable patients (Option B). Palpating a pulse to assess for rhythm irregularities (Option A) requires nursing judgment and assessment skills, which cannot be delegated. Monitoring a patient for signs of a stroke (Option C) requires clinical assessment to catch potential complications of atrial fibrillation (clots) and remains the responsibility of the RN. Reinforcing education (Option D) is within the scope of a Licensed Practical/Vocational Nurse (LPN/LVN) or RN, but cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 81
A client admitted 3 days ago for a heart failure exacerbation has been receiving scheduled intravenous furosemide. The nurse evaluates the daily laboratory results to assess the client's response to therapy. 

**Day 1:** BNP 950 pg/mL, Serum Potassium 4.0 mEq/L, BUN 18 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.0 mg/dL.
**Day 3:** BNP 420 pg/mL, Serum Potassium 3.6 mEq/L, BUN 38 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.9 mg/dL.

Based on the trended data, which action should the nurse take?

A) Notify the provider regarding the rapidly improving renal function.
B) Continue the IV furosemide as the BNP indicates resolving heart failure.
C) Request an order to decrease or hold the furosemide due to suspected acute kidney injury.
D) Administer a PRN dose of IV potassium chloride to treat the severe hypokalemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C 

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens: Data Trend Analysis.</strong> This question requires the nurse to analyze shifting laboratory values over time. While the B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) has significantly decreased, indicating an improvement in the heart failure exacerbation, the blood urea nitrogen (BUN) and creatinine have sharply risen. This indicates over-diuresis leading to prerenal acute kidney injury (AKI). The nurse should intervene by contacting the provider to decrease or hold the diuretic to preserve kidney function. The potassium level is 3.6 mEq/L, which is on the lower end of normal (3.5-5.0 mEq/L), but does not indicate severe hypokalemia requiring IV potassium replacement.
</details>

---

### Question 82
A client with a history of atrial fibrillation is prescribed a daily dose of oral digoxin and is currently scheduled to receive their morning medications. The morning laboratory results reveal a serum digoxin level of 1.0 ng/mL, serum potassium of 3.1 mEq/L, and serum calcium of 9.2 mg/dL. Which action is the most appropriate for the nurse to take prior to administering the digoxin?

A) Hold the digoxin and notify the provider of the abnormal laboratory values.
B) Administer the digoxin as ordered since the drug level is within the therapeutic range.
C) Assess the apical pulse for a full minute before administering the scheduled digoxin dose.
D) Administer the digoxin and encourage the client to consume a banana with breakfast.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens: Drug-Lab Interaction.</strong> This question focuses on medication safety related to laboratory findings. The client's serum digoxin level is 1.0 ng/mL, which is within the normal therapeutic range (0.8–2.0 ng/mL). However, the client's potassium level is 3.1 mEq/L, indicating hypokalemia (normal is 3.5–5.0 mEq/L). Hypokalemia significantly increases the heart's sensitivity to digoxin, increasing the risk of potentially fatal digoxin toxicity and dysrhythmias, even when digoxin levels are technically within normal limits. The safest action is to hold the medication and notify the provider to correct the potassium deficit.
</details>

### Question 83
The nurse provides discharge teaching to a client with peripheral arterial disease (PAD) who experiences frequent claudication in both lower extremities. Which statement by the client indicates a need for further education regarding lifestyle modifications and symptom management?

A) "I will enroll in a supervised walking program to help build collateral circulation in my legs."
B) "I should apply a heating pad directly to my calves when they ache to improve the blood flow."
C) "I will inspect my feet daily for any cuts, sores, or changes in skin color using a hand mirror."
D) "I need to stop smoking completely because nicotine makes my blood vessels constrict."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens: Complex Teaching.</strong> This question evaluates the patient's understanding of managing a chronic cardiovascular illness. Direct application of heat (like a heating pad) to the affected extremities is strictly contraindicated in patients with PAD. The disease often causes decreased sensation, making patients highly susceptible to burns. Additionally, applying heat increases the metabolic oxygen demand in the tissues, which the compromised arteries cannot supply, potentially worsening ischemia. Walking programs, daily foot inspections, and smoking cessation are all correct, evidence-based management strategies for PAD.
</details>

---

### Question 84
A client has been hospitalized for four days receiving treatment for a severe heart failure exacerbation. The interdisciplinary care plan includes a goal to achieve optimal fluid balance and reduce cardiac workload. Which assessment finding provides the best evidence that the multi-day treatment plan is effective?

A) The client's heart rate has increased from 82 beats/min to 98 beats/min.
B) The client's urine output has averaged 30 mL/hour over the last 24 hours.
C) The client reports sleeping with three pillows at night instead of four.
D) The client has lost 3 kg (6.6 lbs) of body weight since admission.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens: Evaluation of Goals.</strong> This question determines if the overall multi-day treatment plan for heart failure is effective. Daily weights are the most reliable indicator of fluid volume status. A loss of 3 kg (which correlates to roughly 3 liters of fluid) over four days strongly demonstrates successful diuresis and a reduction in fluid overload, thus decreasing cardiac workload. An average urine output of 30 mL/hr is the minimum acceptable output and does not reflect vigorous or highly effective diuresis. While a reduction in orthopnea (from four pillows to three) is a slight improvement, the client is still experiencing orthopnea. An increasing heart rate may indicate a compensatory response to decreased cardiac output or over-diuresis, not an improvement.
</details>

---

### Question 85
The registered nurse (RN) is coordinating care for a group of clients on a telemetry unit with the assistance of an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) and a licensed practical nurse (LPN). One of the clients has chronic, rate-controlled atrial fibrillation. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP for this specific client?

A) Teaching the client about the importance of monitoring for bleeding while on oral anticoagulants.
B) Administering a scheduled dose of oral metoprolol for heart rate control.
C) Reapplying the continuous cardiac monitoring electrodes after the client finishes bathing.
D) Assessing the client's radial and apical pulse simultaneously to check for a pulse deficit.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens: Delegation.</strong> This question focuses on safely assigning tasks based on the scope of practice for stable patients. Reapplying non-invasive continuous cardiac monitoring (telemetry) electrodes is a standard, routine task that does not require nursing judgment and falls safely within the UAP's scope of practice. Assessing for a pulse deficit requires clinical evaluation and judgment, and should be performed by the RN (or LPN depending on facility policy). Teaching and educating the client is strictly an RN responsibility. Administering oral medications can be delegated to the LPN, but not to the UAP.
</details>
### Question 86
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I will elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting on the couch."
B) "I should apply a heating pad to my lower legs every evening to improve my circulation."
C) "I will walk until my legs begin to hurt, rest until the pain stops, and then resume walking."
D) "I need to wear tight, knee-high compression stockings every day from morning until night."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br><br>
<strong>A is incorrect:</strong> Elevating the legs above the heart is an intervention for peripheral *venous* disease, not arterial. In PAD, elevating the legs further decreases already compromised arterial blood flow to the extremities.<br>
<strong>B is incorrect:</strong> Clients with PAD often have decreased sensation in their lower extremities. Applying a heating pad directly to the skin increases the risk of thermal injury and burns.<br>
<strong>C is correct:</strong> This statement demonstrates a correct understanding. Exercise therapy for PAD involves walking until the onset of intermittent claudication (pain), resting until the pain subsides, and then continuing to walk. This structured approach helps promote the development of collateral circulation.<br>
<strong>D is incorrect:</strong> Tight compression stockings restrict arterial blood flow and are contraindicated in clients with peripheral arterial disease. They are used for venous insufficiency.
</details>

### Question 87
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed a daily scheduled dose of digoxin. Morning laboratory results reveal the following: Potassium 3.1 mEq/L, Sodium 140 mEq/L, Calcium 9.5 mg/dL, and a Digoxin trough level of 1.1 ng/mL. Based on these laboratory findings, what is the nurse's most appropriate action?

A) Administer the digoxin as prescribed because the digoxin level is within the normal therapeutic range.
B) Hold the digoxin and notify the healthcare provider because the low potassium level increases the risk for drug toxicity.
C) Administer the digoxin and encourage the client to consume a large glass of potassium-rich orange juice.
D) Hold the digoxin because the trough level indicates the client is currently experiencing active drug toxicity.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br><br>
<strong>A is incorrect:</strong> While the digoxin level of 1.1 ng/mL is within the normal therapeutic range (0.5 - 2.0 ng/mL), the client's potassium level is critically low. Administering the medication without addressing the hypokalemia is unsafe.<br>
<strong>B is correct:</strong> Hypokalemia (Potassium < 3.5 mEq/L) heavily potentiates the effects of digoxin and significantly increases the risk for digoxin toxicity and lethal arrhythmias, even if the current serum digoxin level is within normal limits. The nurse should hold the dose and report the hypokalemia.<br>
<strong>C is incorrect:</strong> While oral potassium supplementation may be needed, hypokalemia in the presence of digoxin requires immediate medical evaluation and precise replacement rather than just giving dietary juice and continuing the medication.<br>
<strong>D is incorrect:</strong> A digoxin level of 1.1 ng/mL is therapeutic and does not inherently indicate toxicity. It is the concurrent hypokalemia that creates the danger.
</details>

### Question 88
A client was admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation. The treatment plan included intravenous furosemide, fluid restriction, and a low-sodium diet. Which finding best indicates to the nurse that the multi-day treatment goals are being met?

A) The client's admission weight has decreased from 85 kg to 82 kg.
B) The client's serum brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) increased from 600 pg/mL to 850 pg/mL.
C) The client requires three pillows to sleep comfortably at night instead of two.
D) The client's daily urine output has averaged 700 mL over the last 48 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br><br>
<strong>A is correct:</strong> A weight loss of 3 kg (approximately 6.6 lbs) over three days indicates successful diuresis and fluid volume removal (1 kg of weight loss roughly equals 1 liter of fluid). This demonstrates that the diuretic therapy and fluid restriction are effectively treating the heart failure exacerbation.<br>
<strong>B is incorrect:</strong> An increasing BNP level indicates worsening ventricular stretch and heart failure, not an improvement.<br>
<strong>C is incorrect:</strong> Requiring more pillows to sleep indicates worsening orthopnea (shortness of breath when lying flat), which is a sign of continued or worsening pulmonary congestion.<br>
<strong>D is incorrect:</strong> An average urine output of 700 mL over 48 hours (350 mL/day) is indicative of oliguria. A client on active intravenous diuretic therapy for a heart failure exacerbation should have a much higher urinary output.
</details>

### Question 89
A registered nurse (RN) is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a medical-surgical cardiovascular unit. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Documenting the specific pitting characteristics of pedal edema in a client with a heart failure exacerbation.
B) Educating a client with newly diagnosed atrial fibrillation on the importance of adhering to bleeding precautions.
C) Assisting a client with severe peripheral vascular disease to ambulate down the hallway for the first time post-surgery.
D) Obtaining the daily morning weight using a standing scale for a stable client recovering from a heart failure exacerbation.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br><br>
<strong>A is incorrect:</strong> Assessing and documenting the specific grade and characteristics of edema requires nursing judgment and assessment skills, which cannot be delegated to a UAP.<br>
<strong>B is incorrect:</strong> Initial education and complex teaching regarding medications and bleeding precautions are strictly the responsibility of the RN.<br>
<strong>C is incorrect:</strong> Ambulating a client for the very first time after surgery carries a high risk for orthostatic hypotension, falls, or complications. The RN must perform the initial assessment of the client's tolerance to activity.<br>
<strong>D is correct:</strong> Obtaining a routine daily weight on a stable client is a standard, predictable task with a low potential for immediate harm, making it perfectly appropriate to delegate to the UAP.
</details>
