### Question 90
A nurse is reviewing the laboratory trends over the past 72 hours for a client receiving aggressive intravenous diuretic therapy for a heart failure exacerbation. 

Day 1: BUN 22 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.1 mg/dL, Potassium 4.0 mEq/L
Day 2: BUN 35 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.5 mg/dL, Potassium 3.6 mEq/L
Day 3: BUN 48 mg/dL, Creatinine 2.1 mg/dL, Potassium 3.2 mEq/L

Based on this data trend analysis, what is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A) Continue the current diuresis plan as these laboratory changes are an expected, harmless outcome of treating heart failure.
B) Withhold the next scheduled dose of diuretic and notify the healthcare provider of the laboratory shift.
C) Administer a scheduled oral potassium supplement and significantly increase the intravenous fluid rate.
D) Request a prescription to switch the client from an intravenous loop diuretic to an oral thiazide diuretic.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br><br>
<strong>A is incorrect:</strong> While diuresis reduces fluid volume, a sharp elevation in BUN and Creatinine coupled with dropping potassium indicates acute kidney injury (AKI) from over-diuresis (prerenal azotemia) and hypokalemia. This is not a harmless outcome.<br>
<strong>B is correct:</strong> The upward trend of BUN and Creatinine indicates decreasing renal perfusion, and the downward trend in potassium places the client at risk for cardiac arrhythmias. The safest action is to hold the aggressive diuretic therapy and notify the provider to reassess the treatment plan.<br>
<strong>C is incorrect:</strong> While the client needs potassium, significantly increasing IV fluids contradicts the management of a heart failure exacerbation and could easily throw the client back into fluid overload and pulmonary edema.<br>
<strong>D is incorrect:</strong> Switching to an oral thiazide diuretic does not address the acute kidney injury or hypokalemia, as thiazides also deplete potassium and rely on adequate renal function to be effective. 
</details>
### Question 91
A client with chronic heart failure is attending a routine clinic visit. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse an early complication of a heart failure exacerbation?

A) High-pitched, hyperactive bowel sounds in all four quadrants
B) A new-onset dry, hacking cough when lying down
C) Capillary refill time of 2 seconds in the fingers
D) Increased urine output over the last 24 hours

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) A new-onset dry, hacking cough when lying down

**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION

**Rationale:** A dry, hacking cough, particularly when recumbent (orthopnea), is an early sign of pulmonary congestion associated with left-sided heart failure exacerbation. As fluid begins to back up into the pulmonary vasculature, it irritates the airways, triggering a cough before coarse crackles or severe dyspnea may fully manifest. High-pitched bowel sounds are a gastrointestinal finding, not an early sign of heart failure. A capillary refill time of 2 seconds is a normal finding indicating adequate peripheral perfusion. Increased urine output would indicate a positive response to diuretic therapy, whereas a heart failure exacerbation is typically characterized by fluid retention and decreased urine output due to reduced renal perfusion.
</details>

---

### Question 92
A nurse is preparing to administer morning medications to a client with atrial fibrillation. The client is prescribed digoxin 0.25 mg PO daily and furosemide 40 mg PO daily. The morning comprehensive metabolic panel reveals a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L (3.1 mmol/L). What is the nurse's best action?

A) Administer the digoxin but hold the furosemide.
B) Administer both medications as prescribed and document the lab value.
C) Hold both the digoxin and furosemide, and notify the healthcare provider.
D) Request a prescription to change the furosemide to bumetanide.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Hold both the digoxin and furosemide, and notify the healthcare provider.

**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION

**Rationale:** The client is experiencing hypokalemia (normal potassium is 3.5-5.0 mEq/L). Hypokalemia severely increases the risk of digoxin toxicity, which can lead to life-threatening dysrhythmias. Therefore, the digoxin must be held. Furosemide is a potassium-wasting loop diuretic, meaning its administration will further deplete the client's potassium levels; thus, it must also be held. The nurse should withhold both medications and notify the provider to likely obtain a potassium replacement order. Bumetanide is also a potassium-wasting loop diuretic and would cause the same adverse effect as furosemide.
</details>

---

### Question 93
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client with peripheral arterial disease (PAD) about lifestyle modifications and symptom management. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex teaching?

A) "I will walk until I feel pain in my legs, stop to rest, and then resume walking."
B) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting."
C) "I will use a heating pad on my calves at night to improve blood flow."
D) "I need to wear tight compression stockings every day to prevent swelling."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "I will walk until I feel pain in my legs, stop to rest, and then resume walking."

**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING

**Rationale:** The cornerstone of managing intermittent claudication in peripheral arterial disease (PAD) is an exercise program. Clients are taught to walk until they experience pain (claudication), rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. This process helps build collateral circulation. Elevating legs above the heart decreases arterial blood flow to the lower extremities and worsens PAD ischemia (this intervention is for venous disease). Direct application of heat, such as a heating pad, is strictly contraindicated due to decreased sensation and a high risk of severe burns. Compression stockings restrict arterial blood flow and are contraindicated in PAD. 
</details>

---

### Question 94
A client was admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation and has been receiving continuous intravenous furosemide. The nurse reviews the client's daily laboratory trends:
Day 1: BNP 1,200 pg/mL, BUN 18 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.0 mg/dL. 
Day 3: BNP 450 pg/mL, BUN 42 mg/dL, Creatinine 2.1 mg/dL. 
How should the nurse interpret these findings?

A) The client's heart failure is worsening, leading to severely decreased renal perfusion.
B) The client is experiencing an expected and entirely normal response to diuretic therapy.
C) The client is developing chronic kidney disease as a late complication of heart failure.
D) The client's heart failure is improving, but the diuretic therapy is causing acute kidney injury.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) The client's heart failure is improving, but the diuretic therapy is causing acute kidney injury.

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS

**Rationale:** Brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a marker of ventricular stretch; a decrease from 1,200 to 450 pg/mL indicates that the fluid volume overload and heart failure exacerbation are improving. However, the rapidly rising Blood Urea Nitrogen (BUN) and Creatinine over a 3-day period indicates acute kidney injury (AKI), specifically a prerenal AKI likely caused by over-diuresis and intravascular volume depletion from the continuous IV furosemide. This is an adverse effect requiring intervention (such as adjusting the diuretic dose), not a completely normal expected response, nor does a 3-day shift indicate the onset of chronic kidney disease. 
</details>

---

### Question 95
A registered nurse (RN) is working with a licensed practical nurse (LPN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a cardiovascular step-down unit. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Teaching a client with a new diagnosis of atrial fibrillation how to check their radial pulse.
B) Evaluating the pedal pulses of a client recovering from a femoral artery bypass for peripheral vascular disease.
C) Administering a scheduled oral dose of amiodarone to a client with chronic atrial fibrillation.
D) Obtaining the daily morning weight using a standing scale for a client with a heart failure exacerbation.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Obtaining the daily morning weight using a standing scale for a client with a heart failure exacerbation.

**Lens:** DELEGATION

**Rationale:** The UAP's scope of practice includes gathering basic, routine data on stable clients, such as obtaining daily weights, taking vital signs, and assisting with activities of daily living (ADLs). Daily weights are a critical, yet routine task for heart failure monitoring that can be safely delegated. Teaching (Option A) and evaluating/assessing (Option B) are strictly within the scope of practice of the RN (or LPN for basic data collection, but not primary evaluation). Medication administration (Option C) cannot be delegated to a UAP; it requires nursing licensure (LPN or RN).
</details>
### Question 96
The nurse is assessing a client with a history of chronic left-sided heart failure. Which of the following findings indicates the client is experiencing an *early* complication of a heart failure exacerbation?

A) Expectoration of copious, pink, frothy sputum
B) A dry, hacking cough that occurs primarily when lying flat
C) The development of a harsh, blowing systolic murmur 
D) A sustained resting heart rate of 55 beats per minute

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale</b></summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
A dry, hacking cough that worsens when the client is supine (orthopnea) is an early indicator of pulmonary congestion and worsening left ventricular failure. As fluid begins to back up into the pulmonary vasculature, it irritates the airways. Expectoration of pink, frothy sputum (Option A) is a classic sign of acute, life-threatening pulmonary edema, representing a late and severe complication rather than an early sign. A harsh systolic murmur (Option C) relates to valvular disease, not an acute heart failure exacerbation. Bradycardia (Option D) is not a typical sign of an exacerbation; tachycardia is more likely as the body attempts to compensate for decreased cardiac output.
<b>Lens:</b> Complication Recognition
</details>

---

### Question 97
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed daily digoxin and bumetanide. The client’s morning laboratory results reveal a serum potassium level of 2.9 mEq/L and a serum digoxin level of 1.5 ng/mL. Which action is most appropriate for the nurse to take based on these findings?

A) Administer the medications as prescribed and encourage the intake of potassium-rich foods.
B) Hold the bumetanide but administer the digoxin since the level is within the normal therapeutic range.
C) Withhold both medications and notify the healthcare provider immediately.
D) Administer the bumetanide but withhold the digoxin to prevent immediate toxicity.

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale</b></summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
The client has significant hypokalemia (normal potassium is 3.5-5.0 mEq/L). Bumetanide is a loop diuretic that actively wastes potassium, which will worsen the client's hypokalemia. Hypokalemia dangerously increases the risk of digoxin toxicity and potentially lethal dysrhythmias, even if the serum digoxin level is currently within the normal therapeutic range (0.8-2.0 ng/mL). Therefore, the nurse must hold both medications to prioritize client safety and notify the healthcare provider for orders (such as a potassium supplement). 
<b>Lens:</b> Drug-Lab Interaction
</details>

---

### Question 98
The nurse is providing discharge education to a client newly diagnosed with severe peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the chronic disease management plan?

A) "I will apply a heating pad to my calves on a low setting when they begin to ache."
B) "I should wear tight compression stockings every day to improve the blood flow in my legs."
C) "I will elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am sitting in my recliner."
D) "I will walk until I feel pain in my legs, rest until it stops, and then continue walking."

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale</b></summary>
<b>Correct Answer: D</b><br>
A structured walking program is a cornerstone of chronic PAD management. Walking until the onset of claudication (pain), resting until it subsides, and resuming walking helps promote the development of collateral circulation in the legs. Heating pads (Option A) are strictly contraindicated due to decreased sensation and perfusion, which heavily increases burn risk. Compression stockings (Option B) and elevating the legs above the heart (Option C) are interventions for venous insufficiency, not arterial. Both of these actions would further impede arterial blood flow to the distal extremities, worsening PAD symptoms.
<b>Lens:</b> Complex Teaching
</details>

---

### Question 99
A client was admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation. The nurse reviews the client’s B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels over the course of the admission: Day 1: 1,200 pg/mL, Day 2: 850 pg/mL, Day 3: 400 pg/mL. Based on this data trend, which conclusion should the nurse draw?

A) The current treatment plan is effectively reducing ventricular stretch and fluid volume.
B) The client is experiencing worsening myocardial ischemia and requires urgent intervention.
C) The client’s renal function is deteriorating, causing an altered excretion of the peptide.
D) The client is developing cardiogenic shock and needs immediate inotropic support.

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale</b></summary>
<b>Correct Answer: A</b><br>
B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a hormone secreted by the ventricles of the heart in response to excessive stretching and pressure, which occurs during fluid overload in heart failure. A normal BNP is generally less than 100 pg/mL. The downward trend of the client's BNP levels from 1,200 to 400 pg/mL indicates that the ventricles are experiencing less stretch, meaning the medical interventions (like diuresis) are successfully managing the fluid overload and the client is improving.
<b>Lens:</b> Data Trend Analysis
</details>

---

### Question 100
A client with atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response (RVR) has been receiving a continuous diltiazem infusion, which was successfully transitioned to oral diltiazem 24 hours ago. Which clinical finding best indicates that the multi-day treatment plan has achieved its primary goal?

A) The client’s morning electrocardiogram reveals absent P waves and a perfectly regular ventricular rate.
B) The client reports occasional dizziness upon standing with a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg.
C) The client maintains an apical pulse between 70 and 85 beats per minute during ambulation.
D) The client requires three sublingual nitroglycerin tablets for chest pressure over the last shift.

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale</b></summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
The primary goal of administering diltiazem (a calcium channel blocker) for atrial fibrillation with RVR is rate control. Maintaining an apical pulse between 70 and 85 bpm, even during activity like ambulation, demonstrates that the medication is effectively controlling the ventricular rate. Atrial fibrillation features an "irregularly irregular" rhythm; if the rhythm becomes perfectly regular with absent P waves (Option A), it may indicate an adverse event such as complete heart block or a junctional rhythm. Dizziness/hypotension (Option B) is an adverse effect of diltiazem, not a goal. Requiring nitroglycerin for chest pressure (Option D) indicates ongoing ischemia, meaning the client is unstable.
<b>Lens:</b> Evaluation of Goals
</details>
### Question 101
A nurse is monitoring a client admitted with a severe left-sided heart failure exacerbation. The client is receiving intravenous diuretics and continuous oxygen therapy. Which assessment finding is the earliest indicator that the client is developing the life-threatening complication of pulmonary edema?

A) Expectoration of copious, pink, frothy sputum
B) A decrease in hourly urine output to 40 mL/hr
C) Dependent pitting edema of 3+ in the bilateral ankles
D) New-onset restlessness and an unexplained increase in respiratory rate

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Lens:** Complication Recognition (Identify early signs of a specific system failure)
**System Focus:** Heart Failure Exacerbation

**Rationale:** 
Left-sided heart failure can rapidly deteriorate into pulmonary edema, a life-threatening medical emergency where fluid floods the alveoli. The earliest signs of hypoxia and worsening pulmonary congestion are neurological and respiratory changes, such as new-onset restlessness, anxiety, and an increased respiratory rate (tachypnea) as the body attempts to compensate for impaired gas exchange. Expectoration of pink, frothy sputum (Option A) is a classic but *late* sign of severe pulmonary edema. Decreased urine output (Option B) and dependent pitting edema (Option C) are expected findings of systemic heart failure and decreased renal perfusion, but do not specifically indicate the immediate onset of acute pulmonary edema.
</details>

---

### Question 102
A client with a history of chronic atrial fibrillation is scheduled to receive a morning dose of digoxin 0.25 mg PO. The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results before administering the medication. Based on which laboratory finding should the nurse withhold the medication and notify the healthcare provider?

A) Serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L
B) Digoxin trough level of 1.2 ng/mL
C) Serum sodium level of 136 mEq/L
D) International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 2.5

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction (Safety: Should the nurse hold a med based on a system-specific lab?)
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

**Rationale:** 
Digoxin is an antiarrhythmic and inotropic medication frequently used to control the ventricular rate in atrial fibrillation. Hypokalemia (a serum potassium level less than 3.5 mEq/L) significantly increases the client's risk for digoxin toxicity, which can lead to life-threatening dysrhythmias. Therefore, the nurse must withhold the dose and report the potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L to the provider. A digoxin level of 1.2 ng/mL (Option B) is within the normal therapeutic range (0.8 - 2.0 ng/mL). A serum sodium level of 136 mEq/L (Option C) is normal and does not interact with digoxin. An INR of 2.5 (Option D) is perfectly therapeutic for a client with atrial fibrillation taking warfarin to prevent stroke, and does not impact digoxin administration.
</details>

---

### Question 103
The nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I will elevate my legs above the level of my heart whenever I am resting in my recliner."
B) "I should walk until the leg pain starts, rest until it completely stops, and then resume walking."
C) "I will apply a heating pad to my calves on a low setting when my legs ache at night."
D) "I need to wear tight, knee-high compression stockings every day to improve my blood flow."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Lens:** Complex Teaching (Managing chronic system-related illness and lifestyle changes)
**System Focus:** Peripheral Vascular Disease

**Rationale:** 
The standard exercise therapy for clients with peripheral arterial disease (PAD) is structured walking. Clients are taught to walk until they experience intermittent claudication (leg pain), stop and rest until the pain subsides, and then continue walking. This promotes the development of collateral circulation in the ischemic limbs. Elevating the legs above the heart (Option A) worsens arterial blood flow by working against gravity; legs should be kept in a dependent or neutral position. Applying a heating pad (Option C) is dangerous because PAD clients have decreased sensation and are at a high risk for severe burns. Compression stockings (Option D) are indicated for *venous* insufficiency, but are contraindicated in PAD as they further restrict already compromised arterial blood flow.
</details>

---

### Question 104
A client is receiving inpatient treatment for a severe heart failure exacerbation. The nurse reviews the client's Brain Natriuretic Peptide (BNP) trends and daily weights over the past three days:
- Day 1: BNP 1,200 pg/mL, Weight 85 kg
- Day 2: BNP 950 pg/mL, Weight 83.5 kg
- Day 3: BNP 600 pg/mL, Weight 82 kg

Based on this trend analysis, which conclusion by the nurse is the most accurate regarding the client's status?

A) The treatment plan is ineffective and requires an immediate increase in diuretic dosage.
B) The client is experiencing acute kidney injury secondary to aggressive fluid removal.
C) The client is responding appropriately to therapy with a decrease in ventricular stretch.
D) The client is developing hypovolemia and requires a reduction in fluid restrictions.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis (Interpreting a shift in system-specific labs over time)
**System Focus:** Heart Failure Exacerbation

**Rationale:** 
Brain Natriuretic Peptide (BNP) is a hormone secreted by cardiomyocytes in the heart ventricles in response to stretching caused by increased ventricular blood volume (fluid overload). A normal BNP is typically less than 100 pg/mL. The downward trend in the client's BNP from 1,200 to 600 pg/mL, combined with a steady weight loss of 3 kg over 3 days (indicative of successful diuresis), demonstrates that the client is responding appropriately to the heart failure treatment. The reduction in fluid volume decreases the stretch on the ventricles. There is no evidence in the provided data of acute kidney injury (Option B), hypovolemia (Option D), or an ineffective treatment plan (Option A).
</details>

---

### Question 105
A registered nurse (RN) is planning care for a group of clients on a cardiac step-down unit. Which task regarding a client with atrial fibrillation should the RN safely delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Instruct the client on the importance of reporting new episodes of palpitations.
B) Document the characteristics of the client's apical-radial pulse deficit.
C) Obtain a manual blood pressure on a client whose heart rate suddenly increased to 140 bpm.
D) Assist a client with chronic, rate-controlled atrial fibrillation with morning ambulation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Lens:** Delegation (Assigning tasks for stable vs unstable system-specific patients)
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

**Rationale:** 
The RN can safely delegate routine, standard, and unchanging tasks for stable clients to the UAP. Assisting a stable client with chronic, rate-controlled atrial fibrillation with ambulation (Option D) falls within the UAP's scope of practice. Providing patient education (Option A) and performing nursing assessments, such as evaluating an apical-radial pulse deficit (Option B), are strictly within the RN's scope of practice and cannot be delegated. While UAPs can take routine vital signs, a client who experiences a sudden heart rate increase to 140 bpm (Option C) is considered unstable and requires immediate RN assessment to determine hemodynamic stability.
</details>
### Question 106
A patient with a severe heart failure exacerbation is receiving intravenous diuretics. Which assessment finding is the *earliest* indicator that the patient is developing the life-threatening complication of acute pulmonary edema?

A. Sudden onset of extreme restlessness and anxiety  
B. Expectoration of pink, frothy sputum  
C. Development of severe, audible wheezing  
D. Oxygen saturation dropping to 85% on room air  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Complication Recognition</strong><br>
Acute pulmonary edema is a severe complication of heart failure. Before the hallmark physical signs like pink, frothy sputum (Option B), severe hypoxemia (Option D), or audible wheezing/crackles (Option C) occur, the patient will experience "air hunger." This lack of oxygen reaching the brain typically first presents as sudden neurological and behavioral changes, specifically extreme restlessness, anxiety, or confusion. Identifying these early signs allows the nurse to intervene before respiratory failure occurs.
</details>

---

### Question 107
A nurse is preparing to administer the morning dose of digoxin to a patient with chronic atrial fibrillation. The patient's morning laboratory results show a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L and a serum digoxin level of 1.2 ng/mL. Which action should the nurse take?

A. Administer the digoxin as ordered and document the normal digoxin level.  
B. Hold the digoxin and request an order for a potassium supplement.  
C. Administer the digoxin and encourage the patient to consume a potassium-rich breakfast.  
D. Hold the digoxin because the serum drug level indicates severe toxicity.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Drug-Lab Interaction</strong><br>
While the patient's digoxin level (1.2 ng/mL) is within the normal therapeutic range (0.8–2.0 ng/mL), the potassium level is low (normal is 3.5–5.0 mEq/L). Hypokalemia severely increases the heart's sensitivity to digoxin, increasing the risk of life-threatening digoxin toxicity and dysrhythmias even at normal serum drug levels. The nurse must prioritize safety by holding the medication, notifying the provider, and addressing the hypokalemia. Encouraging dietary potassium (Option C) is insufficient for treating active hypokalemia prior to administering a high-risk medication. 
</details>

---

### Question 108
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required?

A. "I will elevate my legs above the level of my heart whenever I sit on the couch."  
B. "I should wear tight compression stockings every day to improve blood flow to my feet."  
C. "I will walk until the pain starts, stop and rest, and then continue walking."  
D. "I need to apply a heating pad directly to my calves on the highest setting if they feel cold."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Complex Teaching</strong><br>
A structured walking program is a primary non-pharmacological treatment for peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Walking until the onset of claudication (pain), resting until the pain subsides, and then resuming walking promotes the development of collateral circulation. Elevating the legs (Option A) and wearing compression stockings (Option B) restrict arterial blood flow further and are treatments for venous, not arterial, insufficiency. Applying direct heat (Option D) is contraindicated due to decreased sensation in PAD, which increases the risk for severe burns.
</details>

---

### Question 109
A patient with a heart failure exacerbation has been on a treatment plan consisting of a 1.5 L daily fluid restriction, daily weights, and intravenous furosemide for three days. Which outcome best indicates that the collaborative treatment plan is effectively meeting its goals?

A. The patient reports a noticeable increase in urine output over the last 24 hours.  
B. The patient's daily weight fluctuates by roughly 0.5 kg (1.1 lbs) each day.  
C. The patient's blood pressure has decreased from 150/90 mmHg to 140/85 mmHg.  
D. The patient transitions from sleeping in a recliner to sleeping flat in bed without shortness of breath.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Evaluation of Goals</strong><br>
The ultimate goal of diuresis and fluid restriction in a heart failure exacerbation is the relief of congestive symptoms, such as pulmonary edema. Orthopnea (the inability to breathe comfortably when lying flat) is a classic sign of fluid backing up into the lungs. Transitioning to sleeping flat without dyspnea directly demonstrates successful offloading of pulmonary fluid. Increased urine output (Option A) is a mechanism of the treatment, not the ultimate functional goal. Weight fluctuations (Option B) and lowered blood pressure (Option C) are expected findings, but the resolution of orthopnea is the most definitive clinical indicator of treatment success.
</details>

---

### Question 110
A patient recently admitted with a severe heart failure exacerbation asks the nurse, "The doctor said my heart muscle is permanently weakened. Does this mean I am going to die soon?" Which response by the nurse demonstrates appropriate therapeutic communication?

A. "Tell me more about what the doctor discussed with you regarding your prognosis."  
B. "You shouldn't worry about dying right now; we have excellent medications for this."  
C. "Heart failure is a progressive disease, but your medical team will do everything possible."  
D. "Many people live a long time with a weakened heart if they strictly follow their diet."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Therapeutic Communication</strong><br>
Using an open-ended statement (Option A) encourages the patient to express their fears, assesses their baseline understanding of the physician's conversation, and opens the door for targeted emotional support and education. Option B provides false reassurance and dismisses the patient's valid fears. Option C cuts off the patient's emotional expression by stating harsh facts coupled with defensive reassurance. Option D offers unsolicited advice and false reassurance rather than exploring the emotional impact of the diagnosis.
</details>
### Question 111
A nurse is monitoring a client admitted with a severe left-sided heart failure exacerbation. Which of the following assessment findings is the *earliest* indicator that the client is developing the life-threatening complication of pulmonary edema?

A) Expectoration of pink, frothy sputum
B) Worsening of bilateral dependent pitting edema
C) New-onset resting tachypnea and a persistent dry cough
D) Central cyanosis and severe lethargy

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Lens:** Complication Recognition  
**Rationale:** In left-sided heart failure, fluid backs up into the pulmonary vasculature. A persistent, dry, hacking cough (especially when lying down) and new-onset resting tachypnea are early signs of fluid crossing into the alveoli, indicating impending pulmonary edema. Pink, frothy sputum (Option A) and central cyanosis/lethargy (Option D) are classic but *late* signs of severe, decompensated pulmonary edema. Dependent pitting edema (Option B) is a sign of right-sided heart failure and systemic venous congestion, not an early indicator of acute pulmonary edema.
</details>

---

### Question 112
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed daily warfarin to prevent embolic events. The nurse reviews the client’s morning laboratory results and notes an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 3.8. The client denies any signs of bruising or active bleeding. Which action should the nurse anticipate taking regarding the patient's medication?

A) Administer the scheduled dose of warfarin to maintain therapeutic levels.
B) Hold the scheduled dose of warfarin and notify the healthcare provider.
C) Administer a prescribed stat dose of intravenous phytonadione (vitamin K).
D) Request a prescription for an immediate transfusion of fresh frozen plasma.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction  
**Rationale:** The therapeutic INR range for a client with atrial fibrillation is typically 2.0 to 3.0. An INR of 3.8 is supratherapeutic and places the client at an increased risk for bleeding. The safest initial action is to hold the scheduled dose of warfarin and notify the healthcare provider for potential dose adjustment. Because the client has no signs of active bleeding and the INR is not critically elevated (e.g., > 5.0 or 10.0), aggressive reversal agents like intravenous vitamin K (Option C) or fresh frozen plasma (Option D) are not immediately indicated. Administering the dose (Option A) would further increase the risk of hemorrhage.
</details>

---

### Question 113
The nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage the condition?

A) "I will start walking every day, stopping to rest when I feel calf pain, and then resume walking."
B) "I will apply a heating pad to my feet on a low setting when they feel cold at night."
C) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart when resting to reduce swelling."
D) "I should wear knee-high compression stockings daily to improve the blood flow to my feet."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A  
**Lens:** Complex Teaching  
**Rationale:** A structured walking program is a primary lifestyle treatment for PAD. Clients should walk until they experience intermittent claudication (calf pain), rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. This promotes the development of collateral circulation. Applying direct heat (Option B) is strictly contraindicated because clients with PAD have decreased sensation and a high risk of burns. Elevating the legs above the heart (Option C) and wearing compression stockings (Option D) are interventions for peripheral *venous* disease; in PAD, these actions would further restrict arterial blood flow to the lower extremities and worsen ischemia.
</details>

### Question 114
The registered nurse (RN) is managing the care of a client admitted with a heart failure exacerbation and a new onset of rapid atrial fibrillation. The client is currently clinically stable. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Evaluate if the client's oxygen saturation improves after elevating the head of the bed.
B) Educate the client on the importance of adhering to a daily 2-liter fluid restriction.
C) Assess the client's bilateral pedal pulses and degree of pitting edema.
D) Measure the client's urine output from the indwelling catheter and record the amount.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D  
**Lens:** Delegation  
**Rationale:** Emptying a catheter and recording urine output are standard, routine tasks that fall within the scope of practice for an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP). Evaluating the clinical response to an intervention (Option A), providing patient education (Option B), and performing physical assessments (Option C) require the critical thinking, specialized knowledge, and judgment of a registered nurse and cannot legally be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 115
A client has been receiving treatment for a severe heart failure exacerbation over the past four days. The treatment plan includes fluid restriction, a low-sodium diet, and intravenous loop diuretics. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the multi-day treatment goals are being successfully met?

A) The client's serum brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) level has increased from 600 pg/mL to 850 pg/mL.
B) The client’s daily weight has decreased by 3 kg (6.6 lbs) over the past 72 hours.
C) The client states they are sleeping comfortably with three pillows instead of four.
D) The client’s urine output over the last 8 hours was 250 mL.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals  
**Rationale:** Daily weight is the most reliable, objective indicator of fluid volume status in clients with heart failure. A weight loss of 3 kg represents the removal of approximately 3 liters of excess fluid, directly demonstrating that the diuretic therapy and fluid restrictions are effective. An increasing BNP (Option A) indicates worsening ventricular stretch and heart failure. While sleeping with fewer pillows (Option C) shows slight subjective improvement in orthopnea, it is not as objective or definitive as significant weight loss. A urine output of 250 mL over 8 hours (approximately 31 mL/hr) (Option D) is borderline oliguria and does not demonstrate effective diuresis.
</details>
### Question 116
A client is admitted to the medical-surgical unit with an exacerbation of left-sided heart failure. The nurse is monitoring the client's respiratory status. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the client is developing an acute, life-threatening complication?

A) The presence of trace, dependent pitting edema in both ankles.
B) The client reports exertional dyspnea when walking to the bathroom.
C) The development of a dry, hacking cough that progresses to pink, frothy sputum.
D) The client reports needing to sleep propped up on three pillows to breathe comfortably.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**Rationale:** A dry, hacking cough that progresses to pink, frothy sputum is a hallmark sign of acute pulmonary edema, a life-threatening complication of left-sided heart failure exacerbation. This requires immediate medical intervention to improve oxygenation and reduce fluid overload in the alveoli. Trace edema (Option A), exertional dyspnea (Option B), and orthopnea (Option D) are expected, chronic manifestations of heart failure and do not represent a new, acute emergency in this context.
</details>

---

### Question 117
A client with a history of atrial fibrillation is prescribed daily oral digoxin. The morning laboratory results reveal a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L and a serum digoxin level of 1.1 ng/mL. Based on these laboratory results, what is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A) Administer the digoxin as prescribed because the serum drug level is within the normal therapeutic range.
B) Hold the digoxin and notify the healthcare provider regarding the client's potassium level.
C) Request a prescription for sodium polystyrene sulfonate to correct the client's electrolyte imbalance.
D) Administer the digoxin with an aluminum-based antacid to prevent gastrointestinal irritation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**Rationale:** Hypokalemia (potassium < 3.5 mEq/L) significantly increases the client's risk for digoxin toxicity, even if the serum digoxin level is currently within the normal therapeutic range (0.8 - 2.0 ng/mL). The nurse should hold the medication and notify the provider to address the hypokalemia before proceeding. Administering the medication (Option A) could trigger fatal dysrhythmias. Sodium polystyrene sulfonate (Option C) is used to *lower* potassium levels, which would worsen this client's hypokalemia. Antacids (Option D) can interfere with digoxin absorption and do not address the dangerous drug-lab interaction.
</details>

---

### Question 118
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to manage the condition and lifestyle changes?

A) "I will walk until I feel pain in my legs, rest until the pain subsides, and then continue walking."
B) "I should elevate my legs on several pillows above the level of my heart when I am resting or sleeping."
C) "I will apply a heating pad to my lower legs on a low setting when they feel cold at night."
D) "I need to wear snug compression stockings every day to prevent blood from pooling in my lower legs."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**Rationale:** For clients with peripheral arterial disease (PAD), a structured walking program is highly recommended to promote the development of collateral circulation. The client should be taught to walk until the onset of claudication pain, stop and rest until the pain goes away, and then resume walking. Elevating the legs above the heart (Option B) decreases arterial blood flow to the distal extremities and worsens PAD ischemia. Applying a heating pad (Option C) is contraindicated due to decreased sensation in the extremities, creating a severe burn risk. Compression stockings (Option D) are used for venous insufficiency; they further restrict arterial blood flow and are contraindicated in PAD.
</details>

---

### Question 119
A client is receiving aggressive intravenous furosemide therapy for a severe heart failure exacerbation. Over the past three days, the nurse notes the client's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) decreased from 1,200 pg/mL to 450 pg/mL, Blood Urea Nitrogen (BUN) increased from 18 mg/dL to 38 mg/dL, and serum Creatinine increased from 0.9 mg/dL to 1.9 mg/dL. How should the nurse interpret these data trends?

A) The heart failure is worsening, causing decreased renal perfusion and systemic fluid retention.
B) The treatment is completely effective and the laboratory values reflect normal metabolic clearance.
C) The client is developing resistance to loop diuretics and requires a different medication class.
D) The client is experiencing acute kidney injury likely secondary to over-diuresis and volume depletion.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**Rationale:** A decreasing BNP indicates that the heart failure exacerbation and fluid overload are improving. However, the doubling of both BUN and Creatinine indicates a significant decline in renal function. In the context of aggressive diuresis, this trend strongly suggests pre-renal acute kidney injury (AKI) caused by over-diuresis and intravascular volume depletion. The heart failure is not worsening (Option A), as evidenced by the dropping BNP. These are not normal metabolic findings (Option B), and the dropping BNP shows the client is not resistant to the diuretic (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 120
A registered nurse (RN) is working with a licensed practical nurse (LPN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a cardiac step-down unit. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the LPN?

A) Administering a scheduled oral dose of diltiazem to a client with chronic, stable atrial fibrillation.
B) Evaluating the respiratory effectiveness of intravenous furosemide in a client with a heart failure exacerbation.
C) Emptying the urinary catheter bag and recording the output for a client with a heart failure exacerbation.
D) Teaching a client with peripheral arterial disease about proper daily foot care and safe shoe selection.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Delegation
**Rationale:** The LPN's scope of practice includes administering oral medications to stable clients. A client with chronic, stable atrial fibrillation receiving a scheduled oral medication is appropriate for the LPN. Evaluating the effectiveness of interventions (Option B) and providing initial, complex teaching (Option D) require the advanced nursing judgment of the RN and cannot be delegated to an LPN or UAP. Emptying a urinary catheter bag and recording output (Option C) is a routine task that is most appropriately delegated to the UAP.
</details>
### Question 121
A client is receiving inpatient treatment for a heart failure exacerbation. The nurse is monitoring the client for early signs of pulmonary edema. Which assessment finding requires immediate intervention by the nurse as an early indicator of this complication?

A) Development of 2+ pitting edema in bilateral lower extremities
B) Client report of nocturia twice during the previous night
C) A recorded weight gain of 1.5 kg (3.3 lbs) over the last 3 days
D) A new, persistent dry and hacking cough when lying supine

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Complication Recognition<br>
A persistent, dry, hacking cough—especially when the client is lying flat (orthopnea)—is an early sign of pulmonary edema due to fluid beginning to shift into the alveoli. As the condition worsens, the cough may produce pink, frothy sputum. Options A, B, and C are anticipated signs of fluid retention and right-sided heart failure exacerbation, but they do not represent an immediate, life-threatening emergency like early pulmonary edema.
</details>

### Question 122
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed daily digoxin to control their ventricular rate. The morning laboratory results show a serum potassium level of 3.2 mEq/L, sodium of 138 mEq/L, and calcium of 9.2 mg/dL. Based on these laboratory values, what is the appropriate nursing action?

A) Administer the digoxin with a potassium-rich snack.
B) Hold the scheduled dose of digoxin and notify the healthcare provider.
C) Administer the medication as prescribed and document the findings.
D) Request a prescription for a stat electrocardiogram (ECG) before giving the dose.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Drug-Lab Interaction<br>
Hypokalemia (potassium < 3.5 mEq/L) significantly increases the risk for digoxin toxicity, which can lead to life-threatening dysrhythmias. The nurse should hold the medication and notify the healthcare provider, as the client will likely need a potassium supplement to correct the deficit before safely receiving digoxin. Administering the dose (even with a snack) is unsafe, and while an ECG may eventually be ordered, the primary safety action is to hold the drug. 
</details>

### Question 123
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client with newly diagnosed peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting."
B) "I will wear tight compression stockings every day to improve the blood flow."
C) "I will walk until I feel pain, rest until it stops, and then walk a little further."
D) "I should apply a heating pad directly to my calves to help with the cramping pain."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Complex Teaching<br>
Walking until the point of claudication (pain), resting until the pain subsides, and then resuming walking is a key therapeutic exercise for clients with PAD. This process promotes the development of collateral circulation, which improves blood flow to the ischemic limbs over time. Elevating the legs (Option A) and wearing compression stockings (Option B) are interventions for *venous* insufficiency; they actually worsen arterial blood flow by working against gravity and compressing compromised arteries. Option D is dangerous because clients with PAD often have decreased sensation and impaired perfusion, putting them at high risk for severe thermal burns.
</details>

---

### Question 124
The nurse is reviewing the laboratory trends for a client admitted three days ago with a severe heart failure exacerbation. The client's B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) levels are documented as follows: Day 1: 1,400 pg/mL; Day 2: 850 pg/mL; Day 3: 350 pg/mL. Based on this data trend, which clinical assessment finding should the nurse expect to observe?

A) Decreased crackles in the lung bases
B) Increasing dyspnea at rest
C) Worsening jugular venous distention
D) A new-onset S3 ventricular gallop

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Data Trend Analysis<br>
BNP is a hormone secreted by the ventricles in response to volume expansion and pressure overload. A significantly decreasing trend in BNP (from 1,400 to 350 pg/mL) indicates that the heart failure exacerbation is resolving and fluid volume overload is successfully decreasing. Therefore, the nurse would expect to see improved clinical signs, such as decreased crackles in the lung bases. Options B, C, and D are all signs of worsening heart failure and fluid overload, which contradict the improving lab values.
</details>

---

### Question 125
A registered nurse (RN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) are caring for a client with a history of chronic atrial fibrillation who is currently stable and receiving oral anticoagulant therapy. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Instructing the client on the importance of using a soft-bristled toothbrush.
B) Checking the client's apical pulse for a full minute prior to diltiazem administration.
C) Assisting the client with ambulation to the bathroom and recording urine output.
D) Monitoring the client's neurological status for early signs of a cerebrovascular accident.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Delegation<br>
Assisting a stable client with ambulation and recording intake and output are routine, standard tasks that fall directly within the UAP's scope of practice. Option A involves patient education, Option B is a nursing assessment required prior to medication administration, and Option D involves clinical monitoring and evaluation. Teaching, assessing, and evaluating all fall outside the UAP's scope of practice and must be performed by the RN.
</details>
### Question 126
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client diagnosed with peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to manage lifestyle changes for this condition?

A) "I will apply a heating pad to my legs on the highest setting when they feel cold."
B) "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart when I am resting."
C) "I will walk until I feel pain, rest until the pain stops, and then resume walking."
D) "I need to wear tight, compressive knee-high socks to improve my circulation."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Peripheral Vascular Disease

**Rationale:** 
For clients with peripheral arterial disease (PAD), walking is an excellent exercise to promote the development of collateral circulation. The client should be taught to walk until they experience intermittent claudication (pain), rest until the pain subsides, and then continue walking. 
Option A is incorrect because clients with PAD often have decreased sensation and reduced blood flow, which impairs the tissue's ability to dissipate heat, drastically increasing the risk for severe burns. Option B is incorrect because elevating the legs above heart level makes it harder for arterial blood to reach the distal extremities, worsening ischemia. Option D is incorrect because tight compression stockings restrict arterial blood flow and are indicated for venous insufficiency, not arterial disease.
</details>

---

### Question 127
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed a daily dose of warfarin. Morning laboratory results show an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 4.8. Which action should the nurse take next?

A) Administer the warfarin and reinforce teaching regarding bleeding precautions.
B) Hold the scheduled warfarin dose and notify the healthcare provider.
C) Administer vitamin K intravenously immediately to reverse the effects.
D) Request a prescription to increase the client's next warfarin dose.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

**Rationale:** 
The therapeutic INR range for a client with atrial fibrillation is typically 2.0 to 3.0. An INR of 4.8 is supratherapeutic and places the client at a significantly increased risk for spontaneous bleeding. The nurse must hold the scheduled dose of warfarin and notify the healthcare provider for further orders, which will likely include a temporary hold or a dose reduction. 
Option A is incorrect because administering the drug will further elevate the INR and bleeding risk. Option C is incorrect because IV vitamin K is typically reserved for severe bleeding or significantly higher INRs (e.g., >10) due to the risk of anaphylaxis and warfarin resistance; this would require an explicit provider order. Option D is incorrect because increasing the dose would worsen the supratherapeutic level.
</details>

---

### Question 128
A client was admitted three days ago for a severe heart failure exacerbation and has been receiving intravenous diuretics and a 1,500 mL/day fluid restriction. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the multi-day treatment plan is achieving its primary goal?

A) The client's morning weight has decreased by 3 kg (6.6 lbs) since admission.
B) The client's urine output has averaged 30 mL/hr over the last 24 hours.
C) The client reports feeling significantly less anxious when visiting with family.
D) The client's blood pressure has remained steady at 138/82 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** Heart Failure exacerbation

**Rationale:** 
The primary goal in treating a heart failure exacerbation is the removal of excess fluid volume. Daily weights are the most reliable indicator of fluid status, with 1 kg (2.2 lbs) of weight loss equating to approximately 1 liter of fluid removal. A 3 kg weight loss demonstrates clear, objective success of the diuretic therapy and fluid restriction.
Option B is incorrect because 30 mL/hr is the bare minimum for normal kidney perfusion and does not necessarily indicate effective diuresis for a fluid-overloaded client. Option C is a positive psychosocial outcome but does not objectively measure fluid volume resolution. Option D does not demonstrate a reduction in fluid overload, as blood pressure can remain steady despite volume changes.
</details>

---

### Question 129
A client with a history of poorly controlled atrial fibrillation is recovering on the medical-surgical unit. During the morning assessment, the nurse notes the client is suddenly struggling to find the right words and exhibits a subtle right-sided facial droop. Which complication is the client most likely experiencing?

A) An acute myocardial infarction
B) A sudden heart failure exacerbation
C) A ruptured cerebral aneurysm
D) An embolic ischemic stroke

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** Atrial Fibrillation

**Rationale:** 
Atrial fibrillation causes ineffective atrial contraction, leading to blood stasis and the formation of thrombi (clots) in the atria. If a clot dislodges, it can travel to the brain, causing an embolic ischemic stroke. Sudden onset of expressive aphasia (struggling to find words) and unilateral facial droop are classic early signs of a stroke. 
Options A and B do not typically present with sudden focal neurological deficits like aphasia and facial droop. While Option C (ruptured aneurysm) is a neurological emergency, the classic presentation is a sudden, severe "thunderclap" headache, whereas the scenario strongly correlates with the embolic risks associated with atrial fibrillation.
</details>

---

### Question 130
The registered nurse (RN) is coordinating care for a client recovering from a heart failure exacerbation who is now transitioning to oral medications. Which task is most appropriate to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Evaluating the client's lung sounds for decreasing crackles at the bases.
B) Teaching the client how to properly log their daily morning weights at home.
C) Recording the exact volume of the client's urine output from the urinal.
D) Helping the client select appropriate low-sodium choices from the dinner menu.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Delegation
**System Focus:** Heart Failure exacerbation

**Rationale:** 
Measuring and recording intake and output (including emptying urinals/catheters) is a standard skill that falls within the scope of practice for unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) for stable clients. 
Option A requires clinical assessment and interpretation, which is strictly the responsibility of the RN. Option B involves patient education, which cannot be delegated to a UAP. Option D involves dietary teaching and evaluation of a therapeutic diet, which should be handled by the RN or a registered dietitian.
</details>
### Question 131
A nurse is discharging a client with newly diagnosed peripheral arterial disease (PAD). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this condition?

A) "I will keep my legs elevated above the level of my heart whenever I am resting in my recliner."
B) "I will walk until the pain starts, rest until it stops, and then resume walking."
C) "I should apply a heating pad to my calves at night to improve circulation."
D) "I need to wear tight compression stockings every day to prevent blood from pooling in my lower legs."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option B is correct:** This describes a structured exercise program for claudication, which is a cornerstone of PAD management. Walking until the onset of claudication (pain), resting until it resolves, and then resuming walking promotes the development of collateral circulation, improving blood flow over time. (Lens: Complex Teaching)
- **Option A is incorrect:** Elevating the legs above the heart is appropriate for chronic venous insufficiency (venous PVD) to promote venous return. In peripheral arterial disease (PAD), elevating the legs further decreases arterial blood flow to the extremities and worsens ischemia and pain. Legs should be kept in a dependent or neutral position.
- **Option C is incorrect:** Clients with PAD often have decreased sensation due to ischemia. Applying direct heat, such as a heating pad, places the client at a high risk for severe burns.
- **Option D is incorrect:** Tight compression stockings are used for venous disease to prevent pooling. In PAD, compression stockings can dangerously impair already compromised arterial blood flow to the lower extremities.
</details>

---

### Question 132
A client with chronic atrial fibrillation is prescribed oral dofetilide for maintenance of normal sinus rhythm. The morning comprehensive metabolic panel shows a serum magnesium level of 1.2 mEq/L (0.6 mmol/L) and a serum potassium level of 3.8 mEq/L (3.8 mmol/L). Which action should the nurse take next?

A) Instruct the client to eat magnesium-rich foods alongside the morning medication dose.
B) Administer the dofetilide as prescribed because the potassium level is within normal limits.
C) Withhold the dofetilide and immediately report the magnesium level to the healthcare provider.
D) Request a prescription to switch the medication to a beta blocker for safer rate control.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option C is correct:** Dofetilide is a Class III antiarrhythmic used for atrial fibrillation that can prolong the QT interval, leading to fatal arrhythmias such as Torsades de Pointes. Hypomagnesemia (normal: 1.5–2.5 mEq/L) heavily exacerbates this risk. The nurse must withhold the medication and notify the provider to correct the electrolyte imbalance before administration. (Lens: Drug-Lab Interaction)
- **Option A is incorrect:** Dietary intake of magnesium is insufficient to quickly correct an acutely low serum magnesium level in a client scheduled to receive a highly arrhythmogenic medication. Intravenous replacement is typically required.
- **Option B is incorrect:** While the potassium level is normal (3.5–5.0 mEq/L), administering dofetilide when the magnesium level is critically low compromises client safety and ignores a severe drug-lab interaction.
- **Option D is incorrect:** It is not within the nurse's scope to independently decide that a medication switch is necessary without first addressing the acute safety concern of the abnormal lab value with the current prescription.
</details>

---

### Question 133
A client is receiving treatment for a severe heart failure exacerbation, which includes intravenous diuretics, a strict fluid restriction, and a low-sodium diet. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the multi-day treatment plan is successfully achieving its primary goal?

A) The client's body weight has steadily decreased by 2.5 kg (5.5 lbs) over the last three days.
B) The client's total daily urine output strictly equals their restricted daily fluid intake.
C) The client reports coughing up smaller amounts of thin, frothy pink sputum in the morning.
D) The client's resting heart rate has decreased from 105 beats/min to 92 beats/min.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option A is correct:** The most reliable indicator of fluid volume status and the success of diuretic therapy over multiple days in a heart failure exacerbation is a decrease in daily body weight. A loss of 2.5 kg correlates to a loss of approximately 2.5 liters of excess fluid, indicating the treatment is highly effective. (Lens: Evaluation of Goals)
- **Option B is incorrect:** If urine output equals fluid intake, the client is in a neutral fluid balance. To treat a fluid overload exacerbation, the client must achieve a *negative* fluid balance (output greater than intake).
- **Option C is incorrect:** Frothy pink sputum is a hallmark sign of severe pulmonary edema. While coughing up "smaller amounts" might indicate a slight trend in the right direction, the continued presence of this sputum indicates the exacerbation and fluid overload are still actively dangerous. 
- **Option D is incorrect:** While a decreased heart rate shows an improvement in cardiac workload, it is not the most direct or specific measure of fluid volume reduction, which is the primary goal of the diuretic and fluid restriction regimen.
</details>

---

### Question 134
A client with a history of heart failure and recurrent atrial fibrillation has been taking amiodarone for several months. Which client statement requires immediate intervention by the nurse as it indicates a dangerous adverse effect rather than a common side effect?

A) "I have noticed a strange bluish-gray tint to the skin on my face and arms."
B) "My eyes seem much more sensitive to the bright sunlight lately."
C) "I occasionally feel slightly dizzy when I stand up too quickly from my chair."
D) "I have developed a persistent dry cough and feel short of breath even when resting."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option D is correct:** Amiodarone can cause pulmonary toxicity, a rare but potentially fatal adverse effect characterized by a dry cough, dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, and fever. This requires immediate medical evaluation and discontinuation of the drug. (Lens: Adverse vs Side Effect)
- **Option A is incorrect:** A bluish-gray discoloration of the skin ("smurf syndrome") is a known, expected cosmetic side effect of long-term amiodarone use due to lipofuscin accumulation. It is benign and not life-threatening.
- **Option B is incorrect:** Corneal microdeposits are present in almost all clients taking amiodarone for more than a few months. This expected side effect can cause photophobia and halos around lights but does not lead to vision loss or require immediate intervention.
- **Option C is incorrect:** Mild orthostatic hypotension is a common side effect of many cardiovascular medications, including amiodarone, and is managed with slow position changes rather than being treated as a medical emergency. 
</details>

---

### Question 135
A registered nurse (RN) is working with a licensed practical nurse (LPN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) to care for a client recovering from a heart failure exacerbation. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the LPN?

A) Administering the scheduled oral doses of furosemide and potassium chloride.
B) Evaluating the client's lung sounds to determine if bibasilar crackles have resolved.
C) Formulating the discharge teaching plan regarding strict dietary sodium restrictions.
D) Administering a PRN intravenous push dose of bumetanide for sudden shortness of breath.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option A is correct:** Administering routine oral medications, including diuretics and electrolyte supplements for a stable client recovering from an exacerbation, is well within the LPN's scope of practice. (Lens: Delegation)
- **Option B is incorrect:** Evaluating the clinical resolution of a condition (interpreting lung sounds to evaluate treatment efficacy) requires the advanced assessment skills and clinical judgment of the RN.
- **Option C is incorrect:** Formulating a new teaching plan or discharge plan is exclusively the responsibility of the RN. The LPN may reinforce teaching once the RN has established the plan.
- **Option D is incorrect:** Administering high-risk intravenous push medications, particularly PRN medications for acute changes in condition (sudden shortness of breath indicating instability), is restricted to the RN.
</details>
### Question 136
A client admitted 3 days ago for a heart failure exacerbation has the following daily weight and Brain Natriuretic Peptide (BNP) trends:
- Day 1: 85.0 kg, BNP 850 pg/mL
- Day 2: 83.5 kg, BNP 600 pg/mL
- Day 3: 82.0 kg, BNP 420 pg/mL
The client's urine output has averaged 2,500 mL/day. Based on this data trend over the last 72 hours, how should the nurse interpret the client's condition?

A. The client is experiencing acute renal injury secondary to aggressive intravenous diuresis.
B. The client is responding therapeutically to the current medical management plan.
C. The client is developing severe dehydration and requires a reduction in diuretic dosing.
D. The client's heart failure is worsening, necessitating the addition of inotropic support.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Weight loss, steadily decreasing BNP levels, and adequate urine output (approximately 2,500 mL/day) are therapeutic indicators that the medical management (likely diuresis) is successfully treating the heart failure exacerbation. There is no evidence of acute renal injury or severe dehydration based solely on this positive trend, as the urine output remains adequate and the weight loss aligns with expected fluid volume clearance. 
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
</details>

### Question 137
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client with chronic peripheral arterial disease (PAD) who recently underwent a balloon angioplasty. Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of long-term lifestyle management for this condition?

A. "I will walk until I feel pain in my legs, stop and rest, and then resume walking."
B. "I should elevate my legs above the level of my heart whenever I am resting in my recliner."
C. "I will apply a heating pad directly to my calves when they ache during the night."
D. "I should wear tight, knee-high compression stockings every day to improve my blood flow."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** For clients with PAD, a structured exercise program promotes the development of collateral circulation. The recommended technique is to walk until claudication (pain) occurs, rest until the pain subsides, and then resume walking. Elevating the legs above the heart (Option B) decreases arterial blood flow to the distal extremities and worsens PAD pain. Heating pads (Option C) are contraindicated because PAD causes decreased sensation, increasing the risk of severe burns. Compression stockings (Option D) impede arterial blood flow and are used for venous disease, not arterial disease.
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
</details>
