### Question 138
A client with persistent atrial fibrillation is receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion to prevent clot formation. The morning laboratory results reveal a platelet count of 85,000/mm³ (baseline 220,000/mm³) and an activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) of 65 seconds (control 30 seconds). Which action by the nurse is the most appropriate?

A. Administer a PRN dose of intravenous protamine sulfate immediately.
B. Continue the heparin infusion at the current rate, as the aPTT is therapeutic.
C. Hold the heparin infusion and notify the healthcare provider.
D. Increase the heparin infusion rate according to the hospital's anticoagulation protocol.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** A significant drop in platelets (greater than 50% from baseline or a count less than 150,000/mm³) in a client receiving heparin indicates Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT), a paradoxical and dangerous immune-mediated reaction. Even though the aPTT of 65 seconds is within the therapeutic range for heparin therapy (usually 1.5 to 2.5 times the control), the heparin infusion must be stopped immediately due to the development of HIT, and the provider must be notified to switch to a non-heparin anticoagulant. Protamine sulfate (Option A) reverses heparin but is not the primary treatment for HIT; simply stopping the infusion is the immediate first step.
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
</details>

---

### Question 139
A client was admitted four days ago for a severe right- and left-sided heart failure exacerbation. The interdisciplinary treatment plan included intravenous bumetanide, fluid restriction of 1,500 mL/day, and a low-sodium diet. Which assessment finding best indicates that the multi-day treatment plan has successfully met its primary goals?

A. The client's pitting edema in the lower extremities has decreased from 4+ to 2+.
B. The client's lung sounds have transitioned from coarse crackles to scattered fine crackles.
C. The client states they feel less fatigued when performing active range-of-motion exercises in bed.
D. The client is able to sleep completely flat in bed without waking up short of breath.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Orthopnea (shortness of breath when lying flat) and paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea are classic manifestations of pulmonary congestion in left-sided heart failure. The ability to sleep completely flat without shortness of breath is a strong, definitive indicator that central pulmonary edema has resolved. While a reduction in peripheral edema (Option A) and transitioning to fine crackles (Option B) show improvement, they indicate that fluid overload is still present. Sleeping flat without dyspnea indicates the primary goal of clearing the pulmonary congestion has been successfully achieved.
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
</details>

---

### Question 140
A client with refractory atrial fibrillation has been taking oral amiodarone for several months to maintain sinus rhythm. The client reports several new symptoms during a follow-up clinic visit. Which symptom indicates a dangerous adverse reaction requiring immediate medical intervention, rather than a monitored side effect?

A. A mild, intermittent slowing of the resting heart rate to 58 beats per minute.
B. Development of a dry, persistent cough and progressive shortness of breath.
C. A blue-gray discoloration of the skin on the face and neck.
D. Experiencing increased sensitivity to sunlight when spending time outdoors.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Amiodarone can cause pulmonary toxicity, which is a life-threatening adverse effect that can present as a dry cough, dyspnea, and pleuritic chest pain. This requires immediate intervention and discontinuation of the drug. Blue-gray skin discoloration (Option C), photosensitivity (Option D), and mild bradycardia (Option A) are known, common side effects of amiodarone therapy that require monitoring and patient education, but they are not immediately life-threatening emergencies like pulmonary toxicity.
**Lens:** Adverse vs Side Effect
</details>


# SECTION: Endocrine System (Count: 140 Questions - INTERMEDIATE)


### Question 141
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion. Serial metabolic panels over the last 6 hours show an anion gap decreasing from 22 mEq/L to 11 mEq/L, blood glucose decreasing from 580 mg/dL to 230 mg/dL, and pH increasing from 7.15 to 7.32. Based on these data trends, what is the nurse's next most appropriate action?

A) Stop the continuous regular insulin infusion immediately to prevent hypoglycemia.
B) Add 5% dextrose to the continuous intravenous maintenance fluids.
C) Administer a prescribed bolus of 50% dextrose intravenously.
D) Increase the insulin infusion rate to normalize the blood glucose faster.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Add 5% dextrose to the continuous intravenous maintenance fluids.

**Rationale:** This question focuses on Data Trend Analysis. In the treatment of DKA, as the blood glucose level drops to approximately 200–250 mg/dL, the nurse should anticipate adding 5% dextrose (D5W) to the intravenous maintenance fluids. The insulin infusion must continue until the anion gap is completely closed (typically < 12 mEq/L) and acidosis is resolved. Adding dextrose prevents hypoglycemia and cerebral edema while allowing the insulin to clear the remaining ketones and correct the acidosis. Stopping the insulin (Option A) would cause a relapse of ketogenesis. Administering a bolus of 50% dextrose (Option C) is reserved for severe, symptomatic hypoglycemia, which is not present here. Increasing the insulin rate (Option D) is dangerous, as dropping blood glucose too rapidly increases the risk of fluid shifts and cerebral edema.

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
</details>

---

### Question 142
The nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex teaching regarding lifelong disease management?

A) "I should gradually taper off my hydrocortisone once my fatigue and weight loss have completely resolved."
B) "During periods of high emotional stress, I should temporarily decrease my steroid dose to prevent high blood pressure."
C) "If I get a severe stomach virus and cannot keep my pills down, I must go to the emergency department for an injection."
D) "I will need to strictly limit my salt intake while I am taking fludrocortisone to prevent swelling."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) "If I get a severe stomach virus and cannot keep my pills down, I must go to the emergency department for an injection."

**Rationale:** This question focuses on Complex Teaching. Addison's disease requires lifelong glucocorticoid (and often mineralocorticoid) replacement. During times of illness, especially gastrointestinal illnesses where oral medications cannot be absorbed, the client is at high risk for a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. They must seek immediate medical attention for intramuscular or intravenous corticosteroids. Option A is incorrect because steroid therapy is lifelong and must never be stopped abruptly. Option B is incorrect because during periods of physical or emotional stress, the body requires *more* cortisol, meaning the steroid dose must be temporarily increased (stress dosing). Option D is incorrect because patients with Addison's disease generally need adequate or increased dietary sodium, especially when sweating or in hot weather, as they are prone to hyponatremia.

**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
</details>

---

### Question 143
A registered nurse (RN) is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a medical-surgical unit caring for a client with Cushing's syndrome who is stable and awaiting surgical intervention. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Assist the client with daily skin care using moisturizing lotions to prevent skin breakdown.
B) Instruct the client on the importance of strict hand hygiene due to an increased risk of infection.
C) Assess the client's lower extremities for signs of pitting edema and report the findings.
D) Evaluate the client's blood glucose logs to determine if the current insulin regimen is effective.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Assist the client with daily skin care using moisturizing lotions to prevent skin breakdown.

**Rationale:** This question focuses on Delegation. Clients with Cushing's syndrome have elevated cortisol levels, which leads to protein wasting, paper-thin skin, and a high risk for skin breakdown and bruising. Assisting with daily skin care and applying moisturizer is a standard activity of daily living (ADL) that falls safely within the UAP's scope of practice. Option B requires initial teaching, which is an RN responsibility. Option C requires clinical assessment (grading pitting edema), which must be performed by the RN. Option D involves evaluating medical logs and therapeutic effectiveness, which is strictly the RN's responsibility. 

**Lens:** DELEGATION
</details>

---

### Question 144
A client with central Diabetes Insipidus (DI) has been receiving intranasal desmopressin (DDAVP) for the past three days. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the treatment plan. Which assessment finding best indicates that the established goals for this multi-day treatment plan are being met?

A) Serum sodium level steadily rises from 145 mEq/L to 155 mEq/L over 72 hours.
B) Urine output progressively increases from 2,000 mL/day to 5,000 mL/day.
C) The client reports persistent, unquenchable thirst waking them up throughout the night.
D) Urine specific gravity increases from an admission value of 1.002 to 1.018.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Urine specific gravity increases from an admission value of 1.002 to 1.018.

**Rationale:** This question focuses on Evaluation of Goals. Diabetes Insipidus is characterized by a deficiency of antidiuretic hormone (ADH), leading to massive diuresis of dilute urine (low specific gravity < 1.005). Desmopressin is a synthetic ADH analog given to promote renal water reabsorption. A successful treatment plan will concentrate the urine, causing the specific gravity to increase into a normal range (1.005 to 1.030), as seen in Option D. Option A indicates worsening hypernatremia, a complication of DI. Option B indicates an increasing, massive urine output, showing the treatment is failing. Option C demonstrates persistent polydipsia (thirst), which means the DI is still uncontrolled.

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
</details>

---

### Question 145
A client is admitted with Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar State (HHS). The healthcare provider orders a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion. Prior to initiating the infusion, the nurse reviews the client's laboratory results: Blood Glucose 850 mg/dL, Potassium 3.1 mEq/L, and Sodium 138 mEq/L. Based on this drug-lab interaction, what is the safest action by the nurse?

A) Hold the insulin infusion and notify the healthcare provider to request potassium replacement.
B) Start the insulin infusion as ordered and monitor the potassium level closely.
C) Administer a rapid IV push of potassium chloride before starting the insulin drip.
D) Administer subcutaneous insulin instead of intravenous insulin to prevent rapid potassium shifts.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Hold the insulin infusion and notify the healthcare provider to request potassium replacement.

**Rationale:** This question focuses on Drug-Lab Interaction. Intravenous insulin acts by driving glucose into the cells, but it also rapidly shifts potassium from the extracellular space into the intracellular space. This can cause a sudden, severe drop in serum potassium levels, leading to life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmias. According to standard HHS and DKA protocols, if the client's serum potassium is below 3.3 mEq/L, the nurse must hold the insulin infusion and administer potassium replacement first until the level is > 3.3 mEq/L. Starting the infusion (Option B) is dangerous and could be fatal. Option C is a lethal action; potassium chloride must *never* be administered via IV push. Option D is incorrect because IV insulin is the standard of care for HHS, and subcutaneous insulin would still lower potassium while being less effective at treating the crisis.

**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION
</details>
### Question 146
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS 
**System Topic:** Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)

A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) has been receiving a regular intravenous insulin infusion and normal saline for the past 12 hours. The nurse reviews the client’s laboratory trends: 

*Admission:* pH 7.22, Anion Gap 20 mEq/L, Blood Glucose 450 mg/dL. 
*Current (12 hours later):* pH 7.36, Anion Gap 11 mEq/L, Blood Glucose 185 mg/dL. 

Based on this trend, what is the nurse's next expected action?

A. Increase the IV insulin infusion rate to rapidly reduce the blood glucose to 100 mg/dL.
B. Administer an intravenous bolus of 50% dextrose (D50) to prevent hypoglycemia.
C. Prepare to transition the client to a scheduled subcutaneous insulin regimen.
D. Maintain the current regular insulin infusion rate and discontinue all intravenous fluids.

<details>
<summary>Click to view Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** DKA is considered resolved when the blood glucose is less than 200 mg/dL, the blood pH normalizes (>7.30), and the anion gap closes (normal is typically 8–12 mEq/L). Because the client's current labs show a resolved acidosis and a closed anion gap, the nurse should anticipate transitioning the client from the continuous IV insulin infusion to scheduled subcutaneous insulin. Intravenous fluids (often transitioned to a dextrose-containing solution) and IV insulin are typically continued for 1-2 hours *after* the first subcutaneous dose is given to prevent a rebound into DKA, so discontinuing fluids or increasing the IV insulin rate is incorrect. Giving D50 is unnecessary as the blood glucose is not in a hypoglycemic range.
</details>

---

### Question 147
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
**System Topic:** Addison's Disease

The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease regarding the management of their condition during periods of stress and illness. Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the teaching?

A. "If I develop a mild fever or catch a cold, I will need to double my daily dose of hydrocortisone."
B. "I should decrease my daily salt intake to prevent swelling in my legs and feet."
C. "If I experience severe nausea and cannot keep my pills down, I will just skip the dose for that day."
D. "I will take my fludrocortisone right before bedtime on an empty stomach."

<details>
<summary>Click to view Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** Addison's disease requires lifelong glucocorticoid (and often mineralocorticoid) replacement. During periods of physical stress—such as a fever, infection, or minor surgery—the body's demand for cortisol increases. Clients are taught "sick day rules," which include doubling or tripling their daily glucocorticoid (hydrocortisone) dose to prevent an adrenal crisis. Clients should *never* restrict sodium, as Addison's disease causes sodium wasting. If a client is vomiting and cannot tolerate oral medications, they must administer an emergency intramuscular injection of hydrocortisone and seek immediate medical care; they should never skip a dose. Fludrocortisone should be taken in the morning to mimic the body's natural diurnal rhythm and prevent insomnia.
</details>

---

### Question 148
**Lens:** ADVERSE VS SIDE EFFECT
**System Topic:** Diabetes Insipidus (DI)

A client with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is receiving intranasal desmopressin (DDAVP). During the shift assessment, the client reports several new symptoms. Which finding represents a dangerous adverse effect of this medication rather than an expected finding or minor side effect?

A. Occasional dry mouth and thirst
B. Urine specific gravity increasing from 1.002 to 1.015
C. Mild nasal irritation and congestion
D. A 3-pound (1.4 kg) weight gain accompanied by a severe headache

<details>
<summary>Click to view Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** Desmopressin (DDAVP) is a synthetic form of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat DI by promoting renal water reabsorption. A dangerous adverse effect of this medication is water intoxication, which leads to dilutional hyponatremia. Manifestations of water intoxication include rapid weight gain, severe headache, confusion, and potential seizures. This requires immediate intervention. Mild nasal irritation is an expected, minor side effect of the intranasal route. An increase in urine specific gravity demonstrates the therapeutic effect of the drug (concentrating the urine). Dry mouth and thirst are symptoms of the underlying DI, not a dangerous drug effect.
</details>

---

### Question 149
**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION
**System Topic:** Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH)

A client with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) is prescribed oral demeclocycline. Before administering the scheduled dose, the nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results: 

- Serum sodium: 138 mEq/L
- Potassium: 4.2 mEq/L
- Blood urea nitrogen (BUN): 45 mg/dL
- Creatinine: 2.3 mg/dL

What is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A. Administer the scheduled dose since the serum sodium is normalizing.
B. Hold the medication and notify the healthcare provider of the lab results.
C. Administer the dose and request an order for oral potassium chloride.
D. Hold the medication and prepare to administer a 3% hypertonic saline infusion.

<details>
<summary>Click to view Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Demeclocycline is a tetracycline antibiotic that is used off-label for SIADH because it induces a reversible nephrogenic diabetes insipidus, effectively blocking the action of ADH on the renal tubules and promoting water excretion. However, demeclocycline is nephrotoxic. The client's elevated BUN (normal 10-20 mg/dL) and Creatinine (normal 0.6-1.2 mg/dL) indicate significant renal impairment. The nurse must hold the medication and notify the provider to prevent further acute kidney injury. Administering the dose is unsafe. Hypertonic saline is reserved for severe, symptomatic hyponatremia (usually sodium <120 mEq/L), whereas this client's sodium is currently within normal limits.
</details>

---

### Question 150
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
**System Topic:** Cushing's Syndrome vs Addison's Disease

A client is recovering on the medical-surgical unit 24 hours after a bilateral adrenalectomy for the treatment of Cushing's syndrome. Upon entering the room, the nurse notes that the client is newly lethargic. Vital signs are: Blood pressure 86/48 mmHg, Heart rate 122 bpm, and Respiratory rate 22 breaths/min. The client complains of diffuse abdominal pain. The nurse recognizes these findings as early signs of which specific complication?

A. Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS)
B. Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH)
C. Acute adrenal insufficiency (Addisonian crisis)
D. Hypoglycemic encephalopathy

<details>
<summary>Click to view Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** A bilateral adrenalectomy surgically removes both adrenal glands, abruptly eliminating the body's endogenous source of cortisol and aldosterone. If exogenous corticosteroid replacement therapy is inadequate post-operatively, or if the client experiences acute stress, they can rapidly develop acute adrenal insufficiency (Addisonian crisis). Hallmark manifestations include profound hypotension (due to sudden loss of aldosterone and cortisol), tachycardia, lethargy, severe weakness, and diffuse abdominal, back, or leg pain. Immediate administration of IV hydrocortisone and intravenous fluids is required to prevent cardiovascular collapse. HHS, SIADH, and isolated hypoglycemic encephalopathy do not fully align with this classic post-adrenalectomy presentation.
</details>
### Question 151
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING (Managing chronic system-related illness and lifestyle changes)

A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease who is prescribed daily oral hydrocortisone and fludrocortisone. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required for this condition?

A) "I will decrease my hydrocortisone dose if I experience nausea and vomiting."
B) "I should strictly limit my dietary sodium intake to prevent dangerous fluid retention."
C) "I need to increase my daily salt intake, especially during strenuous exercise or hot weather."
D) "I will take my daily fludrocortisone dose at bedtime to prevent morning fatigue."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** Addison's disease is characterized by a deficiency of both glucocorticoids (cortisol) and mineralocorticoids (aldosterone). Aldosterone is responsible for sodium retention and potassium excretion. Without it, patients waste sodium and retain potassium. Therefore, patients with Addison's disease require a high-sodium diet, especially during times of excessive sweating (e.g., hot weather, strenuous exercise) to prevent profound hyponatremia and hypovolemia.
*Option A is incorrect* because during times of physical stress or illness (like nausea/vomiting), steroid doses must be *increased* (often double or triple) to prevent an Addisonian crisis. If vomiting prevents oral intake, the patient requires emergency IM injections.
*Option B is incorrect* because restricting sodium is dangerous for an Addison's patient who is already prone to sodium wasting.
*Option D is incorrect* because fludrocortisone and hydrocortisone should typically be taken in the morning to mimic the body's natural circadian rhythm of cortisol release.
</details>

---

### Question 152
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS (Interpreting a shift in system-specific labs over time)

The nurse is reviewing the laboratory trends of a patient admitted with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) who has been receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion and normal saline for the past 12 hours. 

*   **Admission:** Blood glucose 650 mg/dL, Potassium 5.2 mEq/L, pH 7.15, HCO3 10 mEq/L.
*   **Current:** Blood glucose 220 mg/dL, Potassium 3.4 mEq/L, pH 7.31, HCO3 16 mEq/L.

Based on these specific laboratory trends, which action should the nurse anticipate next?

A) Transitioning the intravenous fluids to 5% dextrose in 0.45% sodium chloride (D5W/0.45% NaCl).
B) Administering a bolus of intravenous regular insulin to rapidly correct the remaining acidosis.
C) Stopping the intravenous insulin infusion entirely and starting subcutaneous glargine.
D) Administering oral sodium polystyrene sulfonate to manage the potassium level.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** In DKA, the primary goal of the continuous insulin infusion is to correct the metabolic acidosis (close the anion gap) and clear ketones. However, blood glucose often falls to normal levels before the acidosis is fully resolved. When the blood glucose reaches approximately 200–250 mg/dL, the nurse must anticipate adding dextrose to the IV fluids (e.g., D5W/0.45% NaCl). This prevents iatrogenic hypoglycemia while allowing the insulin drip to continue until the pH and bicarbonate levels normalize.
*Option B is incorrect* because bolusing insulin will cause a dangerously rapid drop in blood glucose and exacerbate the dropping potassium level.
*Option C is incorrect* because the anion gap is not closed and the patient is still acidotic (pH 7.31, HCO3 16). Stopping the IV insulin too early will cause a relapse into ketogenesis.
*Option D is incorrect* because the patient's potassium is dropping (3.4 mEq/L) as insulin drives potassium back into the cells. Giving sodium polystyrene sulfonate (a potassium-lowering agent) is contraindicated and would cause severe hypokalemia.
</details>

---

### Question 153
**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION (Safety: Should the nurse hold a med based on a system-specific lab?)

A patient with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) is prescribed scheduled demeclocycline. The nurse reviews the patient's morning laboratory results and notes a serum sodium level of 128 mEq/L, blood urea nitrogen (BUN) of 8 mg/dL, and a serum creatinine of 0.7 mg/dL. Based on these findings, what is the most appropriate action by the nurse regarding the scheduled demeclocycline dose?

A) Hold the medication and notify the provider because the serum sodium indicates severe hypernatremia.
B) Hold the medication because the blood urea nitrogen indicates impending renal failure.
C) Administer the medication and immediately request a prescription for a rapid infusion of 3% sodium chloride.
D) Administer the medication as prescribed because it reduces the renal response to antidiuretic hormone to improve the sodium level.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** SIADH results in water intoxication and dilutional hyponatremia. Demeclocycline is a tetracycline antibiotic that is uniquely used off-label for SIADH because it acts as an antidiuretic hormone (ADH) receptor antagonist in the kidneys. By blocking ADH, it promotes diuresis and helps correct the hyponatremia. A sodium level of 128 mEq/L indicates hyponatremia, confirming the continued need for this medication.
*Option A is incorrect* because a sodium of 128 mEq/L is hyponatremia, not hypernatremia.
*Option B is incorrect* because a BUN of 8 mg/dL and a creatinine of 0.7 mg/dL are perfectly normal and do not indicate renal failure (additionally, low BUN is an expected finding in fluid-overloaded SIADH patients).
*Option C is incorrect* because while 3% sodium chloride is used for severe, symptomatic hyponatremia (usually when seizing), a rapid infusion is extremely dangerous due to the risk of osmotic demyelination syndrome. Routine administration of demeclocycline is the appropriate ongoing pharmacological intervention here.
</details>

---

### Question 154
**Lens:** DELEGATION (Assigning tasks for stable vs unstable system-specific patients)

The registered nurse (RN) is creating the shift assignment for a care team consisting of an RN, a licensed practical nurse (LPN), and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP). Which task is most appropriate to delegate to the UAP regarding patients with adrenal disorders?

A) Recording the daily weight and calculating the 24-hour urine volume for a stable patient with Cushing's syndrome.
B) Ambulating a patient with Addison's disease who is actively reporting dizziness and weakness upon standing.
C) Reviewing the low-sodium dietary restrictions with a patient newly diagnosed with Cushing's syndrome.
D) Monitoring the blood pressure every 15 minutes for a patient suspected of having an Addisonian crisis.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** The UAP's scope of practice includes gathering standard, routine data on stable patients. Patients with Cushing's syndrome suffer from fluid retention, making daily weights and strict intake/output (including 24-hour urine collection) a vital but routine task that perfectly aligns with the UAP's role.
*Option B is incorrect* because a patient with Addison's disease reporting active dizziness and weakness upon standing is exhibiting signs of orthostatic hypotension and hypovolemia. This patient is unstable and at high risk for a fall or cardiovascular collapse; ambulation requires an RN or LPN to assess the patient's hemodynamic tolerance.
*Option C is incorrect* because patient teaching and reviewing newly prescribed dietary restrictions is strictly the responsibility of the RN.
*Option D is incorrect* because a patient in an Addisonian crisis is highly unstable, experiencing shock, and requires continuous assessment and intervention by the RN.
</details>

---

### Question 155
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS (Determining if the multi-day system treatment plan is working)

A patient with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) has been receiving aggressive intravenous hydration and a continuous insulin infusion for the past 24 hours. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the current treatment plan. Which of the following findings best indicates that the primary treatment goals for HHS are being met?

A) The patient's serum potassium level has steadily increased from 3.2 mEq/L to 4.5 mEq/L.
B) The patient's serum osmolality has decreased from 345 mOsm/kg to 295 mOsm/kg, and mentation is clear.
C) The patient's blood glucose level dropped from 900 mg/dL to 150 mg/dL within the first two hours of treatment.
D) The patient's urine dipstick shows an absence of ketones, and the arterial pH is 7.40.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) is characterized by extreme hyperglycemia, profound dehydration, hyperosmolarity, and altered mental status (often without the significant ketosis seen in DKA). The primary goals of treatment are to restore vascular volume, normalize serum osmolality (normal is 275–295 mOsm/kg), and reverse neurological deficits. A return to a normal osmolality and clear mentation directly reflects the resolution of the most dangerous pathophysiology of HHS.
*Option A is incorrect* because while restoring potassium is a goal of fluid and insulin therapy, it is not the primary hallmark of HHS resolution.
*Option C is incorrect* because dropping blood glucose from 900 to 150 mg/dL in two hours is dangerously rapid and places the patient at high risk for fatal cerebral edema. Glucose should be lowered gradually.
*Option D is incorrect* because significant ketosis and acidosis are features of DKA, not HHS. While a normal pH is good, the absence of ketones and normal pH are expected baseline findings in HHS rather than proof of treatment efficacy.
</details>
### Question 156
A client with Addison's disease is admitted to the medical-surgical unit with a severe respiratory tract infection. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the client is experiencing an early sign of an impending adrenal (Addisonian) crisis?

A) Bounding peripheral pulses and flushed skin
B) Sudden onset of profound weakness and postural hypotension
C) Unexplained weight gain and prominent purple striae
D) Severe polyuria and excessive, unquenchable thirst

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition (Identify early signs of a specific system failure)

**Rationale:** An adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis) is a life-threatening complication of Addison's disease triggered by stress, such as an infection, trauma, or surgery. The lack of cortisol and aldosterone leads to rapid fluid loss, sodium loss, and potassium retention. Early clinical manifestations include profound weakness, severe postural hypotension, nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain. If left untreated, it progresses rapidly to hypovolemic shock and vascular collapse. Option A describes signs of fluid volume overload or fever, Option C describes signs of Cushing's syndrome, and Option D describes Diabetes Insipidus. 
</details>

---

### Question 157
A nurse is caring for a client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) who is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion. The most recent basic metabolic panel reveals a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L (3.1 mmol/L). What is the nurse's most appropriate action?

A) Increase the rate of the insulin infusion to drive potassium back into the cells.
B) Administer a prescribed dose of sodium polystyrene sulfonate immediately.
C) Hold the insulin infusion and notify the healthcare provider to request a potassium supplement.
D) Continue the insulin infusion as prescribed and recheck the potassium level in 4 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction (Safety: Should the nurse hold a med based on a system-specific lab?)

**Rationale:** Intravenous insulin shifts glucose and potassium from the intravascular space into the intracellular space. Normal serum potassium is 3.5 to 5.0 mEq/L. A potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L indicates hypokalemia. Continuing or increasing the insulin infusion will drive serum potassium even lower, placing the client at extreme risk for life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmias. The nurse must hold the insulin infusion, notify the provider, and administer potassium replacement before resuming the insulin protocol. Option B is a treatment for *hyperkalemia*. 
</details>

---

### Question 158
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client with newly diagnosed Diabetes Insipidus (DI) who has been prescribed intranasal desmopressin. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of how to manage this chronic condition and prevent complications?

A) "I should drink at least 3 to 4 liters of water daily to ensure my kidneys are being flushed effectively."
B) "I need to limit my sodium intake to less than 1,500 milligrams a day to prevent swelling."
C) "If I develop a cold and a runny nose, I should double my dose to make sure the medication absorbs properly."
D) "I will monitor myself for signs of a headache, confusion, or sudden weight gain, which could mean I am holding onto too much water."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Complex Teaching (Managing chronic system-related illness and lifestyle changes)

**Rationale:** Desmopressin is a synthetic analog of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting water reabsorption in the kidneys, thereby reducing urine output. A critical adverse effect of this medication is water intoxication and dilutional hyponatremia. The client must be taught to monitor for signs of fluid volume overload and hyponatremia, such as headache, confusion, lethargy, and sudden weight gain. Option A is incorrect because excessive fluid intake while taking an ADH analog increases the risk of water intoxication. Option C is unsafe; patients should never unilaterally double their medication dose, and absorption issues should be reported to the provider (who may switch them to an oral route). Option B is a dietary intervention for heart failure or hypertension, not specific to DI management.
</details>

---

### Question 159
A client is receiving treatment for Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH). Over the past 48 hours, the nurse notes the client's serum sodium level has shifted from 118 mEq/L to 132 mEq/L, and urine specific gravity has shifted from 1.032 to 1.015. How should the nurse interpret these data trends?

A) The treatment is effective, as the client's water retention is resolving and serum sodium is normalizing.
B) The client is developing Diabetes Insipidus due to an overcorrection of antidiuretic hormone.
C) The treatment plan is failing, as the dilutional hyponatremia is worsening.
D) The client is experiencing severe dehydration, requiring an immediate increase in oral fluids.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis (Interpreting a shift in system-specific labs over time)

**Rationale:** SIADH is characterized by excessive ADH release, leading to profound water retention, dilutional hyponatremia (low serum sodium), and concentrated urine (high urine specific gravity). Normal serum sodium is 135-145 mEq/L, and normal urine specific gravity is 1.005-1.030. The shift in serum sodium from 118 mEq/L to 132 mEq/L indicates that dilutional hyponatremia is improving. The decrease in urine specific gravity from 1.032 to 1.015 indicates that the kidneys are appropriately excreting excess water (diuresis). Therefore, these trends show that the prescribed fluid restriction and/or medications (e.g., tolvaptan, hypertonic saline) are effectively resolving the SIADH.
</details>

---

### Question 160
The registered nurse (RN) is coordinating care for a client recovering from Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS) who is now hemodynamically stable and transitioning to subcutaneous insulin therapy. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Checking the client's capillary blood glucose level and interpreting the result to adjust the insulin dose.
B) Showing the client how to rotate injection sites for the newly prescribed subcutaneous insulin.
C) Documenting the exact volume of oral fluid intake and output during the shift.
D) Assessing the client's skin turgor and mucous membranes for signs of resolving dehydration.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Delegation (Assigning tasks for stable vs unstable system-specific patients)

**Rationale:** The scope of practice for a UAP includes standard, non-invasive tasks for stable clients, such as hygiene, ambulation, and obtaining routine measurements (e.g., vital signs, intake and output). Documenting oral fluid intake and output is safely within the UAP's scope. Option A involves clinical judgment and interpreting data to adjust medications, which requires an RN. Option B involves client teaching, which is an RN responsibility. Option D involves nursing assessment to evaluate physiological status, which cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 161
A patient admitted with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion. Over the last 12 hours, the nurse notes the following laboratory trend: blood glucose decreased from 480 mg/dL to 210 mg/dL, the anion gap decreased from 19 mEq/L to 10 mEq/L, and serum potassium shifted from 5.4 mEq/L to 3.8 mEq/L. Which interpretation of this data trend is the most accurate?

A. The patient is developing insulin resistance and requires an increased infusion rate.
B. The data indicates worsening acidosis and requires the administration of sodium bicarbonate.
C. The DKA is resolving, and the nurse should prepare to transition the patient to subcutaneous insulin.
D. The rapid drop in glucose suggests impending cerebral edema, requiring immediate cessation of all insulin.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**Rationale:** The resolution of DKA is clinically defined by a closed anion gap (typically less than 12 mEq/L) and a blood glucose level falling below 200-250 mg/dL. As insulin drives glucose and potassium into the cells, acidosis resolves, which is reflected in the normalizing anion gap. Because the anion gap has closed to 10 mEq/L and the glucose is 210 mg/dL, the data trend indicates the DKA is resolving. The nurse should prepare to overlap the IV insulin with subcutaneous insulin before discontinuing the drip to prevent a rebound into DKA. 
</details>

### Question 162
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease). Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of complex long-term lifestyle and medication management?

A. "I will need to decrease my daily sodium intake to prevent fluid retention."
B. "I should increase my hydrocortisone dose during times of severe emotional or physical stress."
C. "I can stop taking fludrocortisone once my morning energy levels return to normal."
D. "I will monitor my blood glucose closely because these medications will cause hypoglycemia."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**Rationale:** Patients with Addison's disease lack endogenous glucocorticoids (cortisol) and mineralocorticoids (aldosterone). In times of severe physical or emotional stress (such as infection, surgery, or trauma), the body naturally requires more cortisol to cope. Therefore, patients must be taught to "stress dose" or increase their exogenous glucocorticoid intake during these periods to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Patients with Addison's typically need adequate or increased sodium (not decreased), must never abruptly stop their medications, and are at risk for hyperglycemia (not hypoglycemia) from glucocorticoid therapy.
</details>

---

### Question 163
A patient is receiving treatment for Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) secondary to small cell lung cancer. The nurse observes the patient becoming increasingly restless, reporting a new severe headache, and exhibiting sluggish pupillary responses. These early signs indicate the development of which system-specific complication?

A. Central pontine myelinolysis
B. Hypovolemic shock
C. Acute pulmonary edema
D. Developing cerebral edema

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**Rationale:** SIADH causes excessive retention of free water, leading to dilutional hyponatremia. When serum sodium drops significantly or rapidly, the osmotic gradient shifts fluid into the intracellular space, particularly into brain cells. This results in cerebral edema. Early signs of cerebral edema include restlessness, headache, confusion, lethargy, and altered pupillary responses. Central pontine myelinolysis (osmotic demyelination syndrome) is a complication of correcting hyponatremia *too quickly*, not a direct complication of the hyponatremia itself.
</details>

---

### Question 164
An older adult patient was admitted 48 hours ago with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS) and an initial serum osmolality of 355 mOsm/kg. The patient has been receiving continuous intravenous fluids and insulin therapy. Which finding best evaluates that the multi-day treatment plan is successfully meeting the primary physiological goal for this specific condition?

A. The patient is oriented to person, place, and time with resolved hemiparesis.
B. The patient's urine output has stabilized at an average of 100 mL/hr.
C. The patient's serum ketone level remains negative.
D. The patient's blood pressure is 110/70 mmHg without orthostatic changes.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**Rationale:** The hallmark of HHS is extreme hyperosmolality leading to profound intracellular dehydration, which severely affects the brain. This results in significant central nervous system dysfunction, including altered mental status, confusion, and focal neurologic deficits such as hemiparesis or aphasia. The primary goal of treatment is to safely restore intracellular hydration and normalize osmolality. Resolution of the neurologic deficits (orientation and resolved hemiparesis) is the most direct and specific indicator that the treatment of the hyperosmolar state has been successful. Ketones are characteristically absent or minimal in HHS, making them less relevant for evaluating treatment progress compared to DKA.
</details>

---

### Question 165
A patient with central Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is scheduled to receive a routine morning dose of intranasal desmopressin (DDAVP). The nurse reviews the morning laboratory results and notes a serum sodium level of 127 mEq/L. Based on this system-specific laboratory value, what is the safest action by the nurse?

A. Administer the desmopressin as ordered to prevent further severe sodium loss.
B. Hold the desmopressin and prepare to administer a rapid infusion of 3% normal saline.
C. Hold the desmopressin and notify the healthcare provider of the laboratory result.
D. Administer the desmopressin and encourage the patient to drink more free water.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**Rationale:** Desmopressin (DDAVP) is a synthetic analog of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting renal water reabsorption. A significant adverse effect of this medication is water intoxication and dilutional hyponatremia. A serum sodium level of 127 mEq/L is significantly low (normal is 135-145 mEq/L). Administering desmopressin would cause the patient to retain more free water, further diluting the serum sodium and risking life-threatening complications like seizures or cerebral edema. The nurse must hold the medication and notify the provider. 3% saline is rarely given rapidly and requires a specific order based on severe neurologic symptoms. 
</details>
### Question 166
A client is receiving a continuous regular insulin infusion and intravenous fluid resuscitation for Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the last two hours, the client's blood glucose has decreased from 450 mg/dL to 240 mg/dL. Which early sign indicates a complication of the current treatment plan?

A. The client exhibits a flushed face and fruity breath odor.
B. The client develops generalized muscle weakness and prominent U waves on the ECG.
C. The client reports increasing thirst and polyuria.
D. The client's respiratory rate increases to 28 breaths per minute with deep inspirations.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** DKA vs HHS

**Rationale:** 
During the treatment of DKA, continuous insulin therapy not only drives glucose into the cells but also drives potassium into the intracellular space. This rapid shift can lead to profound, life-threatening hypokalemia, even if the client's initial potassium levels were normal or high. Generalized muscle weakness and the presence of U waves on an electrocardiogram (ECG) are early signs of hypokalemia. Option A, C, and D are classic presenting manifestations of ongoing DKA (hyperglycemia, dehydration, and Kussmaul respirations to blow off excess acid) rather than new complications stemming from the treatment itself. 
</details>

---

### Question 167
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of managing the illness and required lifestyle changes?

A. "I will need to decrease my daily sodium intake to prevent fluid retention."
B. "I should limit my daily fluid intake to 1 liter to avoid swelling."
C. "If I get a fever or an infection, I should increase my corticosteroid dose as directed."
D. "I can stop taking my fludrocortisone once my blood pressure returns to a normal range."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Cushing's vs Addison's

**Rationale:** 
Addison's disease is characterized by an inadequate production of corticosteroids and mineralocorticoids by the adrenal glands. During times of physiological stress (such as a fever, infection, or surgery), the body's demand for cortisol increases significantly. Because the client cannot naturally produce more cortisol, they must proactively increase ("stress dose") their prescribed corticosteroid to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Option A is incorrect because clients with Addison's often need *increased* sodium, as they lose sodium easily due to a lack of aldosterone. Option B is incorrect as these clients are prone to dehydration and should not restrict fluids. Option D is incorrect because fludrocortisone is a lifelong maintenance therapy, not a temporary medication.
</details>

---

### Question 168
A client with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is receiving scheduled doses of intranasal desmopressin. Which laboratory result requires the nurse to hold the medication and immediately contact the healthcare provider?

A. Urine specific gravity 1.002
B. Serum potassium 4.8 mEq/L
C. Serum calcium 9.5 mg/dL
D. Serum sodium 128 mEq/L

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**System Focus:** SIADH / DI

**Rationale:** 
Desmopressin is a synthetic analog of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting water reabsorption in the kidneys. A major adverse effect of this medication is excessive water retention, which leads to dilutional hyponatremia. A serum sodium level of 128 mEq/L is significantly low (normal is 135-145 mEq/L). Administering further desmopressin could worsen the hyponatremia, potentially leading to severe neurological complications such as seizures or a coma. The nurse must hold the dose and notify the provider. Option A indicates highly dilute urine, which is a symptom of DI and indicates the *need* for the medication. Options B and C are within normal limits.
</details>

### Question 169
The nurse is reviewing the flow sheet for a client admitted with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS) 48 hours ago. Which trend in the client's data best indicates that the current fluid and insulin therapy is effective?

A. Serum osmolality decreased from 345 mOsm/kg to 295 mOsm/kg, and the client is oriented to person, place, and time.
B. Blood glucose decreased from 850 mg/dL to 180 mg/dL, and the urine tests positive for moderate ketones.
C. Blood urea nitrogen (BUN) increased from 35 mg/dL to 48 mg/dL, and urine output is 20 mL/hr.
D. Serum sodium increased from 145 mEq/L to 158 mEq/L, and the client has bounding peripheral pulses.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** DKA vs HHS

**Rationale:** 
HHS is characterized by profound hyperglycemia, severe dehydration, and hyperosmolarity, typically without significant ketosis. The primary goal of treatment is aggressive fluid replacement to normalize serum osmolality and improve neurological status, alongside insulin to gradually lower blood glucose. A decreasing serum osmolality (normal is 275-295 mOsm/kg) accompanied by an improved level of consciousness is the best indicator of resolving HHS. Option B is incorrect because the development of moderate ketones would indicate a shift toward DKA or worsening metabolic distress, which is not an expected finding of resolving HHS. Options C and D indicate worsening dehydration, acute kidney injury, and hypernatremia, meaning the treatment is not yet effective.
</details>

---

### Question 170
A client with Cushing's syndrome is on a multi-day treatment plan aimed at managing secondary metabolic complications while awaiting surgical intervention. Which finding best indicates that the treatment goals are being met?

A. The client reports a 2-kilogram weight gain over the past three days with 2+ pitting edema.
B. The client's capillary blood glucose ranges between 100-140 mg/dL and serum potassium is 4.2 mEq/L.
C. The client's blood pressure averages 168/94 mmHg over a 24-hour period.
D. The client demonstrates a depressed mood and states, "I hate how my face looks right now."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** Cushing's vs Addison's

**Rationale:** 
Cushing's syndrome involves an excess of glucocorticoids (cortisol), which typically causes hyperglycemia, sodium and water retention, and potassium excretion (hypokalemia). A multi-day metabolic management plan aims to control these specific imbalances prior to surgery. A stable capillary blood glucose within a controlled target range and a normal serum potassium level (3.5-5.0 mEq/L) indicate that metabolic management goals are being successfully met. Option A and C indicate worsening fluid retention and uncontrolled hypertension, which are untreated complications of the disease. Option D is an expected psychosocial finding due to the physical changes of Cushing's (moon face, buffalo hump), but it does not evaluate the effectiveness of the physiological metabolic treatment plan.
</details>
### Question 171
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion. The latest laboratory results show a blood glucose of 350 mg/dL, an arterial pH of 7.25, and a serum potassium of 3.2 mEq/L. The nurse receives an order to increase the insulin infusion rate. Which action should the nurse take?

A) Increase the insulin infusion as ordered to correct the ongoing metabolic acidosis.
B) Administer intravenous sodium bicarbonate before increasing the insulin rate.
C) Hold the insulin rate increase and notify the provider regarding the potassium level.
D) Hang a secondary infusion of 0.9% Normal Saline to rapidly correct the potassium deficit.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**System/Topic:** Endocrine / DKA
Insulin causes an intracellular shift of potassium, which actively lowers serum potassium levels. A serum potassium of 3.2 mEq/L is already indicative of hypokalemia. Increasing or even continuing an insulin infusion without concurrently replacing potassium can lead to severe, life-threatening arrhythmias. The nurse must hold the increase in insulin and notify the provider to obtain an order for potassium replacement before proceeding. Sodium bicarbonate is rarely used in DKA unless the pH is critically low (typically < 6.9) and can further decrease potassium. Normal saline does not correct a potassium deficit; potassium chloride must be added to the IV fluids.
</details>

---

### Question 172
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of the complex medication regimen and necessary lifestyle adjustments?

A) "I will double my daily dose of hydrocortisone if I experience a severe emotional or physical stressor."
B) "I will need to decrease my salt intake to prevent fluid accumulation and swelling in my legs."
C) "I should immediately stop taking my hydrocortisone if I develop a fever or stomach bug."
D) "I need to check my blood sugar daily because this medication will likely cause hypoglycemia."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System/Topic:** Endocrine / Addison's Disease
Addison's disease involves a deficiency in cortisol and aldosterone, requiring lifelong corticosteroid replacement. During periods of physical or severe emotional stress (e.g., surgery, illness, trauma), the body requires higher levels of cortisol. Patients are taught to "stress dose" (often doubling or tripling their steroid dose) to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Patients with Addison's typically experience hyponatremia and should *not* restrict their salt intake; in fact, they may need to increase it during hot weather or exercise. Stopping steroids abruptly, especially during an illness, will precipitate an Addisonian crisis. Corticosteroids typically cause hyperglycemia as a side effect, not hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 173
A patient is receiving treatment for Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) which includes strict fluid restriction and administration of tolvaptan. Over the last 48 hours, the nurse notes the patient's serum sodium has increased from 124 mEq/L to 134 mEq/L, and urine specific gravity has decreased from 1.032 to 1.015. Which conclusion should the nurse draw from these data trends?

A) The patient is developing a life-threatening complication called central pontine myelinolysis.
B) The data indicates an impending Addisonian crisis due to severe systemic sodium loss.
C) The patient has transitioned into Diabetes Insipidus and requires exogenous desmopressin (DDAVP).
D) The treatment is effective, demonstrating resolution of water intoxication and dilutional hyponatremia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System/Topic:** Endocrine / SIADH vs DI
SIADH is characterized by excessive ADH release, leading to water retention, dilutional hyponatremia, and highly concentrated urine (high specific gravity). Effective treatment (such as fluid restriction and vasopressin receptor antagonists like tolvaptan) promotes water excretion. The data trend shows serum sodium returning toward the normal range (135-145 mEq/L) and urine specific gravity normalizing (1.005-1.030), indicating the treatment is working correctly. Central pontine myelinolysis (osmotic demyelination syndrome) occurs with rapid overcorrection of sodium (usually >10-12 mEq in 24 hours); a 10 mEq rise over 48 hours is safe and appropriate. The data does not reflect Diabetes Insipidus, which would present with hypernatremia and very dilute urine (specific gravity < 1.005).
</details>

---

### Question 174
A registered nurse (RN) is working with a licensed practical nurse (LPN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a medical-surgical unit. Which task is most appropriate to delegate to the LPN regarding patients with adrenal disorders?

A) Provide initial discharge teaching on infection prevention for a patient newly diagnosed with Cushing's syndrome.
B) Administer scheduled oral fludrocortisone to a stable patient with Addison's disease.
C) Assess the vital signs and skin turgor of a patient admitted one hour ago with suspected Addisonian crisis.
D) Evaluate the daily weight trends for a patient with Cushing's syndrome to determine diuretic effectiveness.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Delegation
**System/Topic:** Endocrine / Cushing's vs Addison's
Administering scheduled oral medications to a stable patient falls safely within the scope of practice for an LPN. Providing initial discharge teaching (Option A), assessing an unstable, newly admitted patient in a potential crisis state (Option C), and evaluating the overall effectiveness of a treatment plan or medication (Option D) all require the advanced clinical judgment of the RN and cannot be delegated to an LPN or UAP.
</details>

### Question 175
A patient with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS) has been receiving aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation and a continuous insulin infusion for the past 24 hours. The patient's initial serum osmolality on admission was 345 mOsm/kg. Which assessment finding best indicates that the primary treatment goals for HHS are being met?

A) The patient's serum osmolality is 290 mOsm/kg, and neurological status has returned to baseline.
B) The patient's blood glucose level decreases to 250 mg/dL, and a dextrose infusion is initiated.
C) The patient's urine output is 20 mL/hr, and serum potassium remains stable at 4.0 mEq/L.
D) The patient's arterial blood gas reveals a pH of 7.35 and a bicarbonate level of 22 mEq/L.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System/Topic:** Endocrine / HHS vs DKA
The primary pathophysiological issue in HHS is severe dehydration and hyperosmolarity, which leads to profound neurological impairment (such as confusion, lethargy, or coma). The main goal of therapy is aggressive fluid resuscitation to correct the hyperosmolar state. A normal serum osmolality (275-295 mOsm/kg) coupled with a return to baseline neurological function indicates that the primary goal has been successfully met. While initiating dextrose when blood glucose reaches 250 mg/dL is a standard procedural step to prevent hypoglycemia, it is an intervention benchmark rather than the primary physiological goal of resolving the syndrome. A urine output of 20 mL/hr is inadequate and indicates ongoing fluid deficit. Arterial blood gas normalization evaluates the resolution of ketoacidosis, which is the primary concern in DKA, not HHS.
</details>
### Question 176
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) has been receiving a continuous regular insulin infusion and intravenous normal saline for 6 hours. The nurse reviews the trended laboratory results:
*   **Admission:** Blood glucose 540 mg/dL, pH 7.15, HCO3 12 mEq/L, Anion gap 20 mEq/L
*   **Hour 4:** Blood glucose 250 mg/dL, pH 7.28, HCO3 15 mEq/L, Anion gap 16 mEq/L
*   **Hour 6:** Blood glucose 190 mg/dL, pH 7.32, HCO3 18 mEq/L, Anion gap 14 mEq/L

Based on this data trend, which action should the nurse take regarding the treatment plan?

A) Discontinue the regular insulin infusion and administer subcutaneous glargine.
B) Administer an intravenous bolus of sodium bicarbonate.
C) Add 5% dextrose to the intravenous maintenance fluids.
D) Increase the insulin infusion rate to rapidly correct the remaining acidosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Add 5% dextrose to the intravenous maintenance fluids.

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: Data Trend Analysis**
In the treatment of DKA, the continuous insulin infusion must be maintained until the ketoacidosis resolves, which is indicated by the closure of the anion gap (typically < 12 mEq/L) and normalization of pH/bicarbonate. In this scenario, at Hour 6, the anion gap is 14 mEq/L, meaning the acidosis is improving but not fully resolved. However, the blood glucose has dropped below 200-250 mg/dL. To prevent severe hypoglycemia while allowing the insulin infusion to continue treating the acidosis, dextrose must be added to the intravenous fluids. Discontinuing the insulin (Option A) would cause rebound ketoacidosis. Increasing the insulin rate (Option D) would cause profound hypoglycemia. Sodium bicarbonate (Option B) is not indicated unless the pH is critically low (typically < 6.9).
</details>

---

### Question 177
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the complex lifestyle and medication management required?

A) "I should double my daily dose of hydrocortisone if I develop a fever or a severe illness."
B) "I must strictly limit my daily dietary sodium intake to prevent dangerous fluid retention."
C) "I will gradually taper and stop taking my fludrocortisone once my extreme fatigue resolves."
D) "I can use over-the-counter hydrocortisone cream if I accidentally miss taking my oral pills."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "I should double my daily dose of hydrocortisone if I develop a fever or a severe illness."

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: Complex Teaching**
Clients with Addison's disease (primary adrenal insufficiency) lack endogenous cortisol and aldosterone. They must take lifelong glucocorticoid (hydrocortisone) and mineralocorticoid (fludrocortisone) replacement. During times of physical stress (e.g., fever, infection, surgery), the body naturally requires more cortisol. Because the client's adrenal glands cannot produce it, they must implement "sick day rules" by increasing (often doubling or tripling) their glucocorticoid dose to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis (Option A). Clients with Addison's are prone to hyponatremia and should *not* restrict sodium (Option B). Medication is lifelong and cannot be stopped when symptoms resolve (Option C). Topical creams do not provide adequate systemic absorption to replace oral doses (Option D).
</details>

### Question 178
A client with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is prescribed scheduled intravenous desmopressin (DDAVP). The nurse reviews the morning laboratory results and notes a serum sodium level of 128 mEq/L. Which action should the nurse take?

A) Request a prescription for a loop diuretic to be given concurrently with the medication.
B) Administer the scheduled dose and encourage the client to increase free water intake.
C) Administer the desmopressin along with an intravenous bolus of 3% hypertonic saline.
D) Hold the desmopressin and promptly notify the healthcare provider of the lab result.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Hold the desmopressin and promptly notify the healthcare provider of the lab result.

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: Drug-Lab Interaction**
Desmopressin is a synthetic analogue of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting water reabsorption in the kidneys. While it correctly reduces polyuria and hypernatremia, excessive water retention can cause dilutional hyponatremia. A serum sodium level of 128 mEq/L is significantly low (normal: 135-145 mEq/L). Administering desmopressin in the presence of hyponatremia can lead to severe water intoxication, potentially causing cerebral edema, seizures, or coma. The safest action is to hold the medication and notify the provider to adjust the dose or frequency. Giving the medication and adding free water (Option B) would fatally worsen the hyponatremia. Options A and C are independent medical interventions that the nurse cannot simply initiate without first holding the contraindicated scheduled dose and conferring with the provider.
</details>
