### Question 179
The registered nurse (RN) is creating assignments for the day shift. Which task regarding the care of a stable client with Cushing's syndrome is most appropriate to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A) Assess the client's skin integrity for worsening thinning, excessive bruising, and striae.
B) Assist the client with ambulation and utilize a gait belt to prevent accidental falls.
C) Educate the client on the rationale for monitoring daily weights and blood pressure.
D) Monitor the client's telemetry tracing for early dysrhythmias related to hypokalemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Assist the client with ambulation and utilize a gait belt to prevent accidental falls.

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: Delegation**
Clients with Cushing's syndrome have an excess of corticosteroids, which leads to muscle wasting, weakness, and severe osteoporosis. These factors put the client at a high risk for falls and pathological fractures. Assisting with safe ambulation using a gait belt is a standard, routine task that falls safely within the UAP's scope of practice. Assessing skin integrity (Option A), providing patient education (Option C), and analyzing/monitoring telemetry strips for electrolyte imbalances (Option D) require clinical judgment and specialized nursing knowledge, and must remain the responsibility of the RN.
</details>

---

### Question 180
A client is receiving aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation and a continuous regular insulin infusion for Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS). Over the past 4 hours, the client’s blood glucose has decreased from 950 mg/dL to 400 mg/dL. The nurse notes the client has suddenly become lethargic, increasingly restless, and exhibits sluggish, unequal pupillary responses. What is the nurse's priority interpretation of these clinical findings?

A) The client is experiencing cerebral edema related to rapid intracellular fluid shifts.
B) The client is developing neuroglycopenia from a rapid drop in blood glucose.
C) The client is exhibiting expected neurological manifestations of resolving hyperosmolarity.
D) The client is manifesting late signs of profound insulin-induced hypokalemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The client is experiencing cerebral edema related to rapid intracellular fluid shifts.

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: Complication Recognition**
During the treatment of Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) or DKA, blood glucose and serum osmolarity must be lowered gradually. If the blood glucose drops too rapidly, the sudden shift in serum osmolarity causes extracellular fluid to rush into the still-hyperosmolar brain cells, resulting in cerebral edema. Lethargy, restlessness, altered mental status, and unequal or sluggish pupils are classic, dangerous signs of increased intracranial pressure and cerebral edema. This is a life-threatening complication, not an expected finding (Option C). The blood glucose is 400 mg/dL, so the client is not hypoglycemic/neuroglycopenic (Option B). While insulin does shift potassium intracellularly causing hypokalemia, hypokalemia presents with cardiac dysrhythmias and peripheral muscle weakness, not isolated pupillary changes and altered level of consciousness (Option D).
</details>
### Question 181
A patient with a history of Addison's disease is admitted to the medical-surgical unit with severe viral gastroenteritis. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the patient is developing an early systemic complication requiring immediate intervention?

A. Deep hyperpigmentation of the oral mucosa and palmar creases
B. Sudden onset of profound weakness and severe lower back pain
C. A capillary blood glucose level of 115 mg/dL (6.4 mmol/L)
D. A documented weight loss of 1 lb (0.45 kg) over the past 24 hours

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<em>Lens: Complication Recognition</em><br>
Patients with Addison's disease are at high risk for Addisonian crisis (acute adrenal insufficiency) during periods of physical stress, such as an infection (gastroenteritis). Early signs of an impending Addisonian crisis include sudden profound weakness, severe pain in the lower back, abdomen, or legs, followed rapidly by severe hypotension and vascular collapse. Hyperpigmentation (Option A) is a chronic, expected finding of Addison's disease, not an acute complication. A glucose level of 115 mg/dL (Option C) is normal, whereas hypoglycemia is typical in an adrenal crisis. A minor weight loss (Option D) is expected with gastroenteritis but does not immediately indicate a life-threatening crisis like severe back pain does.
</details>

---

### Question 182
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of regular insulin. The current laboratory results show a blood glucose of 280 mg/dL (15.5 mmol/L) and a serum potassium of 3.1 mEq/L (3.1 mmol/L). What is the appropriate nursing action based on these results?

A. Increase the insulin infusion rate to more rapidly correct the hyperglycemia.
B. Continue the insulin infusion and administer scheduled subcutaneous glargine.
C. Hold the insulin infusion and immediately notify the healthcare provider.
D. Administer an IV bolus of 50% dextrose to prevent hypoglycemic shock.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<em>Lens: Drug-Lab Interaction</em><br>
Insulin drives potassium from the extracellular space into the intracellular space, which can rapidly worsen hypokalemia. According to standard DKA protocols, if the serum potassium falls below 3.3 mEq/L, the continuous insulin infusion must be held, and potassium replacement must be initiated to prevent lethal cardiac arrhythmias. Continuing or increasing the insulin (Options A and B) is dangerous and could lead to severe hypokalemia. Dextrose (Option D) is added to fluids when blood glucose drops below 200-250 mg/dL to prevent hypoglycemia, but holding the insulin due to the critical potassium level is the immediate safety priority.
</details>

---

### Question 183
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with central Diabetes Insipidus (DI) who is prescribed intranasal desmopressin (DDAVP). Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of managing this chronic condition?

A. "I will restrict my dietary sodium intake to prevent fluid retention."
B. "I will take an extra dose of the medication if my urine becomes dark yellow."
C. "I need to drink at least 4 liters of water a day to prevent severe dehydration."
D. "I should weigh myself daily and report a sudden weight gain or persistent headache."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<em>Lens: Complex Teaching</em><br>
Desmopressin (DDAVP) is a synthetic analog of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus. A significant adverse effect of DDAVP therapy is water intoxication, which occurs when fluid is retained, leading to dilutional hyponatremia. Early signs of water toxicity include a sudden weight gain, persistent headache, confusion, and drowsiness. Patients must monitor their weight daily and report these symptoms. Sodium restriction (Option A) is not the primary treatment for DI. Adjusting the dose based on dark urine (Option B) should only be done under specific provider orders, as dark urine means the medication is working to concentrate the urine. With DDAVP therapy, the extreme polyuria will stop, so drinking 4 liters of water daily (Option C) is unnecessary and dangerously increases the risk of water intoxication.
</details>

---

### Question 184
A nurse is reviewing the laboratory trends of a patient admitted with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS) over the past 24 hours. Which data trend best indicates that the current intravenous fluid and insulin therapy is effectively resolving the condition?

A. Serum osmolality decreased from 345 mOsm/kg to 295 mOsm/kg.
B. Serum anion gap decreased from 18 mEq/L to 10 mEq/L.
B. Serum potassium increased from 3.8 mEq/L to 5.2 mEq/L.
D. Urine ketones changed from moderate to trace.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<em>Lens: Data Trend Analysis</em><br>
HHS is characterized by extreme hyperglycemia, profound osmotic diuresis, and severe dehydration, leading to a highly elevated serum osmolality (often >320 mOsm/kg) without significant ketoacidosis. The primary goal of treatment is aggressive rehydration to restore normal serum osmolality. A decrease in serum osmolality to the normal range (275-295 mOsm/kg) indicates successful rehydration and resolution of the hyperosmolar state. An elevated anion gap and the presence of urine ketones (Options B and D) are hallmarks of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA), not HHS. An increase in potassium (Option C) does not specifically indicate resolution of HHS; in fact, insulin therapy typically decreases serum potassium.
</details>

---

### Question 185
The registered nurse (RN) is creating a care plan for a patient with Cushing's syndrome who is hemodynamically stable. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Monitor the patient's daily laboratory results for signs of hypokalemia.
B. Assess the patient's bilateral lower extremities for 3+ pitting edema.
C. Apply a moisturizing barrier cream to the patient's skin after bathing.
D. Educate the patient on the importance of strict infection control practices.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<em>Lens: Delegation</em><br>
Patients with Cushing's syndrome have excess levels of glucocorticoids, which leads to protein wasting, resulting in very thin, fragile skin that is highly susceptible to trauma, bruising, and breakdown. Applying moisturizing cream to maintain skin integrity is a standard, routine task that falls safely within the UAP's scope of practice. Assessing for edema (Option B), interpreting laboratory results (Option A), and providing patient education (Option D) all require the clinical judgment and specialized knowledge of the registered nurse and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 186
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion for the treatment of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the past four hours, the client's blood glucose level has decreased rapidly from 680 mg/dL to 220 mg/dL. Which early assessment finding indicates the client is developing a dangerous complication related to this rapid treatment?

A) New onset of a severe headache and decreasing level of consciousness.
B) A sudden increase in urinary output and extreme thirst.
C) The appearance of tall, peaked T waves on the continuous telemetry monitor.
D) Deep, rapid respirations with a strong fruity odor on the breath.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**Rationale:** Lowering blood glucose too rapidly during DKA treatment can cause rapid fluid shifts from the intravascular space into the cerebrospinal fluid and brain cells, leading to cerebral edema. A new headache, lethargy, or a decreasing level of consciousness are early warning signs of this life-threatening complication. Increased urine output and thirst (B) are signs of ongoing hyperglycemia, not a complication of the treatment itself. Treatment of DKA typically causes potassium to shift back into cells, leading to hypokalemia (flattened T waves and U waves), not hyperkalemia (peaked T waves) (C). Kussmaul respirations and fruity breath (D) are expected manifestations of ongoing DKA, not a new complication of the insulin therapy.
</details>

---

### Question 187
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of chronic management and required lifestyle changes?

A) "I will need to decrease my daily sodium intake to prevent fluid retention."
B) "I should increase my hydrocortisone dose if I get a fever or severe infection."
C) "I will take my fludrocortisone right before bedtime on an empty stomach."
D) "I can stop taking my corticosteroid medication once my energy levels return to normal."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**Rationale:** Addison's disease is characterized by adrenal hypofunction, meaning the body cannot produce adequate cortisol or aldosterone, especially during times of physical or emotional stress. Clients must be taught to "stress dose" (increase) their corticosteroids during illness, fever, or surgery to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Clients with Addison's disease lose sodium and retain potassium; therefore, they should not restrict sodium and may actually need to increase salt intake during hot weather or exercise (A). Fludrocortisone is typically taken in the morning to mimic natural body rhythms and is best taken with food to prevent gastric irritation (C). Corticosteroid replacement therapy is a lifelong requirement and should never be abruptly stopped, regardless of energy levels (D).
</details>

---

### Question 188
A client diagnosed with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) is being managed with strict fluid restriction and oral tolvaptan. Over the past 48 hours, the nurse notes the client's serum sodium level has increased from 122 mEq/L to 134 mEq/L, and the urine specific gravity has decreased from 1.032 to 1.018. How should the nurse interpret these laboratory trends?

A) The client is developing Diabetes Insipidus and requires immediate vasopressin administration.
B) The client is experiencing severe intracellular dehydration and requires intravenous 0.45% normal saline.
C) The treatment plan is effective, as the laboratory values are trending toward normal limits.
D) The fluid restriction is ineffective, and the healthcare provider should decrease the daily fluid allowance.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**Rationale:** SIADH results in water retention, dilutional hyponatremia (low serum sodium), and concentrated urine (high urine specific gravity). The normal range for serum sodium is 135-145 mEq/L, and the normal range for urine specific gravity is 1.005-1.030. The increase in serum sodium (from 122 to 134 mEq/L) and the decrease in urine specific gravity (from 1.032 to 1.018) indicate that the excess water is being appropriately excreted and the dilutional hyponatremia is resolving. Therefore, the treatment is effective. These values do not indicate frank Diabetes Insipidus (A) or severe dehydration (B), but rather a therapeutic shift toward homeostasis. Because the numbers are improving, the fluid restriction is considered effective (D).
</details>

---

### Question 189
A nurse is caring for a client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) whose blood glucose is 480 mg/dL. The healthcare provider prescribes a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion. Before initiating the infusion, the nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results. Which laboratory finding would require the nurse to hold the insulin infusion and contact the provider?

A) Serum sodium 132 mEq/L
B) Arterial blood pH 7.28
C) Blood urea nitrogen (BUN) 35 mg/dL
D) Serum potassium 3.1 mEq/L

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**Rationale:** Insulin administration drives potassium from the extracellular space into the intracellular space, which rapidly lowers serum potassium levels. If a client's baseline potassium is already low (less than 3.3 mEq/L), starting an insulin infusion can cause profound hypokalemia, leading to lethal cardiac arrhythmias. The nurse must hold the insulin and contact the provider to prescribe potassium replacement first. Mild hyponatremia (A), an acidic pH (B), and an elevated BUN due to dehydration (C) are all expected findings in DKA and do not contraindicate the initiation of an insulin infusion. 
</details>

---

### Question 190
A registered nurse (RN) is working with a licensed practical nurse (LPN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a medical-surgical unit. Which task related to the care of a client with Cushing's syndrome is most appropriate to delegate to the UAP?

A) Assisting the client with frequent oral care and turning schedules to protect the skin.
B) Administering the scheduled oral dose of ketoconazole to suppress cortisol production.
C) Evaluating the client's understanding of a new low-carbohydrate, low-sodium diet.
D) Assessing the client's extremities for new onset pitting edema and diminished pulses.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Delegation
**Rationale:** Clients with Cushing's syndrome have extremely fragile skin that bruises and tears easily, and they are at a high risk for infection. Assisting with oral care and turning/repositioning to protect skin integrity are routine activities of daily living (ADLs) that fall safely within the UAP's scope of practice. Administering medications (B) is within the LPN or RN scope of practice. Evaluating teaching (C) and performing physiological assessments (D) strictly require the clinical judgment of the registered nurse (RN). 
</details>
### Question 191
A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion. Over the past 4 hours, the client's blood glucose decreased from 450 mg/dL to 220 mg/dL, and the serum anion gap decreased from 18 mEq/L to 10 mEq/L. The nurse notes the client's current serum potassium is 3.3 mEq/L. Which action should the nurse take based on these laboratory trends?

A. Stop the insulin infusion immediately due to the normalized anion gap.
B. Request a prescription to add dextrose to the IV fluids and notify the provider of the potassium level.
C. Administer a bolus of 50% dextrose IV push to prevent sudden hypoglycemia.
D. Increase the insulin infusion rate to completely clear the remaining blood glucose.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
**Rationale:** In DKA, a closing anion gap (normal is 8-12 mEq/L) indicates that the acidosis is resolving. However, the insulin infusion must continue until ketones are fully cleared. When the blood glucose falls to approximately 200-250 mg/dL, dextrose must be added to the continuous IV fluids (e.g., D5 0.45% NS) to prevent hypoglycemia while the insulin infusion continues. Furthermore, insulin shifts potassium back into the cells, causing hypokalemia. A potassium level of 3.3 mEq/L requires replacement to prevent cardiac dysrhythmias. Stopping the insulin prematurely (Option A) will cause a relapse into ketoacidosis. IV push dextrose (Option C) is for severe acute hypoglycemia, not for maintenance. Increasing the insulin rate (Option D) will cause severe hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 192
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease) who is prescribed oral hydrocortisone and fludrocortisone. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of chronic disease management?

A. "I will need to decrease my salt intake to prevent fluid retention from the fludrocortisone."
B. "I should take the entire dose of hydrocortisone at bedtime to mimic my body's natural cycle."
C. "If I want to stop taking these medications, I must taper them off over a two-week period."
D. "If I get the flu and have a fever, I should double my hydrocortisone dose as directed."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
**Rationale:** Clients with Addison's disease lack endogenous cortisol and require lifelong glucocorticoid (hydrocortisone) replacement. During times of physiological or psychological stress (such as illness, infection, or surgery), the body's demand for cortisol increases. The client must be taught "sick day rules," which include increasing (often doubling or tripling) their hydrocortisone dose to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Salt intake should be increased, not decreased (Option A), especially during hot weather or illness, because lack of aldosterone causes sodium loss. Cortisol naturally peaks in the morning, so doses are typically split (e.g., two-thirds in the morning, one-third in the late afternoon), not taken at bedtime (Option B). Medication for Addison's disease is lifelong and cannot be stopped, even with a taper (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 193
A client with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) has been receiving intranasal desmopressin (DDAVP) for the past three days. Which assessment finding provides the best evidence that the multi-day treatment plan is effective?

A. Urine specific gravity has increased from 1.002 to 1.015.
B. Serum sodium level has increased from 145 mEq/L to 150 mEq/L.
C. The client reports intense thirst and drinks 4 liters of water daily.
D. 24-hour urine output has increased from 2,000 mL to 5,000 mL.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
**Rationale:** Diabetes Insipidus is characterized by a deficiency of antidiuretic hormone (ADH), leading to massive diuresis of highly dilute urine (low specific gravity). Desmopressin is a synthetic ADH replacement. If the treatment is effective, the kidneys will reabsorb water, decreasing urine output and concentrating the urine, which increases the urine specific gravity to a normal range (1.010-1.030). An increasing serum sodium (Option B) indicates worsening hypernatremia due to continued free water loss. Intense thirst (Option C) and increased urine output (Option D) are signs that the DI is not being adequately controlled.
</details>

---

### Question 194
A client with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS) has been receiving aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation with 0.9% normal saline for the past 12 hours. The client's blood glucose is currently 350 mg/dL. Which new assessment finding requires immediate intervention by the nurse?

A. The client complains of a dull, localized ache at the peripheral IV insertion site.
B. The client's urine output for the past hour was 100 mL.
C. The client becomes increasingly lethargic and develops unequal pupillary responses.
D. The client exhibits a heart rate of 98 beats per minute and a blood pressure of 110/70 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
**Rationale:** Aggressive IV fluid resuscitation and rapid reduction in blood glucose during the treatment of HHS (and DKA) can lead to a rapid shift of water into the intracellular space, causing cerebral edema. Increased lethargy, altered mental status, and unequal pupillary responses are classic early signs of increased intracranial pressure secondary to cerebral edema. This is a life-threatening complication requiring immediate intervention (e.g., administering mannitol, adjusting fluid rates). A dull ache at the IV site (Option A) is common and can be addressed locally. Urine output of 100 mL/hr (Option B) is an indicator of adequate kidney perfusion. A heart rate of 98 and BP of 110/70 (Option D) represent stable hemodynamics and successful rehydration. 
</details>

---

### Question 195
A client with Cushing's syndrome is scheduled to receive a prescribed dose of oral spironolactone. The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results and notes a serum sodium of 142 mEq/L, serum potassium of 5.8 mEq/L, and a blood glucose of 165 mg/dL. Which action should the nurse take next?

A. Administer the spironolactone with a fast-acting carbohydrate to manage the blood glucose.
B. Administer the spironolactone and document the laboratory results in the medical record.
C. Administer a potassium supplement simultaneously to prevent life-threatening arrhythmias.
D. Hold the spironolactone and notify the healthcare provider of the laboratory results.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION
**Rationale:** Spironolactone is a potassium-sparing diuretic. It is occasionally used in clients with Cushing's syndrome to treat fluid retention, hypertension, and hypokalemia, which occur due to the excess mineralocorticoid-like activity of high cortisol levels. However, this client's current serum potassium level is 5.8 mEq/L (hyperkalemia). Administering a potassium-sparing diuretic to a client who is already hyperkalemic is dangerous and could lead to severe cardiac dysrhythmias. The nurse must hold the medication and notify the provider. A blood glucose of 165 mg/dL is slightly elevated (expected in Cushing's due to insulin resistance) but does not require a fast-acting carbohydrate (Option A). Giving spironolactone (Option B) or adding a potassium supplement (Option C) would exacerbate the hyperkalemia.
</details>
### Question 196
A nurse is preparing to administer a prescribed continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion to a client admitted with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The client's initial laboratory results include a blood glucose of 540 mg/dL, an arterial pH of 7.25, and a serum potassium level of 3.0 mEq/L. Based on these laboratory findings, which action should the nurse take regarding the insulin administration?

A) Initiate the insulin infusion at a lower weight-based rate to prevent hypoglycemia.
B) Administer the insulin infusion as prescribed to rapidly correct the metabolic acidosis.
C) Withhold the insulin infusion and notify the healthcare provider immediately.
D) Administer a subcutaneous dose of regular insulin before starting the continuous drip.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option C is correct** because this scenario highlights a critical **Drug-Lab Interaction**. Intravenous regular insulin forces extracellular potassium into the intracellular space, which will rapidly lower serum potassium levels. If a client's potassium is already profoundly low (less than 3.3 mEq/L), initiating an insulin drip can precipitate life-threatening hypokalemia and fatal cardiac dysrhythmias. The nurse must hold the insulin and notify the provider so that potassium replacement can be administered first.
- **Option A is incorrect** because lowering the rate does not negate the danger of driving an already low potassium level into a fatal range. 
- **Option B is incorrect** because administering the insulin as prescribed in the presence of severe hypokalemia compromises client safety and can cause cardiac arrest. 
- **Option D is incorrect** because subcutaneous insulin is not the standard of care for the acute management of DKA, and administering any insulin while the potassium is at 3.0 mEq/L is contraindicated.
</details>

---

### Question 197
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease who is prescribed daily oral hydrocortisone. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required to manage this chronic condition?

A) "I will need to decrease my medication dose if I develop a fever or a mild infection."
B) "I should wear a medical alert bracelet and keep an emergency prefilled steroid syringe with me."
C) "I must strictly limit my sodium intake to prevent dangerous spikes in my blood pressure."
D) "I can stop taking my medication gradually once my fatigue and weight loss improve."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option B is correct** because managing Addison's disease (**Complex Teaching**) requires preparation for sudden adrenal insufficiency (Addisonian crisis). The client must wear a medical alert bracelet so emergency personnel know their diagnosis, and they must carry an emergency kit with an injectable corticosteroid (such as Solu-Cortef) to use during sudden, severe physical stress or trauma.
- **Option A is incorrect** because the client must *increase*, not decrease, their corticosteroid dose during times of physical stress, illness, or infection to meet the body's heightened metabolic demands.
- **Option C is incorrect** because Addison's disease is characterized by hyponatremia. The client does not need to strictly limit sodium; in fact, they may need to increase sodium intake during periods of gastrointestinal illness or excessive sweating.
- **Option D is incorrect** because Addison's disease requires lifelong glucocorticoid (and often mineralocorticoid) replacement therapy. Stopping the medication will trigger a life-threatening Addisonian crisis.
</details>

---

### Question 198
A client is receiving inpatient treatment for Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH). The nurse notes the client's urine output for the shift is 200 mL, and the most recent serum sodium level is 118 mEq/L. Which early assessment finding indicates the client is developing a life-threatening complication associated with this systemic imbalance?

A) New-onset confusion, persistent headache, and muscle twitching.
B) Bradycardia, widening pulse pressure, and irregular respirations.
C) Profound weakness, hyperpigmentation, and orthostatic hypotension.
D) Polydipsia, polyuria, and an abrupt drop in blood pressure.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option A is correct** because this evaluates **Complication Recognition**. SIADH results in excess water retention and dilutional hyponatremia. A serum sodium level of 118 mEq/L is severely low and places the client at immediate risk for cerebral edema. Early signs of increased intracranial pressure and water intoxication include persistent headache, confusion, lethargy, and muscle twitching, which can rapidly progress to seizures, coma, or death.
- **Option B is incorrect** because these are signs of Cushing's triad, which represents a *late*, irreversible stage of increased intracranial pressure and brain herniation, not an early sign.
- **Option C is incorrect** because these are classic clinical manifestations of Addison's disease (adrenal insufficiency), not complications of SIADH.
- **Option D is incorrect** because polydipsia, polyuria, and hypotension are signs of Diabetes Insipidus (DI), the pathophysiological opposite of SIADH.
</details>

### Question 199
The nurse is analyzing the laboratory trends of a client admitted three days ago for Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS). Which set of trending data best indicates that the client is experiencing an expected, positive response to the prescribed multi-day therapy?

A) Serum osmolality increased from 330 mOsm/kg to 345 mOsm/kg; BUN increased from 35 mg/dL to 48 mg/dL.
B) Serum potassium fluctuated from 4.8 mEq/L to 6.2 mEq/L; anion gap increased from 10 mEq/L to 18 mEq/L.
C) Urine specific gravity decreased from 1.025 to 1.002; serum sodium increased from 145 mEq/L to 158 mEq/L.
D) Blood glucose decreased from 850 mg/dL to 180 mg/dL; serum osmolality decreased from 340 mOsm/kg to 290 mOsm/kg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option D is correct** because it accurately reflects **Data Trend Analysis** for resolving HHS. HHS is characterized by extreme hyperglycemia (often >600 mg/dL) and profound hyperosmolality (>320 mOsm/kg) leading to severe dehydration without significant ketosis. Effective treatment involves aggressive IV fluid resuscitation and insulin therapy, which will decrease the blood glucose and normalize the serum osmolality (normal is 275-295 mOsm/kg).
- **Option A is incorrect** because an increase in serum osmolality and BUN indicates worsening dehydration and a failure of the current treatment plan.
- **Option B is incorrect** because HHS typically does not present with a wide anion gap (which is a hallmark of DKA). An increasing anion gap and hyperkalemia would indicate a worsening metabolic state.
- **Option C is incorrect** because a decreasing urine specific gravity and increasing serum sodium describe the development of Diabetes Insipidus, not the resolution of HHS.
</details>

---

### Question 200
A client with Cushing's syndrome has been receiving ketoconazole therapy for the past four days to manage excessive endogenous cortisol production. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the multi-day treatment plan is successfully achieving its therapeutic goals?

A) The client has gained 1.5 kg (3.3 lbs) over the past 48 hours.
B) The client's fasting morning capillary blood glucose readings have stabilized between 90 and 110 mg/dL.
C) The client reports profound, new-onset fatigue, severe salt cravings, and dizziness upon standing.
D) The client's morning serum potassium level has decreased from 3.8 mEq/L to 3.1 mEq/L.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
- **Option B is correct** because it involves the **Evaluation of Goals** for an endocrine disorder. Cushing's syndrome (hypercortisolism) stimulates gluconeogenesis, commonly causing secondary diabetes or chronic hyperglycemia. Ketoconazole inhibits cortisol synthesis. As cortisol levels normalize, insulin resistance and excessive glucose production decrease, leading to normal, stable fasting blood glucose levels (70-110 mg/dL).
- **Option A is incorrect** because weight gain and fluid retention are primary manifestations of Cushing's syndrome; continued weight gain indicates the therapy is not yet effective.
- **Option C is incorrect** because profound fatigue, salt cravings, and orthostatic hypotension are signs of adrenal insufficiency. This would suggest over-treatment (iatrogenic Addisonian crisis), not a therapeutic goal.
- **Option D is incorrect** because hypercortisolism causes potassium excretion (hypokalemia). A decreasing potassium level (3.1 mEq/L is below the normal 3.5-5.0 mEq/L range) indicates worsening, uncontrolled disease, not improvement.
</details>
### Question 201
A client with a history of Addison's disease is admitted with a gastrointestinal viral infection. Which early clinical manifestation indicates to the nurse that the client is developing an acute adrenal crisis?

A) Bounding peripheral pulses
B) Unexplained, rapid weight gain
C) Sudden onset of severe back, abdominal, and leg pain
D) Polyuria and extreme thirst

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Lens: COMPLICATION RECOGNITION**
Adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis) is a life-threatening complication of Addison's disease often triggered by physiological stress, infection, or trauma. A classic early sign is sudden, severe pain in the lower back, abdomen, or legs, which results from rapid and severe electrolyte shifts (hyperkalemia, hyponatremia) and severe hypovolemia. Bounding pulses and weight gain indicate fluid overload, which is more commonly associated with Cushing's syndrome. Polyuria and thirst align with Diabetes Insipidus or Diabetes Mellitus, not Addisonian crisis.
</details>

---

### Question 202
A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) has been receiving continuous IV regular insulin and 0.9% sodium chloride for 6 hours. The nurse evaluates the following blood glucose trend: 
- 0800: 640 mg/dL 
- 1000: 480 mg/dL 
- 1200: 310 mg/dL 
- 1400: 240 mg/dL 

Based on this trending data, which action should the nurse anticipate next?

A) Discontinue the continuous IV insulin infusion immediately.
B) Administer a subcutaneous dose of glargine insulin.
C) Administer an IV push bolus of 50% dextrose (D50).
D) Change the IV fluids to include 5% dextrose in 0.45% sodium chloride.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D**

**Lens: DATA TREND ANALYSIS**
In DKA, the goal is to safely lower blood glucose while clearing the blood of ketones. When blood glucose levels fall to approximately 200-250 mg/dL, the nurse must anticipate adding dextrose to the IV maintenance fluids (such as switching to D5W in 0.45% NS). This prevents profound hypoglycemia and rapid fluid shifts leading to cerebral edema, while allowing the continuous IV insulin infusion to keep running until the underlying ketoacidosis is fully resolved. Stopping the insulin drip prematurely would halt the clearance of ketones and prolong the DKA.
</details>

---

### Question 203
The nurse provides discharge teaching to a client managing chronic Cushing's syndrome. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the necessary lifestyle modifications?

A) "I will need to eat a diet high in simple carbohydrates and low in potassium."
B) "I will weigh myself every morning and report a 3-pound gain in one week."
C) "I should immediately stop taking my steroid medications if I develop a fever."
D) "I can use a stiff-bristled toothbrush to help prevent gum disease."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Lens: COMPLEX TEACHING**
Clients with Cushing's syndrome possess excess levels of glucocorticoids and mineralocorticoids, putting them at high risk for severe fluid retention and cardiovascular overload. Weighing themselves daily and reporting a gain of more than 2-3 pounds in a week is critical to managing fluid balance. Their diet should be high in protein and potassium, but low in carbohydrates and sodium. Medications (especially if Cushing's is caused by exogenous steroids) should never be stopped abruptly due to the risk of a secondary adrenal crisis. A soft-bristled toothbrush is required due to fragile skin, capillary fragility, and an increased risk of bleeding.
</details>

---

### Question 204
A client with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is receiving scheduled doses of intranasal desmopressin (DDAVP). The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results. Which finding requires the nurse to hold the medication and contact the healthcare provider?

A) Serum sodium level of 127 mEq/L
B) Urine specific gravity of 1.004
C) Serum calcium level of 9.2 mg/dL
D) Blood urea nitrogen (BUN) of 15 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Lens: DRUG-LAB INTERACTION**
Desmopressin is a synthetic antidiuretic hormone used to treat DI by promoting massive water reabsorption in the kidneys. A highly dangerous adverse effect is water intoxication, leading to dilutional hyponatremia. A serum sodium level of 127 mEq/L (normal: 135-145 mEq/L) indicates severe hyponatremia. The nurse must hold the medication because giving another dose will worsen the water retention, potentially leading to cerebral edema, seizures, and coma. A low urine specific gravity (1.004) is an expected finding in DI that warrants the administration of the medication. The calcium and BUN are within normal limits.
</details>

---

### Question 205
A client with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) has been on a strict fluid restriction of 800 mL/day and receiving oral tolvaptan for three days. Which assessment finding provides the best evidence that the multi-day treatment plan is effective?

A) The client's daily weight shows an increase of 1.5 kg over 48 hours.
B) The client's serum sodium level has increased from 118 mEq/L to 132 mEq/L.
C) The client's urine output remains consistent at 25 mL/hr.
D) The client reports the new onset of a persistent, dull headache.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Lens: EVALUATION OF GOALS**
SIADH causes excessive and inappropriate water retention, leading to dilutional hyponatremia. The primary goal of treatment (which includes fluid restriction and vasopressin receptor antagonists like tolvaptan) is to safely increase serum sodium levels by promoting aquaresis (water excretion). An increase in serum sodium from a dangerously low 118 mEq/L to a much safer 132 mEq/L indicates the treatment is highly effective. Weight gain, persistent oliguria (25 mL/hr), and a new headache (a sign of worsening hyponatremia or cerebral edema) would indicate that the treatment is failing.
</details>
### Question 206
A patient with a history of Addison's disease is admitted to the medical-surgical unit with a severe respiratory infection. During the morning assessment, which clinical finding indicates to the nurse that the patient is experiencing an early complication of their endocrine disorder?

A) New onset of profound generalized weakness and a blood pressure of 88/50 mmHg.
B) A heart rate of 62 bpm and an irregular rhythm.
C) A weight gain of 4 lbs over the last 48 hours and facial puffiness.
D) A capillary blood glucose level of 180 mg/dL after breakfast.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** Cushing's vs Addison's

Patients with Addison's disease lack adequate cortisol and aldosterone. When subjected to physical stress (such as a severe respiratory infection), they require increased amounts of these hormones. Failure to meet this demand can lead to an acute adrenal insufficiency (Addisonian crisis). Early and hallmark signs of a crisis include profound hypotension (due to lack of aldosterone leading to sodium and water loss), generalized weakness, and vascular collapse. Option C describes fluid retention characteristic of Cushing's syndrome, not Addison's. Option D is an elevated blood glucose, whereas Addison's disease is more commonly associated with hypoglycemia due to cortisol deficiency.
</details>

---

### Question 207
A nurse is caring for a patient with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) who is receiving prescribed scheduled desmopressin (DDAVP). The morning laboratory results reveal a serum sodium level of 130 mEq/L. Based on this laboratory result, what is the nurse's most appropriate action?

A) Administer the desmopressin as prescribed to help correct the sodium imbalance.
B) Hold the scheduled dose of desmopressin and notify the healthcare provider.
C) Administer the desmopressin and encourage the patient to increase oral water intake.
D) Request an order to switch the desmopressin from the intravenous to the intranasal route.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**System Focus:** SIADH / DI

Desmopressin (DDAVP) is a synthetic form of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting renal water reabsorption. Because it causes the body to retain free water, a major adverse effect is dilutional hyponatremia, which can lead to water intoxication and seizures. A serum sodium level of 130 mEq/L indicates hyponatremia (normal is 135-145 mEq/L). The nurse must hold the medication to prevent worsening of the hyponatremia and notify the healthcare provider. Encouraging water intake (Option C) or administering the medication (Options A and D) would exacerbate the hyponatremia. 
</details>

---

### Question 208
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient with Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus recovering from an episode of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The nurse is instructing the patient on "sick day rules" to prevent a recurrence. Which statement by the patient indicates a need for further teaching?

A) "I will check my blood sugar every 3 to 4 hours when I feel sick."
B) "I will test my urine for ketones if my blood sugar is consistently over 240 mg/dL."
C) "I will stop taking my insulin if I am vomiting and cannot keep food down."
D) "I will try to drink 4 ounces of sugar-free fluid every 30 minutes to prevent dehydration."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** DKA vs HHS

A common and dangerous misconception among diabetic patients is that insulin should be withheld if they are unable to eat. However, physical illness (like infections or viruses) triggers the release of counterregulatory hormones (epinephrine, cortisol, glucagon), which significantly raise blood glucose levels. Stopping insulin during an illness is a primary trigger for DKA. Patients must be taught to continue taking their basal insulin and to manage their rapid-acting insulin based on frequent blood glucose monitoring. Options A, B, and D are all correct sick day management strategies and do not require further teaching.
</details>

---

### Question 209
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) has been receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion for 24 hours. The nurse evaluates the most recent comprehensive metabolic panel and notes the following trends over the last 12 hours: 
- Blood glucose decreased from 450 mg/dL to 180 mg/dL
- Serum bicarbonate increased from 12 mEq/L to 20 mEq/L
- Anion gap decreased from 20 mEq/L to 10 mEq/L. 

Based on these data trends, what is the appropriate nursing action?

A) Increase the rate of the regular insulin infusion to further elevate the bicarbonate level.
B) Request an order for an intravenous sodium bicarbonate infusion to finish closing the anion gap.
C) Prepare to administer an intravenous bolus of 50% dextrose to prevent cerebral edema.
D) Prepare to transition the patient from continuous intravenous insulin to scheduled subcutaneous insulin.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** DKA vs HHS

The primary goal of DKA treatment is the resolution of acidosis, not just the normalization of blood glucose. Resolution of DKA is clinically defined by a blood glucose < 200 mg/dL, a serum bicarbonate ≥ 18 mEq/L, a venous pH > 7.30, and a closed anion gap (≤ 12 mEq/L). The data provided indicates that the patient's DKA has resolved (glucose 180, bicarb 20, anion gap 10). Once DKA is resolved and the patient is able to eat, the appropriate next step is to administer a dose of subcutaneous insulin and discontinue the IV insulin drip 1 to 2 hours later to prevent rebound hyperglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 210
A patient is being treated for Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) with a strict fluid restriction of 800 mL/day and the administration of a vasopressin receptor antagonist. Which set of evaluation data indicates to the nurse that the treatment plan is effectively meeting its clinical goals?

A) Serum sodium is 128 mEq/L, urine specific gravity is 1.025, and weight increased by 1 kg.
B) Serum sodium is 132 mEq/L, urine specific gravity is 1.035, and weight remains unchanged.
C) Serum sodium is 138 mEq/L, urine specific gravity is 1.015, and weight decreased by 1.5 kg.
D) Serum sodium is 152 mEq/L, urine specific gravity is 1.002, and the patient reports severe thirst.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** SIADH / DI

SIADH involves excessive secretion of antidiuretic hormone (ADH), leading to severe water retention, dilutional hyponatremia, and highly concentrated urine. The goals of therapy (fluid restriction and vasopressin antagonists) are to promote the excretion of excess free water, normalize serum sodium, and decrease urine concentration. Option C shows a normalized serum sodium (135-145 mEq/L), a normalizing urine specific gravity (1.005-1.030), and weight loss, indicating successful elimination of excess fluid. Option D shows overcorrection resulting in hypernatremia and overly dilute urine, resembling Diabetes Insipidus. Options A and B reflect ongoing dilutional hyponatremia and fluid retention.
</details>
### Question 211
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. The patient works as a construction worker in a hot climate. Which statement by the patient indicates an understanding of how to manage their chronic illness and adapt their lifestyle?

A) "I will need to decrease my salt intake during the summer months to prevent swelling."
B) "I should double my daily dose of hydrocortisone if I get a mild illness like a cold."
C) "I can stop taking my fludrocortisone once my energy levels return to normal."
D) "I should restrict my fluid intake to 1 liter per day while working outside."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B
<strong>Lens:</strong> Complex Teaching
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Patients with Addison's disease lack endogenous corticosteroids and cannot mount a natural stress response. During times of physiological stress (such as a cold, infection, or minor surgery), they must be taught to increase (often double or triple) their daily glucocorticoid (hydrocortisone) dose to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Because of their hot-climate occupation, the patient will lose excess sodium through sweat, requiring *increased* (not decreased) salt intake (Option A) and adequate hydration (Option D). Fludrocortisone is a mineralocorticoid replacement that must be taken for life, not stopped when symptoms improve (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 212
A nurse is reviewing the laboratory trends of a patient admitted with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH). Over the past 48 hours, the patient's serum sodium has gradually increased from 118 mEq/L to 127 mEq/L, and urine specific gravity has decreased from 1.032 to 1.015. How should the nurse interpret these findings?

A) The patient is experiencing worsening water intoxication and requires immediate intervention.
B) The patient is developing diabetes insipidus secondary to over-treatment.
C) The patient's renal function is declining, causing decreased urine concentration.
D) The patient is demonstrating an effective response to fluid restriction and medical therapy.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D
<strong>Lens:</strong> Data Trend Analysis
<strong>Rationale:</strong> SIADH causes excess water retention, leading to dilutional hyponatremia (low serum sodium) and highly concentrated urine (high specific gravity). An upward trend in serum sodium (from severe hyponatremia to a safer level) combined with a decreasing urine specific gravity (indicating the kidneys are beginning to excrete excess water) are both positive clinical trends. This indicates that the treatment plan—typically fluid restriction, diuretics, or hypertonic saline—is working effectively. 
</details>

---

### Question 213
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion and normal saline. Over the last few hours, the patient's blood glucose has decreased from 550 mg/dL to 230 mg/dL. The nurse assesses the patient and notes they are becoming increasingly lethargic, complaining of a severe headache, and have sluggish pupils. What complication should the nurse suspect?

A) Severe dehydration leading to hypovolemic shock
B) Transition into Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS)
C) Cerebral edema due to rapid fluid shifts
D) Hypoglycemic encephalopathy

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C
<strong>Lens:</strong> Complication Recognition
<strong>Rationale:</strong> A rapid drop in blood glucose during the treatment of DKA causes a rapid decrease in serum osmolality. This sudden shift can cause water to move from the extracellular space into the intracellular space (including brain cells), leading to cerebral edema. Early signs of cerebral edema include headache, worsening lethargy, changes in level of consciousness, and sluggish pupillary responses. It is a life-threatening complication requiring immediate intervention. The blood glucose is 230 mg/dL, so hypoglycemia (Option D) is incorrect.
</details>

---

### Question 214
A patient with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is prescribed scheduled intranasal desmopressin. Before administering the morning dose, the nurse reviews the patient's laboratory results, which reveal a morning serum sodium level of 127 mEq/L. Based on this system-specific laboratory value, which action is most appropriate?

A) Hold the desmopressin and notify the healthcare provider.
B) Administer the desmopressin and encourage oral water intake.
C) Administer half the prescribed dose of desmopressin to prevent further imbalance.
D) Administer the desmopressin and request an order for hypertonic saline.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A
<strong>Lens:</strong> Drug-Lab Interaction
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Desmopressin is a synthetic analog of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat DI by promoting water reabsorption in the kidneys. However, a major adverse effect is excessive water retention leading to dilutional hyponatremia. A serum sodium level of 127 mEq/L is significantly low and places the patient at risk for neurological complications, such as seizures or coma. Administering the medication will worsen the water retention and hyponatremia. The nurse must hold the dose and notify the provider.
</details>

### Question 215
A patient with Cushing's syndrome has been on a specialized multi-day treatment plan that includes a high-protein, low-sodium diet, fluid restrictions, fall precautions, and ketoconazole therapy. Which assessment finding best indicates that the treatment plan is meeting its therapeutic goals?

A) The patient's fasting morning blood glucose has increased to 145 mg/dL.
B) The patient reports a noticeable increase in proximal muscle weakness.
C) The patient's daily weight has decreased by 3 pounds over the past four days.
D) The patient has trace pitting edema in the bilateral lower extremities.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C
<strong>Lens:</strong> Evaluation of Goals
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Cushing's syndrome is characterized by excess cortisol, which causes sodium and water retention, leading to weight gain, hypertension, and edema. Ketoconazole is an adrenal steroid inhibitor used to suppress cortisol production. A decrease in weight indicates a reduction in fluid overload, showing the therapeutic regimen is effective. Increasing blood glucose (Option A) and increasing muscle weakness (Option B) are signs of worsening Cushing's syndrome (hyperglycemia and myopathy). Edema (Option D) is an expected finding of the disease, not a specific marker of improvement compared to measurable weight loss.
</details>
### Question 216
A client is receiving treatment for Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH). The nurse is monitoring the client for early signs of system-specific complications. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the client is developing a severe, life-threatening complication of this disorder?

A. A sudden, drastic increase in urine output to 250 mL/hr
B. Persistent headache and new-onset restlessness
C. Orthostatic hypotension and dry mucous membranes
D. Capillary blood glucose level of 185 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** SIADH/DI

**Rationale:** SIADH causes excess water retention, leading to dilutional hyponatremia. If serum sodium levels drop too rapidly or too low, water shifts into brain cells, causing cerebral edema. A persistent headache, restlessness, and confusion are early warning signs of cerebral edema, which can rapidly progress to seizures, coma, and death. High urine output (Option A) and orthostatic hypotension (Option C) are associated with Diabetes Insipidus (DI), the opposite of SIADH. Mild hyperglycemia (Option D) is not a direct complication of SIADH.
</details>

---

### Question 217
The nurse is providing discharge instructions to a client newly diagnosed with primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease). Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of how to manage this chronic condition during lifestyle changes and illness?

A. "I should restrict my dietary sodium intake during the summer months to prevent fluid retention."
B. "I will discontinue my daily corticosteroid medication if I experience significant weight gain."
C. "I need to double my daily hydrocortisone dose when I am experiencing a respiratory infection or high fever."
D. "I must limit my daily fluid intake to 1 liter per day to prevent my blood pressure from getting too high."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Cushing's vs Addison's

**Rationale:** Clients with Addison's disease lack endogenous cortisol and cannot mount a normal stress response. During times of physiological stress (such as illness, fever, surgery, or major dental work), the client must increase (often double or triple) their glucocorticoid dosage to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Clients with Addison's waste sodium and often crave it; they should never restrict sodium, especially in summer (Option A). Corticosteroids must never be abruptly stopped, as this triggers a crisis (Option B). Fluid restriction is contraindicated, as Addison's patients are prone to hypovolemia and hypotension (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 218
The nurse is preparing to initiate a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion for a client admitted with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Which laboratory finding requires the nurse to withhold the insulin infusion and contact the healthcare provider immediately?

A. Serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L
B. Blood glucose level of 580 mg/dL
C. Serum bicarbonate level of 14 mEq/L
D. Arterial blood pH of 7.25

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**System Focus:** DKA vs HHS

**Rationale:** Insulin forces both glucose and potassium into the intracellular space. If a client in DKA has a baseline hypokalemia (potassium < 3.3 mEq/L), administering IV insulin will cause a further, precipitous drop in serum potassium, leading to life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmias. The nurse must hold the insulin and advocate for potassium replacement first. Options B, C, and D are all expected laboratory findings in a client with DKA (hyperglycemia, low bicarbonate, and acidosis) and are the exact reasons the insulin infusion is ordered.
</details>

---

### Question 219
A client with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) has been receiving intravenous normal saline and a continuous regular insulin infusion for the past 24 hours. The nurse reviews the client's laboratory trends. Which trend indicates the primary pathology of this condition is resolving?

A. Increasing serum ketones and decreasing arterial blood pH over the last 8 hours.
B. Increasing anion gap and decreasing serum bicarbonate levels over the last 12 hours.
C. Decreasing serum sodium levels alongside an increasing urine specific gravity.
D. Decreasing serum osmolality from 345 mOsm/kg to 295 mOsm/kg over the last 24 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** DKA vs HHS

**Rationale:** The hallmark of Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) is extreme hyperglycemia leading to profound osmotic diuresis, severe dehydration, and hyperosmolality, typically without significant ketosis. A decrease in serum osmolality back toward the normal range (275-295 mOsm/kg) indicates that fluid resuscitation and insulin therapy are effectively rehydrating the client and reducing the concentration of the blood. Options A and B describe a worsening trend of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA), not HHS. Option C indicates worsening dehydration or an inappropriate response not aligned with HHS resolution goals.
</details>

---

### Question 220
A client with Cushing's syndrome has been receiving medical therapy for the past week to suppress cortisol production. The nurse evaluates the client's progress toward the multi-day treatment goals. Which finding indicates the treatment plan is effective?

A. The client exhibits a progressive increase in daily weight of 1 kg per day.
B. The client's serum potassium level drops from 3.8 mEq/L to 3.2 mEq/L.
C. The client’s fasting capillary blood glucose readings consistently range between 95-105 mg/dL.
D. The client reports extreme fatigue and a new craving for salty foods.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** Cushing's vs Addison's

**Rationale:** Cushing's syndrome is characterized by an excess of corticosteroids, which causes gluconeogenesis and insulin resistance, leading to hyperglycemia. A fasting blood glucose dropping into the normal range (95-105 mg/dL) indicates that cortisol levels are decreasing and the treatment is effectively reversing the metabolic imbalances. Progressive weight gain (Option A) and decreasing potassium levels (hypokalemia, Option B) are signs of active or worsening Cushing's syndrome (fluid retention and potassium wasting). Extreme fatigue and salt craving (Option D) indicate overcorrection leading to adrenal insufficiency (Addisonian crisis signs), which is an adverse outcome, not a therapeutic goal.
</details>
### Question 221
A patient admitted with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS) has been receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of regular insulin and 0.9% normal saline. Over the past 4 hours, the nurse notes a data trend showing the patient's blood glucose has decreased from 850 mg/dL to 240 mg/dL. Based on this trend, what should the nurse anticipate doing next?

A. Discontinue the regular insulin infusion completely.
B. Change the intravenous fluid to include 5% dextrose.
C. Administer 1 mg of glucagon intramuscularly.
D. Increase the insulin infusion rate by 2 units/hour.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** DKA vs HHS

In the treatment of HHS (and DKA), when the blood glucose level drops to approximately 250 to 300 mg/dL, intravenous fluids must be switched to a solution containing dextrose (such as D5W or D5.45% NS). Because the insulin infusion is continued at a lower rate to clear the underlying hyperosmolarity or ketoacidosis, adding dextrose prevents a rapid plunge into hypoglycemia and protects the patient against the development of cerebral edema. Discontinuing the insulin entirely (A) would allow the hyperosmolar state to return. Glucagon (C) is reserved for severe hypoglycemia, which is not currently present. Increasing the insulin (D) is contraindicated as the blood glucose is already declining appropriately.
</details>

---

### Question 222
A nurse is providing complex discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease regarding the management of their chronic illness and necessary lifestyle modifications. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of the teaching?

A. "I will need to decrease my hydrocortisone dose if I get a fever to prevent infection."
B. "I should restrict my sodium intake when the weather is very hot."
C. "I must stop taking fludrocortisone immediately if I feel highly anxious."
D. "I will double my daily dose of hydrocortisone if I catch the flu."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Cushing's vs Addison's

Patients with Addison's disease lack the ability to produce adequate intrinsic cortisol, especially during times of physical or psychological stress. They must be taught to increase (often double or triple) their daily glucocorticoid dose (hydrocortisone) during periods of stress, such as illness (the flu), infection, or surgery, to prevent an Addisonian crisis. Decreasing the dose during a fever (A) is the exact opposite of what is required and is highly dangerous. Because these patients are prone to hyponatremia, they should increase, not restrict, sodium intake during hot weather or exercise (B). Abruptly stopping fludrocortisone (C) can lead to rapid fluid loss, hypotension, and shock.
</details>

### Question 223
A registered nurse (RN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) are caring for a stable patient diagnosed with central Diabetes Insipidus (DI). Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A. Measure and document the patient's hourly urine output and oral fluid intake.
B. Administer the scheduled dose of intranasal desmopressin.
C. Evaluate the patient's mucous membranes and skin turgor for signs of dehydration.
D. Instruct the patient on the need to maintain an accessible supply of water at the bedside.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Delegation
**System Focus:** SIADH vs DI

Delegation to a UAP involves tasks that have predictable outcomes and do not require nursing judgment, assessment, or teaching. Measuring and recording strict intake and output (A) is a standard skill within the UAP's scope of practice and is crucial for monitoring a patient with DI. Administering medications like desmopressin (B), evaluating/assessing for dehydration (C), and providing patient instruction (D) are all responsibilities that require the RN's clinical judgment and specific scope of practice.
</details>

### Question 224
A patient with a history of Addison's disease is prescribed oral fludrocortisone 0.1 mg daily. While reviewing the patient's morning laboratory results, the nurse notes a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L and a serum sodium level of 145 mEq/L. What is the nurse's safest action regarding the administration of this medication?

A. Administer the medication as prescribed and document the laboratory values in the chart.
B. Give the fludrocortisone and restrict the patient's oral fluid intake for the remainder of the shift.
C. Hold the fludrocortisone and notify the healthcare provider of the potassium level.
D. Administer a PRN dose of intravenous calcium gluconate before giving the medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
**System Focus:** Cushing's vs Addison's

Fludrocortisone is a mineralocorticoid used to replace aldosterone in patients with Addison's disease. Its mechanism of action causes the kidneys to retain sodium and water while excreting potassium. The patient currently has hypokalemia (normal potassium is 3.5-5.0 mEq/L). Administering fludrocortisone will cause further potassium wasting, which could lead to severe cardiac dysrhythmias. Therefore, the safest action is to hold the medication and notify the provider for potential potassium replacement and dosage adjustment. Administering it as prescribed (A) is unsafe. Restricting fluids (B) does not address the hypokalemia. Calcium gluconate (D) is used to protect the heart in cases of severe *hyperkalemia*, not hypokalemia.
</details>

---

### Question 225
A patient is receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion and normal saline for the treatment of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Upon entering the room, the nurse notes the patient is experiencing new onset of generalized muscle weakness, and the cardiac monitor displays flattened T waves and prominent U waves. Which complication should the nurse immediately recognize?

A. Insulin-induced hypokalemia
B. Worsening metabolic acidosis
C. Impending cerebral edema
D. Rebound hypoglycemia

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** DKA vs HHS

During the treatment of DKA, intravenous insulin therapy directly drives potassium out of the intravascular space and into the intracellular space, which can rapidly lead to profound hypokalemia. The classic early manifestations of hypokalemia include muscle weakness, cramping, and specific ECG changes such as flattened or inverted T waves, ST segment depression, and the presence of prominent U waves. Worsening metabolic acidosis (B) would typically present with Kussmaul respirations and hyperkalemia. Impending cerebral edema (C) is marked by neurological changes like altered mental status or headache. Rebound hypoglycemia (D) would present with diaphoresis, tremors, and tachycardia rather than U waves.
</details>
### Question 226
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) has been receiving a regular insulin infusion and normal saline for the past 8 hours. The nurse evaluates the patient's laboratory trends:

*   **Admission:** Blood glucose 580 mg/dL, Anion gap 24 mEq/L
*   **4 hours ago:** Blood glucose 410 mg/dL, Anion gap 21 mEq/L
*   **Currently:** Blood glucose 245 mg/dL, Anion gap 18 mEq/L

Based on this data trend, which intervention is indicated?

A) Stop the regular insulin infusion and administer subcutaneous insulin glargine.
B) Administer 50% dextrose IV push to prevent an acute hypoglycemic event.
C) Add 5% dextrose to the continuous intravenous fluid replacement.
D) Increase the rate of the regular insulin infusion to close the anion gap rapidly.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Add 5% dextrose to the continuous intravenous fluid replacement.

**Rationale:**
*   **Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
*   **Reasoning:** In the treatment of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA), the goal is to clear ketones and close the anion gap (normal is typically 8-12 mEq/L). However, blood glucose often falls faster than the ketones clear. When the blood glucose reaches approximately 250 mg/dL, 5% dextrose must be added to the IV fluids. This prevents hypoglycemia while allowing the continuous insulin infusion to safely run until the anion gap closes. Stopping the insulin (Option A) or pushing D50 (Option B) is inappropriate and counterproductive. Increasing the insulin rate (Option D) will cause dangerous hypoglycemia.

</details>

---

### Question 227
The nurse is providing comprehensive discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of long-term condition management?

A) "I must contact my provider to increase my steroid dosage when I am sick with a fever."
B) "I should limit my daily salt intake to prevent severe swelling in my lower legs."
C) "I can safely stop taking my fludrocortisone once my blood pressure normalizes."
D) "I will take my daily hydrocortisone on an empty stomach right before bedtime."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "I must contact my provider to increase my steroid dosage when I am sick with a fever."

**Rationale:**
*   **Lens:** Complex Teaching
*   **Reasoning:** Patients with Addison's disease lack endogenous corticosteroids and cannot mount an adequate stress response. During times of physical stress, such as infection, fever, surgery, or trauma, their exogenous steroid dose must be increased (often doubled or tripled) to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Patients with Addison's disease typically require increased sodium intake, not restricted (Option B). Fludrocortisone is a lifelong medication (Option C). Hydrocortisone should be taken in the morning to mimic the body's natural diurnal rhythm and with food to prevent gastric irritation (Option D).

</details>

### Question 228
A patient is receiving treatment for Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH). The nurse performs hourly assessments to monitor for complications. Which finding indicates the patient is developing a severe, early complication of this condition?

A) High urine specific gravity and dark, concentrated urine.
B) Polyuria with pale, dilute urine output.
C) A documented weight loss of 2 kilograms over the last 24 hours.
D) Sudden onset of a persistent headache and progressive lethargy.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Sudden onset of a persistent headache and progressive lethargy.

**Rationale:**
*   **Lens:** Complication Recognition
*   **Reasoning:** SIADH causes excessive water retention, which leads to dilutional hyponatremia. As serum sodium levels fall (especially below 120 mEq/L), fluid shifts into the brain cells, causing cerebral edema. A persistent headache, progressive lethargy, and confusion are early warning signs of cerebral edema and impending seizures, requiring immediate intervention. High specific gravity (Option A) is an expected finding of SIADH, not a complication. Polyuria (Option B) points to Diabetes Insipidus (DI). Weight loss (Option C) indicates the treatment for SIADH (fluid restriction/diuretics) is successfully removing excess fluid.

</details>

---

### Question 229
A patient with Addison's disease is admitted for a routine evaluation of their chronic comorbidities. Morning laboratory results reveal a serum sodium of 132 mEq/L and a serum potassium of 5.6 mEq/L. Which scheduled medication should the nurse withhold and clarify with the healthcare provider?

A) Fludrocortisone
B) Lisinopril
C) Hydrocortisone
D) Pantoprazole

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Lisinopril

**Rationale:**
*   **Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction
*   **Reasoning:** Patients with Addison's disease naturally struggle with hyponatremia and hyperkalemia due to a lack of aldosterone. This patient has a borderline-high potassium level of 5.6 mEq/L. Lisinopril is an ACE inhibitor, which carries a known side effect of potassium retention. Administering an ACE inhibitor to a patient already exhibiting hyperkalemia can lead to dangerous cardiac dysrhythmias. Fludrocortisone (Option A) is a mineralocorticoid replacement that will actually help excrete potassium and retain sodium. Hydrocortisone (Option C) is essential for this patient. Pantoprazole (Option D) does not significantly impact potassium levels.

</details>
