### Question 230
A patient was admitted three days ago in a Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS). The nurse is reviewing the patient's chart to evaluate the effectiveness of the multi-day treatment plan. Which finding best indicates that the primary treatment goals for HHS have been successfully met?

A) The patient's anion gap has successfully closed at 10 mEq/L with negative serum ketones.
B) The patient exhibits a urine output of 100 mL/hr and a serum sodium of 152 mEq/L.
C) The patient is consistently alert and oriented, and serum osmolality is 288 mOsm/kg.
D) The patient's blood glucose level dropped from 950 mg/dL to 180 mg/dL within the first two hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) The patient is consistently alert and oriented, and serum osmolality is 288 mOsm/kg.

**Rationale:**
*   **Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
*   **Reasoning:** The hallmarks of HHS are severe hyperglycemia, profound dehydration, hyperosmolality, and altered mental status (without significant ketoacidosis). The primary indicators of successful treatment in HHS are the normalization of serum osmolality (normal is 275-295 mOsm/kg) and a return to the patient's baseline neurological/mental status. Option A represents the resolution criteria for DKA, not HHS (ketones are typically absent or minimal in HHS). Option B shows ongoing hypernatremia, indicating unresolved dehydration. Option D represents a dangerous complication; dropping glucose too rapidly increases the risk of cerebral edema and is not a therapeutic goal.

</details>
### Question 231
A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) has been receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion and fluid resuscitation for the past 12 hours. The nurse evaluates the trended laboratory data. 

**Initial labs (Admission):**
Glucose: 480 mg/dL 
Potassium: 5.4 mEq/L 
Anion gap: 19 mEq/L 
Arterial pH: 7.15 

**Current labs (12 hours later):**
Glucose: 180 mg/dL 
Potassium: 4.0 mEq/L 
Anion gap: 10 mEq/L 
Arterial pH: 7.36 

Based on the analysis of these laboratory trends, what is the appropriate interpretation by the nurse?

A. The DKA is evolving into a Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS), requiring an increase in IV fluids.
B. The DKA has resolved, and the client is ready to be transitioned to scheduled subcutaneous insulin.
C. The client's acidosis is worsening, requiring an immediate bolus infusion of sodium bicarbonate.
D. The client's potassium is dropping dangerously fast, and the insulin infusion must be stopped immediately.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. The DKA has resolved, and the client is ready to be transitioned to scheduled subcutaneous insulin.

**Rationale:** 
The criteria for the resolution of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) include a blood glucose level generally less than 200 mg/dL, closure of the anion gap (less than 12 mEq/L), and normalization of the arterial pH (> 7.30) and serum bicarbonate. The current laboratory values indicate that the acidosis and ketosis have resolved. At this stage, it is clinically appropriate to transition the client from continuous IV insulin to scheduled subcutaneous insulin (often with a 1- to 2-hour overlap to prevent rebound ketoacidosis). The client is not evolving into HHS, their acidosis is fully resolved, and a potassium level of 4.0 mEq/L is within normal limits, so insulin does not need to be abruptly stopped.

*Lens:* Data Trend Analysis
</details>

---

### Question 232
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease) who has been prescribed daily hydrocortisone and fludrocortisone. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of chronic disease management?

A. "I need to wear a medical alert bracelet at all times and carry an emergency injectable corticosteroid kit."
B. "I will decrease my hydrocortisone dose on days when I am experiencing a minor viral illness."
C. "I should severely restrict my daily sodium intake to prevent fluid retention from the fludrocortisone."
D. "If I experience significant weight gain from the medication, I should immediately stop taking my hydrocortisone."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. "I need to wear a medical alert bracelet at all times and carry an emergency injectable corticosteroid kit."

**Rationale:** 
Clients with Addison's disease lack the ability to produce adequate cortisol and aldosterone. In times of sudden physical stress (e.g., trauma, accidents), they cannot mount a stress response, placing them at high risk for a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. They must wear a medical alert bracelet and carry an emergency kit with injectable corticosteroids (like hydrocortisone or dexamethasone) at all times. During illness or periods of high stress, the corticosteroid dose must be *increased* (stress dosing), not decreased. Because these clients waste sodium, sodium intake is generally encouraged, not restricted. Corticosteroids must never be stopped abruptly, as this can precipitate an acute adrenal crisis.

*Lens:* Complex Teaching
</details>

---

### Question 233
A client with a history of Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is receiving scheduled subcutaneous desmopressin (DDAVP). The nurse reviews the morning comprehensive metabolic panel and notes a serum sodium level of 127 mEq/L. The next scheduled dose of desmopressin is due now. What is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A. Administer the desmopressin as scheduled to maintain urine concentration.
B. Administer a PRN dose of 3% hypertonic saline before giving the desmopressin.
C. Encourage the client to drink at least 1 liter of free water before administering the dose.
D. Hold the desmopressin and contact the healthcare provider.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Hold the desmopressin and contact the healthcare provider.

**Rationale:** 
Desmopressin is a synthetic analogue of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting water reabsorption in the kidneys. A major adverse effect of this medication is water intoxication, which dilutes the blood and leads to severe hyponatremia. A serum sodium level of 127 mEq/L (normal: 135-145 mEq/L) indicates moderate-to-severe hyponatremia. Administering further desmopressin could worsen the condition, leading to cerebral edema, seizures, and death. The nurse should hold the medication and notify the provider. Giving free water would further dilute the serum sodium. Administering 3% hypertonic saline requires a specific order and close ICU monitoring, making it incorrect as a PRN independent action prior to calling the provider.

*Lens:* Drug-Lab Interaction
</details>

---

### Question 234
A client with a long-standing history of Cushing's syndrome is admitted to the medical-surgical unit. Which assessment finding should the nurse recognize as an early sign of a dangerous complication specifically associated with this endocrine disorder?

A. A sudden increase in clear urine output with a specific gravity of 1.002.
B. Development of dark hyperpigmentation on the knuckles and oral mucosa.
C. Reports of worsening fatigue, generalized muscle weakness, and a new-onset low-grade fever.
D. A steady decrease in resting heart rate from 82 to 74 beats per minute.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Reports of worsening fatigue, generalized muscle weakness, and a new-onset low-grade fever.

**Rationale:** 
Cushing's syndrome is characterized by excess systemic cortisol. Chronically elevated cortisol levels cause profound immunosuppression, suppressing the body's normal inflammatory response. Therefore, a serious underlying infection may present with very subtle early signs, such as a low-grade fever, worsening fatigue, and muscle weakness, rather than a high fever or pronounced localized signs. Recognizing these subtle signs of infection is critical for early intervention. Option A describes signs of Diabetes Insipidus. Option B describes signs of Addison's disease (due to high ACTH levels stimulating melanocytes). Option D is a normal, benign variation in vital signs.

*Lens:* Complication Recognition
</details>

---

### Question 235
A client was admitted 48 hours ago for a Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar State (HHS) with an initial blood glucose of 920 mg/dL and severe dehydration. The client has been receiving continuous IV fluid resuscitation and a regulated insulin infusion. Which clinical finding best indicates that the ultimate, multi-day treatment goals for HHS are successfully being met?

A. The client is awake, alert, and fully oriented to person, place, and time.
B. The client's blood glucose level has dropped to a steady 250 mg/dL.
C. The client demonstrates Kussmaul respirations at a rate of 16 breaths per minute.
D. The client's urine output remains elevated at 350 mL/hour.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The client is awake, alert, and fully oriented to person, place, and time.

**Rationale:** 
The primary and most life-threatening characteristic of a Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar State (HHS) is extreme hyperosmolarity, which pulls fluid out of cells (including brain cells), leading to severe dehydration and profound neurological impairment (lethargy, confusion, or coma). The ultimate goal of fluid resuscitation and glucose reduction in HHS is the restoration of normal cellular hydration and a return to baseline neurological status. The client being fully oriented indicates that the hyperosmolar state has resolved. While a blood glucose of 250 mg/dL (Option B) is an interim target to prevent hypoglycemia and cerebral edema, it is not the ultimate indicator of cellular rehydration and condition resolution. Option C is associated with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA), not HHS. Option D indicates ongoing polyuria, suggesting the client is still experiencing osmotic diuresis and has not yet stabilized.

*Lens:* Evaluation of Goals
</details>
### Question 236
A nurse is providing discharge education to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. The teaching focuses on adjusting to living with a chronic endocrine disorder and managing "sick days." Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex teaching?

A. "I should reduce my daily salt intake if I develop a fever or diarrhea."
B. "I will temporarily stop taking my hydrocortisone if I experience severe nausea and vomiting."
C. "I need to contact my provider to double or triple my steroid dose if I get a respiratory infection."
D. "I should check my blood glucose daily because my medications will cause it to drop."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complex Teaching (Managing chronic system-related illness and lifestyle changes)

**Rationale:** 
Clients with Addison's disease (adrenal insufficiency) lack the ability to produce endogenous cortisol and aldosterone, which are critical for the body's stress response. During times of physical stress, such as a respiratory infection, illness, or surgery, the body's demand for cortisol increases significantly. Therefore, the client must be taught "sick day rules," which include doubling or tripling their daily dose of glucocorticoids (e.g., hydrocortisone) to prevent a life-threatening adrenal crisis. 

Option A is incorrect because clients with Addison's disease are prone to hyponatremia due to a lack of aldosterone; they should actually increase sodium intake during episodes of fever, sweating, or diarrhea. Option B is incorrect because stopping steroids abruptly or during illness will precipitate an adrenal crisis; if the client is vomiting and cannot keep oral pills down, they must seek immediate medical attention for parenteral corticosteroids. Option D is incorrect because glucocorticoid replacement therapy tends to *increase* blood glucose, not drop it, though Addison's itself can cause hypoglycemia if undertreated.

</details>

---

### Question 237
A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) has been receiving a continuous regular insulin infusion and intravenous normal saline for the past 6 hours. The nurse evaluates the client's trended laboratory results: 
- Hour 1: Glucose 550 mg/dL, Potassium 5.2 mEq/L, pH 7.25
- Hour 4: Glucose 320 mg/dL, Potassium 4.5 mEq/L, pH 7.30
- Hour 6: Glucose 240 mg/dL, Potassium 3.8 mEq/L, pH 7.33

Based on this data trend analysis, which action should the nurse take next?

A. Increase the continuous regular insulin infusion rate to aggressively close the anion gap.
B. Change the primary intravenous fluid to a dextrose-containing solution (e.g., D5.45% NS).
C. Administer an intravenous bolus of 50% dextrose (D50) immediately.
D. Discontinue the continuous insulin infusion and switch to subcutaneous glargine insulin.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis (Interpreting a shift in system-specific labs over time)

**Rationale:** 
In the management of DKA, standard protocol dictates that once the blood glucose level trends down to approximately 250 mg/dL, intravenous fluids must be changed to include dextrose (such as 5% dextrose in 0.45% normal saline). This is because the continuous insulin infusion must be maintained to resolve the underlying acidosis (indicated by the pH still being 7.33) and close the anion gap. Adding dextrose to the IV fluids prevents the complication of rapid hypoglycemia while the insulin continues clearing the ketones.

Option A is incorrect because increasing the insulin rate could lead to severe, rapid hypoglycemia; the current rate is effectively lowering glucose and correcting the pH. Option C is incorrect because the client is not acutely hypoglycemic; D50 is a rescue medication for severe hypoglycemia. Option D is incorrect because the insulin infusion should not be stopped until the acidosis is fully resolved and the client can tolerate oral intake, at which point a transition to subcutaneous insulin is carefully initiated with overlap.

</details>

---

### Question 238
The nurse is caring for a client with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) who is scheduled to receive a routine dose of intranasal desmopressin. Before administration, the nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results, which reveal a serum sodium level of 128 mEq/L and a serum osmolality of 265 mOsm/kg. Which action should the nurse take?

A. Hold the desmopressin and notify the healthcare provider immediately.
B. Administer the desmopressin and encourage increased oral water intake.
C. Request a prescription for a scheduled oral potassium supplement.
D. Administer the desmopressin as prescribed, as these are expected findings for DI.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction (Safety: Should the nurse hold a med based on a system-specific lab?)

**Rationale:** 
Desmopressin is a synthetic analogue of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting renal water reabsorption. A severe adverse effect of desmopressin therapy is water intoxication, which manifests as dilutional hyponatremia. The client's laboratory results show severe hyponatremia (normal: 135-145 mEq/L) and low serum osmolality (normal: 275-295 mOsm/kg), indicating fluid overload. Administering further desmopressin will worsen the hyponatremia, placing the client at imminent risk for seizures and coma. The nurse must hold the medication and contact the provider.

Option B is incorrect because encouraging water intake and administering the drug would dangerously exacerbate water intoxication. Option C is incorrect because the primary derangement is sodium and water balance, not potassium. Option D is incorrect because untreated DI typically presents with hypernatremia (due to massive water loss), whereas this client's hyponatremia is a dangerous complication of the treatment itself.

</details>

---

### Question 239
A client diagnosed with Cushing's syndrome has been undergoing medical management for several days while awaiting a unilateral adrenalectomy. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the multi-day treatment plan. Which assessment finding provides the best evidence that the prescribed interventions are effectively reducing hypercortisolism?

A. The client's serum potassium level has dropped from 4.2 mEq/L to 3.1 mEq/L.
B. The client's daily weight shows an overall gain of 2 pounds over the last 48 hours.
C. The client reports a sustained increase in the severity of back pain and muscle weakness.
D. The client's fasting blood glucose has steadily decreased from 175 mg/dL to 110 mg/dL.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals (Determining if the multi-day system treatment plan is working)

**Rationale:** 
Cushing's syndrome is caused by excessive levels of circulating cortisol. High cortisol levels induce gluconeogenesis and cause insulin resistance, leading to hyperglycemia. A steady decrease in fasting blood glucose back toward normal ranges (70-100 mg/dL) is an excellent clinical indicator that the medical interventions (such as cortisol-inhibiting drugs like ketoconazole or metyrapone) are successfully lowering cortisol levels.

Option A is incorrect because cortisol has mineralocorticoid effects that cause sodium retention and potassium excretion. Therefore, hypercortisolism causes hypokalemia. A dropping potassium level indicates the condition is worsening, not improving. Option B is incorrect because fluid retention and weight gain are hallmarks of hypercortisolism; an effective plan should result in diuresis and weight loss. Option C is incorrect because muscle wasting and osteoporosis (manifesting as back pain) are complications of Cushing's syndrome; an increase in these symptoms indicates ongoing or worsening disease.

</details>

---

### Question 240
A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS) and an initial serum osmolality of 360 mOsm/kg. The client is receiving rapid intravenous fluid resuscitation with 0.9% normal saline. After receiving 4 liters of fluid over a few hours, the client suddenly complains of a severe, throbbing headache, becomes increasingly lethargic, and exhibits a new-onset pupillary sluggishness. Which complication should the nurse immediately suspect?

A. Hypovolemic shock
B. Cerebral edema
C. Diabetic ketoacidosis
D. Pulmonary embolism

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition (Identify early signs of a specific system failure)

**Rationale:** 
Clients with severe hyperosmolality, such as those with HHS, are at a high risk for cerebral edema during fluid resuscitation. As the hyperosmolar state develops over days or weeks, brain cells produce "idiogenic osmoles" to prevent intracellular dehydration. When rapid intravenous fluids are administered, the sudden drop in extracellular osmolality causes water to shift rapidly back into the brain cells, leading to cellular swelling and increased intracranial pressure. Severe headache, deteriorating level of consciousness (lethargy), and sluggish pupillary responses are classic early signs of cerebral edema, which is a medical emergency.

Option A is incorrect because the client has received a massive amount of fluid, which treats hypovolemia rather than causes it. Option C is incorrect because DKA is characterized by the presence of ketones and acidosis, which are not described here, and the symptoms point to an acute neurological event. Option D is incorrect because while HHS increases thrombosis risk, a pulmonary embolism typically presents with sudden dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, and hypoxia, not a severe headache and sluggish pupils.

</details>
### Question 241
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) has been receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion and normal saline for the past 6 hours. The nurse reviews the patient's laboratory trends: 
*   **Admission:** Glucose 540 mg/dL, pH 7.22, Anion gap 20 mEq/L, Potassium 5.2 mEq/L
*   **4 hours:** Glucose 300 mg/dL, pH 7.28, Anion gap 16 mEq/L, Potassium 4.1 mEq/L
*   **6 hours:** Glucose 220 mg/dL, pH 7.32, Anion gap 14 mEq/L, Potassium 3.6 mEq/L

Based on this data trend analysis, what is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A) Stop the continuous insulin infusion immediately because the blood glucose is approaching normal limits.
B) Administer a rapid intravenous push of 50% dextrose to prevent a sudden hypoglycemic event.
C) Change the intravenous fluid to include dextrose while continuing the insulin infusion.
D) Administer intravenous sodium bicarbonate to rapidly correct the remaining metabolic acidosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** In the treatment of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA), the primary goal of the insulin infusion is to correct the acidosis (close the anion gap), not just lower blood glucose. When the patient's blood glucose falls below approximately 250 mg/dL, dextrose (usually 5% or 10%) must be added to the intravenous fluids. This prevents hypoglycemia while allowing the continuous insulin infusion to remain running until the anion gap is closed (usually < 12 mEq/L) and the blood pH normalizes. Stopping the insulin prematurely (Option A) would cause the patient to revert into ketoacidosis. Administering a bolus of 50% dextrose (Option B) is inappropriate as the patient is not currently hypoglycemic. Sodium bicarbonate (Option D) is rarely used in DKA unless the pH is critically low (typically < 6.9) because it can cause paradoxical intracellular acidosis and hypokalemia.
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
</details>

---

### Question 242
The nurse is providing comprehensive discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. The patient is prescribed daily oral hydrocortisone and fludrocortisone. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of managing this chronic condition?

A) "I should take my daily hydrocortisone dose right before bedtime to mimic my body's natural rhythm."
B) "I will need to increase my hydrocortisone dose if I experience significant physical or emotional stress."
C) "I will need to decrease my salt intake to prevent fluid retention from the fludrocortisone medication."
D) "I should abruptly stop taking my hydrocortisone if I develop a fever so my immune system can fight the infection."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Addison's disease involves a deficiency in cortisol and aldosterone. Patients taking replacement therapy (hydrocortisone) must be taught "stress dosing"—meaning they must increase their corticosteroid dose during times of significant physiological or psychological stress (e.g., surgery, severe illness, trauma, emotional distress) to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Glucocorticoids should be taken in the morning, not at bedtime (Option A), to mimic the body's natural diurnal rhythm and prevent insomnia. Patients with Addison's disease are prone to hyponatremia and should *not* restrict salt intake; in fact, they may need to increase it during periods of excessive sweating or stress (Option C). Corticosteroids must never be abruptly stopped, especially during an infection or fever, as this will rapidly trigger an Addisonian crisis (Option D).
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
</details>

### Question 243
A patient with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) is receiving a hypertonic saline (3% NaCl) infusion via a central line and has been placed on a strict fluid restriction of 800 mL/day. Which task is most appropriate for the registered nurse (RN) to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Evaluate the patient's neurological status for signs of osmotic demyelination syndrome.
B) Assess the central venous catheter insertion site for localized redness or swelling.
C) Explain the clinical rationale for the strict fluid restriction to the patient's visiting family members.
D) Provide the patient with measured hard candies and ice chips within the prescribed fluid limits.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** The RN can safely delegate the provision of ice chips and hard candies to the UAP, provided the UAP is instructed on the strict intake parameters and properly records the amounts on the intake and output (I&O) record. Ice chips and hard candies are standard comfort measures for patients on strict fluid restrictions. Evaluating neurological status (Option A) requires RN assessment skills, especially given the high risk of osmotic demyelination syndrome when correcting hyponatremia with 3% NaCl. Assessing a central line site for complications (Option B) is an RN responsibility. Educating the patient or family about the rationale for a treatment plan (Option C) falls under the RN scope of practice.
**Lens:** DELEGATION
</details>

---

### Question 244
A patient admitted with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) has been receiving treatment with intravenous fluids and an insulin protocol over the past 48 hours. The nurse evaluates the multi-day treatment plan. Which clinical finding best indicates that the primary goals of treatment for HHS are being met?

A) The patient's serum osmolality has decreased from 345 mOsm/kg to 290 mOsm/kg.
B) The patient's urine ketones have transitioned from moderate to negative over 24 hours.
C) The patient's arterial pH has increased from 7.15 to 7.35.
D) The patient now requires 40 mEq of potassium chloride added to their maintenance fluids.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) is characterized by extreme hyperglycemia, profound dehydration, and severe hyperosmolarity, typically *without* significant ketoacidosis. The primary goal of treatment is to safely rehydrate the patient and correct the hyperosmolarity. A normal serum osmolality is typically 275–295 mOsm/kg. A decrease from 345 mOsm/kg to 290 mOsm/kg indicates successful rehydration and resolution of the hyperosmolar state. Options B and C describe the resolution of significant ketonuria and acidosis, which are the hallmark goals for treating Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA), not HHS. Needing potassium supplementation (Option D) is a common expected intervention as insulin drives potassium back into the cells, but it is not the primary indicator that the specific syndrome of HHS has resolved.
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
</details>

---

### Question 245
A patient with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is prescribed scheduled intravenous desmopressin (DDAVP). During the morning chart review, the nurse notes a newly resulted serum sodium level of 128 mEq/L (128 mmol/L). What is the safest action by the nurse?

A) Administer the desmopressin as prescribed, as hyponatremia is an expected finding in Diabetes Insipidus.
B) Administer the desmopressin and request a prescription for a 0.45% sodium chloride intravenous infusion.
C) Hold the desmopressin and promptly notify the healthcare provider of the laboratory result.
D) Encourage the patient to drink large amounts of free water before administering the desmopressin.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** Desmopressin is a synthetic analogue of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting water reabsorption in the kidneys. An adverse effect of desmopressin therapy is water intoxication and dilutional hyponatremia, which can lead to life-threatening complications such as seizures, coma, or death. A normal serum sodium level is 135-145 mEq/L. A level of 128 mEq/L indicates significant hyponatremia. The nurse must hold the dose and notify the provider, as administering more desmopressin will worsen water retention and drive the sodium level even lower. Untreated DI typically causes hypernatremia, not hyponatremia, due to massive water loss (Option A). Giving a hypotonic fluid like 0.45% NaCl (Option B) or encouraging large amounts of free water (Option D) would severely exacerbate the hyponatremia.
**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION
</details>
### Question 246
A patient admitted with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion. The nurse reviews the patient’s most recent basic metabolic panel, which reveals a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L. Based on this laboratory result, which action should the nurse take next?

A. Administer a 50% dextrose IV push to prevent sudden hypoglycemia.
B. Continue the insulin infusion and administer scheduled subcutaneous glargine.
C. Stop the continuous insulin infusion and notify the healthcare provider.
D. Increase the insulin infusion rate to resolve the metabolic acidosis faster.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Stop the continuous insulin infusion and notify the healthcare provider.

**Lens:** Drug-Lab Interaction

**Rationale:** Intravenous insulin therapy rapidly shifts potassium from the extracellular space into the intracellular space, which can cause or exacerbate hypokalemia. Although hyperkalemia is common upon initial presentation of DKA due to acidosis, total body potassium is typically depleted. If the patient's serum potassium drops below 3.3 mEq/L, the insulin infusion must be held, and potassium replacement must be administered first to prevent life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmias. Continuing or increasing the insulin would worsen the hypokalemia. Dextrose is added to IV fluids when glucose drops below 200-250 mg/dL, but it does not address the critical potassium level.
</details>

---

### Question 247
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of how to manage this chronic illness?

A. "I will need to decrease my daily sodium intake to prevent fluid retention."
B. "I must double my daily dose of hydrocortisone if I develop a fever or severe infection."
C. "I can stop taking my fludrocortisone once my blood pressure returns to a normal range."
D. "I should limit my daily fluid intake to 1 liter to prevent swelling in my legs."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. "I must double my daily dose of hydrocortisone if I develop a fever or severe infection."

**Lens:** Complex Teaching

**Rationale:** Addison's disease is characterized by a deficiency of adrenal hormones, primarily cortisol and aldosterone. Patients require lifelong corticosteroid replacement. During times of physical or emotional stress (e.g., fever, infection, surgery, trauma), the body naturally requires more cortisol. Patients with Addison's disease cannot produce this naturally and must "stress dose" (typically doubling or tripling their oral hydrocortisone dose) to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Patients with Addison's are prone to hyponatremia and hypovolemia, so they should not restrict sodium or fluids. Medication therapy is lifelong and should never be stopped abruptly.
</details>

---

### Question 248
A patient diagnosed with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) has been treated with severe fluid restriction and hypertonic saline (3% NaCl) for the past 48 hours. The nurse evaluates the patient's laboratory trends. Which trend indicates that the treatment plan is effective?

A. Urine output decreased from 60 mL/hr to 20 mL/hr over 12 hours.
B. Urine specific gravity increased from 1.025 to 1.035 over 48 hours.
C. Serum sodium decreased from 124 mEq/L to 118 mEq/L over 24 hours.
D. Serum osmolality increased from 265 mOsm/kg to 280 mOsm/kg over 48 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Serum osmolality increased from 265 mOsm/kg to 280 mOsm/kg over 48 hours.

**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis

**Rationale:** SIADH causes excessive secretion of ADH, leading to severe water retention, dilutional hyponatremia, and low serum osmolality (typically < 275 mOsm/kg). Concurrently, the urine becomes highly concentrated. Effective treatment aims to remove excess free water from the vascular space, which will increase serum sodium and increase serum osmolality back toward the normal range (275-295 mOsm/kg). Decreased urine output and increasing urine specific gravity indicate worsening water retention. A drop in serum sodium indicates worsening dilutional hyponatremia. 
</details>

---

### Question 249
A registered nurse (RN) is working with a licensed practical nurse (LPN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on an acute medical-surgical unit. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the LPN?

A. Administering scheduled subcutaneous desmopressin to a stable patient with Diabetes Insipidus.
B. Assessing the initial hydration status of a newly admitted patient with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS).
C. Teaching a patient with Cushing's syndrome about the signs of opportunistic infections.
D. Titrating a continuous intravenous insulin infusion for a patient in Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA).

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Administering scheduled subcutaneous desmopressin to a stable patient with Diabetes Insipidus.

**Lens:** Delegation

**Rationale:** The LPN scope of practice includes administering routine oral, subcutaneous, and intramuscular medications to stable patients. Therefore, administering scheduled subcutaneous desmopressin to a stable patient is highly appropriate. Initial assessments (such as evaluating a newly admitted patient with HHS), initial patient education (such as teaching about Cushing's syndrome risks), and managing high-alert continuous intravenous medications (such as titrating an insulin drip) require advanced clinical judgment and fall strictly within the scope of practice of the RN.
</details>

---

### Question 250
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation and continuous regular insulin. Over the last two hours, the patient's blood glucose rapidly dropped from 650 mg/dL to 180 mg/dL. Which assessment finding requires immediate intervention as an early sign of a dangerous complication related to this rapid shift?

A. Deep, rapid respirations at a rate of 28 breaths per minute.
B. Generalized abdominal pain and severe nausea.
C. New-onset severe headache and declining level of consciousness.
D. Hourly urine output of 45 mL/hr over the last two hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. New-onset severe headache and declining level of consciousness.

**Lens:** Complication Recognition

**Rationale:** A rapid drop in blood glucose during DKA treatment causes a sudden decrease in serum osmolality. Because the brain cells adapt to the previously hyperosmolar state, this rapid fluid shift drives water into the intracellular space of the brain, resulting in cerebral edema. A new-onset headache, lethargy, and a declining level of consciousness are classic early warning signs of cerebral edema, which is a life-threatening complication of DKA therapy. Deep, rapid breathing (Kussmaul respirations) and abdominal pain are expected clinical manifestations of DKA itself, not complications of the therapy. A urine output of 45 mL/hr is normal and reflects adequate hydration.
</details>
### Question 251
A client with Type 2 Diabetes is admitted with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS). Over the past 24 hours of intravenous (IV) fluid and insulin therapy, the nurse analyzes the following laboratory trends:
- Serum glucose decreased from 950 mg/dL to 450 mg/dL
- Serum osmolality decreased from 340 mOsm/kg to 310 mOsm/kg
- Serum potassium shifted from 4.8 mEq/L to 3.2 mEq/L

Based on these data trends, which action should the nurse take next?

A. Prepare to administer an intravenous bolus of sodium bicarbonate.
B. Anticipate adding a potassium chloride infusion to the treatment plan.
C. Discontinue the intravenous regular insulin infusion immediately.
D. Switch the continuous intravenous fluids to 3% sodium chloride.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Data Trend Analysis</strong><br>
In HHS (and DKA), as insulin administration and fluid volume resuscitation correct the extreme hyperglycemia and hyperosmolarity, extracellular potassium is driven back into the intracellular space. This predictably causes serum potassium levels to drop. A trend showing potassium falling to 3.2 mEq/L (hypokalemia) requires prompt intervention with supplemental IV potassium to prevent life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmias. Sodium bicarbonate (Option A) is rarely used, and only for severe acidosis in DKA (pH < 6.9), which is not a feature of HHS. Stopping insulin immediately (Option C) is incorrect; insulin is titrated or maintained with added dextrose once glucose drops to around 250-300 mg/dL to prevent rebound hyperglycemia. 3% sodium chloride (Option D) is hypertonic and contraindicated in HHS, where the goal is to resolve cellular dehydration usually with 0.9% or 0.45% normal saline.
</details>

---

### Question 252
A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to manage their chronic condition and necessary lifestyle changes?

A. "I should significantly reduce my daily salt intake to prevent swelling in my lower legs."
B. "I will need to decrease my daily hydrocortisone dose when I get a cold or the flu."
C. "I must wear a medical alert bracelet and always carry an injectable emergency corticosteroid."
D. "I can slowly taper off my fludrocortisone once my daily energy levels return to normal."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Complex Teaching</strong><br>
Addison's disease is characterized by a deficiency of adrenal hormones (glucocorticoids and mineralocorticoids). Clients must carry emergency supplies of injectable corticosteroids (e.g., hydrocortisone) and wear a medical alert bracelet to ensure immediate treatment during an Addisonian crisis, which can be triggered by stress or trauma. Option A is incorrect because clients with Addison's disease often experience hyponatremia and salt craving; they are encouraged to consume adequate or increased salt, especially during heavy sweating or illness. Option B is incorrect; the client must *increase* their corticosteroid dose during times of physical stress, infection, or illness, not decrease it. Option D is incorrect because Addison's disease is a lifelong chronic illness requiring permanent hormone replacement therapy.
</details>

---

### Question 253
A client diagnosed with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is prescribed scheduled intranasal desmopressin (DDAVP). Before administering the morning dose, the nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results, noting a serum sodium level of 129 mEq/L and a urine specific gravity of 1.028. Which action is most appropriate for the nurse to take regarding the medication?

A. Administer the desmopressin as prescribed to maintain urine concentration.
B. Give the desmopressin and encourage the client to drink at least 1 liter of water.
C. Administer a PRN dose of intravenous furosemide along with the desmopressin.
D. Hold the desmopressin and notify the healthcare provider of the lab results.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Drug-Lab Interaction</strong><br>
Desmopressin is a synthetic analogue of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat Diabetes Insipidus by promoting water reabsorption in the kidneys. A major adverse effect of this medication is water intoxication and dilutional hyponatremia. The client's serum sodium of 129 mEq/L indicates significant hyponatremia (normal is 135-145 mEq/L), and the urine specific gravity of 1.028 shows the urine is already appropriately concentrated (indicating the drug is working or perhaps overworking). Administering further desmopressin (Option A) could fatally worsen the hyponatremia, leading to seizures or coma. Encouraging more free water (Option B) will further dilute serum sodium. Furosemide (Option C) is not an appropriate independent nursing intervention here. The nurse should hold the medication and notify the provider for a dose adjustment.
</details>

---

### Question 254
A client with Cushing's syndrome is on day four of medical management with ketoconazole to suppress adrenal cortisol production. The nurse is evaluating the multi-day treatment plan to determine if the therapeutic goals are being met. Which clinical finding best indicates that the current treatment regimen is effective?

A. The client's capillary blood glucose levels have stabilized between 90 and 130 mg/dL.
B. The client reports the new development of purple striae on the lower abdomen.
C. The client's serum potassium level remains consistently at 3.0 mEq/L.
D. The client experiences a rapid, unintended weight gain of 3 pounds over two days.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Evaluation of Goals</strong><br>
Cushing's syndrome is caused by an excess of corticosteroids (specifically cortisol), which promotes gluconeogenesis and leads to hyperglycemia. When medical therapy (such as ketoconazole, a cortisol synthesis inhibitor) is effective, cortisol levels drop, leading to a resolution of Cushing's symptoms, including stabilization of blood glucose levels within a normal or near-normal range. Option B indicates worsening or ongoing effects of hypercortisolism. Option C is incorrect; excess cortisol causes potassium excretion, leading to hypokalemia. A potassium level of 3.0 mEq/L remains abnormally low and does not indicate therapeutic success. Option D reflects continued fluid retention, a symptom of excess mineralocorticoid effect often seen in Cushing's, which shows the treatment has not yet reached therapeutic goals.
</details>

---

### Question 255
A registered nurse (RN) is caring for a clinically stable client diagnosed with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) who is on a strict 800 mL/day fluid restriction. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A. Monitor the client for early signs of lethargy, confusion, or changes in orientation.
B. Assess the client's lung sounds and check for dependent edema every 4 hours.
C. Assist the client with frequent oral care using mouth swabs to alleviate dry mouth.
D. Explain the rationale for the 800 mL/day strict fluid restriction to the client.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Delegation</strong><br>
Clients with SIADH are placed on strict fluid restrictions to prevent worsening of dilutional hyponatremia. A common comfort issue is severe dry mouth. The UAP can safely assist the stable client with routine hygiene and comfort measures, such as providing oral care with moistened swabs, provided they do not allow the client to swallow excess water that violates the restriction. Assessing for confusion (Option A) and lung sounds/edema (Option B) require nursing judgment and clinical assessment, which fall strictly under the RN's scope of practice. Explaining the rationale for a medical intervention (Option D) is considered patient education, which also cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>
### Question 256
A young adult patient is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of regular insulin and normal saline for Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). During the hourly assessment, the nurse monitors the patient for adverse responses to the rapid fluid and glucose shifts. Which assessment finding indicates the patient is developing a severe, life-threatening complication of the current therapy?

A. The patient's blood glucose drops from 450 mg/dL to 380 mg/dL in one hour.
B. The patient reports a sudden, severe headache and appears lethargic.
C. The patient's urine output decreases from 150 mL/hr to 60 mL/hr. 
D. The patient's serum potassium level is measured at 3.9 mEq/L.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<em>Lens: COMPLICATION RECOGNITION</em><br>
Rapid correction of blood glucose and aggressive fluid resuscitation in DKA can lead to a rapid drop in serum osmolality. This sudden shift causes water to move into the cerebrospinal fluid and brain cells, leading to cerebral edema, especially in younger patients. A sudden, severe headache, lethargy, or a change in the level of consciousness are early neurological signs of this life-threatening complication. A drop in blood glucose of 70 mg/dL in an hour (Option A) is an expected therapeutic response. A urine output of 60 mL/hr (Option C) is within normal limits, and a potassium of 3.9 mEq/L (Option D) is normal, though it requires continued monitoring as insulin shifts potassium intracellularly.
</details>

---

### Question 257
A patient with Addison's disease is scheduled to receive their morning dose of fludrocortisone. Before administering the medication, the nurse reviews the patient's comprehensive metabolic panel. The nurse should hold the medication and contact the healthcare provider based on which laboratory finding?

A. Blood glucose of 72 mg/dL
B. Blood urea nitrogen (BUN) of 22 mg/dL
C. Serum potassium of 2.9 mEq/L
D. Serum sodium of 134 mEq/L

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<em>Lens: DRUG-LAB INTERACTION</em><br>
Fludrocortisone is a synthetic mineralocorticoid used to replace aldosterone in patients with Addison's disease. Its primary action is to promote sodium and water reabsorption while enhancing potassium excretion in the kidneys. A serum potassium level of 2.9 mEq/L is significantly low (hypokalemia). Administering a medication that further depletes potassium could precipitate life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmias. The nurse must hold the drug and notify the provider. A slightly low sodium level (Option D) is common in Addison's disease and is the reason fludrocortisone is given. Blood glucose (Option A) and BUN (Option B) do not require holding this specific medication.
</details>

---

### Question 258
A patient with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) has been receiving an intravenous infusion of hypertonic (3%) saline and strict fluid restrictions for the past 48 hours. The nurse evaluates the patient's laboratory trends to assess the efficacy of the interventions. Which data trend indicates that the treatment plan is achieving the desired outcome?

A. Serum sodium increases from 122 mEq/L to 134 mEq/L; urine specific gravity decreases from 1.032 to 1.018.
B. Urine output increases from 30 mL/hr to 40 mL/hr; serum potassium increases from 3.5 mEq/L to 4.8 mEq/L.
C. Serum sodium decreases from 145 mEq/L to 135 mEq/L; urine osmolality increases from 300 to 800 mOsm/kg.
D. Blood urea nitrogen (BUN) decreases from 25 mg/dL to 10 mg/dL; hematocrit drops from 45% to 38%.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<em>Lens: DATA TREND ANALYSIS</em><br>
SIADH causes excessive secretion of ADH, leading to severe water retention, dilutional hyponatremia, and highly concentrated urine (high urine specific gravity). The goals of treatment (using fluid restriction and hypertonic saline) are to excrete excess water, slowly raise the serum sodium level, and dilute the urine. An increase in serum sodium (towards the normal 135-145 mEq/L) and a decrease in urine specific gravity (towards normal, less concentrated levels) clearly demonstrate that the treatment is effective. Option C shows worsening SIADH. Option D indicates worsening hemodilution (fluid overload), which means the SIADH is not resolving. 
</details>

---

### Question 259
The nurse provides comprehensive discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease regarding lifestyle adjustments and chronic illness management. Which statement by the patient indicates successful comprehension of the teaching?

A. "I need to decrease my hydrocortisone dose if I get a fever to prevent my immune system from weakening."
B. "I will need to restrict my daily sodium intake to less than 2 grams per day to prevent swelling."
C. "I should avoid eating high-potassium foods like bananas and oranges as part of my daily diet."
D. "I must double or triple my hydrocortisone dose if I experience a significant physical stressor or illness."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<em>Lens: COMPLEX TEACHING</em><br>
Patients with Addison's disease have adrenal insufficiency and lack the endogenous glucocorticoids needed to manage physical stress. A critical component of chronic management is "sick day rules," which require the patient to proactively increase (usually double or triple) their corticosteroid dosage during times of illness, fever, or significant stress to prevent a life-threatening adrenal crisis. Decreasing the dose (Option A) would cause a crisis. Patients with Addison's are prone to hyponatremia, so they should not restrict sodium and may even need to increase it (Option B). While hyperkalemia is a risk during an acute crisis, strict avoidance of dietary potassium is not the primary hallmark of routine chronic management compared to stress dosing (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 260
An older adult patient was admitted 3 days ago with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS). The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the multi-day treatment plan, which included aggressive IV hydration and continuous insulin therapy. Which evaluation finding best indicates that the overall goals of HHS treatment have been met?

A. The patient's urine ketones are negative and their arterial blood gas shows a pH of 7.40.
B. The patient's serum osmolality is 285 mOsm/kg and their neurological status has returned to baseline.
C. The patient's serum potassium is 4.0 mEq/L and they exhibit regular, deep Kussmaul respirations.
D. The patient reports increased thirst and their urine specific gravity is 1.005.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<em>Lens: EVALUATION OF GOALS</em><br>
HHS is characterized by extreme hyperglycemia, profound dehydration, hyperosmolarity, and altered mental status, typically without significant ketoacidosis. The primary multi-day goals of treatment are restoring fluid volume, normalizing serum osmolality (normal is 275-295 mOsm/kg), and resolving the neurological deficits caused by the hyperosmolar state. Therefore, a normal serum osmolality and a return to baseline neurological status indicate the treatment has been highly effective. Negative ketones and a normal pH (Option A) or resolution of Kussmaul respirations (Option C) are the primary evaluation goals for Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA), not HHS. Option D describes manifestations consistent with Diabetes Insipidus, not resolved HHS.
</details>
### Question 261
A client with small cell lung cancer is being monitored for Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH). The nurse analyzes the client's morning laboratory trends over the past three days:

*   **Day 1:** Serum Sodium 132 mEq/L, Urine Specific Gravity 1.025
*   **Day 2:** Serum Sodium 126 mEq/L, Urine Specific Gravity 1.032
*   **Day 3:** Serum Sodium 118 mEq/L, Urine Specific Gravity 1.038

Based on this data trend analysis, which action should the nurse take?

A) Encourage increased oral water intake to dilute the concentrated urine.
B) Prepare to administer 3% hypertonic saline and initiate seizure precautions.
C) Administer the ordered desmopressin (DDAVP) to correct the sodium imbalance.
D) Expect a prescription for continuous intravenous 0.45% sodium chloride.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** The data trend shows a rapid decline in serum sodium to a critical level (118 mEq/L) alongside increasing urine concentration, which is a classic presentation of worsening SIADH. Severe hyponatremia places the client at an extremely high risk for cerebral edema, seizures, and coma. The nurse must initiate seizure precautions and anticipate the administration of a hypertonic solution (like 3% NaCl) to slowly correct the profound hyponatremia. Encouraging water intake (Option A) or giving hypotonic fluids (Option D) would worsen the fluid overload and hyponatremia. Desmopressin (Option C) is an antidiuretic hormone replacement used to treat Diabetes Insipidus, which would exacerbate SIADH.
</details>

---

### Question 262
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion for the treatment of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the last three hours, the client's capillary blood glucose level has decreased from 550 mg/dL to 240 mg/dL. During the latest assessment, the nurse notes new-onset frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) on the cardiac monitor and the client complains of severe, generalized muscle weakness. Which early complication should the nurse suspect is developing?

A) Hypoglycemia from rapid insulin administration.
B) Rebound hyperglycemia due to fluid shifting.
C) Hypokalemia resulting from intracellular ion shifts.
D) Cerebral edema caused by rapid glucose correction.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** In DKA, total body potassium is often depleted due to osmotic diuresis, even if initial serum potassium levels appear normal or elevated due to acidosis. As continuous regular insulin is administered, it drives both glucose and potassium back into the intracellular space, rapidly causing profound hypokalemia. Premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) and generalized muscle weakness are classic early signs of hypokalemia, a life-threatening complication of DKA treatment. While dextrose is often added to IV fluids when glucose reaches 250 mg/dL to prevent hypoglycemia (Option A), the specific symptoms described (PVCs and muscle weakness) directly point to a potassium imbalance, not low blood sugar or cerebral edema.
</details>

---

### Question 263
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with Addison's disease who is prescribed daily oral hydrocortisone and fludrocortisone. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle and medication management required?

A) "I will need to temporarily increase my hydrocortisone dose when I get a severe respiratory infection."
B) "I should restrict my daily sodium intake to prevent swelling from the fludrocortisone."
C) "I can stop taking the hydrocortisone once my fatigue and dizziness completely resolve."
D) "I will take both medications on an empty stomach right before bedtime for the best absorption."

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Clients with Addison's disease lack endogenous glucocorticoids and mineralocorticoids and cannot mount an adequate physiological response to stress. They must be taught to increase their glucocorticoid dosage (often called "stress dosing") during periods of physical stress, such as severe illness, infection, or surgery, to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Restricting sodium (Option B) is incorrect; clients with Addison's often need adequate or increased sodium intake, especially in hot weather. Medication therapy is lifelong, so they cannot stop taking it once symptoms improve (Option C). Corticosteroids should generally be taken in the morning to mimic the body's natural circadian rhythm and with food to prevent gastrointestinal upset (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 264
A 68-year-old client with type 2 diabetes has been receiving intravenous fluid resuscitation and continuous insulin therapy for Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar State (HHS) over the past 48 hours. The client's initial admission blood glucose was 920 mg/dL, and serum osmolality was 345 mOsm/kg. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the treatment plan is effectively meeting its primary physiological goal?

A) The client's urine output remains steady at 45 mL/hr without the presence of ketones.
B) The client's capillary blood glucose level has decreased to 150 mg/dL.
C) The client's serum potassium level has normalized to 4.2 mEq/L.
D) The client is now fully alert, oriented, and interacting appropriately with family.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** The hallmark of HHS is extreme hyperglycemia leading to profound osmotic diuresis, severe dehydration, and hyperosmolarity. This hyperosmolarity pulls fluid from brain cells, causing significant neurological manifestations such as profound lethargy, confusion, or coma. The primary goal of HHS treatment is aggressive fluid resuscitation to restore perfusion and correct the hyperosmolality. A return to a normal, alert baseline neurological state is the most comprehensive and direct indicator that the severe intracellular dehydration in the brain has resolved and the treatment goal has been met. While normalization of glucose (Option B) and potassium (Option C) are positive steps, correcting the neurological deficit caused by hyperosmolarity is the ultimate measure of clinical success in HHS.
</details>

---

### Question 265
A client with Cushing's syndrome is hospitalized for an acute exacerbation of symptoms and is currently receiving spironolactone daily. The morning laboratory results show a serum sodium of 142 mEq/L, a blood glucose of 168 mg/dL, and a serum potassium of 5.4 mEq/L. Based on these laboratory findings, what is the nurse's most appropriate action regarding the next scheduled dose of spironolactone?

A) Withhold the medication and alert the healthcare provider due to the potassium level.
B) Administer the scheduled dose as these laboratory results reflect expected disease management.
C) Hold the medication because the client's sodium level demonstrates adequate fluid balance.
D) Administer the dose and concurrently provide sliding-scale insulin to correct the hyperglycemia.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Spironolactone is a potassium-sparing diuretic often used in Cushing's syndrome to treat the fluid retention and hypokalemia typically caused by excess mineralocorticoid activity. However, the client's morning serum potassium is 5.4 mEq/L, which indicates hyperkalemia (normal range: 3.5-5.0 mEq/L). Administering a potassium-sparing diuretic in the presence of hyperkalemia is dangerous and can lead to life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmias. Therefore, the nurse must hold the spironolactone and notify the healthcare provider. The sodium level (142 mEq/L) is within normal limits, and while the glucose is elevated (expected in Cushing's), it does not dictate holding the spironolactone.
</details>
### Question 266
A 24-year-old client is being treated for Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) with an intravenous regular insulin infusion and 0.9% normal saline. The client's blood glucose has decreased from 580 mg/dL to 240 mg/dL over the last 4 hours. Which assessment finding should the nurse recognize as an early sign of a severe complication related to the current treatment?

A) Serum potassium level changing from 5.1 mEq/L to 4.5 mEq/L
B) Deep, rapid respiratory pattern at 28 breaths per minute
C) Urine output of 45 mL/hr over the past two hours
D) New onset of a severe headache and lethargy

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D (New onset of a severe headache and lethargy)

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
*Lens: Complication Recognition*
Cerebral edema is a rare but life-threatening complication of DKA treatment, most often caused by rapid decreases in blood glucose and subsequent fluid shifts into brain cells. Early signs include headache, lethargy, and changes in the level of consciousness. Option A represents an expected and safe shift in potassium as insulin drives glucose and potassium back into the cells. Option B describes Kussmaul respirations, which are an expected compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis in DKA, not a complication of the treatment. Option C is a normal urine output finding, indicating adequate renal perfusion.
</details>

---

### Question 267
A client with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH) is receiving demeclocycline. The nurse reviews the morning laboratory results. Which finding would require the nurse to withhold the medication and contact the healthcare provider?

A) Urine specific gravity of 1.012
B) Serum potassium of 3.8 mEq/L
C) Serum sodium of 152 mEq/L
D) Blood urea nitrogen of 18 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C (Serum sodium of 152 mEq/L)

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
*Lens: Drug-Lab Interaction*
Demeclocycline is an antibiotic that blocks the action of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) in the renal tubules, effectively treating the dilutional hyponatremia associated with SIADH by increasing water excretion. However, a severe adverse effect is overcorrection, leading to drug-induced Diabetes Insipidus and hypernatremia. A serum sodium level of 152 mEq/L indicates hypernatremia (normal is 135-145 mEq/L), requiring the nurse to withhold the medication and notify the provider to prevent severe dehydration and neurological complications. Options A, B, and D are all within normal physiological limits.
</details>

---

### Question 268
A client with Diabetes Insipidus has been receiving intranasal desmopressin (DDAVP) for the past three days. The nurse reviews the daily trend of the client's morning laboratory results:
- Day 1: Urine Specific Gravity 1.002, Serum Sodium 150 mEq/L  
- Day 2: Urine Specific Gravity 1.008, Serum Sodium 145 mEq/L  
- Day 3: Urine Specific Gravity 1.018, Serum Sodium 139 mEq/L  

Based on these laboratory trends, which conclusion should the nurse draw regarding the client's treatment plan?

A) The client is developing water intoxication and needs an immediate dose reduction.
B) The client is demonstrating an expected therapeutic response to the medication.
C) The medication dose is insufficient and requires an increase to prevent dehydration.
D) The client is experiencing nephrogenic resistance to the prescribed medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B (The client is demonstrating an expected therapeutic response to the medication.)

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
*Lens: Data Trend Analysis*
Diabetes Insipidus (DI) is characterized by a lack of ADH, leading to massive water loss, low urine specific gravity, and hypernatremia. Desmopressin acts as a synthetic replacement for ADH, increasing water reabsorption in the kidneys. The provided trend shows the urine specific gravity increasing to a normal, concentrated range (1.005-1.030) and the serum sodium decreasing to a normal range (135-145 mEq/L). This perfectly aligns with a successful therapeutic response. There is no evidence of water intoxication, which would present as hyponatremia (sodium < 135 mEq/L), nor is there evidence of an insufficient dose or resistance, which would result in persistently low specific gravity and high sodium.
</details>

---

### Question 269
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex self-management required for this condition?

A) "I will need to decrease my daily hydrocortisone dose if I develop a fever or severe illness."
B) "I should switch to a low-sodium, high-potassium diet to protect my heart and kidneys."
C) "I must strictly avoid all physical exercise to prevent triggering an adrenal crisis."
D) "I need to double or triple my steroid medication when I have a minor infection or significant stress."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D ("I need to double or triple my steroid medication when I have a minor infection or significant stress.")

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
*Lens: Complex Teaching*
Clients with Addison's disease lack endogenous corticosteroids necessary to mount a physiological response to stress. They must be taught "sick day rules," which mandate doubling or tripling their glucocorticoid dose during periods of physical stress, such as illness, infection, or trauma, to prevent a life-threatening Addisonian crisis. Option A is incorrect because decreasing the dose during illness is exactly the opposite of what is required and would precipitate a crisis. Option B is incorrect because Addison's causes sodium loss and potassium retention; therefore, patients need sufficient dietary sodium and should avoid excess potassium. Option C is incorrect; moderate exercise is permitted as long as the patient's replacement therapy is adequately managed.
</details>

---

### Question 270
A 72-year-old client was admitted three days ago with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic Syndrome (HHS). The client has been receiving aggressive intravenous fluids and subcutaneous insulin management. Which evaluation finding best indicates that the primary treatment goals for HHS have been met?

A) Serum osmolarity is 285 mOsm/kg and the client is alert and oriented.
B) Serum ketones are undetectable and the anion gap is 10 mEq/L.
C) Fasting blood glucose is strictly maintained under 100 mg/dL.
D) Arterial blood gas shows a pH of 7.40 and bicarbonate of 24 mEq/L.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A (Serum osmolarity is 285 mOsm/kg and the client is alert and oriented.)

<strong>Rationale:</strong> 
*Lens: Evaluation of Goals*
The primary pathophysiology of HHS is extreme hyperglycemia leading to profound osmotic diuresis, severe dehydration, and hyperosmolarity, which clinically manifests as significant neurological deterioration. Evaluation of successful treatment is best evidenced by a return to normal serum osmolarity (275-295 mOsm/kg) and the resolution of neurological symptoms (e.g., the client is alert and oriented). Options B and D represent the resolution of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA); significant acidosis and ketosis are hallmarks of DKA, not HHS. Option C is incorrect because strict euglycemia (<100 mg/dL) is not the immediate goal in acute recovery and poses a high risk for iatrogenic hypoglycemia; targeting a glucose level of 150-200 mg/dL is generally preferred until the patient is fully stable.
</details>
### Question 271
A patient with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion and aggressive fluid resuscitation. Which assessment finding indicates an early, severe complication of the current treatment regimen?

A. Blood glucose dropping from 450 mg/dL to 400 mg/dL over one hour.
B. New-onset headache and progressively worsening lethargy.
C. A serum potassium level returning to 4.2 mEq/L.
D. An increased urine output of 100 mL/hr.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. New-onset headache and progressively worsening lethargy.

**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION (Identify early signs of a specific system failure)
**Rationale:** A severe, life-threatening complication of DKA treatment is cerebral edema, which is caused by rapid fluid shifts into the intracellular space when blood glucose levels are lowered too quickly alongside aggressive fluid resuscitation. Early signs of cerebral edema include altered mental status, worsening lethargy, and a new or severe headache. A blood glucose drop of 50 to 75 mg/dL per hour (Option A) is the therapeutic goal to prevent this exact complication. A serum potassium of 4.2 mEq/L (Option C) and a urine output of 100 mL/hr (Option D) are normal findings indicating successful hydration and electrolyte management.
</details>

---

### Question 272
The nurse is reviewing morning laboratory results for a patient with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) who is scheduled to receive an intranasal dose of desmopressin. The patient’s morning serum sodium level is 129 mEq/L. What is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A. Administer the desmopressin as prescribed to correct the hyponatremia.
B. Encourage the patient to drink free water before giving the medication.
C. Hold the desmopressin and notify the healthcare provider.
D. Request a prescription for an intravenous hypertonic saline infusion.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Hold the desmopressin and notify the healthcare provider.

**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION (Safety: Should the nurse hold a med based on a system-specific lab?)
**Rationale:** Desmopressin is a synthetic analogue of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) used to treat DI by promoting water reabsorption in the kidneys. While untreated DI typically presents with hypernatremia due to massive water loss, a low serum sodium level (129 mEq/L) in a patient receiving desmopressin indicates water intoxication (dilutional hyponatremia) as an adverse effect of the medication. The nurse must hold the drug to prevent further water retention, which could lead to cerebral edema and seizures, and notify the provider. Giving the medication or free water would worsen the hyponatremia. Hypertonic saline is a critical care intervention that is not the immediate first nursing action for holding a scheduled med.
</details>

---

### Question 273
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient newly diagnosed with Addison's disease. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of chronic disease management and lifestyle changes?

A. "I will need to increase my daily sodium intake during periods of heavy physical stress."
B. "I should stop taking my hydrocortisone if I develop a mild fever or cold to let my immune system work."
C. "I can expect my skin hyperpigmentation to fade completely within a week of starting medication."
D. "I need to limit my fluid intake to 1.5 liters a day to prevent my legs from swelling."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. "I will need to increase my daily sodium intake during periods of heavy physical stress."

**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING (Managing chronic system-related illness and lifestyle changes)
**Rationale:** Addison's disease is characterized by an insufficiency of both glucocorticoids (cortisol) and mineralocorticoids (aldosterone). Because aldosterone promotes sodium retention, its absence causes sodium loss. Patients must often increase their dietary sodium intake, particularly during times of physical stress, illness, heavy sweating, or hot weather, to prevent life-threatening hyponatremia and hypotension. Hydrocortisone must never be abruptly stopped; in fact, the dose usually needs to be *increased* during times of illness or stress to prevent an Addisonian crisis. Fluid restriction is contraindicated as these patients are prone to hypovolemia. Hyperpigmentation takes much longer to fade, if it does entirely.
</details>

---

### Question 274
A patient is admitted to the intensive care unit with Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS). Over the last 48 hours, the patient has received intravenous fluid replacement and a subcutaneous insulin regimen. Which trend in the patient's laboratory values best indicates that the primary treatment for HHS is effective?

A. Blood glucose fluctuates between 150 mg/dL and 250 mg/dL with sliding scale insulin.
B. Arterial blood gas shows a pH change from 7.25 to 7.40.
C. Serum potassium increases from 3.5 mEq/L to 5.2 mEq/L.
D. Serum osmolality decreases from 340 mOsm/kg to 295 mOsm/kg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Serum osmolality decreases from 340 mOsm/kg to 295 mOsm/kg.

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS (Interpreting a shift in system-specific labs over time)
**Rationale:** HHS is characterized by extreme hyperglycemia, profound dehydration, and severe hyperosmolality without the significant ketoacidosis seen in DKA. The primary goal of treatment is aggressive fluid resuscitation to restore intracellular and extracellular volume, thereby lowering the serum osmolality. A decrease in serum osmolality to a normal range (275-295 mOsm/kg) is the best indicator that the profound dehydration specific to HHS is resolving. An acidic pH resolving to normal (Option B) is indicative of resolving DKA, not HHS (which typically has a normal pH or mild acidosis).
</details>

---

### Question 275
The nurse is evaluating the multi-day care plan of a patient with Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone (SIADH). The patient has been on a strict fluid restriction of 800 mL/day and receiving daily furosemide. Which finding best indicates that the treatment goals are being met?

A. The patient's daily body weight has steadily decreased by 1.5 kg over three days.
B. The patient's serum sodium level decreases from 130 mEq/L to 125 mEq/L.
C. The patient reports intense thirst and a desire to drink large amounts of water.
D. The patient's urine specific gravity increases from 1.025 to 1.035.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The patient's daily body weight has steadily decreased by 1.5 kg over three days.

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS (Determining if the multi-day system treatment plan is working)
**Rationale:** SIADH results in excess antidiuretic hormone, causing the kidneys to retain water, leading to dilutional hyponatremia, fluid volume overload, and decreased urine output. Successful treatment via fluid restriction and loop diuretics promotes water excretion. A steady decrease in body weight is the most reliable, objective indicator of fluid volume loss and resolving water retention. A decreasing sodium level (Option B) and increasing urine specific gravity (Option D) indicate worsening SIADH, as the blood is becoming more dilute and the urine more concentrated. Intense thirst (Option C) is a symptom of fluid restriction but does not clinically evaluate the physiological success of the treatment plan.
</details>


# SECTION: Respiratory System (Count: 140 Questions - INTERMEDIATE)


### Question 276
A patient is recovering on the medical-surgical unit three days after a total right hip arthroplasty. The nurse is assessing the patient to identify early complications. Which of the following assessment findings is the earliest indicator that the patient is developing a pulmonary embolism?

A) Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain and unexplained dyspnea.
B) A gradual increase in a productive cough with rust-colored sputum.
C) Bradycardia accompanied by bounding peripheral pulses.
D) Substernal chest pressure that radiates to the left arm and jaw.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A  
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION  
**Scientific Rationale:** Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain, unexplained dyspnea, and tachypnea are the hallmark early signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE). Patients who have recently undergone major orthopedic surgery are at a high risk for deep vein thrombosis (DVT), which can dislodge and travel to the lungs. Rust-colored sputum is more indicative of pneumonia or tuberculosis. Bradycardia is incorrect; tachycardia is typically seen with a PE due to hypoxemia. Substernal chest pressure radiating to the arm is a classic sign of a myocardial infarction, not a PE.
</details>

---

### Question 277
A patient admitted with a severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) exacerbation has been receiving continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) for the last 6 hours. The nurse compares the patient's admission arterial blood gas (ABG) results to the most recent draw:

*Admission ABG:* pH 7.26, PaCO2 62 mm Hg, HCO3 25 mEq/L, PaO2 65 mm Hg.  
*Current ABG:* pH 7.36, PaCO2 44 mm Hg, HCO3 25 mEq/L, PaO2 88 mm Hg.  

Based on this data trend, how should the nurse interpret the patient's current status?

A) The patient is developing an uncompensated metabolic alkalosis.
B) The treatment plan is effectively resolving the acute respiratory acidosis.
C) The patient is experiencing acute respiratory muscle fatigue and failing.
D) The continuous positive airway pressure needs to be immediately increased.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS  
**Scientific Rationale:** The admission ABG reveals uncompensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, normal HCO3). The current ABG shows values that have returned to normal ranges (pH 7.35-7.45, PaCO2 35-45 mm Hg), indicating that the CPAP therapy is effectively improving ventilation, blowing off excess carbon dioxide, and resolving the respiratory acidosis. There is no evidence of metabolic alkalosis (HCO3 is normal), and the improvement in PaO2 and PaCO2 indicates the patient is responding well, not experiencing muscle fatigue or requiring an increase in CPAP pressure.
</details>

---

### Question 278
The provider is preparing to remove a chest tube from a patient who is recovering from a hemothorax. The nurse is assisting with the procedure and preparing the sterile occlusive dressing. What is the correct technique the nurse should instruct the patient to perform as the tube is being pulled?

A) Instruct the patient to take rapid, shallow breaths during the pull.
B) Ask the patient to exhale forcefully and cough continuously.
C) Instruct the patient to take a deep breath, hold it, and bear down.
D) Maintain the patient on continuous positive airway pressure throughout the procedure.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS  
**Scientific Rationale:** During chest tube removal, the patient should be instructed to perform the Valsalva maneuver (taking a deep breath, holding it, and bearing down). This action increases intrathoracic pressure, which prevents atmospheric air from being sucked back into the pleural space during the brief moment the tube is removed and the sterile occlusive dressing is applied. Taking rapid breaths or coughing can alter intrathoracic pressure unpredictably, increasing the risk of a recurrent pneumothorax.
</details>

---

### Question 279
A patient has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a spontaneous right-sided pneumothorax. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the multi-day treatment plan. Which assessment finding indicates that the primary goal of the chest tube has been met and the patient is ready for tube removal?
