A) Continuous bubbling is observed in the water-seal chamber during expiration.
B) The fluid level in the water-seal chamber actively fluctuates with inspiration and expiration.
C) There is continuous, gentle bubbling present in the suction control chamber.
D) Tidaling has stopped in the water-seal chamber and right-sided breath sounds are clear.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D  
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS  
**Scientific Rationale:** The goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is lung re-expansion. When the lung has fully re-expanded, it fills the pleural space, blocking the eyelets of the chest tube. This causes tidaling (fluctuation of fluid in the water-seal chamber with breathing) to stop. The cessation of tidaling, combined with the return of normal, clear breath sounds on the affected side, indicates the goal has been met. Continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber indicates an active air leak. Fluctuation (tidaling) indicates the tube is still patent and the lung is not yet fully re-expanded. 
</details>

---

### Question 280
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient recovering from a massive pulmonary embolism. The patient will be going home on a prescription for rivaroxaban. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of managing their chronic risk factors and lifestyle changes?

A) "I will need to schedule weekly blood draws to monitor my INR while taking this medication."
B) "I will prevent blood pooling on my upcoming road trip by stopping to walk every two hours."
C) "I must strictly limit my intake of green leafy vegetables to prevent medication interactions."
D) "If I experience sudden chest pain, I should take an extra dose of my blood thinner and lie down."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING  
**Scientific Rationale:** A key lifestyle change for patients with a history of a pulmonary embolism (PE) or deep vein thrombosis (DVT) is preventing venous stasis. Stopping to walk every few hours during long trips helps prevent blood pooling and recurrence of clots. Rivaroxaban is a direct oral anticoagulant (Factor Xa inhibitor) that does not require routine INR monitoring (unlike warfarin) and does not have dietary restrictions regarding Vitamin K (green leafy vegetables). Sudden chest pain is a sign of a recurrent PE or medical emergency, requiring immediate medical attention, not self-medicating and resting.
</details>
### Question 281
A client is recovering on the surgical unit 3 days after a right total hip arthroplasty. During morning rounds, the nurse assesses the client. Which clinical finding is the most critical early indication that the client is developing a pulmonary embolism?

A. Bilateral crackles auscultated in the lung bases.
B. Sudden onset of unexplained anxiety and tachypnea.
C. Tracheal deviation shifting toward the unaffected side.
D. A persistent cough producing pink, frothy sputum.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs

**Explanation:** 
Sudden, unexplained anxiety (a feeling of impending doom), restlessness, and tachypnea are classic early signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE) caused by sudden hypoxemia and the release of inflammatory mediators. Bilateral crackles and pink, frothy sputum (Options A and D) are classic signs of pulmonary edema or left-sided heart failure, not typically early signs of a PE. Tracheal deviation (Option C) is a late and severe sign of a tension pneumothorax. Recognizing the early manifestations of a PE is critical for initiating rapid life-saving interventions.
</details>

---

### Question 282
A client presents to the emergency department with a severe asthma exacerbation and has been receiving continuous albuterol nebulizer treatments. The nurse evaluates the client's arterial blood gas (ABG) trend over the past four hours:

- **0800:** pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mm Hg, PaO2 85 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L
- **1200:** pH 7.36, PaCO2 44 mm Hg, PaO2 78 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L

What is the nurse's best interpretation of this ABG trend?

A. The client is hyperventilating due to severe anxiety from the nebulizer treatments.
B. The client's respiratory status is improving due to effective bronchodilation.
C. The client is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.
D. The client has developed a fully compensated metabolic alkalosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** ABG interpretation

**Explanation:** 
During the early stages of an asthma attack, the client hyperventilates, causing a respiratory alkalosis (low PaCO2, high pH), as seen at 0800. As the client's respiratory muscles begin to fatigue, they can no longer maintain the rapid respiratory rate needed to blow off CO2. The PaCO2 levels begin to rise, and the pH "normalizes," as seen at 1200. In the context of a severe asthma attack, a "normal" or rising PaCO2 is a medical emergency indicating impending respiratory failure, not improvement. The client has not developed metabolic alkalosis (the HCO3 is normal). 
</details>

---

### Question 283
The nurse is assisting the healthcare provider with the removal of a client's chest tube. To ensure safety and maintain sterile technique during the procedure, which instruction should the nurse provide to the client immediately before the provider pulls the tube?

A. "Take a deep breath, exhale completely, and bear down."
B. "Pant rapidly and shallowly through your mouth."
C. "Inhale normally and hold your breath for ten seconds."
D. "Exhale slowly while humming a low pitch."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Explanation:** 
When a chest tube is removed, it is imperative to prevent air from entering the pleural space, which could cause a recurrent pneumothorax. Instructing the client to take a deep breath, exhale, and bear down (the Valsalva maneuver) increases intrathoracic pressure, effectively preventing atmospheric air from being drawn into the chest cavity while the tube is pulled and the sterile occlusive dressing is applied. Panting, inhaling normally, or exhaling slowly do not increase intrathoracic pressure adequately and increase the risk of an iatrogenic pneumothorax.
</details>

---

### Question 284
A client is being discharged home on oral warfarin therapy following hospitalization for a pulmonary embolism. The nurse is providing comprehensive discharge education regarding medication management and lifestyle modifications. Which statement by the client indicates a need for further teaching?

A. "I will report any dark, tarry stools or unusually heavy bleeding to my doctor immediately."
B. "I will use a soft-bristled toothbrush and an electric razor for my personal hygiene."
C. "I will wear a medical alert bracelet indicating that I am taking a daily blood thinner."
D. "I should completely avoid eating dark green, leafy vegetables while taking this medication."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism management

**Explanation:** 
Clients taking warfarin do not need to completely avoid foods rich in Vitamin K (such as dark green, leafy vegetables). Instead, they must be taught to maintain a *consistent* daily intake of these foods so their International Normalized Ratio (INR) remains stable. Sudden increases or decreases in Vitamin K intake will alter the effectiveness of warfarin. The other statements demonstrate a correct understanding of bleeding precautions and safety measures associated with anticoagulant therapy. 
</details>

---

### Question 285
A client with a massive pulmonary embolism has been receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion for the past 3 days. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the current treatment plan. Which clinical finding best indicates that the primary goal of this pharmacological therapy is being met?

A. The client reports no new episodes of sudden pleuritic chest pain or worsening dyspnea.
B. The client's existing pulmonary clot has completely dissolved on a repeat CT angiogram.
C. The client's activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) is strictly maintained at 110 seconds.
D. The client's D-dimer assay result is reported as negative on day 3 of the infusion.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs/Treatment

**Explanation:** 
The primary goal of heparin therapy for a pulmonary embolism is to prevent the existing clot from enlarging and to prevent the formation of new clots. A clinical presentation showing no new or worsening symptoms (no new chest pain or dyspnea) indicates that the treatment is successfully preventing clot propagation. Heparin is an anticoagulant, not a thrombolytic; it does not dissolve existing clots (the body's natural fibrinolytic system does this over time). An aPTT of 110 seconds is generally supratherapeutic (normal therapeutic range is 1.5 to 2.5 times the baseline, typically 45–75 seconds) and increases bleeding risk. A D-dimer will not instantly become negative while the clot is still physically present and breaking down.
</details>
### Question 286
A client is recovering on the orthopedic unit three days after an open femur fracture repair. The nurse is monitoring the client for postoperative complications. Which assessment finding represents the earliest indication that the client may be developing a pulmonary embolism (PE)?

A) Gradual increase in heart rate over the past three days.
B) Hemoptysis accompanied by a sudden drop in blood pressure.
C) Sudden onset of a sense of impending doom and tachypnea.
D) Bilateral crackles auscultated in the lung bases with peripheral edema.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**Rationale:** A sudden onset of unexplained apprehension, restlessness, a sense of impending doom, and tachypnea are classic early signs of a pulmonary embolism, which is a severe risk following long bone fractures (due to fat emboli or venous thromboembolism). Hemoptysis and hypotension (Option B) are late signs of a massive pulmonary embolism resulting in right ventricular failure and cardiogenic shock. A gradual increase in heart rate (Option A) does not typically characterize the acute onset of a PE. Bilateral crackles and peripheral edema (Option D) are classic signs of heart failure, not the hallmark initial signs of a PE.
</details>

---

### Question 287
A client with a severe asthma exacerbation has been receiving continuous bronchodilator therapy for the past 6 hours. The nurse evaluates the client's serial arterial blood gas (ABG) results:

Initial ABG: pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mmHg, PaO2 75 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L
Current ABG: pH 7.34, PaCO2 48 mmHg, PaO2 65 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L

How should the nurse interpret this shift in the ABG data?

A) The client is developing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.
B) The client is compensating for the initial alkalosis by retaining bicarbonate.
C) The client's ventilation is improving because the pH is shifting closer to the normal range.
D) The client has developed a superimposed metabolic acidosis from prolonged tachypnea.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**Rationale:** In the early stages of an asthma attack, hyperventilation leads to respiratory alkalosis (low PaCO2, high pH), as seen in the initial ABG. As the client becomes fatigued, air trapping worsens, and alveolar ventilation drops, the PaCO2 begins to rise. A shift from respiratory alkalosis to a normal or acidotic pH with an elevated PaCO2 (as seen in the current ABG) is a highly dangerous trend indicating respiratory muscle fatigue and impending hypercapnic respiratory failure. It does not indicate improvement (Option C). Bicarbonate (HCO3) is normal in both, indicating no metabolic compensation or superimposed metabolic acidosis (Options B and D).
</details>

---

### Question 288
The nurse is preparing to change the dressing on a client's chest tube insertion site. Which action demonstrates the correct technique when performing this sterile procedure?

A) Cutting a sterile 4x4 gauze pad with sterile scissors to create a custom slit to fit around the chest tube.
B) Taping the final dressing circumferentially around the client's torso to ensure a tight, airtight seal.
C) Cleansing the insertion site in a straight line starting from the outer edge of the chest inward toward the tube.
D) Applying a sterile petroleum-impregnated gauze directly around the chest tube insertion site.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills
**Rationale:** When changing a chest tube dressing, placing a sterile petroleum-impregnated gauze directly around the insertion site is the correct technique to maintain an airtight seal and prevent air leaks into the pleural space. Standard gauze should never be cut with scissors (Option A) because loose frayed fibers can enter the wound; pre-slit drain sponges should be used instead. Dressings should be taped securely but never circumferentially around the chest (Option B), as this restricts chest wall expansion and impairs breathing. The site should be cleansed in a circular motion moving from the insertion site outward, not inward (Option C), to avoid dragging microorganisms toward the wound.
</details>

---

### Question 289
A nurse is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) to care for a client who has a chest tube connected to a traditional water-seal drainage system. Which task is appropriate for the nurse to delegate to the UAP?

A) Securing the chest tube connections with tape to prevent accidental disconnection.
B) Assessing the water-seal chamber for the presence of continuous bubbling.
C) Reading and documenting the amount of drainage in the collection chamber on the intake/output record.
D) Milking the external chest tube tubing to prevent the formation of obstructing blood clots.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Delegation
**Rationale:** Recording the volume of chest tube drainage by reading the numbers on the outside of the collection chamber is within the UAP's scope of practice, provided they are trained to measure output. The UAP should not empty the system, but they can document the volume observed. Assessing the water-seal chamber for bubbling (Option B) requires nursing judgment and assessment skills. Securing connections to maintain the integrity of a closed sterile drainage system (Option A) is a nursing responsibility. Milking or stripping a chest tube (Option D) creates dangerous negative pressure, is generally contraindicated, and if ordered, must be performed by the registered nurse.
</details>

---

### Question 290
A client with a spontaneous pneumothorax has had a chest tube in place for three days. The multi-day treatment goal is complete lung re-expansion and readiness for chest tube removal. Which assessment finding best indicates that this goal has been met?

A) Tidaling in the water-seal chamber has ceased and breath sounds are present bilaterally.
B) The client's resting respiratory rate fluctuates between 20 and 24 breaths per minute.
C) Continuous, gentle bubbling is present in the water-seal chamber during exhalation.
D) The water level in the suction control chamber remains continuously stable at -20 cm H2O.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**Rationale:** Tidaling (fluctuation of water with respiration) in the water-seal chamber stops when the lung has fully re-expanded or if there is an obstruction/kink in the tubing. When the cessation of tidaling is accompanied by the return of equal, bilateral breath sounds, it indicates successful lung re-expansion, meaning the treatment goal has been met and the chest tube may be ready for removal. Continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber (Option C) indicates an ongoing air leak, meaning the pneumothorax has not resolved. A respiratory rate of 20-24 (Option B) is slightly elevated and does not confirm lung re-expansion. A stable suction control chamber (Option D) only indicates that the suction apparatus is functioning correctly, not the clinical status of the lung.
</details>
### Question 291
A client is recovering on the medical-surgical unit two days after a right total femur fracture repair. The nurse is monitoring the client for postoperative complications. Which finding represents an early sign of a pulmonary embolism?

A. A gradual increase in dependent lower extremity edema
B. Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain and dyspnea
C. Development of a productive cough with thick green sputum
D. New-onset bradycardia accompanied by an elevated temperature

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Complication Recognition<br>
<strong>System Focus:</strong> Pulmonary Embolism signs<br>
A sudden onset of dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain (pain upon deep inspiration), tachypnea, and tachycardia are classic early signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE), especially in a client at high risk due to recent orthopedic surgery. Lower extremity edema (Option A) may indicate a deep vein thrombosis (DVT) but is not a direct sign of a PE. A productive cough with purulent sputum (Option C) points toward a respiratory infection such as pneumonia. Bradycardia (Option D) is incorrect, as a pulmonary embolism typically triggers tachycardia due to hypoxia and anxiety.
</details>

---

### Question 292
A client admitted with an acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) has had serial arterial blood gas (ABG) draws over the last 12 hours. 
0800: pH 7.28, PaCO2 62 mmHg, HCO3 28 mEq/L 
2000: pH 7.36, PaCO2 58 mmHg, HCO3 30 mEq/L 
Which interpretation is most accurate based on this trend in the client's lab data?

A. The client is deteriorating due to the acute retention of bicarbonate.
B. The client is experiencing uncompensated respiratory acidosis requiring intubation.
C. The client has developed a combined respiratory and metabolic alkalosis.
D. The client is demonstrating full renal compensation for respiratory acidosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Data Trend Analysis<br>
<strong>System Focus:</strong> ABG interpretation<br>
At 0800, the client was in a state of partially compensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, slightly elevated HCO3 trying to compensate). By 2000, the pH has returned to the normal range (7.35-7.45), while both the PaCO2 and HCO3 remain elevated. Because the pH is now normal, this indicates full compensation. The kidneys have retained enough bicarbonate (HCO3 of 30 mEq/L) to buffer the high carbon dioxide (PaCO2 of 58 mmHg), restoring the pH balance. The client is improving, not deteriorating.
</details>

---

### Question 293
The nurse is preparing to perform a sterile dressing change on a client's chest tube insertion site. The nurse has removed the old dressing, discarded it, performed hand hygiene, and donned sterile gloves. What is the correct next step in the procedure sequence?

A. Instruct the client to perform a Valsalva maneuver while wiping the site.
B. Apply an occlusive petroleum gauze directly over the insertion site.
C. Clean the insertion site with a chlorhexidine swab using a circular motion moving outward.
D. Tape the chest tube firmly to the client's bed rail to prevent accidental dislodgement.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Sterile Technique/Skills<br>
<strong>System Focus:</strong> Chest Tube management<br>
When performing a sterile dressing change for a chest tube, after removing the old dressing and donning sterile gloves, the nurse must first cleanse the insertion site. Chlorhexidine is typically used, moving from the insertion site outward in a circular motion to prevent introducing skin flora into the wound. Applying the petroleum gauze (Option B) comes after cleaning the site. The Valsalva maneuver (Option A) is used during chest tube removal, not a standard dressing change. Taping the tube to the bed rail (Option D) is a major safety risk; it should be secured to the client's body or left with enough slack to allow movement.
</details>

---

### Question 294
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a right-sided pneumothorax. The multi-day treatment plan aims to resolve the pneumothorax and prepare the client for tube removal. Which evaluation finding best indicates the treatment plan is successfully meeting this goal?

A. Fluctuation (tidaling) in the water-seal chamber has stopped, and lung sounds are clear bilaterally.
B. Continuous bubbling is observed in the water-seal chamber during expiration.
C. The client reports sharp, stabbing pain at the insertion site upon deep inspiration.
D. There is a total of 150 mL of serosanguinous drainage in the collection chamber over the past hour.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Evaluation of Goals<br>
<strong>System Focus:</strong> Chest Tube management<br>
The goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is to allow air to escape the pleural space, allowing the lung to re-expand. When tidaling (fluctuation of water with respirations) in the water-seal chamber completely stops and bilateral lung sounds are clear, it is a strong clinical indicator that the lung has fully re-expanded and the pneumothorax has resolved. Continuous bubbling (Option B) indicates an ongoing air leak. Localized pain (Option C) is an expected but non-diagnostic side effect of the tube. Excessive drainage (Option D) is abnormal for a simple pneumothorax (which primarily involves air, not large volumes of fluid) and 150 mL/hr would require immediate reporting.
</details>

---

### Question 295
A client is being discharged on oral rivaroxaban following treatment for an acute pulmonary embolism (PE). The nurse is providing comprehensive discharge teaching regarding lifestyle changes and chronic management. Which statement by the client indicates a need for further instruction?

A. "I will take breaks to walk around every couple of hours if I am on a long road trip."
B. "I will use a soft-bristle toothbrush and an electric razor for my daily grooming."
C. "I need to go to the emergency room if I start coughing up blood or have chest pain."
D. "I must ensure my intake of leafy green vegetables is exactly the same every day."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> Complex Teaching<br>
<strong>System Focus:</strong> Pulmonary Embolism<br>
Rivaroxaban is a direct Factor Xa inhibitor used for the treatment and secondary prevention of pulmonary embolism. Unlike warfarin (a vitamin K antagonist), direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) like rivaroxaban do not require the client to maintain a consistent dietary intake of vitamin K (found in leafy green vegetables). Therefore, Option D indicates a confusion with warfarin teaching and requires further instruction. Options A, B, and C reflect correct understanding of preventing DVT/PE recurrence, employing bleeding precautions, and identifying signs of a recurrent pulmonary embolism or hemorrhage.
</details>
### Question 296
A client is 48 hours post-operative from a total hip arthroplasty and is resting in bed. The nurse is conducting a routine assessment. Which clinical finding represents the earliest indication that this client may be developing a pulmonary embolism?

A) Hemoptysis and a sudden drop in systemic blood pressure.
B) Sudden onset of unexplained apprehension and tachypnea.
C) Gradual bradypnea and a progressively decreasing oxygen saturation.
D) Petechiae developing over the client's upper chest and axillae.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**Rationale:** The earliest and most common signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE) are a sudden onset of unexplained apprehension, restlessness, and tachypnea. Hemoptysis and hypotension (Option A) are late or severe signs indicating massive PE or infarction. Bradypnea (Option C) is incorrect, as the body compensates for hypoxia with tachypnea, not bradypnea. Petechiae over the chest and axillae (Option D) are classic signs of a fat embolism, which typically occurs after long bone fractures (like the femur), not standard venous thromboembolism, and usually present alongside the earlier respiratory signs.
</details>

---

### Question 297
A nurse is reviewing the arterial blood gas (ABG) trends of a client admitted with a severe asthma exacerbation. 
At 0800, the ABGs were: pH 7.48, PaCO2 30 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L, PaO2 88 mm Hg. 
At 1200, the repeat ABGs are: pH 7.36, PaCO2 42 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L, PaO2 76 mm Hg. 
How should the nurse interpret this data trend over the last four hours?

A) The client is developing metabolic acidosis due to an increase in lactic acid from the continuous work of breathing.
B) The client is hyperventilating, leading to fully compensated respiratory alkalosis.
C) The client is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue and is at severe risk for impending respiratory failure.
D) The client's condition is improving as the pH and PaCO2 have successfully returned to normal baseline ranges.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**Rationale:** In the early stages of an asthma exacerbation, clients hyperventilate to increase oxygen intake, resulting in respiratory alkalosis (low PaCO2, high pH) as seen at 0800. If the asthma attack persists without resolution, the respiratory muscles begin to fatigue. As the client tires, they can no longer maintain hyperventilation, causing the PaCO2 to rise back to "normal" (42 mm Hg) and the pH to normalize (7.36), while the PaO2 drops (from 88 to 76 mm Hg). This deceptive "normalization" of the PaCO2 and pH in a patient still experiencing severe airway obstruction is a medical emergency indicating impending respiratory failure. Option A is incorrect because the HCO3 is normal, not low. Option B is incorrect because the hyperventilation is decreasing, not causing compensation. Option D falsely interprets normal numbers as clinical improvement without considering the dropping PaO2 and clinical context.
</details>

---

### Question 298
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a right-sided spontaneous pneumothorax. The multi-day treatment plan's primary goal is complete lung re-expansion. Which observation during the nurse's assessment best indicates that this goal has been met?

A) Fluctuation (tidaling) in the water-seal chamber has ceased during both inspiration and expiration.
B) Continuous, vigorous bubbling is present in the water-seal chamber.
C) The drainage in the collection chamber has shifted from serosanguinous to bright red.
D) Gentle, continuous bubbling is noted in the suction control chamber.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**Rationale:** Fluctuation (or tidaling) in the water-seal chamber reflects the changes in intrathoracic pressure during respiration. When the lung has fully re-expanded and fills the pleural space, the chest tube eyelets are blocked by the lung tissue, causing tidaling to cease. (Note: Tidaling can also stop if there is a kink or blockage in the tube, but combined with normal breath sounds, it is the primary indicator of re-expansion). Vigorous bubbling in the water-seal chamber (Option B) indicates an active air leak. A shift to bright red drainage (Option C) indicates active bleeding, which is an adverse complication. Gentle bubbling in the suction control chamber (Option D) simply means the wall suction is working properly, not that the lung has healed.
</details>

---

### Question 299
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client with a chest tube. Which action demonstrates the correct technique for managing this specific procedure?

A) Securing the chest tube connections tightly with standard paper tape to prevent accidental dislodgement.
B) Cleansing the insertion site with normal saline using a vigorous circular motion from the outside inward.
C) Removing the old dressing while instructing the client to take a deep breath and hold it.
D) Applying a sterile, split-drain gauze around the insertion site followed by an occlusive dressing.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills
**Rationale:** When performing a sterile chest tube dressing change, the standard of care is to place a sterile, split-drain gauze (drain sponge) around the tube at the insertion site to absorb drainage and provide cushioning, followed by an occlusive dressing (such as petroleum gauze or a tight transparent/tape cover, depending on facility policy) to prevent air from entering the pleural space around the tube. Option A is incorrect because standard paper tape is not strong enough or airtight; waterproof or silk tape is preferred, and connections are taped in a specific spiral or "meshed" fashion. Option B is incorrect because cleansing should be done from the inside outward (clean to dirty). Option C is incorrect because the Valsalva maneuver (bearing down) is required during the *removal* of the chest tube itself, not during a simple dressing change. 
</details>

---

### Question 300
The registered nurse (RN) is working with a licensed practical nurse (LPN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) on a medical-surgical respiratory unit. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the LPN?

A) Emptying the closed chest drainage system and recording the total shift output for a client with a hemothorax.
B) Interpreting a newly drawn set of arterial blood gases (ABGs) for a client on continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP).
C) Administering a scheduled subcutaneous enoxaparin dose to a stable client recovering from a pulmonary embolism.
D) Performing an initial assessment on a client reporting a sudden onset of sharp chest pain and dyspnea.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Delegation
**Rationale:** Delegating the administration of routine medications, such as subcutaneous enoxaparin, to a stable client is within the scope of practice for an LPN. Option A is incorrect because a closed chest drainage system should not be opened or emptied; it must be replaced as a whole unit when full to maintain the sterile, closed system. Option B is incorrect because interpreting diagnostic data like ABGs is an advanced skill that requires the RN's clinical judgment. Option D is incorrect because a sudden onset of chest pain and dyspnea indicates instability (a possible new pulmonary embolism or pneumothorax) and requires an initial, comprehensive RN assessment.
</details>
### Question 301
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client with a chest tube. Which action indicates the nurse is using the correct technique for this procedure?

A. Having the client bear down (Valsalva maneuver) while the new occlusive dressing is applied.
B. Cleaning the insertion site with alcohol swabs in a continuous vertical scrubbing motion.
C. Temporarily clamping the chest tube with padded forceps while the old dressing is being removed.
D. Cutting a standard sterile 4x4 gauze pad with sterile scissors to fit closely around the chest tube.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** When changing a chest tube dressing, having the client perform the Valsalva maneuver (bearing down) increases intrathoracic pressure. This prevents atmospheric air from being sucked into the pleural space while the occlusive seal is temporarily compromised. Cleaning should be done with chlorhexidine (or per facility protocol) using a circular motion from the insertion site outward, not a vertical scrubbing motion with alcohol. Chest tubes should never be routinely clamped during a dressing change, as this can lead to a tension pneumothorax. Standard gauze should never be cut with scissors because frayed fibers can enter the wound and increase the risk of infection; instead, pre-slit drain sponges should be used.

**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS
</details>

---

### Question 302
A client with a history of asthma is admitted to the emergency department with severe bronchospasm. The nurse analyzes the trend of the client's arterial blood gases (ABGs) over a 4-hour period:

- 0800: pH 7.48, PaCO2 30 mmHg, HCO3 22 mEq/L, PaO2 88 mmHg.
- 1200: pH 7.31, PaCO2 52 mmHg, HCO3 23 mEq/L, PaO2 65 mmHg.

Based on the shift in these ABG values, what is the most accurate interpretation of the client's respiratory status?

A. The client is entering a state of compensated metabolic acidosis due to cellular hypoxia.
B. The client is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue, indicating impending respiratory failure.
C. The client is exhibiting a successful response to bronchodilator therapy and oxygen administration.
D. The client is hyperventilating due to severe anxiety, causing a progression of respiratory alkalosis.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** The ABG trend demonstrates a classic and dangerous shift in a severe asthma exacerbation. At 0800, the client was experiencing respiratory alkalosis (high pH, low PaCO2) due to hyperventilation in an attempt to compensate for airway narrowing. By 1200, the client has developed uncompensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, dropping PaO2). This indicates the client's respiratory muscles are fatiguing, causing them to retain CO2. This is an ominous sign of impending respiratory failure requiring immediate intervention, potentially including mechanical ventilation.

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
</details>

---

### Question 303
A client is recovering on the orthopedic unit three days after a right total knee arthroplasty. The nurse is assessing the client for potential pulmonary complications. Which assessment finding is the earliest indication that the client is developing a pulmonary embolism?

A. Tracheal deviation to the left side and distended neck veins.
B. Development of a low-grade fever and a productive cough with green sputum.
C. Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain and unexplained tachypnea.
D. Diminished breath sounds at the lung bases that clear after incentive spirometry.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** A pulmonary embolism (PE) is a life-threatening complication, particularly in postoperative orthopedic clients due to immobility and venous stasis. The classic early signs of a PE are a sudden onset of dyspnea, unexplained tachypnea, and pleuritic chest pain. Tracheal deviation and distended neck veins are signs of a tension pneumothorax. A low-grade fever with a productive cough suggests a respiratory infection, such as pneumonia. Diminished breath sounds that clear with deep breathing indicate atelectasis, a common and easily correctable postoperative finding.

**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
</details>

---

### Question 304
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion for the treatment of a pulmonary embolism. The nurse reviews the client's latest laboratory results: Platelet count 190,000/mm³ (down from 210,000/mm³ yesterday), activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) 115 seconds (control is 30 seconds), and International Normalized Ratio (INR) 1.1. Which action should the nurse take regarding the heparin infusion?

A. Stop the heparin infusion immediately because the decreasing platelet count indicates heparin-induced thrombocytopenia.
B. Continue the heparin infusion at the current rate, as the aPTT is within the targeted therapeutic range for a pulmonary embolism.
C. Administer prescribed intravenous vitamin K to rapidly reverse the effects of the severely elevated coagulation times.
D. Hold the heparin infusion, assess the client for signs of bleeding, and notify the healthcare provider of the aPTT result.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** The therapeutic aPTT for a client on a continuous heparin infusion is typically 1.5 to 2.5 times the control value (e.g., 45 to 75 seconds). An aPTT of 115 seconds is supratherapeutic and places the client at a high risk for spontaneous bleeding. The nurse must hold the infusion, assess for bleeding, and notify the provider (or follow the facility's specific heparin protocol to adjust/hold the dose). Vitamin K is the antidote for warfarin, not heparin (protamine sulfate is the antidote for heparin). The platelet count, while slightly decreased, is still within the normal range (150,000-400,000/mm³) and does not represent the >50% drop characteristic of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT). 

**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION
</details>

---

### Question 305
The nurse is coordinating care on a respiratory unit. Which task related to chest tube management is most appropriate to delegate to an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Replacing the disposable chest tube drainage system when the collection chamber becomes full.
B. Assisting a stable client who is three days post-chest tube insertion with ambulation in the hallway.
C. Verifying that the fluid level in the water seal chamber gently tidals with the client's respirations.
D. Applying a new sterile occlusive petroleum dressing around the chest tube insertion site.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Delegation must fall within the UAP's scope of practice, which includes basic activities of daily living (ADLs) and assisting stable clients with mobility. Assisting a stable client with ambulation is appropriate. Changing the chest tube drainage system involves disconnecting the tube, which carries a risk of pneumothorax and requires nursing judgment and sterile technique. Assessing the water seal chamber for tidaling is an assessment of system patency and client status, strictly requiring an RN or LPN/LVN. Applying a new sterile dressing requires sterile technique and assessment of the insertion site, which cannot be delegated to a UAP.

**Lens:** DELEGATION
</details>
### Question 306
A client who underwent a right total hip arthroplasty three days ago reports feeling newly anxious and slightly short of breath. The nurse assesses the client and suspects a developing pulmonary embolism (PE). Which of the following early clinical manifestations most strongly supports this complication?

A. Bilateral crackles auscultated in the lung bases.
B. Expiratory wheezing scattered throughout all lung fields.
C. Sudden onset of localized pleuritic chest pain.
D. Tracheal deviation shifting toward the unaffected side.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION

**Rationale:** 
A sudden onset of localized pleuritic chest pain, along with unexplained dyspnea and apprehension, is a classic early sign of a pulmonary embolism (PE), especially in a high-risk postoperative client. A PE causes ischemia and inflammation of the pleura, leading to sharp pain that typically worsens on inspiration. 
Option A indicates fluid accumulation, such as in heart failure or atelectasis, rather than a PE. 
Option B describes bronchospasm, which is characteristic of asthma or an exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). 
Option D is a late and life-threatening sign of a tension pneumothorax, not a pulmonary embolism.

</details>

### Question 307
A client with an acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is receiving BiPAP therapy. Over the last 12 hours, the nurse notes the following arterial blood gas (ABG) trend:
- Initial ABG: pH 7.28, PaCO2 68 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 62 mm Hg.
- Current ABG: pH 7.34, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 29 mEq/L, PaO2 68 mm Hg.

Based on this data trend, what is the most accurate interpretation of the client's current respiratory status?

A. The client is demonstrating an improvement in partially compensated respiratory acidosis.
B. The client is experiencing worsening respiratory failure requiring endotracheal intubation.
C. The client has transitioned into a fully compensated respiratory acidosis.
D. The client is developing an uncompensated metabolic alkalosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS

**Rationale:** 
The initial ABG showed significant respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2) with early partial compensation (HCO3 slightly elevated at 28). The current ABG shows the pH rising toward normal (7.34 is still slightly acidotic but improved), the PaCO2 decreasing (from 68 to 58 mm Hg), and the PaO2 improving. The HCO3 remains elevated at 29 mEq/L to buffer the acid. Because the pH is not yet within the normal range (7.35–7.45), it is still *partially* compensated, but the overall trend demonstrates clinical improvement in response to the BiPAP therapy.
Option B is incorrect because the numbers are improving, not worsening.
Option C is incorrect because full compensation requires the pH to return to the normal range (7.35–7.45). 
Option D is incorrect as the primary issue is respiratory acidosis, not metabolic alkalosis.

</details>

---

### Question 308
The nurse is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) to care for a client who has a chest tube connected to a traditional water-seal drainage system for a pneumothorax. Which task is appropriate to delegate to the UAP?

A. Replacing the sterile occlusive dressing around the chest tube insertion site.
B. Verifying that the water seal chamber fluctuates appropriately with the client's respirations.
C. Adjusting the wall suction to ensure gentle bubbling in the suction control chamber.
D. Assisting the client with ambulation while keeping the drainage system below the level of the chest.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** DELEGATION

**Rationale:** 
Assisting a stable client with ambulation and maintaining the chest tube drainage system below the level of the chest is a standard safety procedure that falls within the UAP's scope of practice. 
Option A is a sterile procedure that requires nursing judgment and skill to ensure the airtight seal is maintained; it cannot be delegated to a UAP. 
Option B involves clinical assessment (evaluating for tidaling or air leaks), which is strictly the responsibility of the registered nurse. 
Option C involves adjusting medical equipment to deliver a prescribed therapeutic intervention, which must be performed by the nurse.

</details>

### Question 309
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a spontaneous right-sided pneumothorax. The multi-day treatment plan has a stated goal of "Complete resolution of pneumothorax." Which clinical finding indicates to the nurse that this goal has been met and the chest tube may be ready for removal?

A. The drainage in the collection chamber has changed from serosanguineous to serous.
B. Tidaling in the water-seal chamber has stopped, and clear lung sounds are present in all fields.
C. Continuous, rapid bubbling is observed in the water-seal chamber during exhalation.
D. The client reports a pain level of 2 out of 10 at the insertion site during deep inspiration.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS

**Rationale:** 
Tidaling (the rise and fall of fluid in the water-seal chamber with respirations) stops when the lung has fully re-expanded or if there is an obstruction in the tubing. When the cessation of tidaling is accompanied by the return of clear, bilateral lung sounds, it indicates the goal of pneumothorax resolution has been met and the tube may be ready for removal. 
Option A indicates a normal progression of wound healing but does not evaluate the resolution of the pleural air leak. 
Option C indicates an active air leak, meaning the pneumothorax is still present and unresolved. 
Option D is a subjective pain assessment; while decreased pain is a positive finding, it does not confirm lung re-expansion.

</details>

### Question 310
The nurse is preparing to change a soiled chest tube dressing for a client. The provider has prescribed a sterile occlusive dressing change. Which action by the nurse represents the correct application of sterile technique and skills for this procedure?

A. Cleanse the insertion site with an antiseptic swab, wiping from the outer edges of the skin inward toward the tube.
B. Briefly clamp the chest tube with padded hemostats while removing the old dressing to prevent air entry.
C. Wrap sterile petroleum gauze around the base of the chest tube insertion site before applying dry split-drain sponges.
D. Have the client bear down and perform the Valsalva maneuver while peeling away the old petroleum gauze.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS

**Rationale:** 
When changing a chest tube dressing, placing sterile petroleum gauze directly around the base of the chest tube at the insertion site provides an airtight seal, preventing atmospheric air from entering the pleural space. Dry sterile split-drain sponges are then placed over the petroleum gauze.
Option A is incorrect because cleansing should always occur from the insertion site (cleanest area) outward toward the surrounding skin to prevent pulling skin flora into the wound.
Option B is incorrect; chest tubes should never be routinely clamped during a dressing change as this can cause a tension pneumothorax. 
Option D is incorrect; the Valsalva maneuver is utilized during the actual *removal* of the chest tube from the body to prevent air entry, not during the removal of the old dressing.

</details>
### Question 311
A client with a severe exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is placed on non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (BiPAP). The nurse evaluates the client’s arterial blood gas (ABG) results over the first four hours of treatment:

- **Admission:** pH 7.28, PaCO2 68 mm Hg, HCO3 30 mEq/L, PaO2 55 mm Hg
- **2 hours post-BiPAP:** pH 7.32, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 30 mEq/L, PaO2 65 mm Hg
- **4 hours post-BiPAP:** pH 7.36, PaCO2 50 mm Hg, HCO3 30 mEq/L, PaO2 72 mm Hg

Based on this data trend, what is the nurse's most appropriate conclusion?

A) The client is developing severe metabolic acidosis.
B) The client's partially compensated respiratory acidosis is resolving. 
C) The client is exhibiting compensatory respiratory alkalosis.
D) The BiPAP should be discontinued immediately due to oxygen toxicity.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
**Rationale:** The admission ABG shows respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2) with partial metabolic compensation (elevated HCO3). Over the four hours on BiPAP, the PaCO2 steadily decreases, the pH normalizes to the lower end of normal (7.36), and the PaO2 improves. This trend indicates that the non-invasive ventilation is effectively blowing off excess carbon dioxide and improving oxygenation, meaning the respiratory acidosis is resolving. The client is not developing metabolic acidosis or respiratory alkalosis, and discontinuing the BiPAP would likely cause a relapse into acute respiratory failure.
</details>

---

### Question 312
A nurse is assessing a client who is three days post-operative following a right total knee arthroplasty. The client has been reluctant to ambulate and suddenly becomes highly restless, reporting a sense of impending doom. Which of the following findings would most strongly suggest the client is developing a pulmonary embolism?

A) A sudden, sharp chest pain that worsens with deep inspiration.
B) Gradual onset of bilateral lower extremity pitting edema.
C) A slow, steady decrease in resting heart rate over the last hour.
D) The presence of inspiratory stridor and generalized urticaria.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
**Rationale:** Post-operative immobility places the client at high risk for deep vein thrombosis (DVT) and subsequent pulmonary embolism (PE). Classic early signs of a PE include sudden onset dyspnea, restlessness, apprehension (impending doom), and pleuritic chest pain (sharp pain worsened by taking a deep breath). Bilateral edema is typically associated with heart failure, not a PE (a DVT would cause unilateral swelling). Tachycardia, not bradycardia, is a hallmark sign of PE. Stridor and urticaria indicate an anaphylactic reaction.
</details>

---

### Question 313
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client's chest tube insertion site. Which action by the nurse demonstrates the correct technique for this procedure?

A) Cleaning the site using a vigorous friction scrub starting from the outer edge of the chest and moving inward toward the tube.
B) Securing the chest tube connection points using standard paper tape to allow for easy removal.
C) Applying a sterile, petroleum-impregnated gauze directly around the chest tube insertion site.
D) Removing the soiled dressing while the client takes quick, shallow breaths.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS
**Rationale:** Applying a sterile, petroleum-impregnated gauze (often split-drain style) directly around the chest tube insertion site is critical because it creates an airtight seal, preventing atmospheric air from entering the pleural space and causing a tension pneumothorax. Cleaning should always occur from the insertion site outward (clean to dirty), not outer to inward. Chest tube connections should be secured with waterproof tape, silk tape, or specific securement ties, not standard paper tape which can easily detach. The client should be instructed to bear down (Valsalva maneuver) or hold their breath during dressing removal and application to prevent air entry, not take quick, shallow breaths.
</details>

---

### Question 314
A client was admitted three days ago with a pulmonary embolism and has been receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion and supplemental oxygen via nasal cannula. Which client outcome best indicates that the multi-day treatment plan for the pulmonary embolism is effective?

A) The client's activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) is 30 seconds.
B) The client reports coughing up small amounts of bright red blood.
C) The client's D-dimer level is significantly increased compared to the admission value.
D) The client's PaO2 is 92 mm Hg on room air with unlabored respirations.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
**Rationale:** The primary physiological consequence of a pulmonary embolism is a ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) mismatch resulting in hypoxemia. A PaO2 of 92 mm Hg on room air with unlabored respirations indicates that perfusion to the lungs has improved, gas exchange is adequate, and the primary goal of the treatment plan has been met. An aPTT of 30 seconds is normal (control) and is subtherapeutic for a client on a heparin drip (therapeutic range is usually 1.5 to 2.5 times the control). Hemoptysis (coughing up blood) is a sign of pulmonary infarction, which is a negative outcome. An increasing D-dimer indicates ongoing clot formation or breakdown, not resolution.
</details>

---

### Question 315
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client recovering from a spontaneous pneumothorax whose chest tube was successfully removed two days ago. Which statement by the client indicates a proper understanding of the lifestyle modifications needed to prevent a recurrence?

A) "I can schedule my upcoming scuba diving trip for next month."
B) "I should avoid traveling by airplane until I am cleared by my physician."
C) "I can resume smoking my pipe as long as I do not inhale the smoke deeply."
D) "I must wear a tight-fitting chest binder while sleeping for the next year."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
**Rationale:** Clients who have recently recovered from a pneumothorax are at a high risk for recurrence, especially when exposed to significant changes in atmospheric pressure. Flying in unpressurized or pressurized commercial aircraft must be avoided until specifically cleared by a healthcare provider (usually a few weeks to months post-recovery). Scuba diving is also strictly contraindicated due to extreme pressure changes. Smoking of any kind increases the risk of recurrence and should be completely ceased. Chest binders are not indicated and can restrict lung expansion, increasing the risk of atelectasis or pneumonia.
</details>
### Question 316
A client is 48 hours postoperative following an open reduction and internal fixation of the right femur. Which combination of assessment findings should alert the nurse to the early development of a pulmonary embolism?

A) Bradycardia, elevated temperature, and a newly productive cough.
B) Sudden onset of dyspnea, tachycardia, and a feeling of impending doom.
C) Gradual worsening of incisional pain, localized swelling, and dependent pallor.
D) Confusion, bounding peripheral pulses, and increased urine output.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Complication Recognition<br>
A pulmonary embolism (PE) is a life-threatening complication, particularly in patients recovering from lower extremity orthopedic surgeries. Early and classic signs of a PE include a sudden onset of dyspnea, tachycardia, tachypnea, chest pain, and a distinct feeling of apprehension or "impending doom." Bradycardia (Option A) and bounding pulses (Option D) do not align with the sympathetic nervous system response triggered by hypoxia and stress in a PE. Option C describes signs of potential deep vein thrombosis or surgical site complications, not a pulmonary embolism.
</details>

---

### Question 317
The nurse reviews the daily arterial blood gas (ABG) trends for a client admitted with a severe exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). 

Day 1: pH 7.35, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 30 mEq/L 
Day 2: pH 7.34, PaCO2 62 mm Hg, HCO3 31 mEq/L 
Day 3: pH 7.28, PaCO2 74 mm Hg, HCO3 32 mEq/L 

Based on this trend, what is the most accurate interpretation of the client's current respiratory status on Day 3?

A) The client's renal system has successfully and fully compensated for the hypercapnia.
B) The client is experiencing a mixed respiratory and metabolic acidosis.
C) The client's condition is improving, as indicated by the progressively increasing bicarbonate levels.
D) The client is demonstrating acute-on-chronic respiratory acidosis requiring immediate intervention.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: D</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Data Trend Analysis<br>
Clients with severe COPD typically live in a state of fully compensated chronic respiratory acidosis (seen on Day 1, with a high PaCO2 but a pH within the lower limit of normal due to elevated HCO3). On Day 3, the PaCO2 has spiked significantly to 74 mm Hg, and the pH has dropped aggressively to 7.28 (acidemia). Despite the kidneys attempting to compensate (high HCO3), the sudden and severe drop in pH indicates acute-on-chronic respiratory failure (uncompensated or partially compensated acute respiratory acidosis superimposed on chronic retention). This requires immediate clinical intervention, such as non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (BiPAP) or intubation. 
</details>

---

### Question 318
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a spontaneous right-sided pneumothorax. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the treatment plan has met its primary goal and the lung has fully re-expanded?

A) The water seal chamber exhibits continuous, vigorous bubbling during inspiration.
B) There is a sudden, sharp increase in serosanguineous drainage in the collection chamber.
C) Fluctuation (tidaling) in the water seal chamber has completely stopped, and breath sounds are present bilaterally.
D) The client reports a pain level of 2 out of 10 at the insertion site during deep breathing.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Evaluation of Goals<br>
The primary goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is to evacuate air from the pleural space and allow the lung to re-expand. When the lung fully re-expands, it fills the pleural cavity, blocking the eyelets of the chest tube. This causes the fluctuation (tidaling) in the water seal chamber to cease. The combination of stopped tidaling and the return of clear, bilateral breath sounds indicates successful re-expansion. Continuous bubbling (Option A) indicates an ongoing air leak. A sudden increase in drainage (Option B) is unexpected for a simple pneumothorax and could indicate hemorrhage. Mild pain (Option D) is an expected finding but does not objectively evaluate lung re-expansion.
</details>

---

### Question 319
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client who recently experienced a pulmonary embolism and is newly prescribed the oral anticoagulant rivaroxaban. Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the complex lifestyle and safety modifications required?

A) "I should switch to a soft-bristle toothbrush and use an electric razor to minimize bleeding risks."
B) "I must strictly avoid all green leafy vegetables to prevent interacting with this medication."
C) "I will need to visit the clinic every two weeks to have my PT and INR levels checked."
D) "I can stop taking this medication safely as soon as my shortness of breath completely goes away."

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: A</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Complex Teaching<br>
Rivaroxaban is a direct oral anticoagulant (DOAC) prescribed to treat and prevent deep vein thrombosis and pulmonary embolisms. Because it impairs the clotting cascade, the client is at a high risk for bleeding; utilizing a soft-bristle toothbrush and an electric razor are essential safety modifications (Option A). Unlike warfarin, rivaroxaban does not require strict dietary monitoring of Vitamin K (Option B) or routine PT/INR laboratory checks (Option C). The medication must be taken for the entire duration prescribed by the provider (often 3-6 months or longer), even after acute symptoms resolve, to prevent recurrent clots (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 320
The nurse is preparing to perform a sterile dressing change for a client's chest tube insertion site. Which action represents the correct technique and sequence during this procedure?

A) Remove the old dressing using sterile gloves to prevent contaminating the surrounding intact skin.
B) Apply a sterile occlusive petroleum gauze directly around the insertion site to prevent air leaks.
C) Cleanse the insertion site with plain sterile water and tightly wrap the tubing with paper tape.
D) Instruct the client to cough forcefully throughout the entire dressing change process.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> Sterile Technique/Skills<br>
When changing a chest tube dressing, maintaining an airtight seal is critical to prevent atmospheric air from entering the pleural space, which could cause a tension pneumothorax. Applying a sterile occlusive dressing, typically petroleum gauze, directly around the insertion site is the correct technique (Option B). The old dressing is removed using *clean* gloves, not sterile gloves, to protect the nurse from biohazards (Option A). The site should be cleansed with an approved antiseptic like chlorhexidine, not plain water (Option C). The client should breathe normally or perform the Valsalva maneuver if the tube becomes dislodged or exposed during the change, but forcefully coughing throughout the procedure (Option D) could increase intrathoracic pressure unnecessarily and risk tube dislodgement.
</details>
### Question 321
The nurse is caring for a client recovering from a right total knee arthroplasty performed 48 hours ago. Which new-onset clinical manifestation best indicates the client is experiencing a pulmonary embolism complication rather than an expected postoperative respiratory shift?

A. Bilateral crackles auscultated in the lung bases
B. Sudden, localized pleuritic chest pain accompanied by tachypnea
C. A new productive cough with thick, yellow sputum
D. A respiratory rate of 20 breaths per minute with an SpO2 of 94% on room air

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Sudden, localized pleuritic chest pain accompanied by tachypnea

**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs

**Rationale:** A pulmonary embolism (PE) is a life-threatening complication of deep vein thrombosis, heavily associated with large joint orthopedic surgeries. Classic, early signs of a PE include the sudden onset of dyspnea, tachypnea, tachycardia, and sharp, pleuritic chest pain that worsens on inspiration. Bilateral crackles in the bases often indicate atelectasis or fluid volume overload. A productive cough with yellow sputum points to a respiratory infection (e.g., pneumonia). A respiratory rate of 20 breaths per minute and an oxygen saturation of 94% are within acceptable postoperative parameters and do not indicate a PE.
</details>

---

### Question 322
The nurse is caring for a client admitted with a severe asthma exacerbation. Yesterday, the client's initial arterial blood gas (ABG) on room air showed: pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mm Hg, and HCO3 24 mEq/L. Today, the client appears lethargic, and the repeat ABG reveals: pH 7.32, PaCO2 52 mm Hg, and HCO3 24 mEq/L. How should the nurse interpret this shift in data?

A. The client is entering a state of respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.
B. The client's pulmonary condition is improving as the initial respiratory alkalosis has resolved.
C. The client is experiencing expected renal compensation in response to the initial hyperventilation.
D. The client is actively hyperventilating due to increased anxiety and oxygen demand.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The client is entering a state of respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
**System Focus:** ABG interpretation

**Rationale:** During the early stages of an asthma exacerbation, clients hyperventilate to increase oxygenation, which blows off CO2, resulting in respiratory alkalosis (pH 7.50, PaCO2 28). As the exacerbation progresses without relief, the respiratory muscles fatigue, leading to hypoventilation, CO2 retention, and a shift into respiratory acidosis (pH 7.32, PaCO2 52). This trend, combined with lethargy, is a critical warning sign of impending respiratory failure requiring immediate intervention (such as intubation or mechanical ventilation). It does not represent improvement, hyperventilation, or renal compensation (the HCO3 remained unchanged at 24 mEq/L).
</details>

---

### Question 323
The nurse is preparing to perform a sterile dressing change for a client with a chest tube connected to a closed-water seal drainage system. Which action demonstrates the correct technique for this procedure?

A. Pre-medicate the client for pain 5 minutes before removing the old dressing.
B. Cleanse the insertion site with normal saline or chlorhexidine, working from the outside of the wound inward toward the tube.
C. Instruct the client to perform the Valsalva maneuver during the entire dressing change process.
D. Place sterile split-drain (Y-cut) sponges around the tube and cover securely with an occlusive dressing.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Place sterile split-drain (Y-cut) sponges around the tube and cover securely with an occlusive dressing.

**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** Correct sterile technique for a chest tube dressing change requires placing sterile split-drain (Y-cut) sponges around the insertion site to absorb drainage and protect the skin, followed by a sterile occlusive dressing (often secured tightly with tape, depending on facility protocol) to prevent atmospheric air from entering the pleural space. Pre-medicating the client should be done 30 to 60 minutes before the procedure, not 5 minutes. Cleansing should always occur from the insertion site moving *outward* to prevent tracking environmental bacteria into the wound. The Valsalva maneuver is utilized during chest tube *removal* to prevent air entry, not continuously during a standard dressing change.
</details>

---

### Question 324
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client who experienced a massive pulmonary embolism and is newly prescribed oral warfarin. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle and dietary management required for this therapy?

A. "I will need to stop taking my prescribed low-dose aspirin immediately so I don't bleed."
B. "I should completely eliminate all dark green, leafy vegetables from my daily meals."
C. "I will maintain a consistent daily intake of foods that contain vitamin K."
D. "I should use a firm-bristled toothbrush to toughen my gums against potential bleeding."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. "I will maintain a consistent daily intake of foods that contain vitamin K."

**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs/management

**Rationale:** Warfarin is a vitamin K antagonist used to prevent future pulmonary embolisms. Clients taking warfarin do not need to eliminate vitamin K from their diet; rather, they must maintain a *consistent* intake to ensure their INR levels remain stable and within the therapeutic range. Sudden increases or decreases in vitamin K can cause the INR to become subtherapeutic or supratherapeutic. Stopping prescribed aspirin without provider approval can be dangerous; concurrent use requires monitoring but shouldn't be abruptly self-discontinued. Clients should use a *soft-bristled* toothbrush to prevent gum bleeding, not a firm one.
</details>

---

### Question 325
A client is on day three of a multi-day treatment plan for a right-sided spontaneous pneumothorax, utilizing a chest tube connected to a closed-water seal drainage system. Which clinical finding best indicates that the treatment plan is achieving its primary therapeutic goal?

A. Arterial blood gas results show a pH of 7.42, PaCO2 of 40 mm Hg, and PaO2 of 94 mm Hg on room air.
B. The water seal chamber exhibits continuous, vigorous bubbling during both inspiration and expiration.
C. The drainage collection chamber shows an output of 150 mL of bright red fluid over the past hour.
D. The client demonstrates shallow, guarded breathing to avoid triggering pain at the insertion site.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Arterial blood gas results show a pH of 7.42, PaCO2 of 40 mm Hg, and PaO2 of 94 mm Hg on room air.

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management and ABG interpretation

**Rationale:** The primary goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is to re-establish negative pressure in the pleural space, re-expand the lung, and restore normal gas exchange. Normal ABG parameters (pH 7.35–7.45, PaCO2 35–45 mm Hg, and PaO2 80–100 mm Hg) on room air signify that adequate ventilation and oxygenation have been restored. Continuous, vigorous bubbling in the water seal chamber indicates an active air leak, meaning the pneumothorax is not yet resolved. Output of 150 mL/hr of bright red blood is a sign of active hemorrhage (a complication, not a goal). Guarded, shallow breathing restricts lung expansion and increases the risk of atelectasis.
</details>
### Question 326
A client is recovering on the surgical unit three days after a right femur fracture repair. During morning rounds, the nurse notes a sudden change in the client's condition. Which assessment finding represents the earliest clinical manifestation of a developing pulmonary embolism?

A. Copious amounts of frothy, pink-tinged sputum
B. Sudden onset of tachypnea and a feeling of impending doom
C. Tracheal deviation to the unaffected side of the chest
D. Bilateral fine crackles auscultated in the lung bases

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Sudden onset of tachypnea and a feeling of impending doom

**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism Signs

**Rationale:** 
Tachypnea, sudden pleuritic chest pain, apprehension, and a feeling of impending doom are classic, early clinical manifestations of a pulmonary embolism (PE). A client recovering from a major orthopedic surgery (like a femur fracture repair) is at high risk for deep vein thrombosis (DVT) and subsequent PE. Tracheal deviation (Option C) is a late sign of a tension pneumothorax. Pink, frothy sputum (Option A) is a hallmark sign of severe pulmonary edema. Bilateral crackles in the bases (Option D) typically indicate fluid overload, heart failure, or atelectasis, rather than the sudden vascular occlusion seen in a PE.
</details>

---

### Question 327
A client is admitted to the emergency department with a severe asthma exacerbation. The nurse analyzes serial arterial blood gas (ABG) results drawn two hours apart:
*   0800: pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mmHg, PaO2 70 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L
*   1000: pH 7.36, PaCO2 44 mmHg, PaO2 62 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L

Based on this ABG trend, what does the nurse conclude regarding the client's condition?

A. The client is responding appropriately to bronchodilator therapy.
B. The client's renal system has begun compensating for the initial alkalosis.
C. The client is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending failure.
D. The client has achieved normal alveolar ventilation and baseline oxygenation.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. The client is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending failure.

**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** ABG Interpretation

**Rationale:** 
During an initial asthma attack, clients typically hyperventilate, "blowing off" carbon dioxide, which leads to respiratory alkalosis (seen in the 0800 ABG with a low PaCO2 of 28 mmHg and high pH of 7.50). As the asthma attack continues and the client tires out, their breathing becomes less effective, causing PaCO2 levels to rise back to "normal" and eventually into acidotic ranges. A "normal" or rising PaCO2 in the setting of an ongoing asthma attack (as seen at 1000) is an ominous sign indicating respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure. The drop in PaO2 further supports clinical deterioration, refuting options A and D. There is no change in HCO3, meaning renal compensation (Option B) has not occurred.
</details>

---

### Question 328
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client with a chest tube connected to a closed-chest drainage system. Which action demonstrates the correct technique for this procedure?

A. Placing sterile, pre-cut drain sponges (split gauze) snugly around the chest tube insertion site.
B. Cleansing the skin in a circular motion starting from the outer border and moving inward toward the tube.
C. Temporarily clamping the chest tube to maintain system sterility while the old dressing is removed.
D. Securing the new dressing with standard paper tape to allow the insertion site to breathe.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Placing sterile, pre-cut drain sponges (split gauze) snugly around the chest tube insertion site.

**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills
**System Focus:** Chest Tube Management

**Rationale:** 
When changing a chest tube dressing, sterile technique is paramount. The nurse should use sterile, pre-cut drain sponges (split gauze) to fit securely around the tube, followed by covering the area with sterile gauze and securing it with an occlusive tape or dressing (not standard paper tape, making Option D incorrect). The site should be cleansed starting at the insertion site and moving outward in a circular motion, not outside-in, to prevent bringing surrounding skin flora into the wound (Option B is incorrect). A chest tube should never be clamped during a routine dressing change as this can rapidly cause a life-threatening tension pneumothorax (Option C is incorrect).
</details>

---

### Question 329
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a right-sided spontaneous pneumothorax. The nurse evaluates the daily progress of the closed-chest drainage system to determine if the treatment goal is being met. Which assessment finding best indicates that the pneumothorax has resolved?

A. Continuous, vigorous bubbling is observed in the water-seal chamber.
B. The client reports a pain score of 2 out of 10 at the insertion site during inspiration.
C. The fluid level in the water-seal chamber fluctuates with the client's respirations.
D. Symmetrical chest expansion is noted and tidaling in the water-seal chamber has ceased.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Symmetrical chest expansion is noted and tidaling in the water-seal chamber has ceased.

**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** Chest Tube Management

**Rationale:** 
The primary goal of chest tube therapy for a pneumothorax is re-expansion of the lung. Tidaling (the fluctuation of fluid in the water-seal chamber with breathing) occurs while the lung is still collapsed. When the lung fully re-expands, it presses against the eyelets of the chest tube, causing tidaling to cease. This, combined with symmetrical chest expansion and clear breath sounds, indicates successful treatment. Continuous bubbling (Option A) indicates an ongoing air leak. Tidaling (Option C) indicates the system is patent but the lung has not completely re-expanded yet. A low pain score (Option B) is a positive comfort finding but does not objectively evaluate lung re-expansion.
</details>

---

### Question 330
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client who experienced a pulmonary embolism (PE) and will be managing their condition at home with oral warfarin. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the complex lifestyle management required?

A. "If my gums bleed slightly when brushing, I should stop taking the medication immediately."
B. "I should take a daily low-dose aspirin to further reduce my risk of developing another clot."
C. "I will wear medical alert identification and maintain a consistent daily intake of vitamin K."
D. "I will need to schedule routine blood tests to carefully monitor my aPTT levels."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. "I will wear medical alert identification and maintain a consistent daily intake of vitamin K."

**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism Management

**Rationale:** 
Clients on warfarin must maintain a *consistent* intake of dietary vitamin K (found in dark green leafy vegetables), rather than completely avoiding it or drastically increasing it, as fluctuations will alter the drug's therapeutic effect. Wearing medical alert identification ensures emergency personnel are aware of the bleeding risk. Warfarin therapy is monitored using PT/INR, not aPTT (which is used for Heparin), making Option D incorrect. Taking daily aspirin (Option B) concurrently with warfarin increases the risk for severe hemorrhage and should only be done if explicitly prescribed by the healthcare provider. Bleeding gums are a known side effect; the client should use a soft-bristle toothbrush but should not stop the medication abruptly without contacting their provider (Option A is incorrect).
</details>
### Question 331
A patient is recovering from a right total hip arthroplasty performed two days ago. The nurse is conducting a routine assessment when the patient suddenly appears distressed. Which combination of subtle, early findings should the nurse recognize as indicative of a developing pulmonary embolism (PE)?

A) Gradual onset of exertional dyspnea, barrel chest, and digital clubbing.
B) Unexplained restlessness, a sudden onset of tachypnea, and tachycardia.
C) Bradypnea, confusion, and dull, localized chest pain.
D) Productive cough with thick green sputum, low-grade fever, and chills.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Unexplained restlessness, a sudden onset of tachypnea, and tachycardia.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus: Complication Recognition.** The clinical manifestations of a pulmonary embolism (PE) can be varied, but early recognition is critical to prevent fatal outcomes.
- Unexplained restlessness and anxiety are frequent, subtle, early signs of hypoxemia resulting from a pulmonary embolus. Sudden onset tachypnea (rapid breathing to compensate for poor gas exchange) and tachycardia (heart pumping faster to maintain cardiac output) are classic early hallmark signs of PE.
- Option A describes chronic changes associated with COPD, not an acute PE.
- Option C is incorrect; a PE causes tachypnea, not bradypnea, and the chest pain is typically sharp and pleuritic, not dull and localized. 
- Option D describes an infectious process such as pneumonia, not a vascular occlusion like a PE.
</details>

### Question 332
A patient with a chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) exacerbation has been receiving bilevel positive airway pressure (BiPAP) therapy. The nurse reviews the patient's trended Arterial Blood Gas (ABG) results:
- **Day 1, 0800:** pH 7.28, PaCO2 68 mmHg, HCO3 30 mEq/L, PaO2 55 mmHg.
- **Day 2, 0800:** pH 7.36, PaCO2 58 mmHg, HCO3 32 mEq/L, PaO2 65 mmHg.

Based on this trend, what does this data analysis indicate regarding the patient's respiratory status?

A) The patient's condition is deteriorating into uncompensated respiratory acidosis.
B) The patient has developed an acute superimposed metabolic alkalosis.
C) The patient is improving and demonstrating fully compensated respiratory acidosis.
D) The patient requires immediate intubation due to impending respiratory failure.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) The patient is improving and demonstrating fully compensated respiratory acidosis.
