**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus: Data Trend Analysis.** 
- On Day 1, the patient had a pH of 7.28 (acidosis) with an elevated PaCO2 (respiratory cause) and an elevated HCO3 (attempting to compensate), indicating *partially compensated respiratory acidosis*. 
- On Day 2, the pH has returned to the normal range (7.35–7.45), specifically to 7.36 (which is on the acidic side of normal, pointing to a respiratory primary issue). The PaCO2 remains elevated (58), but the HCO3 has increased further (32) to bring the pH into normal range. This indicates *fully compensated respiratory acidosis*. Furthermore, the PaO2 has improved from 55 to 65.
- Option A is incorrect because the pH is normalizing, indicating compensation, not uncompensated deterioration. 
- Option B is incorrect as the elevated bicarbonate is a compensatory mechanism by the kidneys for chronic CO2 retention, not an acute metabolic alkalosis.
- Option D is incorrect because the objective ABG data demonstrates clinical improvement, so immediate intubation is not indicated based solely on these numbers.
</details>

---

### Question 333
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a patient with a pleural chest tube. Which sequence of actions demonstrates the correct technique for this procedure?

A) Remove the old dressing using sterile gloves, clean the site with an antiseptic using friction, apply regular 4x4 gauze over the tube, and secure tightly with paper tape.
B) Don sterile gloves, remove the old dressing, clean the site with sterile saline from outer to inner edges, apply a split-drain sterile gauze, and secure with tape.
C) Remove the old dressing with clean gloves, don sterile gloves, clean the insertion site with an approved antiseptic, place a sterile pre-slit gauze around the tube, and secure it with occlusive tape.
D) Clean the skin around the dressing with chlorhexidine, peel off the old transparent film, apply an occlusive petroleum gauze bare-handed, and wrap with an elastic bandage.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Remove the old dressing with clean gloves, don sterile gloves, clean the insertion site with an approved antiseptic, place a sterile pre-slit gauze around the tube, and secure it with occlusive tape.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus: Sterile Technique/Skills.** 
- A chest tube requires a sterile dressing change to prevent the introduction of pathogens directly into the pleural space. The correct sequence involves removing the soiled dressing with clean gloves (to avoid contaminating the new sterile gloves). After removing the old dressing, the nurse washes hands, sets up the sterile field, dons sterile gloves, cleans the site (usually with chlorhexidine or another approved antiseptic, wiping inner to outer), applies a sterile pre-slit (drain) sponge around the tube, and secures it occlusively to prevent air leaks.
- Option A is incorrect because using sterile gloves to remove a dirty dressing contaminates them before the sterile steps begin, and a regular 4x4 does not fit properly around the tube without cutting it (which can leave fibers).
- Option B is incorrect because cleaning should occur from the inner edge (cleanest at the insertion site) to the outer edge, and removing an old dressing with sterile gloves ruins their sterility. 
- Option D violates sterile technique entirely.
</details>

---

### Question 334
A patient had a chest tube placed three days ago for a spontaneous right-sided pneumothorax. While evaluating the closed-chest drainage system, the nurse notes that there is no continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber, but the fluid level in the water-seal chamber gently rises and falls with the patient's inspiration and expiration. How should the nurse evaluate this finding?

A) The treatment plan is successful because the air leak has resolved and the lung is re-expanding.
B) The system has developed a blockage and requires immediate stripping of the tubing to restore patency.
C) The chest tube has become dislodged from the pleural space and the provider must be notified immediately.
D) The wall suction pressure is set too low and should be increased until gentle continuous bubbling returns.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The treatment plan is successful because the air leak has resolved and the lung is re-expanding.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus: Evaluation of Goals.** 
- In a chest drainage system treating a pneumothorax, continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber indicates an active air leak. The cessation of bubbling in the water-seal chamber (while tidaling is still present) means the air leak has resolved and the lung is likely re-expanded. 
- The gentle rising and falling of water with respirations is called "tidaling" and is an expected finding that confirms the chest tube is patent and correctly communicating with the pleural space.
- Option B is incorrect; stripping chest tubes causes dangerously high negative pressure and is contraindicated. Furthermore, tidaling indicates patency, not a blockage.
- Option C is incorrect; tidaling confirms the tube is still in the pleural space. 
- Option D is incorrect; bubbling in the *suction control* chamber indicates proper suction, but continuous bubbling in the *water-seal* chamber is an air leak, which we do not want to force to return.
</details>

---

### Question 335
The registered nurse (RN) is providing care with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) for a stable patient recovering from a pulmonary embolism who currently has a chest tube connected to wall suction for a pleural effusion. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Palpate the skin around the chest tube insertion site for the presence of subcutaneous emphysema.
B) Adjust the wall suction dial if the fluid in the suction control chamber stops gently bubbling.
C) Assist the patient with repositioning in bed while ensuring the chest tube tubing remains free of kinks.
D) Instruct the patient on the signs and symptoms of recurrent pulmonary embolism prior to discharge.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Assist the patient with repositioning in bed while ensuring the chest tube tubing remains free of kinks.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus: Delegation.** 
- Assisting a stable patient with routine turning and repositioning is well within the scope of practice for a UAP. Ensuring that tubings (like chest tubes, IVs, and catheters) are not kinked, compressed, or pulled during movement is a basic safety measure that UAPs are trained to perform.
- Option A requires assessment skills to identify crepitus (subcutaneous emphysema), which is strictly the responsibility of the RN.
- Option B involves adjusting a therapeutic medical device (wall suction), which requires nursing judgment and assessment, and cannot be delegated.
- Option D involves patient education/teaching, which is entirely within the RN scope of practice and cannot be delegated.
</details>
### Question 336
A patient who recently underwent an open reduction internal fixation of the right femur is recovering on the surgical unit. The nurse is conducting a routine assessment and monitoring for postoperative complications. Which assessment finding is the most critical early indicator that the patient is developing a pulmonary embolism?

A) Gradual onset of bilateral crackles in the lower lung bases.
B) Sudden onset of dyspnea and sharp, pleuritic chest pain.
C) Low-grade fever accompanied by a productive morning cough.
D) Continuous, unilateral wheezing auscultated over the upper lobes.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs

**Rationale:** 
A sudden onset of dyspnea (shortness of breath) and sharp, pleuritic chest pain that worsens on inspiration are classic, early hallmark signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE), especially in a patient with a high-risk factor like recent long-bone or orthopedic surgery. Gradual onset of bilateral crackles (Option A) is more indicative of fluid overload or heart failure. A low-grade fever with a productive cough (Option C) suggests a developing respiratory infection like pneumonia. Unilateral wheezing (Option D) usually indicates a localized airway obstruction or inflammation, rather than the vascular occlusion seen in a PE.
</details>

---

### Question 337
A patient is admitted to the progressive care unit with a severe asthma exacerbation and has been receiving continuous albuterol nebulizer treatments. The nurse reviews the patient’s arterial blood gas (ABG) trends over the past four hours:
- 10:00 AM: pH 7.48, PaCO2 30 mm Hg, PaO2 85 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L
- 02:00 PM: pH 7.37, PaCO2 42 mm Hg, PaO2 76 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L

Based on this data trend over time, what is the most accurate interpretation of the patient's respiratory status?

A) The patient is effectively compensating, and the asthma exacerbation is resolving.
B) The patient is experiencing hyperventilation secondary to anxiety from the nebulizers.
C) The patient is developing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.
D) The patient has developed a metabolic acidosis due to medication side effects.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
**System Focus:** ABG interpretation

**Rationale:** 
In the early stages of an asthma attack, patients typically hyperventilate, "blowing off" carbon dioxide and causing a respiratory alkalosis (seen at 10:00 AM with a low PaCO2 of 30 mm Hg and elevated pH). As the attack progresses and the patient becomes exhausted, their breathing slows or becomes less effective. The PaCO2 will begin to rise into the "normal" range (seen at 2:00 PM with a PaCO2 of 42 mm Hg). In a patient with a severe asthma exacerbation, a "normalizing" or rising PaCO2 is a dangerous sign of respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure, not improvement (Option A). The lack of change in the HCO3 rules out a metabolic shift (Option D), and hyperventilation would result in a persistently low PaCO2, not a rising one (Option B).
</details>

---

### Question 338
The nurse is preparing to perform a scheduled sterile dressing change for a patient with a closed water-seal chest drainage system. After donning clean gloves, carefully removing the old dressing, assessing the insertion site, and donning sterile gloves, what is the correct next action in the sterile procedure sequence?

A) Cleanse the insertion site with an antiseptic swab using a circular motion moving outward.
B) Apply a sterile split-drain gauze sponge directly around the chest tube insertion site.
C) Secure a petroleum gauze over the insertion site followed by an occlusive tape dressing.
D) Instruct the patient to take a deep breath and bear down while manipulating the tube.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** 
When performing a sterile dressing change for a chest tube, once the old dressing is removed and sterile gloves are applied, the very next step is to properly cleanse the insertion site to prevent infection. This is done using an antiseptic (often chlorhexidine or betadine) in a circular motion, starting at the insertion site and moving outward (Option A). Applying the split-drain sponge (Option B) or petroleum gauze (Option C) comes *after* the site has been cleansed and allowed to dry. Instructing the patient to bear down (Valsalva maneuver) (Option D) is used when a chest tube is being removed, not during a routine dressing change.
</details>

---

### Question 339
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a patient with a chronic malignant pleural effusion who is going home with a mobile chest drain featuring a flutter (Heimlich) valve. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of how to manage this device at home?

A) "I need to clamp the chest tube temporarily whenever I am taking a shower."
B) "I must keep the drainage collection bag strictly above the level of my chest at all times."
C) "I will inject sterile water into the flutter valve daily to keep it from clogging."
D) "I should expect the valve to make a slight venting sound when I exhale."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** 
A flutter (Heimlich) valve is a one-way valve used for chest drainage that allows air and fluid to escape the pleural space but prevents it from re-entering. When the patient exhales, air is pushed out of the pleural space through the valve, which can create a venting or flutter sound; this is normal and expected (Option D). The chest tube should never be clamped during normal daily activities like showering, as this can cause a tension pneumothorax (Option A). The drainage collection system must remain *below* the level of the chest to promote gravity drainage and prevent backflow (Option B). Patients should never inject fluids into the valve, as this compromises the closed system and risks infection (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 340
A patient with a long history of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is receiving supplemental oxygen at 2 L/min via nasal cannula. The patient complains of severe incisional pain and requests their scheduled dose of intravenous morphine. The nurse reviews the morning arterial blood gas (ABG) results: pH 7.31, PaCO2 59 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, and PaO2 62 mm Hg. Which action should the nurse take?

A) Withhold the narcotic dose and contact the provider because the results indicate acute respiratory depression.
B) Administer the dose as prescribed since these ABG values reflect fully compensated chronic COPD.
C) Administer the medication but increase the oxygen flow rate to 5 L/min to correct the hypoxemia.
D) Withhold the narcotic dose because the elevated bicarbonate level indicates a severe metabolic emergency.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** DRUG-LAB INTERACTION
**System Focus:** ABG interpretation

**Rationale:** 
The ABG reveals uncompensated or partially compensated respiratory acidosis (low pH of 7.31, elevated PaCO2 of 59 mm Hg). While COPD patients often have a baseline of retained CO2, a pH below 7.35 indicates acute decompensation or an acute-on-chronic respiratory acidosis. Morphine is a central nervous system depressant that suppresses the respiratory drive. Administering it to a patient in an actively acidotic, hypercapnic state can precipitate severe respiratory failure. The nurse must hold the medication and notify the provider (Option A). The ABG is not fully compensated because the pH is abnormal (Option B). Increasing oxygen to 5 L/min in a COPD patient without an explicit order can dangerously suppress their hypoxic drive to breathe (Option C). The elevated bicarbonate (28 mEq/L) is a compensatory mechanism by the kidneys for chronic CO2 retention, not a severe primary metabolic emergency (Option D).
</details>
### Question 341
A client is 48 hours post-operative following a right total knee arthroplasty. Which assessment finding should the nurse recognize as an early indication of a pulmonary embolism rather than an expected post-operative finding?

A) Oxygen saturation of 94% on room air.
B) Sudden onset of apprehension and pleuritic chest pain.
C) Mild incisional pain radiating down the right calf.
D) Fine crackles auscultated in the lung bases.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
**Rationale:** A sudden onset of apprehension, restlessness, and pleuritic chest pain (pain that worsens on inspiration) are classic early signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE), a life-threatening complication for post-operative clients. An oxygen saturation of 94% (Option A) and fine crackles in the lung bases (Option D) are common post-operative findings often related to mild atelectasis and can be managed with incentive spirometry. Incisional pain radiating down the calf (Option C) does not describe a PE, though calf pain itself could indicate a deep vein thrombosis (DVT), which is a precursor to a PE, not a PE itself.
</details>

---

### Question 342
A client with severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is receiving treatment for an acute respiratory exacerbation. The nurse reviews the client’s arterial blood gas (ABG) results over the past 48 hours. 
Day 1: pH 7.28, PaCO2 68 mm Hg, HCO3- 26 mEq/L. 
Day 3: pH 7.36, PaCO2 65 mm Hg, HCO3- 35 mEq/L. 
How should the nurse interpret this trend?

A) The respiratory treatment is failing to eliminate excess bicarbonate from the blood.
B) The client has transitioned into a state of acute respiratory alkalosis.
C) The kidneys are retaining bicarbonate to compensate for the elevated carbon dioxide.
D) The client is developing a mixed acid-base disorder requiring immediate intubation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
**Rationale:** On Day 1, the client is in uncompensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, normal HCO3-). By Day 3, the pH has returned to normal (7.36), the PaCO2 remains elevated, and the HCO3- has significantly increased. This trend indicates fully compensated respiratory acidosis. The kidneys take 24 to 48 hours to compensate for chronic respiratory acidosis by retaining bicarbonate (HCO3-) to buffer the excess acid (CO2) and normalize the pH. Therefore, the kidneys are appropriately compensating. The client is not in respiratory alkalosis, nor does this indicate a failing treatment or a mixed disorder.
</details>

---

### Question 343
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a right-sided spontaneous pneumothorax. Which observation best indicates to the nurse that the treatment goal has been met and the pneumothorax is resolving?

A) Equal bilateral breath sounds with cessation of tidaling in the water-seal chamber.
B) Continuous bubbling is observed in the water-seal chamber during inspiration.
C) Gentle tidaling occurs in the water-seal chamber with each respiratory cycle.
D) The collection chamber shows a steady increase in serosanguinous drainage.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
**Rationale:** The goal of chest tube therapy for a pneumothorax is to re-expand the collapsed lung. Tidaling (the fluctuation of fluid in the water-seal chamber) naturally occurs as the patient breathes, due to changes in intrathoracic pressure. When the lung fully re-expands, it presses against the chest tube eyelets, causing tidaling to stop. Coupled with equal bilateral breath sounds, the cessation of tidaling strongly indicates the pneumothorax has resolved. Continuous bubbling (Option B) indicates an active air leak. Gentle tidaling (Option C) indicates the chest tube is patent but the lung is not yet fully re-expanded. Increased drainage (Option D) is unrelated to the resolution of a pneumothorax, which primarily involves air removal.
</details>

---

### Question 344
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client with a chest tube. Which action demonstrates the correct technique for this procedure?

A) Removing the old dressing using sterile gloves to prevent auto-contamination.
B) Securing the chest tube to the client's bed frame to prevent accidental dislodgement.
C) Cleansing the insertion site with normal saline starting from the outer edge inward.
D) Applying a sterile petroleum gauze dressing directly around the insertion site.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS
**Rationale:** When performing a chest tube dressing change, applying sterile petroleum gauze directly around the insertion site is the standard of care to maintain an airtight seal and prevent atmospheric air from entering the pleural space. Old dressings should be removed with clean gloves, while sterile gloves are donned for cleaning and applying the new dressing (Option A is incorrect). A chest tube should never be secured to the bed frame, as moving the bed could pull out the tube; it should be secured to the patient's body or bedding with enough slack to allow movement (Option B is incorrect). Cleansing should always occur from the insertion site (cleanest area) outward to prevent dragging microbes into the stoma (Option C is incorrect).
</details>

---

### Question 345
The registered nurse (RN) is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) to care for a client who has a chest tube connected to a traditional water-seal drainage system. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Evaluating the suction control chamber for appropriate bubbling.
B) Marking the drainage fluid level on the outside of the collection chamber at the end of the shift.
C) Emptying the chest tube drainage system and recording the volume.
D) Adding sterile water to the water-seal chamber to maintain the 2 cm level.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** DELEGATION
**Rationale:** Marking the drainage volume on the outside of the chest tube collection chamber and documenting the output is a standard data-gathering task that falls within the scope of practice for a UAP. Evaluating the system (Option A) requires nursing judgment and must be done by the RN. Traditional chest tube drainage systems are never emptied; the entire unit is replaced when full (Option C). Adding sterile water to maintain the water-seal mechanism (Option D) involves intervening with the therapeutic device and is the responsibility of the RN.
</details>
### Question 346
A client is recovering on the orthopedic unit three days after an open reduction and internal fixation of a femur fracture. Which assessment finding represents an early complication indicating the potential development of a pulmonary embolism?

A) Bradycardia accompanied by bounding peripheral pulses
B) A gradual increase in the production of thick, green sputum
C) Unexplained tachypnea and a sudden onset of apprehension 
D) The appearance of petechiae across the client's chest and axillae

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION (Identify early signs of a specific system failure)
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs

**Rationale:** 
Unexplained tachypnea (rapid breathing) and a sudden feeling of apprehension or impending doom are classic, early signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE). Because the client is immobile after a major long-bone surgery, they are at high risk for deep vein thrombosis (DVT) which can embolize to the lungs. Petechiae (Option D) is a classic sign of a *fat* embolism, which is also a risk with long bone fractures, but it is typically a late sign, not an early one. Bradycardia (Option A) is incorrect; tachycardia is expected with a PE due to hypoxia. A gradual increase in purulent sputum (Option B) suggests a developing respiratory infection like pneumonia, rather than the acute vascular event of a PE.
</details>

---

### Question 347
A client with a history of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is admitted for a severe exacerbation. Over the past 12 hours, the nurse monitors the client's arterial blood gas (ABG) trends. 
Initial ABGs: pH 7.32, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 65 mm Hg. 
Current ABGs: pH 7.28, PaCO2 68 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 55 mm Hg. 
What does this data trend indicate about the client's condition?

A) The kidneys are effectively compensating for the respiratory acidosis.
B) A mixed respiratory and metabolic acidosis is currently occurring.
C) The client's condition is stabilizing with expected chronic hypercapnia.
D) The client is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending failure.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS (Interpreting a shift in system-specific labs over time)
**System Focus:** ABG interpretation

**Rationale:** 
The trend shows a worsening uncompensated respiratory acidosis. The pH has dropped further (from 7.32 to 7.28), the PaCO2 is rising (from 58 to 68 mm Hg), and the PaO2 is falling (from 65 to 55 mm Hg). In a client with COPD, this acute worsening of hypercapnia and hypoxemia strongly suggests respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure, requiring immediate medical intervention (such as BiPAP or intubation). The kidneys are not effectively compensating (Option A), as the HCO3 remains static and the pH is dropping. The HCO3 is normal/slightly elevated, so there is no metabolic acidosis occurring (Option B). The condition is deteriorating, not stabilizing (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 348
A nurse is preparing to perform a dressing change for a client with a chest tube inserted for a pneumothorax. Which sequence of actions demonstrates the correct sterile technique for this procedure?

A) Remove the old dressing with clean gloves, don sterile gloves, apply petroleum gauze around the tube, and cover with sterile split-drain sponges.
B) Put on sterile gloves, remove the soiled dressing, cleanse the site with alcohol, and apply a transparent adhesive film.
C) Cleanse the site with sterile saline, apply a dry woven gauze directly over the insertion site, and tape all four sides firmly.
D) Disconnect the chest tube from the drainage system temporarily to slip a circular sterile dressing over the tube and secure.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS (The correct sequence for system-specific sterile procedures)
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** 
The standard sterile procedure for a chest tube dressing change requires the nurse to remove the soiled dressing with clean gloves to avoid contaminating the sterile gloves. After performing hand hygiene and donning sterile gloves, the nurse typically cleanses the site (usually with chlorhexidine or povidone-iodine, not alcohol), applies petroleum gauze around the insertion site to maintain an occlusive seal and prevent air leaks, and covers it with sterile split-drain sponges (drainage swabs) and regular gauze, secured with tape. Option B breaks sterility by removing the old dressing with sterile gloves. Option C fails to provide an occlusive seal (petroleum gauze). Option D is highly dangerous; the chest tube should never be disconnected from the drainage system to apply a dressing, as this compromises the closed system and can cause a tension pneumothorax.
</details>

---

### Question 349
A registered nurse (RN) and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) are providing care for a client with a resolving spontaneous pneumothorax and a closed-chest drainage system. Which task is appropriate for the RN to delegate to the UAP?

A) Evaluating the water seal chamber for continuous bubbling.
B) Assisting the client to a chair while keeping the drainage unit below chest level.
C) Re-taping the chest tube connections if they appear loose.
D) Drawing radial arterial blood gases to assess current respiratory status.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Lens:** DELEGATION (Assigning tasks for stable vs unstable system-specific patients)
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** 
Assisting a stable client with ambulation or transferring to a chair is a standard task within the scope of practice for a UAP. The RN must ensure the UAP is instructed to keep the chest drainage system upright and below the level of the client's chest to prevent fluid backflow. Evaluating the water seal chamber for bubbling requires nursing assessment (Option A). Re-taping or manipulating the chest tube connections is a nursing intervention (Option C). Drawing arterial blood gases is an invasive procedure requiring specialized training and falls outside the UAP's scope of practice (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 350
A client admitted three days ago with a pulmonary embolism has been receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the multi-day treatment plan has been effective?

A) The client's aPTT is 35 seconds and they require 4L of oxygen to maintain saturation above 90%.
B) The client expectorates blood-tinged sputum and reports sudden, sharp shortness of breath.
C) The client's ABGs show a pH of 7.40, PaO2 of 92 mm Hg on room air, and they report absent chest pain.
D) The D-dimer level remains significantly elevated and bilateral lower extremity pitting edema is present.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS (Determining if the multi-day system treatment plan is working)
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs & ABG

**Rationale:** 
The primary goals in treating a pulmonary embolism are to stabilize oxygenation, prevent further clot formation, and allow the body to lyse the existing clot, which relieves symptoms like pleuritic chest pain. A normal pH (7.40), a normal PaO2 on room air (92 mm Hg), and the absence of chest pain indicate that gas exchange has normalized and the client is recovering effectively. Option A shows an aPTT of 35 seconds, which is typically subtherapeutic for heparin therapy (normal control is usually 25-35, therapeutic is 1.5-2.5 times control), and the client still requires significant oxygen. Option B indicates a worsening condition or new embolus. Option D indicates ongoing issues and a potential unaddressed deep vein thrombosis (DVT) source.
</details>
### Question 351
A client is 2 days postoperative following a right total knee arthroplasty. The nurse is monitoring the client for postoperative complications. Which assessment finding should the nurse recognize as the *earliest* indication of a developing pulmonary embolism?

A) Development of a productive cough with thick, blood-tinged sputum.
B) New-onset anxiety, restlessness, and unexplained tachypnea.
C) Presence of petechiae scattered across the anterior chest and axilla.
D) Right-sided heart failure and visible jugular venous distention.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens: COMPLICATION RECOGNITION</strong><br>
Restlessness, tachypnea, and an unexplained feeling of anxiety or impending doom are often the earliest signs of impaired gas exchange and hypoxia associated with a pulmonary embolism. Hemoptysis (A) and right-sided heart failure or cor pulmonale (D) are typically later or more severe manifestations of a large pulmonary infarction. Petechiae (C) is a classic sign of a fat embolism, not a standard thromboembolic pulmonary embolism, and usually appears later.
</details>

---

### Question 352
The nurse is caring for a client admitted with an acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). Reviewing the client's arterial blood gas (ABG) results over the past 24 hours, the nurse notes the following trend:
*   Admission: pH 7.32, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 62 mm Hg.
*   Current: pH 7.24, PaCO2 70 mm Hg, HCO3 29 mEq/L, PaO2 55 mm Hg.

How should the nurse interpret this shift in the client's ABG data?

A) The client is compensating effectively for an acute respiratory alkalosis.
B) The client is developing a superimposed acute metabolic acidosis.
C) The client is experiencing worsening respiratory acidosis.
D) The client's renal buffer system has reversed the primary acid-base imbalance.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens: DATA TREND ANALYSIS</strong><br>
The trend shows a decreasing pH (progressive acidemia) and a significant increase in PaCO2 (worsening hypercapnia), indicating worsening respiratory acidosis. While the elevated HCO3 (28 and 29 mEq/L) indicates a chronic renal compensatory mechanism typical in COPD, the decreasing pH demonstrates that this compensation is failing to keep up with the acute accumulation of CO2. There is no evidence of metabolic acidosis (which would present with a dropping HCO3) or respiratory alkalosis (which would present with a dropping PaCO2).
</details>

---

### Question 353
A nurse is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) and a licensed practical nurse (LPN) on a respiratory step-down unit. Which task regarding a client with a continuous pleural chest tube for a spontaneous pneumothorax is appropriate to delegate to the UAP?

A) Documenting the specific volume and color of drainage in the collection chamber at the end of the shift.
B) Verifying that the fluid level in the water-seal chamber continues to fluctuate with the client's respirations.
C) Securing the chest tube connections with tape to ensure the closed drainage system remains intact.
D) Ambulating the client in the hallway while ensuring the drainage unit is maintained strictly below chest level.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens: DELEGATION</strong><br>
Ambulating a stable client and maintaining the physical positioning of the chest tube drainage system (keeping it upright and below the level of the chest) is within the UAP's scope of practice. Assessing the water seal for tidaling (B) and assessing or documenting drainage clinical characteristics (A) are nursing assessments that must be performed by a licensed nurse. Taping connections (C) is a nursing intervention to ensure system integrity and is not delegated to the UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 354
A client with a right-sided hemothorax has had a chest tube in place for three days connected to a wet suction drainage system. The nurse evaluates the effectiveness of the current treatment plan. Which finding indicates that the therapeutic goals for the chest tube are being met?

A) The fluid in the water-seal chamber has stopped tidaling, and breath sounds are present at the right lung base.
B) Continuous, vigorous bubbling is observed throughout the shift in the water-seal chamber.
C) The drainage in the collection chamber has changed from dark red to bright red with clots.
D) The water level in the suction control chamber fluctuates rapidly with each of the client's breaths.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens: EVALUATION OF GOALS</strong><br>
Cessation of tidaling (fluctuation) in the water-seal chamber, accompanied by the return of normal breath sounds, indicates that the lung has re-expanded and the hemothorax has resolved, meaning the therapeutic goal has been met. Continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber (B) indicates an ongoing air leak. A change to bright red drainage (C) indicates active, fresh bleeding, which is an adverse complication. Rapid fluctuation in the suction control chamber (D) does not indicate lung re-expansion; gentle bubbling should be present in a wet suction control chamber, while fluctuation with breaths occurs in the water-seal chamber.
</details>

---

### Question 355
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client's chest tube insertion site. After donning clean gloves and carefully removing the old dressing, what is the strictly correct *next* step in the sequence for this sterile procedure?

A) Apply a sterile occlusive petroleum gauze directly around the base of the chest tube.
B) Remove the clean gloves, perform hand hygiene, and don a pair of sterile gloves.
C) Cleanse the insertion site with sterile saline, working from the outside border toward the tube.
D) Secure the chest tube directly to the client's lateral chest wall using strong adhesive tape.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens: STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS</strong><br>
During a sterile dressing change, after removing the old dressing with clean gloves, the nurse must remove the contaminated clean gloves, perform hand hygiene, and then don sterile gloves before proceeding with the sterile cleansing and application of the new dressing materials. Applying the petroleum gauze (A) occurs later and requires sterile gloves. Cleansing the site (C) also requires sterile gloves and must be done from the cleanest area (closest to the tube) outward, not outside inward. Securing the tube (D) is the final step after the sterile dressing is applied.
</details>
### Question 356
The nurse is preparing to perform a dressing change on a client's chest tube site. Which sequence of actions demonstrates the correct sterile technique for this procedure?

A) Open sterile supplies, don clean gloves to clean the site with chlorhexidine, apply a sterile split-drain sponge, and secure completely with tape.
B) Don sterile gloves to remove the old dressing, clean the site with normal saline, apply a sterile petroleum gauze, and secure with paper tape.
C) Don clean gloves to remove the old dressing, switch to sterile gloves, clean the site with chlorhexidine, and apply a sterile split-drain sponge with an occlusive dressing.
D) Don clean gloves to remove the old dressing, clean the site with alcohol, apply standard sterile gauze, and leave one side untaped to vent air.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** The correct sterile technique for a chest tube dressing change involves using clean gloves to remove the contaminated old dressing to avoid soiling sterile gloves. The nurse then performs hand hygiene, dons sterile gloves, cleans the insertion site with an appropriate antiseptic (like chlorhexidine), and applies a sterile split-drain sponge followed by an occlusive dressing to prevent air from entering the pleural space. Option A is incorrect because clean gloves cannot be used to handle sterile cleaning supplies or dressings. Option B is incorrect because clean gloves should be used to remove the old dressing to preserve the sterility of the sterile gloves for the actual wound care. Option D describes a vented dressing, which is an emergency intervention for a tension pneumothorax, not a routine sterile chest tube dressing change.
</details>

---

### Question 357
A client is recovering on the surgical unit three days after a right total hip arthroplasty. Which combination of early clinical manifestations should immediately alert the nurse to the complication of a pulmonary embolism (PE)?

A) Bradycardia, elevated blood pressure, and inspiratory stridor.
B) Sudden onset of dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, and a feeling of impending doom.
C) Gradual increase in peripheral edema, bounding pulses, and jugular vein distention.
D) Low-grade fever, productive cough with thick green sputum, and expiratory wheezing.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs

**Rationale:** A pulmonary embolism (PE) is a medical emergency often originating from a deep vein thrombosis (DVT) after major orthopedic surgeries. The classic triad of early clinical manifestations includes a sudden onset of dyspnea (shortness of breath), pleuritic chest pain (sharp pain upon inspiration), and a profound feeling of apprehension or impending doom. Option A describes signs more closely associated with an upper airway obstruction (stridor) or increased intracranial pressure (Cushing's triad features). Option C describes signs of right-sided heart failure or fluid volume overload. Option D describes manifestations of a respiratory infection, such as pneumonia or bronchitis. 
</details>

---

### Question 358
A client with a severe asthma exacerbation has been receiving continuous albuterol nebulizer treatments. The nurse reviews the client's arterial blood gas (ABG) trends over the past four hours:

- **0800:** pH 7.48, PaCO2 30 mm Hg, PaO2 65 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L
- **1200:** pH 7.37, PaCO2 44 mm Hg, PaO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L

How should the nurse interpret this trend?

A) The client is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.
B) The client is developing metabolic acidosis due to prolonged hypoxemia.
C) The client is improving because the pH has returned to the normal physiological range.
D) The client is compensating effectively with increased renal excretion of bicarbonate.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
**System Focus:** ABG interpretation

**Rationale:** During an initial asthma attack, the client hyperventilates, blowing off CO2 and creating a state of respiratory alkalosis (0800 ABG: high pH, low CO2). As the attack progresses and the client's respiratory muscles begin to fatigue, they can no longer maintain the hyperventilation. Carbon dioxide begins to accumulate, causing the PaCO2 to rise and the pH to drop back into the "normal" range (1200 ABG: normal pH, normal CO2, worsening PaO2). In the context of a severe asthma attack, a "normalizing" PaCO2 and pH alongside worsening hypoxemia is a highly ominous sign of respiratory muscle exhaustion and impending acute respiratory failure. It does not indicate improvement (Option C) or metabolic acidosis (Option B). Renal compensation takes days to occur and is not reflected here (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 359
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a spontaneous right-sided pneumothorax. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the multi-day treatment plan. Which assessment finding best indicates that the treatment goals are being met and the chest tube may be ready for removal?

A) Continuous vigorous bubbling is observed in the water-seal chamber during inspiration and expiration.
B) There is a sudden collection of 150 mL of bright red drainage in the collection chamber over the last hour.
C) The water level in the suction control chamber drops significantly when the client takes a deep breath.
D) A chest x-ray shows full lung expansion, and fluctuation (tidaling) in the water-seal chamber has ceased without bubbling.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** The goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is to evacuate air from the pleural space and facilitate lung re-expansion. When the lung fully re-expands and presses against the chest wall, tidaling (fluctuation of fluid in the water-seal chamber with respirations) will cease. The absence of bubbling indicates that the air leak has successfully resolved. Therefore, full expansion on x-ray combined with a cessation of tidaling and bubbling means the goal has been met and the tube can be removed. Option A indicates an active, ongoing air leak. Option B indicates a new hemorrhage (hemothorax) or active bleeding, which is a complication. Option C simply indicates evaporation or a change in suction pressure, not lung recovery.
</details>

---

### Question 360
The charge nurse on a respiratory step-down unit is assigning tasks for the upcoming shift. Which task is most appropriate to delegate to the Licensed Practical Nurse (LPN)?

A) Interpreting the morning arterial blood gas (ABG) results for a client on continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP).
B) Administering a scheduled subcutaneous dose of enoxaparin to a stable client recovering from a pulmonary embolism.
C) Emptying the collection chamber of a chest tube and recording the output for a client with a recovering hemothorax.
D) Teaching a client about the importance of early ambulation to prevent a recurrent pulmonary embolism.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** DELEGATION
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism / ABGs / Chest Tubes

**Rationale:** The LPN's scope of practice includes administering medications (such as subcutaneous enoxaparin) to stable clients. Option A is incorrect because analyzing and interpreting diagnostic data, such as ABGs, is an advanced skill that requires RN clinical judgment. Option C is incorrect because a chest tube drainage system is a closed system; the collection chamber is never "emptied." If the chamber is full, the RN must change the entire system. Option D is incorrect because initial or complex discharge teaching regarding disease prevention and management is exclusively the responsibility of the Registered Nurse.
</details>
### Question 361
A nurse is monitoring a client who has been on prolonged bedrest following major pelvic surgery. Which cluster of early clinical manifestations should the nurse recognize as indicative of a developing pulmonary embolism (PE)?

A) Bradycardia, elevated temperature, and hemoptysis
B) Restlessness, sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain, and tachypnea
C) Unilateral leg swelling, localized warmth, and a weak pedal pulse
D) Productive cough with thick green sputum and a low-grade fever

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Restlessness, tachypnea, and sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain are hallmark early signs of a pulmonary embolism, which causes a sudden disruption in pulmonary perfusion leading to hypoxemia. Hemoptysis (Option A) can occur but is typically a later or less common sign, and PE is associated with tachycardia, not bradycardia. Unilateral leg swelling and warmth (Option C) are signs of a deep vein thrombosis (DVT), which is a precursor to a PE but not a sign of the PE itself. A productive cough with green sputum (Option D) is indicative of a respiratory infection such as pneumonia.
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
</details>

---

### Question 362
A nurse is reviewing the arterial blood gas (ABG) trends over the last 24 hours for a client with an exacerbation of Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD). 
- Morning ABG: pH 7.31, PaCO2 58 mmHg, HCO3 28 mEq/L
- Evening ABG: pH 7.36, PaCO2 56 mmHg, HCO3 30 mEq/L

How should the nurse interpret the shift in these laboratory values?

A) The client has developed a mixed acid-base disorder.
B) The client is showing signs of uncompensated metabolic alkalosis.
C) The client's kidneys are compensating for the respiratory acidosis.
D) The client is experiencing acute respiratory failure requiring intubation.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** The morning ABG indicates partially compensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, slightly elevated HCO3). By the evening, the pH has returned to the normal range (7.35-7.45), while the PaCO2 remains elevated and the HCO3 has increased further to 30 mEq/L. This trend indicates that the kidneys are actively retaining bicarbonate (HCO3) to buffer the acidic environment caused by the elevated CO2. Because the pH is now within normal limits, this represents fully compensated respiratory acidosis. It does not indicate a mixed disorder, metabolic alkalosis, or a need for immediate intubation given the improving pH.
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
</details>

---

### Question 363
A registered nurse (RN) is working with a licensed practical nurse (LPN) on a progressive care respiratory unit. Which task related to chest tube management can the RN safely delegate to the LPN?

A) Evaluating the continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber.
B) Teaching the client how to splint the chest tube site when coughing.
C) Clamping the chest tube to assess the client's readiness for tube removal.
D) Changing the sterile occlusive dressing around the chest tube site of a stable client.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** Changing a sterile dressing on a stable client is a procedure that falls within the scope of practice for an LPN. Evaluating the meaning of bubbling in the water-seal chamber requires advanced nursing assessment (Option A). Initial teaching regarding splinting must be done by the RN (Option B), though the LPN can reinforce it. Clamping a chest tube is a high-risk procedure that requires close RN assessment for tension pneumothorax and is not delegated to an LPN (Option C).
**Lens:** Delegation
</details>

---

### Question 364
The nurse is preparing to perform a sterile dressing change for a client with a pleural chest tube. Which sequence of actions represents the correct sterile technique for this procedure?

A) Remove the old dressing with clean gloves, don sterile gloves, cleanse the site, apply a sterile pre-split drain sponge, and secure with occlusive tape.
B) Put on sterile gloves, remove the soiled dressing, wipe the insertion site with alcohol, and apply an intact 4x4 gauze directly over the tube.
C) Cleanse the insertion site using bare hands and sterile forceps, manually cut a sterile gauze to fit around the tube, and secure it with hypoallergenic tape.
D) Remove the old dressing with clean gloves, apply sterile petroleum gauze to the site, and cover tightly with a transparent film dressing without using any split gauze.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** The correct technique involves removing the contaminated dressing with clean gloves, performing hand hygiene, donning sterile gloves, cleansing the site (typically with chlorhexidine), and using a sterile *pre-split* drain sponge. Option C is incorrect because nurses should never manually cut a standard sterile gauze to fit around a chest tube; cutting gauze leaves frayed threads that can enter the stoma and act as a nidus for infection. Option B improperly uses sterile gloves to remove a dirty dressing and uses intact gauze rather than split gauze. Option D omits the cleansing step and incorrectly omits the standard absorbent split drain sponge.
**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills
</details>

---

### Question 365
A client has been receiving a continuous heparin infusion and supplemental oxygen for a pulmonary embolism for the past three days. Which outcome best indicates to the nurse that the collaborative multi-day treatment plan has been effective?

A) The client's activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) is 32 seconds and D-dimer is elevated.
B) The client's PaO2 is 88 mmHg on room air and they report no shortness of breath during ambulation.
C) The client requires 4 L/min of oxygen via nasal cannula to maintain an SpO2 of 92%.
D) The client reports a pain level of 5/10 upon deep inspiration and coughing.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** The primary goals in treating a pulmonary embolism are to restore adequate pulmonary perfusion, resolve hypoxemia, and prevent further clot formation. A PaO2 of 88 mmHg on room air with no shortness of breath during exertion demonstrates that gas exchange has normalized and the client is tolerating activity without supplemental oxygen, indicating treatment success. An aPTT of 32 seconds (Option A) is subtherapeutic for a client on a heparin drip (normal is 25-35 seconds; therapeutic is typically 1.5 to 2.5 times the baseline). Requiring 4 L/min of oxygen (Option C) and experiencing continued pleuritic pain (Option D) indicate that the PE symptoms have not fully resolved.
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
</details>
### Question 366
A patient is recovering from a right total knee arthroplasty on a surgical unit. During a routine assessment on postoperative day 3, the nurse notes subtle changes in the client's baseline condition. Which combination of clinical manifestations should the nurse identify as an early complication of a developing pulmonary embolism?

A) Unexplained restlessness, mild tachycardia, and a dry cough.
B) Sudden onset of petechiae on the chest, bradypnea, and dependent edema.
C) Bounding peripheral pulses, lethargy, and an oxygen saturation of 94% on room air.
D) Hemoptysis, profound hypotension, and tracheal deviation to the right.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
Unexplained restlessness (often due to mild, early hypoxia), mild tachycardia, and tachypnea or a dry cough are early, often subtle, signs of a developing pulmonary embolism (PE). Recognizing these signs early allows for prompt intervention before the embolism worsens. Option B describes signs more aligned with a fat embolism (petechiae) or right-sided heart failure (edema), while bradypnea is not typical of a PE. Option C describes signs uncharacteristic of a PE; a PE typically presents with a rapid heart rate, not bounding pulses or primary lethargy early on. Option D describes late, massive signs of a pulmonary embolism or a tension pneumothorax. 
</details>

---

### Question 367
A client with an acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) has had serial arterial blood gases (ABGs) drawn over the last 12 hours to monitor the effectiveness of BiPAP therapy. 
0800: pH 7.31, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 62 mm Hg.
2000: pH 7.26, PaCO2 66 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 55 mm Hg.
Which conclusion should the nurse draw regarding the client's current respiratory status based on these trends?

A) The client is developing compensated respiratory acidosis.
B) The client is experiencing worsening uncompensated respiratory acidosis.
C) The client is successfully responding to the prescribed BiPAP therapy.
D) The client is transitioning into a mixed metabolic and respiratory acidosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
The ABG results demonstrate a worsening trend. The normal pH is 7.35–7.45, and the normal PaCO2 is 35–45 mm Hg. Over 12 hours, the pH dropped further below the normal range (from 7.31 to 7.26), indicating worsening acidosis. The PaCO2 increased (from 58 to 66 mm Hg), confirming a respiratory cause (hypercapnia). Because the pH remains abnormal and is decreasing, the condition is uncompensated and deteriorating. The HCO3 is stable at 28 mEq/L, ruling out a mixed metabolic issue (Option D). Since the values are worsening, the client is not responding successfully to the BiPAP therapy (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 368
A nurse is working with an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) to care for a client who has a chest tube connected to a traditional water-seal drainage system for a spontaneous pneumothorax. Which specific task is appropriate for the nurse to delegate to the UAP?

A) Milking the chest tube tubing to prevent clot formation in the dependent loops.
B) Assessing the water-seal chamber for the presence of continuous bubbling.
C) Assisting the client with ambulation while keeping the drainage unit below chest level.
D) Securing the chest tube connection sites with waterproof tape to prevent air leaks.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Delegation
Delegation of tasks to a UAP must involve interventions that do not require nursing judgment, assessment, or teaching. Assisting a stable patient with ambulation is within the UAP's scope of practice, provided they are instructed on the safety parameter to keep the drainage system upright and below the level of the patient's chest. Assessing for bubbling (Option B) requires nursing judgment to determine if an air leak is present. Securing connections (Option D) requires assessment of the system's integrity. Milking the tubing (Option A) is generally contraindicated without a specific provider order as it can cause dangerously high intrathoracic pressure, and it requires clinical judgment.
</details>

---

### Question 369
A client was admitted three days ago with a spontaneous right-sided pneumothorax and had a chest tube placed to water-seal drainage. The nurse is reviewing the client's progress to determine if the multi-day treatment plan is effective. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the primary goals of the treatment plan have been achieved and the tube may be ready for removal?

A) There is constant, gentle bubbling present in the water-seal chamber.
B) The fluid in the water-seal chamber fluctuates evenly during respiration.
C) The drainage collection chamber shows 50 mL of serosanguinous fluid over 24 hours.
D) The client's breath sounds are clear and equal bilaterally.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
For a pneumothorax (air in the pleural space), the primary goal of chest tube therapy is lung re-expansion. Clear and equal bilateral breath sounds indicate that the right lung has successfully re-expanded. Fluctuation (tidaling) in the water-seal chamber (Option B) indicates that the pneumothorax is still present and the tube is still actively communicating with the pleural space. Constant bubbling (Option A) indicates an active air leak. While low drainage (Option C) is a positive sign for a hemothorax, the primary evaluation criterion for a pneumothorax resolving is the return of normal breath sounds and the cessation of tidaling.
</details>

---

### Question 370
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client's chest tube insertion site. After removing the old dressing, performing hand hygiene, and donning sterile gloves, what is the correct sequence of actions the nurse should take to properly secure the site?

A) Clean the site with chlorhexidine, apply petroleum gauze around the tube, place a precut drain sponge, and seal with an occlusive dressing.
B) Apply a precut drain sponge, clean the site with chlorhexidine, and cover the entire site with a large occlusive dressing.
C) Clean the site with sterile saline, apply a dry gauze pad, place petroleum gauze over the dry pad, and seal with waterproof tape.
D) Place a precut drain sponge around the tube, apply petroleum gauze over the sponge, clean the outer area with chlorhexidine, and tape the edges securely.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills
The correct sterile technique for a chest tube dressing change involves a specific sequence to prevent infection and air leaks. First, the insertion site must be cleaned with an appropriate antiseptic (e.g., chlorhexidine) and allowed to dry. Second, an airtight seal must be created at the insertion site, typically using sterile petroleum gauze wrapped directly around the tube at the skin level to prevent air from entering the pleural space. Third, a precut drain sponge (slit gauze) is placed around the tube to absorb any drainage. Finally, the entire site is covered with an occlusive, sterile dressing (such as a large border sponge or transparent film) securely taped to maintain the seal.
</details>
### Question 371
A client is 2 days post-operative from a total hip arthroplasty. The client suddenly reports sharp, localized pain in the right side of the chest that worsens upon deep inspiration. Which additional finding should the nurse recognize as an early clinical complication indicating a developing pulmonary embolism?

A. Bilateral wheezing and prolonged expiratory phase. 
B. Bradycardia and elevated systolic blood pressure.
C. Unexplained restlessness and tachypnea.
D. Productive cough with thick, green sputum.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism Signs

Unexplained restlessness, anxiety, and tachypnea are classic early signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE), driven by sudden hypoxemia as a blood clot impairs pulmonary perfusion. Pleuritic chest pain (sharp pain on inspiration) is also a hallmark symptom. Wheezing (Option A) and purulent sputum (Option D) are more commonly associated with asthma, COPD, or respiratory infections like pneumonia. A PE typically causes tachycardia and potentially hypotension, not bradycardia and hypertension (Option B). 
</details>

---

### Question 372
The nurse is reviewing the arterial blood gas (ABG) trend of a client admitted with a severe acute asthma exacerbation. Over the last 4 hours, the client's ABGs have shifted from a pH of 7.52, PaCO2 28 mm Hg, and PaO2 75 mm Hg to a pH of 7.36, PaCO2 42 mm Hg, and PaO2 68 mm Hg. How should the nurse interpret this data trend?

A. The client's kidneys have fully compensated for the initial respiratory alkalosis.
B. The client is showing signs of respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.
C. The client is developing a primary metabolic acidosis from prolonged tissue hypoxia.
D. The client is responding well to bronchodilator therapy as the acid-base balance normalizes.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** ABG Interpretation

During an acute asthma attack, clients initially hyperventilate, leading to respiratory alkalosis (low PaCO2, high pH). If the airway obstruction persists, the respiratory muscles begin to fatigue. As the client tires, they can no longer maintain hyperventilation, causing the PaCO2 to rise back into the "normal" range (35-45 mm Hg) and the pH to drop toward normal or acidic levels. In the context of an ongoing asthma attack, a "normalizing" PaCO2 is a highly dangerous sign of impending respiratory failure. It does not indicate improvement (Option D), renal compensation (Option A), or metabolic acidosis (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 373
A client has been treated with a chest tube for a right-sided pneumothorax for the past three days. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the primary therapeutic goal of the chest tube therapy has been successfully achieved?

A. Fluctuation (tidaling) in the water-seal chamber has ceased, and bilateral breath sounds are equal.
B. Gentle, continuous bubbling is observed within the water-seal chamber.
C. The drainage in the collection chamber has transitioned from sanguineous to serous.
D. The client reports an absence of pain during deep inspiration and coughing exercises.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**System Focus:** Chest Tube Management

The primary goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is to remove air from the pleural space and allow the lung to re-expand. When the lung fully re-expands, it blocks the eyelets of the chest tube, causing the fluctuation (tidaling) in the water-seal chamber to stop. This, combined with equal, clear breath sounds bilaterally, indicates the goal of re-expansion has been met. Continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber (Option B) indicates an ongoing air leak. A change in drainage color (Option C) or absence of pain (Option D) does not directly indicate lung re-expansion.
</details>

---

### Question 374
The registered nurse (RN) is planning care for a client recovering from a pulmonary embolism who also has a newly placed chest tube for a pleural effusion. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Strip the chest tube tubing to facilitate the drainage of heavy blood clots.
B. Secure the chest tube connections with tape to prevent accidental disconnection.
C. Monitor the water-seal chamber for the presence of continuous or intermittent bubbling.
D. Ambulate the client in the room while keeping the drainage system below chest level.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Delegation
**System Focus:** Chest Tube Management

Ambulation is a routine task that falls within the scope of practice for a UAP, provided they are instructed on safety measures such as keeping the chest drainage system upright and below the level of the client's chest. Stripping the chest tube (Option A) is rarely recommended due to the dangerous increase in intrathoracic pressure it causes, and any tube manipulation must be done by the RN. Securing connections (Option B) and assessing the water-seal chamber (Option C) require nursing judgment and assessment skills, which cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 375
The nurse is preparing to perform a sterile dressing change on a client's chest tube insertion site. Which action by the nurse demonstrates the correct sequence and technique for this procedure?

A. Placing sterile petroleum gauze directly around the insertion site to maintain an airtight seal.
B. Donning clean gloves to apply the new sterile split-drain sponge and secure the tape.
C. Cleansing the skin by wiping from the outer border of the chest wall inward to the tube.
D. Instructing the client to pant rapidly during the removal of the old dressing.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills
**System Focus:** Chest Tube Management

When changing a chest tube dressing, sterile petroleum gauze is commonly wrapped tightly around the insertion site to maintain an airtight seal, preventing atmospheric air from entering the pleural space and causing a pneumothorax. Sterile gloves, not clean gloves, must be used to apply the new sterile dressing (Option B). Cleansing should occur in a circular motion moving from the insertion site outward (clean to dirty), not inward (Option C). If the client is asked to alter their breathing (usually during tube removal, not necessarily a dressing change), they would perform the Valsalva maneuver (bearing down), not panting (Option D).
</details>
### Question 376
A client is recovering from a right total knee arthroplasty performed two days ago. While conducting a routine assessment, the nurse notes a change in the client's vital signs and behavior. Which clinical manifestation should the nurse recognize as the *earliest* indication of a developing pulmonary embolism?

A) Development of a productive cough with pink, frothy sputum.
B) Sudden onset of unexplained tachycardia and tachypnea.
C) Diffuse crackles auscultated throughout bilateral lung bases.
D) Unilateral leg swelling, localized warmth, and erythema.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Sudden onset of unexplained tachycardia and tachypnea.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus (Complication Recognition):** Recognizing the early signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE) is critical for prompt intervention. 
- A sudden onset of tachypnea, tachycardia, and dyspnea are classic early, compensatory signs of hypoxia caused by a PE. 
- Option A is incorrect because pink, frothy sputum is a classic sign of pulmonary edema or severe heart failure, not typically an early sign of a PE. 
- Option C indicates fluid volume overload or atelectasis, not a primary indication of a PE. 
- Option D describes a deep vein thrombosis (DVT). While a DVT is the primary cause of a PE, these localized leg symptoms indicate the source, not the actual systemic pulmonary complication.
</details>

---

### Question 377
A nurse is monitoring the arterial blood gas (ABG) trends for a client admitted with a severe asthma exacerbation. The baseline ABG on admission showed: pH 7.48, PaCO2 30 mm Hg, and HCO3 24 mEq/L. Four hours later, the repeat ABG shows: pH 7.35, PaCO2 42 mm Hg, and HCO3 24 mEq/L. The client remains visibly dyspneic with intercostal retractions. How should the nurse interpret this trend?

A) The client is developing a metabolic acidosis secondary to medication therapy.
B) The client is compensating effectively and their respiratory status is improving.
C) The client's respiratory muscle fatigue is causing a shift toward respiratory failure.
D) The client is hyperventilating, leading to an acute respiratory alkalosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) The client's respiratory muscle fatigue is causing a shift toward respiratory failure.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus (Data Trend Analysis):** Interpreting a shift in ABG values over time is crucial for respiratory failure prevention.
- Initially, the client was in respiratory alkalosis (low PaCO2) due to hyperventilation from asthma-induced hypoxia. The second ABG shows a "normal" PaCO2 (42 mm Hg). However, because the client is still visibly dyspneic and working hard to breathe, this "normalization" of PaCO2 is an ominous sign. It indicates that respiratory muscles are fatiguing and the client is losing the ability to hyperventilate, leading to carbon dioxide retention and impending respiratory failure.
- Option A is incorrect; the bicarbonate is normal, indicating no metabolic component.
- Option B is incorrect because a "normal" ABG in a severely symptomatic asthmatic is a danger sign, not an improvement.
- Option D describes the baseline ABG (respiratory alkalosis), but the trend shows a shift away from this state toward acidosis.
</details>

---

### Question 378
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to treat a right-sided spontaneous pneumothorax. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the treatment plan. Which finding indicates that the primary goal of chest tube therapy has been successfully met?

A) Tidaling in the water seal chamber has ceased and breath sounds are equal bilaterally.
B) The client requires 4 L/min of oxygen via nasal cannula to maintain an SpO2 of 94%.
C) Continuous gentle bubbling is observed in the water seal chamber.
D) The suction control chamber exhibits steady fluid fluctuation during inspiration.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Tidaling in the water seal chamber has ceased and breath sounds are equal bilaterally.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus (Evaluation of Goals):** Determining if the chest tube has successfully resolved the pneumothorax.
- The goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is to evacuate air and re-expand the lung. When tidaling (fluctuation of water with respiration) in the water seal chamber stops completely *and* breath sounds have returned bilaterally, it strongly indicates that the lung has fully re-expanded and the pleural space is healed.
- Option B indicates an ongoing oxygenation issue, meaning respiratory goals are not fully met.
- Option C indicates an active air leak in the system or the pleural space, which means the pneumothorax is not yet resolved.
- Option D describes normal functioning of the suction control chamber (if using a wet suction system), but it does not indicate that the therapeutic goal (lung re-expansion) has been met.
</details>

---

### Question 379
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client's chest tube insertion site. Which action demonstrates the correct technique for this procedure?

A) Cleansing the insertion site in a circular motion moving from the outer skin inward toward the tube.
B) Temporarily clamping the chest tube at the insertion site while the new dressing is applied.
C) Removing the soiled dressing with sterile gloves to maintain the sterility of the field.
D) Placing a sterile petroleum gauze directly around the insertion site before applying the split-drain sponges.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Placing a sterile petroleum gauze directly around the insertion site before applying the split-drain sponges.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus (Sterile Technique/Skills):** Knowing the correct sequence and materials for chest tube management.
- A sterile petroleum (petrolatum) gauze is placed directly around the chest tube at the insertion site to create an airtight seal, preventing atmospheric air from entering the pleural space and causing a tension pneumothorax.
- Option A is incorrect because cleansing should always occur from the inner site (cleanest area) moving outward (dirtiest area).
- Option B is incorrect because chest tubes should never be clamped during a routine dressing change; clamping can cause an accumulation of air or fluid in the pleural space, leading to a tension pneumothorax.
- Option C is incorrect; clean gloves are used to remove the old dressing, and then the nurse switches to sterile gloves to clean the site and apply the new dressing.
</details>

---

### Question 380
A client is being discharged on oral warfarin therapy following hospitalization for a pulmonary embolism. The nurse is providing complex discharge teaching regarding lifestyle modifications and medication management. Which statement by the client indicates a *need for further teaching*?

A) "I need to completely eliminate leafy green vegetables from my diet to keep my blood thin."
B) "I will use an electric razor instead of a manual blade to shave my face."
C) "I should get up and walk around the cabin every few hours when taking long flights."
D) "I will notify my dentist that I am taking warfarin before having any teeth cleaned or extracted."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "I need to completely eliminate leafy green vegetables from my diet to keep my blood thin."

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens Focus (Complex Teaching):** Managing chronic illness, lifestyle changes, and medications post-pulmonary embolism.
- The client needs further teaching regarding Option A. Clients on warfarin do not need to eliminate Vitamin K-rich foods (like leafy greens) from their diet; instead, they must maintain a *consistent* intake of these foods. Sudden increases or decreases in Vitamin K can wildly alter the INR and the drug's effectiveness.
- Option B is a correct statement; using an electric razor prevents cuts and bleeding, which is crucial for a patient on anticoagulants.
- Option C is a correct statement; frequent ambulation during long periods of immobility prevents venous stasis, reducing the risk of recurrent DVTs and subsequent pulmonary emboli.
- Option D is a correct statement; healthcare providers and dentists must be informed of anticoagulant use due to the high risk of procedural bleeding.
</details>
### Question 381
A client recovering from an open reduction and internal fixation of the right femur suddenly reports pleuritic chest pain. Which additional finding most strongly suggests the development of a pulmonary embolism?

A. Bilateral expiratory wheezes
B. Blood-tinged sputum and tachycardia
C. Bradycardia and bounding peripheral pulses
D. Diffuse crackles in the lung bases

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION (Identify early signs of a specific system failure)
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism signs

**Rationale:** 
Hemoptysis (blood-tinged sputum), sudden pleuritic chest pain, dyspnea, and tachycardia are classic early signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE), a severe complication following orthopedic surgeries of the lower extremities. Bilateral expiratory wheezes (A) typically indicate bronchospasm, such as in asthma. Bradycardia and bounding pulses (C) do not align with PE, which usually triggers a sympathetic response leading to tachycardia. Diffuse crackles in the lung bases (D) are more indicative of fluid volume overload or congestive heart failure rather than an acute thromboembolic event.
</details>

---

### Question 382
A nurse is reviewing the arterial blood gas (ABG) trends for a client admitted with an acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). 
Day 1: pH 7.28, PaCO2 62 mm Hg, HCO3 26 mEq/L, PaO2 58 mm Hg. 
Day 3: pH 7.36, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 32 mEq/L, PaO2 65 mm Hg. 
What does the trend in these ABG results indicate?

A. The client has developed uncompensated metabolic alkalosis.
B. The client's condition is deteriorating into acute respiratory failure.
C. The kidneys are compensating for the chronic respiratory acidosis.
D. The client is hyperventilating, leading to respiratory alkalosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS (Interpreting a shift in system-specific labs over time)
**System Focus:** ABG interpretation

**Rationale:** 
On Day 1, the client exhibited uncompensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, normal HCO3). By Day 3, the pH has returned to the normal range (7.35–7.45), while the PaCO2 remains elevated. However, the bicarbonate (HCO3) level has increased to 32 mEq/L. This trend indicates that the kidneys are retaining bicarbonate to buffer the elevated carbon dioxide, successfully achieving fully compensated respiratory acidosis. The client is not deteriorating (B); rather, their acid-base balance is stabilizing. The primary issue remains respiratory, not metabolic alkalosis (A) or respiratory alkalosis (D).
</details>

---

### Question 383
A nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client with a chest tube connected to a water-seal drainage system. Which action indicates the correct sequence and technique for this procedure?

A. Apply a precut sterile drain sponge around the tube and secure it with an occlusive dressing.
B. Clamp the chest tube close to the insertion site before removing the soiled dressing.
C. Cleanse the site from the outside of the drain site moving inward toward the tube.
D. Leave the old dressing in place while donning sterile gloves to maintain asepsis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS (The correct sequence for system-specific sterile procedures)
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** 
During a sterile chest tube dressing change, applying a precut sterile drain sponge (split sponge) around the insertion site and securing it with an occlusive dressing (such as petroleum gauze or airtight tape) prevents atmospheric air from entering the pleural space. Clamping the chest tube (B) is contraindicated for routine dressing changes as it can cause a tension pneumothorax. Cleansing should always occur from the insertion site moving outward in a circular motion to avoid bringing surface bacteria toward the wound (C). The old dressing must be removed with clean gloves before setting up the sterile field and donning sterile gloves (D).
</details>

---

### Question 384
A client is preparing for discharge after being treated for a pulmonary embolism. The client will be going home on warfarin therapy. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the complex lifestyle modifications required?

A. "I should stop taking my medication if I notice bruising on my arms."
B. "I will need to have my arterial blood gases checked monthly."
C. "I must limit my fluid intake to prevent swelling in my legs."
D. "I will eat a consistent amount of green leafy vegetables every week."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING (Managing chronic system-related illness and lifestyle changes)
**System Focus:** Pulmonary Embolism / Anticoagulant Management

**Rationale:** 
Warfarin is a Vitamin K antagonist.
