Clients do not need to completely avoid Vitamin K-rich foods (like green leafy vegetables), but they must maintain a consistent dietary intake to ensure their INR levels remain stable and therapeutic. Abruptly stopping warfarin due to minor bruising (A) is dangerous; bruising is a common side effect, but the provider should be notified if bleeding is severe. Monitoring for warfarin involves checking PT/INR, not arterial blood gases (B). Restricting fluids (C) is inappropriate; adequate hydration is necessary to prevent hemoconcentration and venous stasis, which increase the risk of recurrent VTE.
</details>

---

### Question 385
A client with a right-sided pneumothorax has had a chest tube in place connected to a water-seal drainage system for three days. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the treatment plan. Which finding indicates that the primary goal of chest tube therapy has been achieved?

A. Gentle, continuous bubbling is observed in the water-seal chamber.
B. There is no fluctuation (tidaling) in the water-seal chamber, and clear breath sounds are present at the right apex.
C. The client's arterial blood gas shows a PaO2 of 88 mm Hg on room air.
D. The drainage in the collection chamber has shifted from serosanguineous to purely serous.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS (Determining if the multi-day system treatment plan is working)
**System Focus:** Chest Tube management

**Rationale:** 
The primary goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is lung re-expansion. Tidaling (fluctuation of water with respirations) occurs when the lung is collapsed and air is actively moving in the pleural space. When the lung fully re-expands, it presses against the chest tube eyelets, stopping the tidaling. The presence of clear breath sounds in the previously affected area confirms this re-expansion. Continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber (A) indicates an active air leak, meaning the pneumothorax is not resolved. Normal PaO2 (C) and serous drainage (D) are positive signs of recovery but do not definitively prove the mechanical re-expansion of the collapsed lung.
</details>
### Question 386
A patient is recovering on the surgical unit three days after a right total knee replacement. The nurse is assessing the patient after they report feeling "strange" and out of breath. Using the lens of **Complication Recognition**, which combination of early signs most strongly indicates the development of a pulmonary embolism?

A) Bradypnea, bounding peripheral pulses, and localized calf warmth.
B) Sudden onset dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, and apprehension.
C) Gradual onset of a productive cough, high fever, and bronchial breath sounds.
D) Tracheal deviation, absent breath sounds on the right, and severe hypotension.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** A pulmonary embolism (PE) is a life-threatening complication, especially in postoperative patients with limited mobility. The classic *early* presentation of a PE includes the triad of sudden onset dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain (pain on inspiration), and a feeling of apprehension or impending doom. Tachycardia and tachypnea are also common early signs.
*Option A* is incorrect because a PE typically causes tachypnea (rapid breathing) and tachycardia, not bradypnea. Calf warmth is a sign of a deep vein thrombosis (DVT), which is a precursor, but does not indicate the embolus has reached the lungs.
*Option C* describes signs of pneumonia, not a pulmonary embolism.
*Option D* describes the classic late signs of a tension pneumothorax.
</details>

---

### Question 387
A patient admitted with an acute asthma exacerbation has serial arterial blood gases (ABGs) drawn to monitor their respiratory status. The initial ABG at 0800 showed: pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L, PaO2 85 mmHg. The repeat ABG at 1100 shows: pH 7.35, PaCO2 45 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L, PaO2 65 mmHg. Using the lens of **Data Trend Analysis**, how should the nurse interpret this shift?

A) The patient is improving as the pH and PaCO2 levels have returned to normal ranges.
B) The patient is developing metabolic acidosis due to increased lactic acid from the work of breathing.
C) The patient is fatiguing and at high risk for impending acute respiratory failure.
D) The patient is hyperventilating due to increased anxiety and requires a sedative.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** In an acute asthma exacerbation, patients initially hyperventilate, "blowing off" CO2 and causing respiratory alkalosis (seen in the 0800 ABG: high pH, low PaCO2). However, as the patient fatigues, they can no longer maintain the increased work of breathing, leading to hypoventilation. The PaCO2 begins to rise and the pH drops back toward normal. This phenomenon is known as "pseudonormalization." Coupled with the dropping PaO2 (hypoxemia), this trend is an ominous sign of respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.
*Option A* is incorrect because the "normal" values are actually a sign of deterioration in this context.
*Option B* is incorrect because the primary shift is respiratory, not metabolic; the HCO3 remains normal.
*Option D* is incorrect because hyperventilation would cause the PaCO2 to decrease further, not increase to 45 mmHg. Furthermore, sedatives are generally contraindicated in asthma exacerbations as they depress respiratory drive.
</details>

---

### Question 388
A patient has had a closed-chest drainage system (chest tube) in place for three days to treat a right-sided spontaneous pneumothorax. Using the lens of **Evaluation of Goals**, which assessment finding best indicates that the treatment plan is successful and the chest tube may be ready for removal?

A) Continuous, vigorous bubbling is noted in the water seal chamber.
B) Complete cessation of tidaling in the water seal chamber with a fully re-expanded lung on x-ray.
C) Symmetrical chest expansion with dullness to percussion noted on the right side.
D) Drainage of 150 mL of dark red blood into the collection chamber over the past 8 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** The goal of a chest tube for a pneumothorax is to remove air from the pleural space and allow the lung to re-expand. Tidaling (fluctuation of water in the water seal chamber with respiration) indicates that the tube is patent and there is still an open space in the pleural cavity. When the lung fully re-expands and fills the pleural space, tidaling stops. This, combined with radiographic confirmation, indicates the goal of therapy has been met.
*Option A* indicates an active air leak in the system or the patient, meaning the pneumothorax is not resolved.
*Option C* is incorrect; while symmetrical expansion is good, dullness to percussion indicates fluid (like hemothorax or effusion), whereas a normal, re-expanded lung should be resonant.
*Option D* is a sign of hemothorax drainage, not pneumothorax resolution, and an output of 150 mL still requires ongoing monitoring.
</details>

---

### Question 389
The charge nurse is assigning tasks to a Registered Nurse (RN), a Licensed Practical Nurse (LPN), and an Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP) on a busy respiratory step-down unit. Using the lens of **Delegation**, which task is most appropriate to assign to the UAP?

A) Assisting a patient with a stable, resolving pneumothorax and a chest tube to ambulate in the hallway while keeping the drainage unit upright and below chest level.
B) Emptying the chest tube collection chamber and recording the output volume in the medical record for a patient with a resolving hemothorax.
C) Teaching a postoperative patient how to correctly use an incentive spirometer to prevent the development of a pulmonary embolism and atelectasis.
D) Monitoring the oxygen saturation of a patient who just presented with sudden onset shortness of breath and pleuritic chest pain.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** A UAP can assist stable patients with ambulation, including those with chest tubes, provided the nurse instructs them on standard safety measures (like keeping the drainage system below the level of the chest and upright). 
*Option B* is incorrect; chest tube drainage chambers are typically not emptied but rather changed out when full by an RN or LPN depending on facility policy. Measuring and interpreting the drainage from a closed system is a nursing responsibility.
*Option C* involves initial patient education, which is solely the scope of the RN.
*Option D* involves a patient experiencing acute, potentially unstable symptoms (possible PE), requiring immediate RN assessment and intervention; tasks for unstable patients cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 390
The nurse is preparing to perform a scheduled sterile dressing change for a patient's chest tube insertion site. Using the lens of **Sterile Technique/Skills**, which action demonstrates the correct sequence and procedure for this skill?

A) Removing the old, soiled dressing using sterile gloves to prevent exposing the insertion site to bacteria.
B) Cleansing the insertion site in a straight line from the outer edge of the chest wall inward toward the chest tube.
C) Taping the new dressing securely around the entire circumference of the patient's chest to ensure a perfectly airtight seal.
D) Applying a sterile, non-porous petroleum gauze directly around the insertion site before placing sterile split-drain sponges.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** When changing a chest tube dressing, maintaining an airtight seal at the insertion site is critical to prevent air from entering the pleural space. Applying a sterile, occlusive dressing, such as a petroleum-impregnated gauze, directly around the tube at the insertion site achieves this. This is then typically covered with sterile split-drain (Y-cut) sponges and taped securely.
*Option A* is incorrect because clean gloves are used to remove the old dressing; sterile gloves are donned after removal to clean the site and apply the new dressing.
*Option B* is incorrect; the site should be cleansed in a circular motion moving from the cleaner inner area (at the insertion site) outward, not outside-in.
*Option C* is incorrect; while the dressing itself must be securely taped to prevent air leaks, taping circumferentially around the entire chest wall restricts chest expansion and breathing, and is contraindicated.
</details>
### Question 391
A patient is recovering on the medical-surgical unit two days after an open reduction and internal fixation of the right femur. The nurse is closely monitoring the patient for postoperative respiratory complications. Which of the following clinical manifestations is the most reliable early indicator that the patient is developing a pulmonary embolism?

A) Gradual onset of bilateral crackles in the lower lung bases.
B) Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain and unexplained tachypnea.
C) Development of a harsh, barking cough accompanied by inspiratory stridor.
D) A new productive cough with thick, green sputum and a low-grade fever.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain and unexplained tachypnea.

**Rationale:**
- **Option B is correct:** A pulmonary embolism (PE) is a life-threatening complication, especially common after long bone fractures or major surgeries due to the risk of deep vein thrombosis (DVT) or fat emboli. The classic triad of early PE symptoms includes sudden dyspnea (tachypnea), pleuritic chest pain, and a feeling of impending doom.
- **Option A is incorrect:** Gradual onset of crackles in the lower lobes is more indicative of fluid overload, heart failure, or early atelectasis, not typically an acute pulmonary embolism.
- **Option C is incorrect:** A harsh, barking cough with stridor indicates upper airway obstruction or croup, not a lower respiratory vascular issue like a PE.
- **Option D is incorrect:** A productive cough with green sputum and fever points toward an infectious process, such as pneumonia, which typically has a more gradual onset compared to a PE.

**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION
</details>

---

### Question 392
A patient admitted with a severe COPD exacerbation has had serial arterial blood gases (ABGs) drawn on room air over the last 24 hours to monitor their respiratory status. 

- **Initial ABG:** pH 7.28, PaCO2 68 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 55 mm Hg. 
- **Current ABG:** pH 7.36, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 32 mEq/L, PaO2 65 mm Hg. 

Based on the trend of these ABG results, which conclusion by the nurse is the most accurate?

A) The patient is demonstrating fully compensated respiratory acidosis with improved oxygenation.
B) The patient's condition is actively deteriorating, as evidenced by the rising bicarbonate level.
C) The patient is experiencing a new onset of uncompensated metabolic alkalosis.
D) The patient is developing acute respiratory alkalosis due to compensatory hyperventilation.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The patient is demonstrating fully compensated respiratory acidosis with improved oxygenation.

**Rationale:**
- **Option A is correct:** The initial ABG shows uncompensated or partially compensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, slightly elevated HCO3). The current ABG shows a pH of 7.36, which has returned to the normal range (7.35-7.45) but is on the acidic side. The PaCO2 remains elevated at 58 mm Hg (normal 35-45), and the HCO3 has risen to 32 mEq/L (normal 22-26) to compensate. Because the pH is now normal, it is *fully compensated* respiratory acidosis. The PaO2 has also increased from 55 to 65, indicating improved oxygenation.
- **Option B is incorrect:** The rising bicarbonate is a normal and desired renal compensatory mechanism for chronic or prolonged respiratory acidosis, not a sign of deterioration.
- **Option C is incorrect:** The primary issue is respiratory, as evidenced by the high PaCO2 correlating with the initial low pH. The elevated HCO3 is compensatory, not a primary metabolic alkalosis.
- **Option D is incorrect:** The patient is not in respiratory alkalosis; the PaCO2 is elevated (acidic), not decreased.

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS
</details>

---

### Question 393
The nurse is assisting the healthcare provider with the removal of a chest tube from a patient whose pneumothorax has successfully resolved. To prevent the reintroduction of air into the pleural space during the procedure, which specific instruction should the nurse give the patient?

A) Take shallow, rapid breaths through the mouth until the dressing is fully secured.
B) Pant continuously to prevent sudden lung expansion while the tube is being pulled.
C) Take a deep breath, exhale completely, and bear down forcefully during the removal.
D) Inhale forcefully and hold the breath with the mouth wide open without straining.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Take a deep breath, exhale completely, and bear down forcefully during the removal.

**Rationale:**
- **Option C is correct:** During chest tube removal, the patient should be instructed to perform the Valsalva maneuver (taking a deep breath, exhaling, and bearing down). This increases intrathoracic pressure, which prevents atmospheric air from being sucked back into the pleural space while the tube is pulled out and the occlusive dressing is applied.
- **Option A is incorrect:** Shallow, rapid breathing creates negative intrathoracic pressure on inspiration, which could suck air into the pleural cavity, causing a recurrent pneumothorax.
- **Option B is incorrect:** Panting does not increase intrathoracic pressure steadily and leaves the patient vulnerable to air entering the chest cavity.
- **Option D is incorrect:** While taking a deep breath and holding it (an alternative form of the Valsalva maneuver) can be used, leaving the mouth wide open without bearing down does not reliably generate the necessary positive intrathoracic pressure.

**Lens:** STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS
</details>

---

### Question 394
A patient has had a chest tube in place for three days to manage a right-sided hemothorax resulting from blunt force trauma. The nurse is evaluating the effectiveness of the current treatment plan. Which clinical finding best indicates that the therapeutic goals for the chest tube have been met and it may be ready for removal?

A) Continuous, vigorous bubbling is noted in the water-seal chamber.
B) The drainage is consistently measuring 150 mL of bright red blood per hour.
C) The fluid level in the water-seal chamber fluctuates smoothly with respiratory effort.
D) Chest x-ray shows full lung re-expansion and drainage is less than 50 mL over 24 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Chest x-ray shows full lung re-expansion and drainage is less than 50 mL over 24 hours.

**Rationale:**
- **Option D is correct:** The primary goals of a chest tube for a hemothorax are to drain the blood from the pleural space and facilitate lung re-expansion. A radiograph showing full re-expansion and minimal drainage (<50-100 mL in 24 hours) indicates that the bleeding has stopped and the lung is fully inflated, meaning the therapeutic goal has been met.
- **Option A is incorrect:** Continuous bubbling in the water-seal chamber indicates an active air leak, not resolution.
- **Option B is incorrect:** Drainage of 150 mL/hr of bright red blood indicates active, potentially dangerous bleeding that requires immediate medical intervention, not resolution.
- **Option C is incorrect:** Fluctuation (tidaling) in the water-seal chamber indicates that the chest tube is patent and the lung has *not* yet fully re-expanded. When the lung fully re-expands, tidaling typically stops.

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS
</details>

---

### Question 395
A patient is preparing for discharge on oral warfarin therapy following successful acute treatment for a pulmonary embolism. The nurse is providing comprehensive teaching regarding lifestyle modifications and safety. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of the teaching?

A) "I should completely eliminate all green, leafy vegetables from my daily diet."
B) "I will ensure I use an electric razor and a soft-bristle toothbrush at home."
C) "I can take over-the-counter ibuprofen for headaches as long as I take it with food."
D) "I will stop taking the warfarin immediately if I notice sudden bruising on my arms."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) "I will ensure I use an electric razor and a soft-bristle toothbrush at home."

**Rationale:**
- **Option B is correct:** Warfarin is an anticoagulant that increases the risk of bleeding. Patients must adopt safety measures to prevent injury, such as using an electric razor instead of a straight blade and a soft-bristle toothbrush to prevent gingival bleeding.
- **Option A is incorrect:** Patients on warfarin do not need to eliminate vitamin K-rich foods (like green leafy vegetables) from their diet; instead, they need to maintain a *consistent* intake so their INR levels and medication dosing remain stable.
- **Option C is incorrect:** NSAIDs (like ibuprofen) inhibit platelet function and can cause gastric ulcers, significantly increasing the risk of severe bleeding when combined with warfarin. Acetaminophen is generally the preferred analgesic.
- **Option D is incorrect:** The patient should never abruptly stop their anticoagulant without consulting their healthcare provider. While bruising should be reported to the provider for an INR check, stopping the medication increases the immediate risk of another life-threatening pulmonary embolism.

**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING
</details>
### Question 396
A client with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is admitted with an acute exacerbation. The nurse reviews the arterial blood gases (ABGs) drawn over the last 24 hours. 
Initial ABG: pH 7.28, PaCO2 65 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 55 mm Hg. 
Current ABG: pH 7.35, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, HCO3 28 mEq/L, PaO2 62 mm Hg. 
Based on this data trend analysis, how should the nurse interpret the client's current status?

A) The client is developing uncompensated metabolic alkalosis.
B) The client is showing signs of impending respiratory failure.
C) The client is experiencing worsening acute respiratory acidosis.
D) The client is demonstrating improvement toward their compensated baseline.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D  
**Rationale:** The initial ABG shows partially compensated respiratory acidosis with hypoxemia, which is typical of an acute exacerbation in a client with COPD. The current ABG shows an improvement in pH towards the normal range (7.35–7.45), a decreased PaCO2 (moving closer to their chronic baseline), and an improved PaO2. This trend indicates that the medical interventions are effective and the client is returning to their typical compensated chronic respiratory acidosis state. There is no evidence of metabolic alkalosis, worsening respiratory acidosis, or impending respiratory failure.
</details>

---

### Question 397
A client is recovering on the surgical unit 48 hours after a total right hip arthroplasty. The client suddenly reports sharp pleuritic chest pain when taking a deep breath and expresses a feeling of impending doom. Which of the following additional findings is the earliest complication recognition sign that the client is developing a pulmonary embolism?

A) Sudden onset of unexplained tachypnea
B) Bradycardia with a bounding peripheral pulse
C) Expectoration of frothy, pink-tinged sputum
D) Diffuse bilateral wheezing on auscultation

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A  
**Rationale:** Tachypnea (rapid breathing) and dyspnea are the most common and earliest signs of a pulmonary embolism (PE), especially when combined with sudden pleuritic chest pain and a sense of impending doom in an at-risk post-surgical client. Tachycardia, not bradycardia, is an expected compensatory response to a PE. Pink, frothy sputum is a classic sign of pulmonary edema, not a PE. While localized wheezing or crackles may be heard in some cases of PE, diffuse bilateral wheezing is more indicative of a systemic allergic reaction (anaphylaxis) or an asthma exacerbation.
</details>

---

### Question 398
A client has a chest tube connected to a traditional water-seal drainage system for a right-sided spontaneous pneumothorax. On day three of therapy, the nurse observes that tidaling in the water-seal chamber has completely stopped, but the client's lung sounds are clear and equal bilaterally. How should the nurse evaluate this finding regarding the multi-day treatment goals?

A) The system has a massive air leak that requires immediate clamping of the tube.
B) The chest tube is obstructed by a clot and requires milking to restore patency.
C) The treatment is successful, and the lung has likely fully re-expanded.
D) The wall suction source has become disconnected and must be reattached immediately.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Rationale:** In a traditional water-seal drainage system, tidaling (the fluctuation of water with respirations) is expected while the pneumothorax is resolving. Tidaling stops for two primary reasons: the lung has fully re-expanded or the tube is obstructed. Because the nurse assesses clear and equal bilateral breath sounds, the goal of lung re-expansion has likely been successfully met. An air leak is indicated by continuous bubbling, not the cessation of tidaling. Milking or stripping a chest tube is generally contraindicated as it creates dangerous negative pressure. Suction disconnection does not cause tidaling to stop; tidaling occurs in the water-seal chamber independently of active suction.
</details>

---

### Question 399
The nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a client's chest tube insertion site. Which sequence of actions is most appropriate when utilizing sterile technique for this skill?

A) Remove the old dressing with sterile gloves, clean the site with chlorhexidine from the outer edge inward, apply a petrolatum gauze directly to the wound, and tape completely.
B) Remove the old dressing with clean gloves, don sterile gloves, clean the site with chlorhexidine from the insertion site outward, apply a sterile split-drain sponge, and secure with an occlusive dressing.
C) Don sterile gloves, remove the old dressing, clean the site starting from the outer edge working inward, apply a regular 4x4 gauze pad, and tape only three sides.
D) Don clean gloves, remove the old dressing, don new clean gloves, cleanse the site with alcohol in a circular motion, apply a split-drain sponge, and secure with silk tape.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Rationale:** The correct sterile sequence involves removing the old, potentially soiled dressing using clean gloves, performing hand hygiene, and then donning sterile gloves to handle the sterile supplies. The insertion site must be cleansed from the cleanest area (the insertion site itself) outward to the dirtier peripheral skin. A sterile split-drain (Y-drain) sponge is placed around the tube, and it is secured with an occlusive dressing or tape to prevent air leaks around the tube insertion site. Taping on three sides is an emergency intervention for an open pneumothorax (sucking chest wound), not a standard chest tube dressing.
</details>

---

### Question 400
A registered nurse (RN) is planning care for a group of clients with respiratory disorders. Which task is most appropriate to delegate to an experienced Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A) Interpreting the results of a morning arterial blood gas (ABG) for a client with chronic asthma.
B) Instructing a client on how to avoid placing tension on their chest tube during ambulation.
C) Documenting the volume of fluid accumulated in the chest tube collection chamber at the end of the shift.
D) Assessing the respiratory rate and rhythm of a client who just developed sharp pleuritic chest pain.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Rationale:** An experienced UAP can safely observe and document the volume of drainage in a chest tube collection chamber, as this is a routine measurement task that does not require nursing judgment or intervention. Interpreting laboratory results (ABGs), providing patient teaching (chest tube safety during ambulation), and performing an assessment on an unstable patient (new onset pleuritic chest pain, which is a sign of a potential pulmonary embolism) require the clinical judgment, knowledge, and advanced skill of the RN and cannot be delegated.
</details>
### Question 401
A client recovering from an open reduction and internal fixation of the right femur suddenly becomes restless and anxious. The nurse is monitoring the client for post-operative complications. Which assessment finding represents an early sign of a developing pulmonary embolism (PE)?

A. Gradual increase in a productive cough and a low-grade fever.
B. Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain and unexplained dyspnea.
C. Development of resting bradycardia and bilateral dependent edema.
D. Inspiratory stridor and the sudden appearance of generalized urticaria.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**Rationale:** The classic triad of early clinical manifestations for a pulmonary embolism includes sudden onset of dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain (pain that worsens on inspiration), and hemoptysis (though hemoptysis is less common). Tachypnea and tachycardia (not bradycardia) are also frequent early signs. A gradual increase in cough and fever (Option A) suggests pneumonia. Inspiratory stridor and urticaria (Option D) are indicative of an anaphylactic reaction. Dependent edema (Option C) is a sign of right-sided heart failure, and while massive PE can cause right heart strain, it presents with tachycardia, not bradycardia.
</details>

---

### Question 402
A client with a severe asthma exacerbation is receiving continuous nebulizer treatments. Serial arterial blood gases (ABGs) are drawn over a 4-hour period to monitor the client's status. The initial ABG showed a pH of 7.50, PaCO2 of 28 mmHg, and PaO2 of 75 mmHg. The most recent ABG shows a pH of 7.36, PaCO2 of 42 mmHg, and PaO2 of 68 mmHg. How should the nurse interpret this data trend?

A. The client's respiratory status is improving due to the normalization of the pH and PaCO2.
B. The client is hyperventilating and requires a non-rebreather mask to retain carbon dioxide.
C. The client is developing respiratory muscle fatigue and is at high risk for impending respiratory failure.
D. The client is experiencing metabolic compensation for an acute respiratory alkalosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**Rationale:** During an acute asthma attack, the client initially hyperventilates, blowing off carbon dioxide and causing a respiratory alkalosis (low PaCO2, high pH). If the PaCO2 begins to rise and return to "normal" (35-45 mmHg) while the client remains in respiratory distress (and PaO2 continues to fall), this is a dangerous sign. It indicates that the client's respiratory muscles are fatiguing and they are no longer able to maintain the work of breathing, signaling impending respiratory failure. It is not a sign of improvement (Option A) or metabolic compensation (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 403
The nurse is assisting the healthcare provider with the removal of a client's chest tube. Which action correctly demonstrates the proper technique for this procedure?

A. Instruct the client to take a deep breath, exhale, and bear down while the tube is pulled, then immediately apply an occlusive dressing.
B. Administer pain medication 5 minutes before the procedure, clamp the tube, and ask the client to inhale forcefully during removal.
C. Have the client breathe normally, pull the tube out slowly over a 30-second interval, and apply a dry sterile gauze dressing.
D. Turn off the wall suction, disconnect the tube from the drainage system, and instruct the client to cough continuously during removal.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills
**Rationale:** During chest tube removal, the client is instructed to perform the Valsalva maneuver (taking a deep breath, exhaling, and bearing down, or holding the breath). This increases intrathoracic pressure, preventing ambient air from rushing back into the pleural space and causing a recurrent pneumothorax. An occlusive dressing (such as petroleum gauze) is immediately applied to seal the insertion site. The tube should be removed swiftly, not slowly (Option C), and coughing or forceful inhalation (Options B and D) could introduce air into the pleural space.
</details>

---

### Question 404
A client has had a chest tube in place for three days to manage a right-sided spontaneous pneumothorax. The nurse is evaluating the client to determine if the treatment goals have been met. Which finding best indicates that the pneumothorax has resolved?

A. Continuous, vigorous bubbling is observed in the water-seal chamber.
B. The drainage in the collection chamber has decreased to less than 10 mL/hr.
C. The client reports a pain level of 2 out of 10 at the insertion site.
D. Fluctuation of fluid in the water-seal chamber has ceased without the tube being kinked.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals
**Rationale:** Tidaling (fluctuation) in the water-seal chamber is a normal finding that corresponds with the changes in intrathoracic pressure during respiration. When the lung fully re-expands and the pneumothorax resolves, the visceral and parietal pleura come back together, blocking the tip of the chest tube and causing the tidaling to stop. As long as the tube is not kinked or obstructed, cessation of tidaling indicates clinical resolution. Continuous bubbling (Option A) indicates an active air leak. Decreased drainage (Option B) evaluates fluid removal (like a hemothorax), but is not the primary indicator for a resolved pneumothorax. Pain assessment (Option C) does not confirm lung re-expansion.
</details>

---

### Question 405
A client diagnosed with a massive pulmonary embolism (PE) is visibly anxious, diaphoretic, and tells the nurse, "I feel like I'm going to die. Every time I take a breath, I'm terrified the clot is going to move into my heart." Which response by the nurse demonstrates the most effective therapeutic communication?

A. "The intravenous blood thinners you are receiving will dissolve the clot very quickly, so there is no need to worry."
B. "Try to take slow, deep breaths; severe anxiety will only increase your heart rate and make your shortness of breath worse."
C. "It is completely normal to feel terrified right now. Let's talk about the specific treatments we are using to keep you safe."
D. "The clot is already lodged in the blood vessels of your lungs, so structurally, it cannot move into your heart at this point."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Therapeutic Communication
**Rationale:** Option C directly acknowledges and validates the client's severe anxiety (often described as a feeling of impending doom, which is highly common in massive PE) while gently shifting the focus to the safety and efficacy of the medical interventions. This approach builds trust and reduces panic. Option A provides false reassurance, as anticoagulants prevent further clotting but do not dissolve existing clots quickly (thrombolytics do, but "no need to worry" is still dismissive). Option B dismisses the client's psychological distress by turning it into a physiological reprimand. Option D relies purely on logical anatomy, entirely ignoring the client's emotional crisis.
</details>
### Question 406
A nurse is performing a sterile dressing change for a patient with a left-sided chest tube. Which action indicates the nurse is using the correct technique for this system-specific procedure?

A. Cleansing the insertion site with normal saline using an outward circular motion and applying the dressing while the skin is damp.
B. Applying sterile split-drain gauze around the tube and covering the site with a secure, occlusive dressing.
C. Clamping the chest tube with padded hemostats while the old dressing is removed to prevent an air leak.
D. Removing the soiled dressing only while the patient holds their breath in peak inspiration.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Applying sterile split-drain gauze around the tube and covering the site with a secure, occlusive dressing.

**Rationale:** 
- **Option B is correct:** During a sterile chest tube dressing change, the nurse should use sterile split-drain gauze around the tube insertion site and cover it with an occlusive dressing (often using tape to secure all edges) to prevent air from entering the pleural space.
- **Option A is incorrect:** The insertion site is typically cleansed with chlorhexidine (or per facility policy), and the skin must be allowed to dry completely before applying the new dressing to prevent infection and ensure tape adherence.
- **Option C is incorrect:** A chest tube should never be routinely clamped during a dressing change, as this can rapidly cause a life-threatening tension pneumothorax.
- **Option D is incorrect:** While the Valsalva maneuver (holding breath and bearing down) is used during chest tube *removal* to prevent air entry, it is unnecessary simply for removing an old dressing. 
- **Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills.

</details>

---

### Question 407
A patient is recovering on the surgical unit three days after a total knee arthroplasty. Which set of clinical manifestations should prompt the nurse to immediately recognize the early onset of a pulmonary embolism?

A. Bradycardia, severe crushing chest pain radiating to the jaw, and profound diaphoresis.
B. Tracheal deviation to the left, absent breath sounds on the right side, and hypotension.
C. Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain, unexplained tachypnea, and a profound feeling of impending doom.
D. Gradually increasing dyspnea on exertion, a barrel-shaped chest, and clubbing of the fingernails.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain, unexplained tachypnea, and a profound feeling of impending doom.

**Rationale:** 
- **Option C is correct:** The classic early triad of a pulmonary embolism (PE) includes sudden pleuritic chest pain (pain that worsens on inspiration), sudden tachypnea, and intense anxiety or a feeling of "impending doom" due to acute hypoxemia.
- **Option A is incorrect:** These are classic signs of an acute myocardial infarction (MI), not a pulmonary embolism. PEs typically present with tachycardia, not bradycardia.
- **Option B is incorrect:** These represent a tension pneumothorax, which causes a mediastinal shift (tracheal deviation) away from the affected side. 
- **Option D is incorrect:** These are chronic signs associated with Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD), not an acute embolic event.
- **Lens:** Complication Recognition.

</details>

---

### Question 408
A patient with a severe asthma exacerbation has serial arterial blood gases (ABGs) drawn. The initial ABG reveals: pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mm Hg, PaO2 75 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L. Two hours later, a repeat ABG shows: pH 7.35, PaCO2 45 mm Hg, PaO2 62 mm Hg, HCO3 24 mEq/L. What is the nurse's best interpretation of this trend?

A. The patient is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.
B. The patient's respiratory status is improving due to a normalizing pH and PaCO2.
C. The patient is developing metabolic acidosis secondary to lactic acid buildup.
D. The patient's asthma exacerbation has resolved, leading to stable alveolar ventilation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The patient is experiencing respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure.

**Rationale:** 
- **Option A is correct:** Initially, patients in an asthma attack hyperventilate, leading to respiratory alkalosis (low PaCO2, high pH). If a subsequent ABG shows the PaCO2 rising into the "normal" range (35-45 mm Hg) and the pH dropping, this is a dangerous pseudonormalization. It indicates the patient's respiratory muscles are fatiguing; they are no longer able to maintain the hyperventilation needed to overcome the airway resistance, signaling impending respiratory failure.
- **Option B & D are incorrect:** The nurse must not misinterpret the "normal" pH and PaCO2 as an improvement. The concurrent drop in PaO2 (from 75 to 62) further confirms clinical deterioration.
- **Option C is incorrect:** The bicarbonate (HCO3) is normal (24 mEq/L), indicating no metabolic acidosis is present.
- **Lens:** Data Trend Analysis.

</details>

---

### Question 409
The nurse is managing a stable patient who has a closed-chest drainage system in place for a resolving hemothorax. Which task is appropriate for the nurse to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Emptying the chest tube collection chamber at the end of the shift to record output.
B. Documenting the clinical characteristics, such as color and consistency, of the chest drainage.
C. Checking the water seal chamber for continuous bubbling before the patient ambulates.
D. Assisting the patient with ambulation while keeping the drainage unit upright and below chest level.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Assisting the patient with ambulation while keeping the drainage unit upright and below chest level.

**Rationale:** 
- **Option D is correct:** A UAP can assist a stable patient with a chest tube with activities of daily living, including ambulation. The UAP must be instructed on basic safety measures, such as keeping the drainage unit upright and below the level of the patient's chest to prevent fluid backflow.
- **Option A is incorrect:** Chest tube drainage systems are sealed and should not be opened to be emptied; the unit is changed entirely when full. Even if it were drainable, handling the sealed system's output is an RN/LPN responsibility.
- **Option B is incorrect:** Assessing and evaluating the clinical characteristics of the drainage falls outside the UAP's scope of practice.
- **Option C is incorrect:** Assessing the water seal chamber for bubbling (which indicates an air leak) requires nursing judgment and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
- **Lens:** Delegation.

</details>

---

### Question 410
A patient is being discharged on long-term oral warfarin therapy following a pulmonary embolism. Which statement by the patient indicates successful teaching regarding chronic management and lifestyle modifications?

A. "I should use a soft-bristle toothbrush and avoid participating in contact sports."
B. "I will need to stop eating all green, leafy vegetables while taking this medication."
C. "If I miss a dose, I should take two pills the next day to catch up on my prescription."
D. "I will take my warfarin in the morning so my blood levels can be drawn at night."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. "I should use a soft-bristle toothbrush and avoid participating in contact sports."

**Rationale:** 
- **Option A is correct:** Warfarin is an anticoagulant that increases the risk of bleeding. The patient must adopt bleeding precautions, which include using a soft-bristle toothbrush, using an electric razor, and avoiding activities that could lead to trauma, such as contact sports.
- **Option B is incorrect:** Patients on warfarin do not need to eliminate vitamin K-rich foods (like green leafy vegetables). Instead, they must maintain a *consistent* daily intake so their warfarin dose can be accurately calibrated against their diet.
- **Option C is incorrect:** Patients should never double a dose of warfarin to "catch up" if they miss a dose, as this dramatically increases the risk of hemorrhage.
- **Option D is incorrect:** Warfarin is typically taken in the evening. This allows the healthcare provider to check the INR lab level in the morning and adjust the evening dose on the same day if necessary.
- **Lens:** Complex Teaching.

</details>


# SECTION: Neurological System (Count: 140 Questions - INTERMEDIATE)


### Question 411
A client is preparing for discharge after experiencing a right-hemisphere ischemic stroke. The client has residual left-sided hemiparesis and left homonymous hemianopsia. When providing complex discharge teaching to the client and family about home management and safety, which instruction should the nurse include?

A. "Place the client's favorite items exclusively on their left side to encourage them to reach with their weaker arm."
B. "Remind the client to actively turn their head to the left to scan the room when walking or eating."
C. "Keep the environment dimly lit and quiet at all times to prevent sensory overload and agitation."
D. "Assist the client by feeding them all their meals to completely eliminate the risk of aspiration."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Left homonymous hemianopsia is the loss of the left half of the visual field in both eyes, a common complication of right-hemisphere ischemic strokes. Because the client cannot see items on their left side, they are at a high risk for injury and malnutrition (ignoring half their food). The nurse must teach the client to consciously turn their head to scan the affected side to compensate for this visual loss. Placing items exclusively on the affected (left) side (Option A) will cause frustration because the client cannot easily see or reach them; initially, essential items should be placed within the intact visual field. Dimming the lights (Option C) increases fall risk. Total feeding (Option D) reduces independence; instead, the client should be taught safe swallowing techniques to foster rehabilitation. <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> COMPLEX TEACHING (Managing chronic system-related illness and lifestyle changes)
</details>

---

### Question 412
A client is recovering from an extensive ischemic stroke and is being closely monitored for the development of cerebral edema and increased intracranial pressure (ICP). Which subtle assessment finding should the nurse recognize as the earliest indication of rising ICP?

A. A new onset of restlessness, confusion, and difficulty maintaining attention.
B. A widening pulse pressure accompanied by profound bradycardia.
C. Fixed and dilated pupils that do not respond to direct or consensual light.
D. An onset of decorticate posturing when a noxious stimulus is applied.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
The earliest sign of increased intracranial pressure (ICP) is typically a change in the client's level of consciousness (LOC). This often presents as subtle behavioral changes, restlessness, confusion, lethargy, or difficulty maintaining attention, due to impaired cerebral oxygenation. Widening pulse pressure with bradycardia (part of Cushing's triad) (Option B), fixed and dilated pupils (Option C), and abnormal motor posturing such as decorticate or decerebrate posturing (Option D) are all late and ominous signs of critically high ICP and impending brainstem herniation.<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> COMPLICATION RECOGNITION (Identify early signs of a specific system failure)
</details>

---

### Question 413
The nurse is planning care for a client experiencing an acute exacerbation of multiple sclerosis (MS). The client is currently stable but has severe fatigue, muscle spasticity, and bilateral ataxia. Which task is most appropriate for the nurse to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A. Assess the client's risk for falls prior to getting them out of bed for the first time.
B. Evaluate the client's swallowing ability during their morning breakfast.
C. Assist the client with prescribed active range-of-motion exercises in bed.
D. Administer scheduled oral baclofen to help reduce the client's muscle spasticity.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Delegation to Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP) must follow the scope of practice, which includes standard, predictable tasks for stable clients. Assisting a client with active range-of-motion (ROM) exercises is within the UAP's scope of practice and helps manage the spasticity associated with MS. Assessing fall risk (Option A) and evaluating swallowing ability (Option B) require nursing judgment and must be performed by the registered nurse. Administering oral medications (Option D) is out of the UAP's scope of practice and must be done by licensed nursing personnel.<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> DELEGATION (Assigning tasks for stable vs unstable system-specific patients)
</details>

---

### Question 414
A nurse is changing the sterile dressing on a client's external ventricular drain (EVD) site, which was placed to treat critically increased intracranial pressure (ICP). Which action by the nurse demonstrates proper sterile technique for this neurological procedure?

A. Donning sterile gloves prior to removing the old, soiled transparent dressing.
B. Cleansing the catheter site with chlorhexidine and wiping it dry with sterile gauze.
C. Securing the drainage tubing directly to the bed rail to ensure a sterile field.
D. Applying a sterile, occlusive dressing over the site after the antiseptic has completely air-dried.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
When changing a central or EVD dressing, strict sterile technique must be maintained to prevent severe complications like ventriculitis or meningitis. The nurse must allow the chlorhexidine antiseptic to completely air-dry before applying the sterile occlusive dressing to ensure maximum antimicrobial efficacy and prevent skin irritation. Old dressings should be removed using clean gloves, not sterile gloves, to prevent contaminating the sterile gloves before the actual site care (Option A). Antiseptics must air-dry and should never be wiped or fanned away (Option B). Securing EVD tubing to a bed rail (Option C) is highly dangerous and contraindicated, as lowering the bed rail could dislodge the catheter or rapidly alter ICP by draining too much cerebrospinal fluid.<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> STERILE TECHNIQUE/SKILLS (The correct sequence for system-specific sterile procedures)
</details>

---

### Question 415
A client with relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis (MS) is scheduled to receive their prescribed dose of interferon beta-1a. The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results, which reveal an alanine aminotransferase (ALT) of 210 U/L and an aspartate aminotransferase (AST) of 185 U/L. Based on these findings, what is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A. Administer the medication as prescribed, since transient transaminitis is an expected effect.
B. Give the medication and instruct the client to limit acetaminophen use to protect the liver.
C. Withhold the interferon beta-1a and notify the healthcare provider of the laboratory results.
D. Administer half the prescribed dose of the medication to minimize further hepatic impairment.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Interferon beta-1a is an immunomodulator used to treat MS, but it carries a significant risk of severe hepatotoxicity. Normal AST and ALT levels are typically between 10 and 40 U/L. The client's levels are significantly elevated (roughly 5 times the upper limit of normal), indicating acute liver injury. The nurse must withhold the medication and immediately notify the healthcare provider. Administering the medication (Options A and B) could result in fatal liver failure. Nurses cannot alter medication dosages (Option D) without a specific order from the healthcare provider.<br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> DRUG-LAB INTERACTION (Safety: Should the nurse hold a med based on a system-specific lab?)
</details>
### Question 416
The nurse is monitoring a client admitted with a severe traumatic brain injury who has an intraventricular catheter in place to measure intracranial pressure (ICP). Which assessment finding represents an early complication of increasing ICP that requires immediate intervention?

A) The client's right pupil becomes fixed and dilated to 8 mm.
B) The client develops a widening pulse pressure and bradycardia.
C) The client exhibits new-onset restlessness and disorientation to time and place.
D) The client becomes completely unarousable to a deep sternal rub.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Complication Recognition (Identify early signs of a specific system failure)
**Rationale:** The earliest sign of increasing intracranial pressure is a change in the client's level of consciousness (LOC), which can manifest as new-onset restlessness, confusion, or disorientation. This occurs due to early cerebral hypoxia as pressure within the cranial vault rises.
*Option A* is incorrect because a fixed, dilated pupil (a "blown" pupil) is a late sign of increased ICP indicative of brain herniation (specifically uncal herniation) compressing cranial nerve III.
*Option B* is incorrect because a widening pulse pressure and bradycardia are components of Cushing's Triad, which is a late and ominous sign of brainstem compression.
*Option D* is incorrect because becoming completely unarousable (coma) is a late sign of neurological deterioration resulting from prolonged or severe increased ICP.
</details>

---

### Question 417
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with relapsing-remitting Multiple Sclerosis (MS). Which statement by the client indicates an accurate understanding of the lifestyle modifications needed to manage this chronic neurological illness?

A) "I will spread my household chores throughout the day and take frequent rest periods."
B) "I should take hot baths in the evening to help relax my frequent muscle spasms."
C) "I will schedule my most physically demanding daily tasks for the late afternoon."
D) "I need to severely limit my daily fluid intake to prevent urinary incontinence."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Complex Teaching (Managing chronic system-related illness and lifestyle changes)
**Rationale:** Fatigue is one of the most common and debilitating symptoms of Multiple Sclerosis. Clients are taught to pace their activities, prioritize tasks, and incorporate frequent rest periods throughout the day to conserve energy.
*Option B* is incorrect because extreme heat (such as hot baths or saunas) can exacerbate MS symptoms and cause severe weakness, a phenomenon known as Uhthoff's sign.
*Option C* is incorrect because energy levels typically deplete as the day goes on. Strenuous activities should be scheduled in the morning when the client's energy levels are highest.
*Option D* is incorrect because severely limiting fluids increases the risk of urinary tract infections (UTIs) and constipation, both of which are already common complications in MS. A voiding schedule should be used to manage incontinence, not fluid restriction.
</details>

---

### Question 418
A client is on day 4 of recovery following an ischemic stroke affecting the right cerebral hemisphere. The multidisciplinary team established a primary goal to improve the client's left-sided unilateral neglect. Which observation during a meal best indicates that the current treatment plan is effective?

A) The client requests the nurse to move their beverage to the right side of the tray.
B) The client demonstrates intact sharp and dull sensation when the nurse assesses the left arm.
C) The client independently uses their right hand to correctly position their paralyzed left arm.
D) The client deliberately turns their head to scan the left side of the meal tray to finish their food.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Evaluation of Goals (Determining if the multi-day system treatment plan is working)
**Rationale:** Unilateral neglect is a syndrome commonly seen after a right-hemisphere stroke where the client fails to attend to the left side of their body and environment. Actively turning the head to scan the affected side shows that the client is implementing compensatory strategies learned in therapy, indicating the treatment plan is working.
*Option A* is incorrect because asking to move items to the unaffected side demonstrates accommodation of the neglect rather than improvement or active compensation.
*Option B* is incorrect because intact sensation is an assessment of the sensory pathways, not a behavioral evaluation of unilateral neglect.
*Option C* is incorrect because while managing the paralyzed limb is an excellent goal for hemiplegia, deliberately scanning the environment directly addresses the cognitive deficit of unilateral neglect.
</details>

---

### Question 419
A client with Multiple Sclerosis is prescribed interferon beta-1a via subcutaneous injection. During a follow-up clinic visit, the client reports several symptoms experienced over the last month. Which symptom represents a dangerous adverse effect that requires the nurse to withhold the medication and notify the provider?

A) Erythema and mild tenderness at the subcutaneous injection site.
B) New onset of profound sadness, hopelessness, and thoughts of self-harm.
C) Low-grade fever, chills, and muscle aches 24 hours after the injection.
D) Mild headache and fatigue that resolve with acetaminophen and rest.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Adverse vs Side Effect (Differentiating expected vs dangerous system-specific drug reactions)
**Rationale:** Interferon beta-1a carries a significant risk for severe depression and suicidal ideation. A new onset of profound sadness and thoughts of self-harm is a dangerous adverse effect requiring immediate discontinuation of the drug and psychological intervention.
*Option A* is incorrect because injection site reactions (erythema, pain, mild swelling) are common, expected side effects managed by rotating sites and applying ice.
*Option C* is incorrect because flu-like symptoms (fever, chills, myalgia) are common and expected side effects of interferon therapy, especially early in treatment.
*Option D* is incorrect because a mild headache and fatigue are expected side effects that can be safely managed with over-the-counter analgesics like acetaminophen.
</details>

---

### Question 420
The nurse is preparing to perform a sterile dressing change on a client's external ventricular drain (EVD), which is being used for continuous intracranial pressure (ICP) monitoring and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) drainage. Which action is essential for the nurse to perform to maintain system integrity and safety during this sterile procedure?

A) Ensure the system stopcock is turned off to the drain before removing the old dressing.
B) Cleanse the insertion site with alcohol swabs working from the outer edge inward toward the catheter.
C) Flush the ventricular catheter with 10 mL of sterile normal saline after applying the new dressing.
D) Level the system transducer to the phlebostatic axis prior to scrubbing the site.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Sterile Technique/Skills (The correct sequence for system-specific sterile procedures)
**Rationale:** When manipulating the EVD system for a dressing change, the nurse must turn the stopcock off to the drain (or off to the patient, per hospital policy) to prevent accidental rapid overdrainage of CSF, sudden shifts in ICP, or introduction of air into the ventricular system while the catheter is being moved.
*Option B* is incorrect because sterile cleansing should always move from the clean insertion site outward (inner to outer) to avoid bringing flora toward the direct entry to the brain.
*Option C* is incorrect because flushing an EVD is generally contraindicated for nurses as it can cause a rapid, lethal spike in ICP and introduce infection; if flushing is necessary, it is highly regulated and typically done by the neurosurgeon.
*Option D* is incorrect because an EVD transducer is leveled to the Foramen of Monro (often aligned externally with the tragus of the ear), not the phlebostatic axis (which is used for hemodynamic monitoring like CVP or arterial lines).
</details>
### Question 421
A client with a severe traumatic brain injury has an external ventricular drain (EVD) in place to monitor intracranial pressure (ICP). The nurse is conducting hourly neurological assessments. Which finding is the most critical early indicator that the client is experiencing a complication related to increasing ICP?

A) Decorticate posturing in response to painful stimuli
B) Sudden restlessness and confusion without a clear cause
C) A widening pulse pressure with profound bradycardia
D) Unilateral fixed and dilated pupil

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Sudden restlessness and confusion without a clear cause

**Lens:** Complication Recognition
**System Focus:** Increased ICP Monitoring

**Explanation:**
A change in level of consciousness (LOC), such as sudden restlessness, confusion, or increased drowsiness, is the most sensitive and earliest indicator of increasing intracranial pressure. As the brain tissue experiences mild hypoxia and compression, cognitive and behavioral changes occur first. Decorticate posturing (Option A), widening pulse pressure with profound bradycardia (Cushing's triad) (Option C), and a unilateral fixed and dilated pupil (Option D) are all late and ominous signs of critically high ICP and impending brain herniation. Identifying the early signs allows the nurse to intervene before irreversible brain damage occurs.
</details>

---

### Question 422
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with relapsing-remitting Multiple Sclerosis (MS) who is experiencing frequent muscle spasticity and fatigue. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the necessary complex lifestyle modifications?

A) "I will break up my daily chores into smaller tasks and rest in a cool room afterward."
B) "I should schedule my most physically demanding activities for the late afternoon."
C) "Taking a prolonged hot bath in the evening will help soothe my muscle spasticity."
D) "I need to limit my fluid intake to 1 liter a day to avoid bladder incontinence."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "I will break up my daily chores into smaller tasks and rest in a cool room afterward."

**Lens:** Complex Teaching
**System Focus:** Multiple Sclerosis

**Explanation:**
Management of Multiple Sclerosis requires balancing activity with rest to manage severe fatigue. Pacing activities (breaking them up) prevents exhaustion. Additionally, MS symptoms, including fatigue and nerve conduction issues, are highly sensitive to heat (Uhthoff's phenomenon); resting in a cool environment helps mitigate symptoms. Clients should schedule demanding tasks in the morning when energy levels are highest (Option B is incorrect). Hot baths elevate core body temperature and can severely exacerbate MS symptoms (Option C is incorrect). Fluid restriction can lead to concentrated urine and urinary tract infections, which can trigger an MS exacerbation; rather than restricting fluids, the client should establish a regular voiding schedule (Option D is incorrect).
</details>

---

### Question 423
The registered nurse (RN) is coordinating care for a client who is 4 days post-ischemic stroke and exhibits right-sided hemiparesis. The client is currently stable and preparing for discharge to an acute rehabilitation facility. Which task is most appropriate for the RN to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Performing the initial bedside swallow screen prior to the client's first meal.
B) Teaching the client how to use a reacher tool to grab objects from the floor.
C) Assessing the client's right calf for redness, swelling, and warmth.
D) Assisting the client to put on a shirt, threading the right arm through the sleeve first.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Assisting the client to put on a shirt, threading the right arm through the sleeve first.

**Lens:** Delegation
**System Focus:** Ischemic Stroke Recovery

**Explanation:**
Assisting a stable client with activities of daily living (ADLs), such as dressing, falls well within the UAP's scope of practice. When assisting a client with hemiparesis to dress, the general rule is to dress the affected side first, making Option D a safe and appropriate delegated task. Swallow screening (Option A) requires nursing judgment and assessment to prevent aspiration. Teaching the use of adaptive equipment (Option B) is an educational task reserved for the RN or occupational therapist. Assessing for deep vein thrombosis (Option C) requires nursing assessment skills and cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 424
A nurse is monitoring a client with an external ventricular drain (EVD) following a severe head injury. Over the last 4 hours, the client's mean arterial pressure (MAP) has gradually dropped from 90 mm Hg to 75 mm Hg, and the intracranial pressure (ICP) has steadily risen from 12 mm Hg to 18 mm Hg. Based on this data trend, what is the nurse's primary clinical deduction regarding the client's neurological status?

A) The cerebral perfusion pressure is improving, indicating effective cerebral autoregulation.
B) These changes represent a normal physiological fluctuation during the acute injury phase.
C) The client's cerebral perfusion pressure has dropped below the threshold for adequate blood flow.
D) Systemic hypotension is actively decreasing the client's overall intracranial pressure.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) The client's cerebral perfusion pressure has dropped below the threshold for adequate blood flow.

**Lens:** Data Trend Analysis
**System Focus:** Increased ICP Monitoring

**Explanation:**
Cerebral Perfusion Pressure (CPP) is calculated by subtracting ICP from MAP (CPP = MAP - ICP). A normal CPP is between 60 to 100 mm Hg. Four hours ago, the client's CPP was 78 mm Hg (90 - 12). Currently, the client's CPP is 57 mm Hg (75 - 18). A CPP below 60 mm Hg indicates inadequate cerebral blood flow, placing the brain at high risk for ischemia and hypoxia. The trend indicates a dangerous deterioration, not an improvement or normal fluctuation (Options A and B). Systemic hypotension is actually worsening the CPP, not decreasing the ICP (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 425
A client with relapsing-remitting Multiple Sclerosis is receiving subcutaneous interferon beta-1a injections. During a follow-up visit, the client reports several symptoms. Which statement requires the nurse to withhold the next dose and contact the healthcare provider to report a dangerous adverse effect?

A) "I have noticed a yellow tinge to the whites of my eyes today."
B) "I get a low-grade fever and chills about four hours after my injection."
C) "The area where I inject the medication gets red and slightly itchy."
D) "I feel achy in my joints and muscles the morning after I take my dose."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "I have noticed a yellow tinge to the whites of my eyes today."

**Lens:** Adverse vs Side Effect
**System Focus:** Multiple Sclerosis

**Explanation:**
Interferon beta-1a is an immunomodulator used to reduce the frequency of MS exacerbations. A rare but severe adverse effect of interferon therapy is hepatotoxicity, which can manifest as jaundice (yellowing of the sclera). This requires immediate intervention, withholding of the drug, and liver function testing. Options B, C, and D describe expected, common side effects of interferon therapy. Flu-like symptoms (fever, chills, myalgia) and injection site reactions are generally self-limiting and can often be managed symptomatically (e.g., with acetaminophen) without discontinuing the medication.
</details>
### Question 426
A client who sustained a traumatic brain injury has an external ventricular drain (EVD) in place for intracranial pressure (ICP) monitoring. Which finding by the nurse represents the earliest complication of increasing intracranial pressure?

A) A unilateral fixed and dilated pupil during neuro checks
B) Sudden onset of restless behavior and mild confusion
C) A heart rate of 48 beats per minute with a widening pulse pressure
D) Decorticate posturing in response to painful stimuli

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> COMPLICATION RECOGNITION<br>
An acute change in level of consciousness (LOC), such as sudden restlessness, agitation, or mild confusion, is the earliest, most sensitive indicator of increasing intracranial pressure (ICP) and impaired cerebral perfusion. A unilateral fixed and dilated pupil (Option A) indicates brain herniation, a late and life-threatening complication. A low heart rate with a widening pulse pressure (Option C) is part of Cushing's triad, which is a late sign of dangerously high ICP. Decorticate posturing (Option D) is also a late sign indicating significant motor tract damage.
</details>

---

### Question 427
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly diagnosed with Multiple Sclerosis (MS) who is experiencing muscle weakness and fatigue. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of lifestyle management to prevent exacerbations?

A) "I should schedule my most physically demanding activities for the late afternoon."
B) "I will take a hot bath every evening to help relax my frequent muscle spasms."
C) "I need to spread my daily chores out over the week and rest frequently between tasks."
D) "I will limit my daily fluid intake to prevent having to make frequent trips to the bathroom."

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> COMPLEX TEACHING<br>
Fatigue is a primary and debilitating symptom of Multiple Sclerosis. Clients are taught energy conservation techniques, such as pacing activities, spreading chores throughout the week, and resting when tired. Fatigue is generally worse in the afternoon; therefore, demanding activities should be scheduled in the morning (Option A). Heat intolerance is a hallmark of MS (Uhthoff's phenomenon) and taking a hot bath can trigger weakness and exacerbate symptoms (Option B). Restricting fluids (Option D) increases the risk of urinary tract infections, which are common in MS and can trigger a systemic exacerbation. 
</details>

---

### Question 428
A nurse is monitoring a client with an external ventricular drain for increased intracranial pressure (ICP). Over the last 4 hours, the client's Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) decreased from 90 mm Hg to 75 mm Hg, while the ICP gradually increased from 12 mm Hg to 20 mm Hg. What is the nurse's most accurate interpretation of these data trends?

A) The cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) is critically low, indicating a high risk for brain ischemia.
B) The client is demonstrating neurological improvement as the MAP has lowered closer to baseline.
C) The widening gap between the MAP and ICP indicates that the client is developing brain herniation.
D) The cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) remains stable, requiring continued routine monitoring.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: A</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> DATA TREND ANALYSIS<br>
Cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) is calculated by subtracting ICP from MAP (CPP = MAP - ICP). The normal CPP range is 60 to 100 mm Hg. Initially, the client's CPP was 78 mm Hg (90 - 12). After 4 hours, the CPP has dropped to 55 mm Hg (75 - 20). A CPP less than 60 mm Hg indicates compromised blood flow to the brain, which can lead to cerebral ischemia and death. This trend requires immediate medical intervention, not routine monitoring (Option D). A decreasing MAP in the setting of rising ICP does not indicate improvement (Option B). A widening pulse pressure (systolic vs. diastolic), not the gap between MAP and ICP, is an indicator of herniation (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 429
The nurse is planning care for a client who is 4 days post-ischemic stroke and is recovering from left-sided hemiparesis. Which task is most appropriate to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A) Assessing the client's gag reflex prior to providing the breakfast tray.
B) Evaluating the client's ability to safely use the call light with the unaffected hand.
C) Modifying the client's dietary tray to include only mechanical soft items.
D) Assisting the client with transferring from the bed to a chair using a gait belt.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: D</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> DELEGATION<br>
Assisting a stable client with transfers using a gait belt is a standard procedure that falls safely within the scope of practice for a UAP. Assessing a gag reflex (Option A) and evaluating safety or task completion (Option B) are nursing assessments and evaluations that must be performed by the Registered Nurse. Modifying dietary orders or contents based on swallowing difficulty (Option C) requires clinical judgment and collaboration with the dietary department or speech therapy, which cannot be delegated to the UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 430
A client is in the rehabilitation phase following a severe right-sided ischemic stroke. The multidisciplinary team established a goal for the client to demonstrate improvement in unilateral neglect within one week. Which observation best indicates that the current treatment plan is effective?

A) The client asks the nurse to place their bedside table on the right side of the bed.
B) The client consistently turns their head to scan the left side of their dinner tray.
C) The client successfully dresses the right side of the body before the left side.
D) The client uses the left hand to successfully feed themselves independently.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
<b>Lens:</b> EVALUATION OF GOALS<br>
A right-sided ischemic stroke typically causes left-sided hemiplegia and left-sided unilateral neglect (the client is unaware of or ignores the left side of their body and environment). The most effective way to evaluate improvement in unilateral neglect is observing the client consciously compensating for it, such as actively turning their head to scan the left side of their environment or food tray. Placing items on the right side (Option A) accommodates the neglect rather than showing improvement in overcoming it. Clients should be taught to dress the affected (left) side first, not the right side (Option C). The client would likely use their right (unaffected) hand to feed themselves, as the left hand would be impaired (Option D). 
</details>
### Question 431
A nurse is caring for a client with an external ventricular drain (EVD) in place for continuous intracranial pressure (ICP) monitoring following a traumatic brain injury. Which clinical manifestation should the nurse recognize as the *earliest* sign of an impending complication related to worsening intracranial hypertension?

A) A widening pulse pressure and severe bradycardia.
B) Sudden onset of a unilaterally fixed and dilated pupil.
C) Increased restlessness and confusion without a clear cause.
D) A continuous ICP reading fluctuating between 10 and 12 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Strategy</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C) Increased restlessness and confusion without a clear cause.**

**Lens:** COMPLICATION RECOGNITION

**Rationale:** Early signs of increased intracranial pressure (ICP) primarily involve subtle changes in the client's level of consciousness (LOC). This occurs because the cerebral cortex is highly sensitive to early hypoxia and shifting pressure, manifesting as restlessness, confusion, lethargy, or agitation. 
- *Option A* describes Cushing's triad (widening pulse pressure, bradycardia, irregular respirations), which is a late and ominous sign of brainstem compression.
- *Option B* (a fixed and dilated pupil) indicates impending or occurring brain herniation, which is a life-threatening, late-stage complication.
- *Option D* describes a normal ICP reading (normal range is 5 to 15 mmHg), which does not indicate a complication.
</details>

---

### Question 432
The nurse is providing comprehensive discharge education to a client newly diagnosed with relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis (MS). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the necessary lifestyle modifications to prevent disease exacerbations?

A) "I should schedule my most physically demanding activities for the late afternoon."
B) "I will implement frequent rest periods and spread my daily tasks evenly."
C) "I will take a hot bath every evening to help relax my lower extremity muscle spasms."
D) "I will limit my fluid intake to 1 liter a day to prevent urinary incontinence issues."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Strategy</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B) "I will implement frequent rest periods and spread my daily tasks evenly."**

**Lens:** COMPLEX TEACHING

**Rationale:** Fatigue is one of the most common and debilitating symptoms of multiple sclerosis. Energy conservation techniques, such as pacing activities and scheduling frequent rest periods throughout the day, are essential to prevent overexertion, which can trigger an exacerbation.
- *Option A* is incorrect because energy levels are typically highest in the morning for clients with MS; strenuous activities should not be delayed until the afternoon when fatigue peaks.
- *Option C* is incorrect due to Uhthoff's phenomenon, where exposure to excessive heat (like a hot bath or sauna) temporarily worsens neurological symptoms in MS.
- *Option D* is incorrect because limiting fluid intake can lead to urinary tract infections (UTIs) and constipation, both of which are common triggers for MS exacerbations. Fluid intake should be maintained around 2 liters per day.
</details>

---

### Question 433
The registered nurse (RN) is planning care for a group of clients recovering from ischemic strokes. Which task is most appropriate to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Performing passive range-of-motion exercises on the flaccid extremities of a clinically stable client.
B) Assisting a client who recently failed a bedside swallow screen with their first oral meal.
C) Assessing the gag reflex of a client prior to assisting them with bedside oral hygiene.
D) Educating a client's family on how to properly transfer the client to a wheelchair using a gait belt.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Strategy</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A) Performing passive range-of-motion exercises on the flaccid extremities of a clinically stable client.**

**Lens:** DELEGATION

**Rationale:** Delegation to a UAP encompasses routine, standard, and unchanging procedures for stable clients. Performing passive range-of-motion (ROM) exercises is a standard intervention to prevent contractures in flaccid extremities post-stroke and is well within the UAP's scope of practice.
- *Option B* is unsafe to delegate because a client who failed a swallow screen has a high risk for aspiration and requires the RN or speech-language pathologist to manage their oral intake safely.
- *Option C* requires clinical assessment skills, which strictly belong to the RN and cannot be delegated.
- *Option D* involves providing initial education, which is solely an RN responsibility. The UAP may reinforce previous teaching but cannot initiate it.
</details>

---

### Question 434
A client is in the intensive care unit with continuous intracranial pressure (ICP) and arterial blood pressure monitoring following a severe head injury. Over the past four hours, the nurse notes the client's ICP has steadily increased from 14 mmHg to 22 mmHg, while the mean arterial pressure (MAP) has decreased from 85 mmHg to 70 mmHg. Based on this trend analysis, what is the most accurate interpretation of the client's neurological status?

A) The cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) is adequately compensating for the increased intracranial pressure.
B) The data indicates an impending brain herniation as evidenced by an increasing mean arterial pressure.
C) The client is showing expected physiological recovery trends for a severe traumatic brain injury.
D) The client's cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) has fallen to a dangerously low level, risking severe cerebral ischemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Strategy</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D) The client's cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) has fallen to a dangerously low level, risking severe cerebral ischemia.**

**Lens:** DATA TREND ANALYSIS

**Rationale:** Cerebral Perfusion Pressure (CPP) represents the pressure gradient driving blood flow to the brain and is calculated by subtracting the ICP from the MAP (CPP = MAP - ICP). 
Initially, the client's CPP was 71 mmHg (85 MAP - 14 ICP). Over four hours, the CPP has dropped to 48 mmHg (70 MAP - 22 ICP). The normal CPP range is 60 to 100 mmHg. A CPP falling below 50 mmHg indicates inadequate blood flow to the brain, placing the client at severe risk for ischemia and brain death. 
- *Option A* is incorrect because the CPP is failing to compensate and has dropped below the normal threshold. 
- *Option B* is incorrect because the MAP is steadily decreasing, not increasing. 
- *Option C* is incorrect because an rising ICP and falling MAP represent active neurological deterioration.
</details>

---

### Question 435
The interdisciplinary healthcare team has established a multi-day rehabilitation plan aimed at promoting functional independence for a client recovering from a left hemispheric ischemic stroke. Which observation by the nurse best indicates that the primary functional goals of this specific treatment plan are being met?

A) The client's blood pressure remains consistently below 140/90 mmHg while resting in bed.
B) The client correctly states the purpose of their newly prescribed daily antiplatelet medication.
C) The client utilizes their unaffected left arm and adaptive utensils to independently feed themselves.
D) The client exhibits an absence of left-sided visual neglect during a bedside neurological assessment.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Strategy</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C) The client utilizes their unaffected left arm and adaptive utensils to independently feed themselves.**

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF GOALS

**Rationale:** A left hemispheric stroke typically results in right-sided hemiparesis (weakness/paralysis on the right side of the body) and potential speech deficits (aphasia). The overarching goal of stroke rehabilitation is to achieve maximum *functional independence* despite residual neurological deficits. The client demonstrating the ability to adapt by using their unaffected left arm and specialized utensils to complete an Activity of Daily Living (ADL) such as eating directly shows the functional goals are being achieved.
- *Option A* indicates medical stability rather than functional rehabilitation progress.
- *Option B* evaluates the success of knowledge and medication teaching goals, but not physical functional independence.
- *Option D* is incorrect because visual/spatial neglect is a classic sign of a *right* hemispheric stroke, not a left hemispheric stroke. Therefore, evaluating for left-sided neglect is not the primary focus for this specific client's recovery.
</details>
